> The enjoyment of ˹worldly˺ desires—women, children, treasures of gold and silver, fine horses, cattle, and fertile land—has been made appealing to people. These are the pleasures of this worldly life, but with Allah is the finest destination

**A List Built to Be Recognised**

The verse names seven attractions, and the order is not random. It begins with two that a person loves — women and children — then moves to what he accumulates — gold and silver — then to what he uses to display and defend that wealth — horses — then to what feeds him — cattle — then to the ground he holds, described as sown land. The list travels from the heart outward to the estate, and every reader finds himself somewhere along it.

The ancient commentators noticed that the sequence follows the stages of a settled life: attachment, then family, then property, then the means of protecting property. That is also why the list has never aged. Horses are not the modern measure of status, but the category — the vehicle that advertises a man's standing — has kept its place in every economy since, and the verse's final item, productive land, remains the object of the oldest form of competition.

**"Made Appealing" — the Passive Voice and Its Meaning**

*Zuyyina* is passive: the appeal was made, laid on, arranged. The verse does not say that people chose to desire these things from a neutral starting point; it says the desire was placed in them, like decoration on a surface. The same verb is used of the world's attraction for those who reject the message: *"The life of this world has been made appealing to the disbelievers, and they mock the believers"* (2:212).

Nothing in the sentence calls the objects evil. A wife and children, gold and silver, horses, herds, and farmland are all described elsewhere in the Book as good things, and gratitude for them is commanded. What the verse establishes is that the appeal is an arrangement rather than a verdict, that the world is designed to be wanted. A person who understands the design is not fooled by his own appetite, and a person who mistakes appetite for evidence will spend his life measuring his worth by what he happens to want.

**"Pleasures of This Worldly Life" — the Word matāʿ**

The noun rendered "pleasures" is *matāʿ*, which carries the sense of goods, provisions, and the gear a traveller takes for a journey. Arabic uses it for luggage and for the merchandise of a market. It is not the word for a treasure that constitutes a person's wealth in himself; it is the word for what serves a purpose and then stops.

The Qur'an uses the term this way throughout its descriptions of the world. This life is *"no more than play, amusement, luxury, mutual boasting, and competition in wealth and children"*, like crops that flourish and then dry up (57:20), and like rain whose harvest the earth presents beautifully until it is mown down (10:24). The sūrah of Competition states the same with two lines of extraordinary brevity: *"Competition for more ˹gains˺ diverts you ˹from Allah˺, until you end up in ˹your graves˺"* (102:1–2). The word *matāʿ* is doing the work at the end of this verse, and it is a quiet word for a serious idea: what looks like a destination is equipment.

**The Diction That Punctuates the List**

Two details in the Arabic repay attention. Gold and silver are gathered in *qanāṭīr*, a plural of *qinṭār*, a measure the grammarians disputed — some described it as a weight of gold, others as a quantity that could not be counted. The translation's "treasures" preserves the amplification without inventing a figure. The commentators mostly agree that in this context the number is beside the point; the word is there to sound large, and it does.

The horses are *al-khayl al-musawwama*, which the lexicographers explained either as animals marked with a brand or as animals kept at pasture and left to graze freely. "Fine horses" captures the sense without choosing between the two readings; both describe a herd of value, whether the value lies in the brand on its flank or in the freedom of its pasture. The sentence is written to make the reader feel the weight of possessions, then to ask what they weigh.

**The Comparison, Not the Condemnation**

The verse ends by turning, and it turns without a rebuke: *but with Allah is the finest destination*. The word *maʾāb* is a place of return, from the root that gives "return" and "resort". The sentence does not say the world is worthless; it says the world is not the end of the road, and that the road's end is better.

The sūrah's ethics follow from that comparison. The man who owns all seven items on the list and knows where he is going has goods. The man who needs them to tell him who he is has a master. Nothing in the Book commands the abandonment of the list — the Prophet's companions owned wives, children, gold, horses, and lands — but everything in it refuses to let the list be the answer to the question of a man's worth.

**Where the Danger Actually Lies**

A reader could take this verse as an attack on desire itself, and the sūrah will not support that reading. What it attacks is the scaling of desire above everything else, and the Book states the danger in a form that leaves no room for ambiguity: *"If your parents and children and siblings and spouses and extended family and the wealth you have acquired and the trade you fear will decline and the homes you cherish—˹if all these˺ are more beloved to you than Allah and His Messenger and struggling in His Way, then wait until Allah brings about His Will"* (9:24).

The list in that verse is this list, expanded and given names. The issue is never the ownership of goods. It is the ranking of loves, and the ranking is settled by the question of what a person would sacrifice. The next verse answers the comparison with what the Book says is better.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:15

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "Shall I inform you of what is better than ˹all of˺ this? Those mindful ˹of Allah˺ will have Gardens with their Lord under which rivers flow, to stay there forever, and pure spouses, along with Allah's pleasure." And Allah is All-Seeing of ˹His˺ servants

**A Question That Makes the Reader Choose**

The verse opens with an invitation: *shall I inform you of what is better than all of this?* The question presupposes that the previous verse's list is already attractive — that the reader has already felt its pull — and it does not begin by attacking the list. It begins with comparison.

The rhetorical form does two things at once. It flatters the listener's attention, since a question draws a person in where a declaration leaves him at a distance, and it frames the rest of the verse as an answer. From here, everything said about the Garden stands in explicit competition with the seven attractions of the world. The reader is not being ordered to despise what he wants; he is being asked to compare it with something the speaker has information about.

**Rivers and Spouses and "To Stay There Forever"**

The description begins with the standard marks of the Garden: gardens belonging to the Lord, rivers running beneath them, permanence, and pure spouses. Each element corrects a limitation of the world it is being weighed against.

Rivers that flow under a garden are an image of abundance that does not have to be fought for; the Book's other descriptions add that they flow with rivers of water, milk, wine, and honey (47:15), and the dwellers are told that the rivers are theirs to manage and the fruits never exhausted. The spouses *made pure* are named in 2:25 and 4:57 with the promise that the dwellers will remain there always, and the purity is not only moral: it removes the weariness and disappointment that attend every human intimacy. Permanence is the last item precisely because it is the answer to the first verse's deepest problem — that everything desirable here is temporary.

The comparison is silent but total. Nothing on the earlier list is immune to loss. Nothing here can be lost.

**"Along With Allah's Pleasure" — the Item at the Top**

The verse does not end with the gardens. It adds *along with Allah's pleasure*, and the place of that clause is the point. In the sūrah of Repentance the same arrangement is stated with a decisive break in the sentence: *"and—above all—the pleasure of Allah. That is ˹truly˺ the ultimate triumph"* (9:72).

The Book describes this contentment as reciprocal, and its language for it is intimate. At 5:119 the declaration is *"Allah is pleased with them and they are pleased with Him"*, and the same pair of clauses closes 98:8. The fullest statement is given to the soul at the gate: *"Return to your Lord, well pleased ˹with Him˺ and well pleasing ˹to Him˺"* (89:28). If the gardens are the answer to the world's instability, this clause answers the world's loneliness — the deepest human want, which is not to own good things but to be welcomed by the one who made them.

The term rendered as pleasure is the same word the Book uses for the pursuit that begins in the present life. It speaks of people who seek God's good pleasure in their conduct long before they reach any reward (5:16; 48:29; 57:27; 59:8), and it sets that pursuit against its opposite in the form of a question: *"Are those who seek Allah's pleasure like those who deserve Allah's wrath?"* (3:162). The pair appears again in the sūrah of Muḥammad, where the people of the second kind are described as following what displeases God (47:28). The contentment named in this verse is therefore the end of a pursuit that begins now, and its opposite is not indifference but wrath.

**Who Enters: the Mindful, Not the Ascetic**

The condition given is a single word: *those mindful*. *Taqwā* here is the same quality the Book names at the beginning of the sūrah of the Cow: *"This is the Book! There is no doubt about it—a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah"* (2:2). It is a watchfulness, a guardedness about one's own conduct before God.

The choice of that word rather than a list of achievements matches the previous verse. If the world's goods are to be handled rather than renounced, the virtue required is not poverty. A person with money, family, and land can be mindful, and a person with nothing can be careless. What the Book is looking for is the awareness that keeps every possession in its place, and the promise is made to whoever carries it.

**"All-Seeing of His Servants" — Why the Verse Ends There**

The verse closes with an attribute, and the attribute is chosen to fit the promise: God sees His servants. The word *baṣīr* is not about eyesight in the ordinary sense; it means seeing with knowledge, seeing what a person actually is.

The clause guards the promise from exactly the abuse a promise invites. If the reward belonged to the poor or the celibate or the landless, people would compete for the outward state. Because the entry is mindfulness, and mindfulness lives where no one can inspect it, the reward depends on a knowledge that reaches inside a person. Public standing settles nothing, and hidden effort is not lost. A servant may be invisible to everyone in the world and fully visible where the decision is made.

**What the Verse Promises Is Not the Same Kind of Thing**

The comparison only works because the two terms are not two versions of one thing. What the world offers is *matāʿ*, gear for a road; what the verse offers is a destination, a permanent home, and the contentment of the one who built it. A traveller who mistakes a full saddlebag for the end of his journey does not become a man who arrives.

That is why the Book never asks a person to stop wanting. It asks him to want the right thing at the right rank, and it supplies the argument by putting the two on one page: seven lines of goods, then three gifts that cannot be taken away. The question at the top of the verse stays open for as long as the reader looks at both.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:16

> who pray, "Our Lord! We have believed, so forgive our sins and protect us from the torment of the Fire."

**The Clause That Tells Us Who They Are**

The previous verse ends with the phrase *All-Seeing of His servants*, and this verse picks the sentence up without a new beginning: *who pray*. The Arabic relative construction ties the people described here to the servants just named, so the reader learns that the mindful ones are identified by what they say to God when nobody else is listening.

That is the whole function of the verse in the sūrah's argument. A list of virtues could have been given; instead a prayer is given, because the Book's interest in this passage is not the catalogue of good behaviour but the relationship that produces it. The people who inherit the gardens are described in the act of addressing their Lord.

**The Qur'an Defines Groups by Their Supplication**

Naming a community by its prayer is a habit of this Book, and the habit is instructive. The people of understanding are introduced by what they ask for: *"˹They are˺ those who remember Allah while standing, sitting, and lying on their sides... ˹and pray˺, 'Our Lord! You have not created ˹all of˺ this without purpose. Glory be to You! Protect us from the torment of the Fire'"* (3:191). The balanced worshipper is the one who asks: *"Our Lord! Grant us the good of this world and the Hereafter, and protect us from the torment of the Fire"* (2:201). Even the angels who carry the Throne are quoted in prayer, interceding for the believers before God: *"Our Lord! You encompass everything in ˹Your˺ mercy and knowledge. So forgive those who repent and follow Your Way, and protect them from the torment of the Hellfire"* (40:7).

The same pattern appears here. A group is named by the words it repeats, which means the reader can test his own identity by what he says when he is alone and afraid.

**Faith Stated First, Forgiveness Asked Second**

The order inside the prayer is not accidental. The suppliant begins with a declaration — *we have believed* — and only then makes a request. Faith is presented as the ground on which a person can ask at all; there is no bargaining in the sentence, no accounting of merits, no argument that the request is deserved.

This is the Qur'an's ordinary grammar of prayer. The servant states his position before God honestly, both what he has carried out and what he has failed at, and the appeal rests on who God is rather than on what the servant has done. In the prayer taught at the end of the sūrah of the Cow, the same structure holds together a long list of petitions: each one is grounded in a recognition of the servant's weakness rather than in a claim of strength.

**The Two Requests and the Shape of Human Danger**

The petition itself has two halves, and the halves correspond to the direction a person's danger comes from. What is past needs *forgiveness* — the record already written, which no effort can now change. What is coming needs *protection from the Fire* — the outcome that has not yet been decided and can still be altered by how a person lives.

Holding both in one sentence is a small education. A person who thinks only about the future becomes anxious and self-absorbed; a person who thinks only about the past becomes a defendant who has already lost. The prayer asks for both directions at once, and it does so in a single breath, which is how the Qur'an usually teaches a balanced religious psychology.

**"Shield Us" and "Forgive": the Roots Behind the Verbs**

The word rendered "protect us" is *qinā*, from the same root as *taqwā*, the mindfulness that verse 15 made the condition of entry. A shield is a thing that stands between a person and a blow; the verb means to place such a covering over someone. The connection between the two verses is therefore deliberate at the level of the lexicon: the mindful person is one who has been shielded, and his mindfulness is the trace of that protection in his conduct. The same root appears in the praise of the Helpers of Madinah, who preferred others over themselves — *"And whoever is saved from the selfishness of their own souls, it is they who are ˹truly˺ successful"* (59:9), a promise repeated at 64:16. The danger a person most needs shielding from turns out to be inside him. Forgiveness covers what is done; the shield stands between a man and what he might still do.

The verb *ighfir* comes from a root whose first sense is to cover, to conceal, to draw a screen over something. A *mighfar* is the covering a fighter wears over his head. The Arabic word for forgiveness is therefore not primarily about forgetting; it is about covering, and the covering has a purpose, since what is covered is no longer exposed to the punishment it deserved.

This is why the Book joins forgiveness with mercy so often and so tightly. The sin is not annulled as a fact — the record of a person's life is not falsified — it is covered, and the covering is an act of the one who has the right to expose. The suppliant in this verse knows what he is asking. He is not requesting a technicality or a lenient reinterpretation of his acts. He is asking the owner of the record to draw something over it.

**A Prayer Said in the Plural**

Both requests are phrased in the first person plural: *forgive us*, *protect us*. The verse is describing a group of people who speak as one even when they are alone, and the grammar repeats the opening — *our Lord* — which is the same address the well-grounded used in verse 8 and again in verse 9.

The plural does more than express solidarity. It puts the person praying inside a body of people who are all making the same admission, which removes the two temptations that attend private confession: despair at one's own case, and pride at being better than others. A servant who asks for the community is in no position to hold a rank above its members, and a servant who includes himself in the request has already admitted that the request is necessary. The community of the mindful is constituted in this verse by the words they say together.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:17

> ˹It is they˺ who are patient, sincere, obedient, and charitable, and who pray for forgiveness before dawn

**Five Marks, and the Order They Come In**

The verse draws a portrait in a line. The people promised the gardens are *patient*, meaning steadfast under pressure; *sincere*, meaning truthful in word and intention; *obedient* in the specific sense of devotion that stands and waits before God; *charitable*, spending from what they have; and finally those who ask forgiveness in the last hours of the night.

The sequence rewards a slow reading. Steadfastness comes first because nothing else can be sustained without it; truthfulness follows because a task performed under pressure invites compromise in speech; devotion follows truthfulness, since a person who has settled the question of honesty can then settle into worship; generosity comes after, because giving is what a settled soul does with its goods. And behind all of it, unannounced, stands the night prayer, which is what the verse considers the last mark of the group.

**Qānitūn: the Posture Behind the Word**

The translation renders *qānit* as "obedient", which is accurate but thin. The word carries standing, constancy, silence, and a settled conformity to what is asked — the posture of someone who has taken his place and stayed there. The Book uses it of Mary, told to be *devout to her Lord* along with those who bow (3:43), and of the women and men who obey with devotion in 33:31.

In older Arabic a *qānit* could be the one who stands silent in prayer, and the meanings connect: devotion here is not an emotional warmth that comes and goes, but a stance. A person who has understood what he is before God does not need to be persuaded into position again each time his feelings change. That is why the word sits between truthfulness and charity in a list of durable qualities; it is the quality that makes the others durable.

**Patient Without Qualification**

The first word of the portrait is *ṣābirīn*, patient or steadfast, and the Book leaves it unqualified. No object is attached, no period is specified, and no kind of hardship is singled out, which is how the Qur'an usually uses the term. The patience in view is not one virtue among several but the capacity to hold a position under weight of any kind.

The commentators grouped the applications under three headings and left them open: patience in obeying what has been commanded, patience in avoiding what has been forbidden, and patience with what cannot be changed — loss, illness, and the wrongs of other people. All three require a person to keep doing something he does not feel like doing, and all three are invisible when they succeed. A patient man at the funeral of his child looks like a man who is not weeping rather than like a man performing a virtue, which is one reason the Book tends to name patience first and reward it last.

**Charity in the Qur'an's Sense Is Not Only Money**

The participle rendered "charitable" belongs to the verb *anfaqa*, to spend. The commentaries were careful to say that the spending in view is not limited to currency: effort, knowledge, time, and influence all fall under it. The Book's praise for those who spend attaches a condition to the act — that it be in the way of God — which is what distinguishes the generosity being praised from generosity performed for reputation.

The Book also insists that the giving of the poorest is not disqualified by its size. The widow's two coins in the prophetic tradition, and the repeated Qur'anic emphasis that God does not look at the quantity of a gift but at what the giver had and intended, belong to the same teaching. The verse's list therefore has room for the person who owns almost nothing, provided he is willing to let go of what he has.

**"Before Dawn": an Hour Chosen for Its Darkness**

The final phrase translates *asḥār*, the last part of the night before the sky begins to lighten, and the choice is deliberate. The Book describes the night prayer as a habit of the righteous in more than one place: they *"used to sleep only little in the night, and pray for forgiveness before dawn"* (51:17–18), and the Prophet is told to rise at the last part of the night in additional prayer, so that his Lord may raise him to a station of praise (17:79).

The hour has nothing to recommend it to a person seeking an audience. It is the time when sleep is heaviest and no one is watching, when the body argues against getting up and there is no reputation to be gained by doing so. The Book mentions those who stand in it as a class of their own: *"˹They are˺ those who spend ˹a good portion of˺ the night, prostrating themselves and standing before their Lord"* (25:64), and the command to the Prophet runs to the same effect: *"Stand all night ˹in prayer˺ except a little"* (73:2).

**Why the List Ends in the Dark**

Placing the night prayer last makes the verse's argument complete. The earlier items can be seen; a patient man, a truthful man, and a generous man are all visible to neighbours, and even the most sincere have ways of knowing and receiving the respect their conduct draws. The pre-dawn request for forgiveness cannot be seen by anyone but the one addressed.

That is the note this verse chooses to end on, and it is consistent with the sūrah's insistence that the accounting happens where God sees. The same people who confessed their sins at the beginning of the previous verse are here shown confessing again while the world sleeps. The habit of asking forgiveness in the dark is the sign that the earlier virtues were not being performed for anyone.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:18

> Allah ˹Himself˺ is a Witness that there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—and so are the angels and people of knowledge. He is the Maintainer of justice. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—the Almighty, All-Wise

**A Testimony, Stated in Court Form**

The verse is built like a legal document. It names a testifier, gives the content of the testimony, and then adds two further witnesses: the angels and the people of knowledge. What is being testified to is not an opinion about God but a fact about Him — that there is no deity except Him — and the fact is presented as something that can be established by witnesses.

That rhetorical shape is itself an argument. In ordinary religious language, the unity of God is a doctrine to be believed. Here it is a matter on which testimony has been given, by God first and by two classes of created witnesses after Him. The reader is not being asked to accept an assertion; he is being shown a sworn statement and told who signed it.

**The Three Witnesses and the Order Between Them**

God's witness comes first, and it needs no corroboration: He testifies about Himself, which the Book elsewhere describes as a sufficiency — *"And Allah ˹alone˺ is sufficient as a Witness"* (4:166). The angels come next because they are the created beings whose knowledge of God is unclouded by appetite or interest. The people of knowledge come last, and their inclusion is the verse's real surprise.

A human being qualifies as a witness to the unity of God not by lineage, authority, or office, but by knowledge. That single word opens the testimony to anyone who studies the evidence in the world and in revelation and reaches the conclusion the verse states, and it also sets the standard against which a scholar's claim is measured. The *ʿulamāʾ* of this verse are not the custodians of a privilege; they are the ones whose learning leads them to bear witness.

**"Maintainer of Justice": the Phrase in the Middle**

Between the two halves of the testimony sits a sentence about God's own standing: *He is the Maintainer of justice*. The participle *qāʾim* conveys standing, upholding, and keeping something in place, and *qisṭ* is equity — the giving to each what is due. God is not described as merely just in His rulings. He is described as the one who keeps justice standing in existence.

This is why the verse can move directly from a doctrine about God to a fact about the world. Justice in the Book is not a matter of chance good behaviour among people; it is a structure that God maintains, which is why injustice in the human record is always temporary and always accounted for. The attribute also explains why the two names at the end of the verse are power and wisdom: justice requires both the capacity to enforce it and the knowledge of what is actually due.

**Testifying to God and Testifying in Court**

The Book repeatedly takes the vocabulary of this verse into the public sphere. Believers are commanded to become witnesses in the same sense: *"O believers! Stand firm for Allah and bear true testimony. Do not let the hatred of a people lead you to injustice"* (5:8), and the command is sharpened to include the hardest cases: *"Stand firm for justice as witnesses for Allah even if it is against yourselves, your parents, or close relatives"* (4:135).

The connection is not decorative. A person who has understood that God's unity is a witnessed fact has accepted a world in which facts are established by testimony and not by power, and the habits of that acceptance travel with him into the courtroom and the market. The Qur'an treats perjury as a religious crime rather than a procedural one, which is consistent with a Book that argues from the reliability of witnesses.

**The Sentence That Opens and Closes the Verse**

The declaration *there is no god except Him* appears twice in this verse, once as the content of the testimony and once attached to the names Almighty and All-Wise. The repetition is a frame, and it mirrors the structure of the sūrah itself: the same words opened it at 3:2, and they will return at intervals as the argument develops.

A frame does work that a single statement cannot. It tells the reader that everything between the two declarations belongs inside them, that the intervening material about books, messengers, desires, and arguments is a set of consequences of the fact stated first and restated last. In a sūrah that will address communities with competing scriptures, that frame is a claim about jurisdiction: no dispute arising in this sūrah stands outside the testimony of verse 18.

**What a Person Does With a Fact Like This**

A testimony produces obligation in a way that a preference does not. A person may argue about taste, but a witness who has given evidence on a matter of fact can only be believed or contradicted. The verse leaves no third option, and the angels and the learned are named precisely so that the reader cannot claim he stands alone with an opinion.

The response the Book expects is not enthusiasm but uprightness. If there is one God, then the question of how a person's life is ordered is no longer a matter of private inclination, and the testimony becomes the standard against which conduct is judged.

The three witnesses also settle a question about certainty that readers of this sūrah are likely to be carrying. The verse does not present the unity of God as a doctrine that reasonable people may or may not reach; it presents it as a fact that has been attested by God, by the angels who know Him directly, and by the learned who have examined what He made. A reader who doubts is not being asked to trust a rumour from a distant age. He is being placed in front of a record of testimony and asked what he intends to do with it. The next verse names what accepting that testimony is called, and how the communities that had received earlier books handled the same question.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:19

> Certainly, Allah's only Way is Islam. Those who were given the Scripture did not dispute ˹among themselves˺ out of mutual envy until knowledge came to them. Whoever denies Allah's signs, then surely Allah is swift in reckoning

**"Islam" as a Description Before It Became the Name of a Community**

The Arabic word *islām* means handing oneself over, submitting, placing one's affairs in the hands of another. Long before it named the community that gathered around the Prophet Muhammad, the Book applies its participle to people who lived centuries earlier. Abraham and Jacob are recorded advising their children: *"Indeed, Allah has chosen for you this faith; so do not die except in ˹a state of full˺ submission"* (2:132), and the disciples of Jesus answer him in the same vocabulary — we believe in Allah, so bear witness that we have submitted (3:52; 5:111).

The sūrah's later verse — whoever seeks a way other than Islam, it will never be accepted from them (3:85) — is often read back into this one, and the two do belong together. What the Book presents here is a single demand made of every person: not membership in a particular community, but the surrender of the self to God. The community that bears the name is the community that has been given the task of carrying that demand to everyone else.

**The Dispute Began in Envy, Not in Ignorance**

The second sentence gives the diagnosis of the division among the scriptured peoples: they fell into disagreement *out of mutual envy*, and they did so *after* knowledge reached them. The accusation is precise. The problem was never an absence of information; the clearer the truth became, the harder the parties pushed against one another.

The Book states the same history elsewhere with the same emphasis: it was only after the clear proof came to the People of the Book that they became divided (98:4). The Arabic term for envy and transgressive rivalry, *baghy*, carries the sense of wanting what another has and being unwilling to see him hold it. Applied to revelation, it means the parties could not accept that the truth might arrive through someone from outside their own community.

**Knowledge That Made Division Worse**

The most troubling element of the verse is not the division but its timing. Disagreement in ignorance can be excused as a stage; disagreement after knowledge carries a different weight, and the Book treats it as a moral failure rather than an intellectual one.

The Qur'an's wider account of knowledge explains why. Knowledge is a test as well as a gift, and the Book says so about its own parables: some hear them and are guided, others argue about them and go further astray (2:26). A person can be taught every relevant fact and still use it to defend a position he wants. The verse's point is that evidence does not force agreement; it removes the excuse for disagreement.

**The Envy That Keeps Communities Apart**

Read in its historical setting, the verse explains the shape of the religious landscape the Prophet faced in Madinah: communities who shared a great deal of scripture, a common claim about God, and a settled unwillingness to recognise truth arriving from anywhere but among themselves. The Book's response to that unwillingness is not to deny each community's heritage but to insist on its purpose.

This is why the sūrah spends so much of its length addressing the People of the Book with respect for what they hold and severity about what they do with it. The charge is not that they possess scripture. The charge is that a shared truth became a possession to be defended, and that the defence became more important than the truth it was supposed to serve.

**"Swift in Reckoning": the Word ḥisāb**

The verse ends with a warning, and the word chosen is accounting: *Allah is swift in reckoning*. *Ḥisāb* means both the counting of a debt and the settling of it, and the Book uses it for the final audit of a life. The accompanying attribute, swiftness, belongs to the same image: the accounts are not delayed by the volume of business.

The Qur'an describes the same audit with an image of scales that will not miss the smallest weight: *"We will set up the scales of justice on the Day of Judgment, so no soul will be wronged in the least"* (21:47). The verses about the reckoning of deeds are not there to terrorise; they are the background against which the human claim to superiority loses its force. Whoever denies the signs, whether out of envy or out of pride, will meet an account that has no interest in the rank he held among his people.

**The Verse's Three Movements**

The sentence moves in three steps, and each of them is taken up again later in the sūrah. The first names the religion that God accepts, and the passage that follows states that whoever seeks a way other than submission will not have it accepted from him (3:85). The second describes the dispute that began after knowledge arrived, and the warning given to the believers against dividing after the proofs reached them repeats the same diagnosis for a new community (3:105). The third declares that God is swift in reckoning, and the same word for reckoning is used at the end of the sūrah, where it closes the description of those whose reward is with their Lord (3:199).

Read as a unit, the verse states a fact, explains a history, and names an outcome, and the three parts reappear in the chapter in the same order. The community that recites it is being taught to expect that a message will be disputed after it is known, and to understand the reckoning that follows as the Book's own answer to that dispute. There is a further consequence hidden in the opening words *Allah's only Way*. If the criterion is submission rather than the name of a community, then the history of the communities that received revelation becomes a warning rather than a source of standing. The people who possessed the Book lost nothing by possessing it; the Book says they lost their footing because they used it as a position to defend. A reader from any community is meant to see his own situation in that sentence, which is why the verse does not end in a verdict on someone else's ancestors.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:20

> So if they argue with you ˹O Prophet˺, say, "I have submitted myself to Allah, and so have my followers." And ask those who were given the Scripture and the illiterate ˹people˺, "Have you submitted yourselves ˹to Allah˺?" If they submit, they will be ˹rightly˺ guided. But if they turn away, then your duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah is All-Seeing of ˹His˺ servants

**When the Argument Ends, a Position Is Stated**

The previous verse described a dispute that had nothing to do with missing information. This verse answers the situation: the Prophet is told what to say when the arguing begins, and what he is told to say is not an argument at all. He states his own position and asks his questioners to state theirs.

The move is more powerful than another round of proofs. Debates in which each side holds a partial claim can continue indefinitely; a plain declaration of where a person stands ends the exchange by transferring the matter to the audience. The Prophet is instructed to say what he has done with his own life and to ask the others what they have done with theirs, which converts a contest of interpretations into a question every listener must answer privately.

**"I Have Submitted Myself" — a Verb, Not a Label**

The Arabic is *aslamtu*, from the same root as *islām* in the verse before it: I have handed myself over. The statement is an act, described in the first person singular, and it is followed immediately by a claim about a community: *and so have my followers*.

That second clause is a remarkable thing to say at the beginning of a mission. The Prophet speaks for a body of people who have entered the same submission, and his own claim is put on the same footing as theirs. The verse also quietly locates the standard of *islām* where the sūrah has already placed it: not in genealogy, not in the possession of books, but in the handing over of the self. A messenger who declares that he and his followers have submitted is inviting his questioners to compare their own standing with that, not with an ancestry.

**The Two Groups Who Are Asked**

The question goes to two audiences: *those who were given the Scripture* and *the illiterate*. The second term, *al-ummīyūn*, describes people without a revealed book — those who had no scripture to argue from, and among whom the Prophet himself appeared, which is why the Book calls him an unlettered prophet (7:158).

Pairing the two groups is the sūrah's way of removing a distinction that had organised the religious world. People with ancient scriptures and people without them are asked exactly the same question, and the same consequence is attached to the same answer. Neither possessing a book nor lacking one substitutes for the submission the question demands, and no community can claim that the question was addressed to someone else.

**"If They Submit, They Are Guided"**

The answer given is not a reward for joining a movement. The verse says that submission *is* guidance, and the equation is deliberate: the thing that saves a person is the thing the verse names, not the community he joins. A reader who takes the sentence seriously is being told that the boundary runs through individuals rather than around nations.

The mercy in this construction deserves notice too. The Book does not say that whoever submits becomes a member of a favoured party; it says his path has been set right. Guidance in this vocabulary is a direction rather than a status, and a person who has taken the direction holds it as a direction — something he must continue to travel, not a certificate he once acquired.

**"Your Duty Is Only to Deliver the Message"**

The verse closes the invitation by removing a burden the Prophet might have carried: the outcome is not his responsibility. If the questioners turn away, his task is finished rather than failed, and the Book says as much elsewhere with unusual directness: *"So, ˹continue to˺ remind ˹all, O Prophet˺, for your duty is only to remind. You are not ˹there˺ to compel them ˹to believe˺"* (88:21–22).

The same limit is stated about guidance generally: *"You are not responsible for people's guidance ˹O Prophet˺—it is Allah Who guides whoever He wills"* (2:272). The instruction shapes how the message is delivered in practice. A messenger who believes he must produce the result will be tempted to coerce, to exaggerate, or to trade away parts of the message; a messenger who knows his duty ends at delivery is free to be honest. The Book's model of preaching rests on that division of labour.

**The Attribute That Sees Servants**

The verse ends with the same closing attribute that ended verse 15: *Allah is All-Seeing of His servants*. Its placement is a final reminder about where the delivery is judged, and by whom. The messenger is not asked to grade his audience, and the audience is not asked to grade the messenger.

Both are servants of the one who sees them, and the sūrah has already told its readers how accurately He sees. The people who refuse and the people who submit are not merely counted; they are seen, with their reasons and their private arguments included. Nothing in the closing clause invites a community to assess its own membership. It invites each person to consider what is visible to the one who will assess him.

There is also a practical lesson about how a believer handles disagreement. The Prophet is given a formula that avoids both silence and escalation: state the position, ask the question, leave the outcome. Applied to the arguments in the following verses, this means the invitation stays open even while the record of what the other side has done is being described without flinching. The messenger's duty ends at delivery, and the Book repeats the point because the temptation to go past it is constant — in a person, in a preacher, and in a community with power.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:21

> Indeed, those who deny Allah's signs, kill the prophets unjustly, and kill people who stand up for justice—give them good news of a painful punishment

**A Charge in Three Steps, Each Worse Than the Last**

The verse lists three acts and builds them into a single indictment. It begins with denial of God's signs, moves to the killing of prophets, and ends with the killing of people whose only distinguishing feature is that they stand up for justice. The sequence is not a list of unrelated crimes; it is a description of one temper as it advances.

The logic connecting the three is that each step makes the next possible. A person who has decided that the signs do not oblige him has removed the authority that would restrain him; a community that has killed the messengers who corrected it has established that correction is fatal; and what remains is a settled hostility to anyone, prophet or not, who says that something is wrong. The verse describes a religious establishment that has turned its own corruption into policy.

**Who Are "Those Who Enjoin Justice"?**

The middle of the verse concerns prophets, but the end concerns people who stand up for justice. The Arabic phrase describes those who command equity — a category that includes prophets and extends past them to ordinary men and women who insist on what is right in the presence of power.

The commentators found the point significant, and their evidence is prophetic. The Prophet is reported to have said that among the greatest forms of struggle is *a word of justice spoken before a tyrannical ruler* (Sunan Abī Dāwūd 4344; Sunan al-Tirmidhī 2174, graded ḥasan by virtue of corroborating narrations). The Book itself provides a portrait of such a man at the edge of a city, running toward danger to support the messengers (28:20–21, discussed at 28:81's context in this tafsīr's treatment of that sūrah): a figure whose brief narrative ends with the reward of Paradise. The verse's indictment therefore covers more than the murder of prophets. It covers the murder of anyone who says that the powerful must be just.

**"Give Them Good News" — the Inversion of a Kind Word**

The command to announce a painful punishment uses the language of glad tidings, and the word is deliberately wrong. In the Book, *bushrā* is the announcement of good: a child to an old man, mercy to the righteous, the garden to the faithful. Here the verb is applied to the sentence of a court.

The same startling inversion appears elsewhere: *"Give good news of a painful punishment to hypocrites"* (4:138). The figure works because it refuses to soften the message at the last moment. A sentence delivered as news is spoken in the tone of a messenger reporting what has been decided, and the reader who flinches at the wording has understood the point the Book is making: the most terrible events in this verse are reported in the calmest grammar available.

**The Killing of the Prophets in the Record of Israel**

The Book presents the killing of messengers as a recurring feature of the history of the Children of Israel, and it does so without hedging. In the wilderness narrative, the punishment is traced directly to it: *"they invited the displeasure of Allah for rejecting Allah's signs and unjustly killing the prophets"* (2:61). The charge is repeated in the account of the covenant broken and renewed: *"˹They were condemned˺ for breaking their covenant, rejecting Allah's signs, killing the prophets unjustly"* (4:155).

The pattern is stated as normal rather than exceptional in 5:70: *"Whenever a messenger came to them with what they did not desire, they denied some and killed others."* The verse above inherits that record and treats it as a habit that generates its own future, and the Book addresses the community that carried the habit in the present tense.

**What the Commentators Passed Down**

Commentators relate that this verse was understood to refer to particular groups among the Children of Israel, and some transmit a report that forty-three prophets were killed in a single morning and that a company of men who stood up to denounce the killing were killed with them on the same day. The report is found in the tafsīr works of Ibn Abī Ḥātim, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr rather than in the sound collections, and its chains are not reliable. What may be said with confidence is the pattern the Qur'an itself asserts: persecution of the prophets ran through the generations, and those who spoke for justice after them shared their fate. The verse gives the reader the pattern in one sentence, which is all that the argument requires.

**The Warning for a Community That Kills Its Conscience**

The verse closes with the punishment and leaves it there; the following verse will describe what happens to such people's deeds. Read on its own, the sentence sets out a rule that applies to any establishment in any period, religious or otherwise: the killing of the ones who correct you does not silence the judgement, it confirms it.

A community may be certain that it is defending God's religion while removing the people who keep insisting that God's religion requires something of it. The verse names that conduct, and it refuses to call it devotion. It calls it murder, and it attaches to it an announcement phrased as though the condemned were receiving a favour.

Two words in the sentence carry the weight: *unjustly*, which rules out the case of a true legal sentence carried out by a legitimate authority, and the present tense of the participles, which describes the conduct as an ongoing practice rather than a single historical crime. The verse is not reporting an atrocity for the record. It is describing a class of people whom the reader may meet in any generation: those who deny what God has shown, silence those who speak for Him, and have no doubt about their own standing while doing it.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:22

> They are the ones whose deeds are wasted in this world and the Hereafter. And they will have no helpers

**Deeds That Come to Nothing**

The Arabic verb is *ḥabiṭa*, used of something that loses its value and effect, and the Book applies it to the works of people whose religious effort was considerable. The word appears in several places with the same meaning: associating others with God voids a person's deeds (39:65), and so does despising what God has revealed — *"That is because they detest what Allah has revealed, so He has rendered their deeds void"* (47:9).

The verse therefore concerns a category of person who would be surprised to hear the sentence. The previous verse described people who denied the signs, killed the messengers, and killed the advocates of justice — not people who did nothing. What is wasted is not idleness but activity: prayer, charity, pilgrimage, recitation of the scriptures they inherited, all of it undone. The Book's warning here is not about effort; it is about the ground in which effort is planted.

**"In This World and the Hereafter"**

The loss is stated twice, and the two halves describe different kinds of failure. In this world, the work loses its fruit: the Book compares the deeds of those who disbelieve in their Lord to *"ashes fiercely blown away by wind on a stormy day. They will gain nothing from what they have earned"* (14:18). The same image returns at the Day of Judgment, when God turns to whatever good they did and reduces it to scattered dust (25:23).

In the Hereafter the verdict is simpler: what weight there was has gone. *"It is they who reject the signs of their Lord and their meeting with Him, rendering their deeds void, so We will not give their deeds any weight on Judgment Day"* (18:105). A scale with nothing on it settles the question, and the verse's double statement makes sure the reader understands that both currencies — the reputation of the world and the reward of the next — are lost by the same act.

**Why the Intention Decides the Deed**

The commentators explained the waste by pointing at intention. A good act performed by someone who has withheld the recognition owed to God is not a partial good that God overlooks; it is an act whose author has refused the relationship that would give it meaning.

The principle is uncomfortable because it applies to worship. The Book never treats religious performance as a deposit that earns automatic return; it treats it as a gift offered to someone, and a gift offered to the wrong party or with contempt for the recipient is not a gift. The men of Madinah and Makkah who recited scripture and prayed were not being told their practice was too small. They were being told that what they had built was standing on the wrong foundation.

**"No Helpers" — the Removal of the Social Net**

The verse ends with a phrase that carries a great deal of the ancient world's reality. In that culture a person's security was his clan: helpers who would avenge him, ransom him, or plead for him. The Book repeatedly uses the impossibility of helpers in the Hereafter as the closing note of a warning, and it names each of the supports a person might hope for.

*"Guard yourselves against the Day on which no soul will be of help to another. No intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken, and no help will be given"* (2:48). The wrongdoers will have neither a close friend nor an intercessor to be heard (40:18), and where they had counted on the mediation of the righteous, the answer is flat: *"So the pleas of intercessors will be of no benefit to them"* (74:48). The verse above states the same in two words. No alliance survives into a court where the judge needs no allies.

**The Individual Standing Alone**

Taken with the previous verse, this verse produces an image that the sūrah returns to from different angles. A person arrives with an entourage of supporters, or he arrives alone. The text describes the second possibility without softening it, and the fact that the Book has to state it at all tells the reader how strong the illusion of collective protection can be.

The verses that follow will show people trying to settle their affairs in this world by turning away from an authority they dislike. The sentence here is the reminder that the settling they avoid in this life will be conducted without a single advocate once the accounts are opened.

**Why the Warning Is Worth Reading Carefully**

A reader may be tempted to assign this verse to a distant group and move on. The Book makes that reading difficult by the way it phrases the charge, and the sūrah itself is careful about quantity: the previous verse described people who deny, kill, and kill again, and the sentence here follows that description.

What the reader is given is a principle rather than a demographic. Effort invested against God is not a lesser investment; it is a loss, and the loss is reported in advance so that it can be avoided while a person still has time to change the ground of his work.

The order of the two verses is also a lesson in how the Qur'an argues. It states the crime first — denial, killing, killing — and the consequence second, so that the penalty is seen as an answer to something rather than as an arbitrary decree. Readers who find the Book's warnings severe are being invited to trace the severity back to its cause. In this case the cause is a pattern of conduct that persisted generation after generation in the presence of messengers, and the sentence is proportionate to it.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:23

> Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scriptures? Yet when they are invited to the Book of Allah to settle their disputes, some of them turn away heedlessly

**"A Portion of the Scriptures" — the Precision of the Phrase**

The verse does not say that these people were given the Scripture; it says they were given *a portion* of it, and the difference is the whole portrait. The Book uses the same phrase at 4:44 and 4:51 about people whose relationship with revelation is partial, and the choice of wording marks the gap between possessing texts and being possessed by their purpose.

A portion is enough to be held responsible for, and not enough to live on. The reader is looking at people who have inherited a real sacred book, studied it, built institutions around it, and yet whose use of it is selective enough that the Book describes their share as a fraction. The next verse will describe the doctrine that made the fraction comfortable.

**Invited to the Book of Allah**

The invitation is described as a call to *the Book of Allah* to settle a dispute. Commentators discussed which book is meant here, and the readings differ: some understood the reference to the Torah, since the Torah was the book these people already possessed and the one that could settle a matter within their own community; others read it more broadly as the revelation that had now come to the Prophet.

The ambiguity is not damaging, because the argument works either way. The Book's complaint at 5:43 uses the narrower reading as its premise: *"But why do they come to you for judgment when they ˹already˺ have the Torah containing Allah's judgment, then they turn away after all? They are not ˹true˺ believers"* (5:43). If the disputants are asked to consult their own scripture and refuse, the refusal is a verdict on their attachment to it. If they are asked to accept the judgement of God's latest messenger and refuse, the refusal is a verdict on their willingness to let God decide at all. Both roads end at the same wall.

**The Judicial Setting and the Refusal**

The scene is a courtroom rather than a classroom. The invitation is to have a case decided, and the Book's other account of the same behaviour makes the setting explicit: *"And as soon as they are called to Allah and His Messenger so he may judge between them, a group of them turns away"* (24:48).

There is a reason the Book frames the issue as judgment instead of instruction. A person can listen to teaching and postpone; a person who accepts a judge has already conceded the authority of the decision before he knows what the decision will be. That concession is what the people of this verse refuse, and the sūrah states the standard elsewhere without qualification: *"they will never be ˹true˺ believers until they accept you ˹O Prophet˺ as the judge in their disputes, and find no resistance within themselves against your decision and submit wholeheartedly"* (4:65). The hesitation being described is not doubt about evidence. It is a refusal in advance.

**"Some of Them" — the Verse Stops Short of Collectivising**

The turn away is attributed to *some of them*, and the qualifier is deliberate. The Book distinguishes carefully within communities, and it does so in verses that would have been much easier to write as general condemnations.

The same restraint appears in the stories of covenant and covenant-breaking told elsewhere: *"Do you believe in some of the Scripture and reject the rest?"* (2:85) addresses a specific conduct without declaring every member of a community guilty of it. The Qur'an's polemics are aimed at positions and practices, and the reader is expected to keep the distinction that the text itself keeps. A community has custodians, dissenters, and quiet people, and the sentence here reaches only one of those groups.

**Turning Away "Heedlessly" — the Posture of the Refusal**

The last word describes the manner of the turn: heedless, inattentive, walking away. The Arabic participle *muʿriḍūn* comes from the motion of turning one's side to someone, and the Book uses it repeatedly for the physical gesture of refusal — the shoulder presented to a speaker so that his words no longer arrive.

It is a telling detail for a verse about people who own texts, because it suggests the refusal is not the result of a difficult argument. They have not been defeated by reasoning or exhausted by a debate; they have decided not to listen. In the account the Book gives of the same problem elsewhere, the custodians of the scripture knew the answer before the question was put, and the turning away had already happened in the heart.

**What the Invitation Offered**

Nothing in the sentence suggests that the invitation was hostile. The disputants were offered a resolution, at their own level, from a source they claimed to honour. The Book's account of that possibility is worth holding on to, since it explains why the following verses move from description to a warning about the Day of Judgment.

An invitation refused is not the same as an invitation never made. The verse ends with the refusal and leaves the reader to draw the conclusion about what remains.

The verse also records a detail about the community's inner life that is easy to miss. These are people whose disputes were real enough that they sought judgments, and who nevertheless rejected the forum that would have decided them. The Book's interest is not in what the cases were. It is in the fact that a community with a sacred text in its possession could still prefer a settlement it controlled to one it would have to accept. That preference, and not the absence of scripture, is what the sūrah keeps identifying as the disease.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:24

> This is because they say, "The Fire will not touch us except for a few days." They have been deceived in their faith by their wishful lying

**The Claim That Made the Refusal Comfortable**

The previous verse described people turning away from a book that could settle their disputes. This verse gives the reason: a doctrine about their own future. They believed that whatever fire awaited them would be brief, and the belief was doing steady work in their lives, because a person who expects to be scorched lightly can afford to ignore the corrections he is offered. The convenience of the doctrine is part of what the sūrah is exposing, since a claim that removes the cost of disobedience will be defended long after its grounds have failed.

The Qur'an quotes the claim in an earlier sūrah in nearly the same words: *"˹Some of˺ the Jews claim, 'The Fire will not touch us except for a number of days.' Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ 'Have you taken a pledge from Allah—for Allah never breaks His word—or are you ˹just˺ saying about Allah what you do not know?'"* (2:80). The formulation there exposes the logic. An exemption from punishment is a promise, and promises are made by the one who owns the punishment. Nobody had been told.

**Where the Number Came From**

Commentators searched for the origin of the "few days" and came back with more than one report. Some said the figure referred to the forty nights during which the Israelites worshipped the calf, on the theory that a matching term of punishment had been fixed. Others reported a scheme of seven days, one for each millennium of the world's duration as they calculated it.

The reports are not agreed, and the disagreement itself is the interesting part. What the Book records is not a fixed arithmetic but the habit of producing one. A community that has decided it will not be punished permanently will find a text, a calculation, or a tradition to justify the conclusion, and the calculation will look precise enough to be repeated from the pulpit. The verse names the builder of the doctrine as *wishful lying*, a phrase that locates the origin in desire rather than in evidence.

**A Doctrine That Serves Its Owners**

This is the reason the Book treats the claim as serious. A limited sentence for one group is not a private opinion; it is a theory of privilege, and its practical effect is to remove the fear that would otherwise enforce the commandments. If the punishment is brief, the rules are recommendations.

The Qur'an answers the theory elsewhere in the same tone: *"The Jews and the Christians each say, 'We are the children of Allah and His most beloved!' Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ 'Why then does He punish you for your sins? No! You are only humans like others of His Own making'"* (5:18). The answer does not argue about days. It denies the premise that any community's ancestry or standing changes the terms of the accounting, and it puts the claimants back where the sūrah put everyone: human beings made by the same hands.

**"Deceived in Their Faith" — the Grammar of Self-Deception**

The second sentence says they have been deceived *in their faith* by their own fabrications, and the phrasing keeps the responsibility where it belongs. The Book does not present them as victims of a trick played by someone else. What has deceived them is what they invented.

The Qur'an uses the vocabulary of deception for the world's false promises in other places, and it always names the deceiver plainly. The life of this world is a delusion (57:20); the promise of God is true, so do not let the life of this world deceive you (35:5); what emboldened a person against his Lord if not the assumption that generosity meant approval? (82:6). The pattern is consistent: the texts of the Book treat self-deception as an act a person performs on himself, with his own desires supplying the material.

**Why a Borrowed Certainty Feels Like Knowledge**

The doctrine had one more quality that made it powerful: everyone around the person believed it. A claim repeated by neighbours, teachers, and ancestors acquires the texture of a fact, and few people audit the things their community considers obvious.

The verse supplies the audit that was never performed. It asks about the source of the guarantee — whether a pledge had been received, or whether the claim came from the community's own storytelling. The test is portable. It can be applied to a person's expectations about his own standing, and it usually returns the same answer: what felt like knowledge had no owner behind it.

**A Warning the Book Repeated for Its Own**

The Book does not restrict this warning to the people addressed here. It warns those who recite it against the same disease, because the mechanism does not belong to any one religion. Whenever a group concludes that its identity settles its account, the verse above is the diagnosis, and the cure offered is the same: hold no promise except the ones God has made, and make no claim about the Day that the Day's owner has not made.

Notice how much of the sūrah's medicine is contained in one sentence. The Book does not respond to the claim with a longer calculation of its own, or with a list of the texts that might support a stricter reading. It asks a single question about the source of the guarantee and then leaves the claim where it found it — in the mouth of the person making it. That restraint is itself an argument. A doctrine that depends on its own repetition does not need to be refuted; it needs to be identified. The following verse will show what such certainty is worth when the gathering begins.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:25

> But how ˹horrible˺ will it be when We gather them together on the Day about which there is no doubt—when every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged

**An Exclamation Instead of a Scene**

The verse opens with a rhetorical question — how will it be when We gather them — and the question carries the whole weight of what follows. The Qur'an is describing a moment of immense consequence, and instead of painting a picture, it asks the reader to imagine the position of people who confidently expected a short punishment and now stand inside the gathering.

The choice is deliberate. A detailed description of the Day would occupy pages and invite arguments about its imagery; an exclamation focuses the reader on the contrast between the claim of verse 24 and the reality that answers it. The question is left hanging and then completed by the sentence about accounts that follow: the answer to "how will it be" is that every soul will be settled with, without exception.

**The Day About Which There Is No Doubt**

The phrase attached to the Day has already appeared in this sūrah, in the prayer of the well-grounded who asked God to gather all humanity on a Day about which there is no doubt (3:9). It recurs across the Book with the same formula: at 4:87 God swears by His own truthfulness that He will gather all of humanity, at 6:12 He has taken mercy upon Himself with the gathering as a settled fact, and at 45:26 the gathering is announced to a people who do not know.

The repetition is a strategy. A statement that no one can successfully doubt, repeated in different contexts, removes the possibility of treating the Day as a speculative item that some groups believe in strongly. The Book treats the gathering as part of the structure of reality, and it uses the same formula whenever it wants a warning to land without the padding of an argument.

**"Every Soul Paid in Full"**

The sentence about payment uses the vocabulary of debts and wages, and the Book applies it consistently to the judgment. *"Every soul will be paid in full for its deeds, for Allah knows best what they have done"* (39:70). *"˹Consider˺ the Day ˹when˺ every soul will come pleading for itself, and each will be paid in full for what it did, and none will be wronged"* (16:111). The same formula closes the last of the legislative verses of the sūrah of the Cow (2:281, quoted at that verse's discussion).

The metaphor of payment is exact rather than decorative. It tells the reader that the settlement is not a gesture, that it is owed and will be rendered, and it keeps the terms of the transaction where everyone can follow them: what was done, by whom, settled in full. The one who is paid may find the amount greater than expected; the one who pays will find nothing missing from the bill.

**"None Will Be Wronged" — the Guarantee Inside the Threat**

The verse's final clause is an assurance, and it is attached to a scene that has just been described as horrifying. The Book joins the two repeatedly: the scales of justice will be set up and no soul will be wronged in the least (21:47), the record laid open will not leave any deed, large or small, unlisted (18:49), and *"Indeed, Allah does not wrong ˹anyone˺ by even an atom's weight"* (4:40).

For the reader who had reason to fear the account, the clause is a strange comfort and a real one. The horror of the Day, as the Qur'an presents it, is not that the judgment is arbitrary. It is that it is exact. Nothing hidden is lost, nothing forgotten is forgiven by default, and no one stands in a queue behind the powerful. The same guarantee that terrifies the man who hoped his community's claim would cover him protects the man who has no advocate at all.

**The Answer to a Borrowed Certainty**

Set beside verse 24, this one dismantles the doctrine without arguing about numbers. The people who had calculated that the Fire would touch them for a few days are now shown in the position of being paid in full. The calculation is not corrected in detail; it is simply irrelevant, because the day it concerned has arrived and its terms are set by someone else.

That is how the Book habitually disposes of comfortable theology. It does not spend pages refuting the arithmetic of privilege. It announces the appointment and describes the accounting, and leaves the reader to notice which version of the future he has been living in.

**What the Verse Asks Its Reader to Do**

Nothing in the sentence is directed at the reader, and that is the reason it makes its demand so effectively. The reader is shown a scene, told that it is not doubtful, and left to place himself in it. Every clause — the gathering, the payment, the guarantee of fairness — is a fact about him, stated in the third person, waiting for the moment he answers it.

The sūrah will continue to address nations and communities. This verse remains the floor under the whole discussion: one gathering, one account per soul, and a settlement made without favour. A man who expects an account cannot spend his time on the theory that the account will be lenient because of who he is, and a community that recites this verse cannot build its confidence on the arithmetic of the previous one. Everything the Book says afterwards about prayer, charity, courage, and patience is the behaviour of people who have accepted the floor this verse lays down. Everything it says about the arrogance of the comfortable is a description of people who have not.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:26

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "O Allah! Lord over all authorities! You give authority to whoever You please and remove it from who You please; You honour whoever You please and disgrace who You please—all good is in Your Hands. Surely You ˹alone˺ are Most Capable of everything"

**A Prayer Commanded, Not Improvised**

The verse opens with the imperative *say*, which places this supplication in the same category as the rest of the revelation: it is taught rather than discovered. The Prophet is not reported here as a man overwhelmed by circumstances who poured out his feelings; he is told what words to use, and those words are addressed to God about power.

This is the Qur'an's way of disciplining political emotion. A community that had been defeated, exiled, and outnumbered could easily produce prayers for revenge or despair. Instead it is given a prayer about God's ownership of authority, which puts the current arrangement of power under a larger fact and leaves the community asking for nothing narrower than what God's own will decides.

**Mālik al-Mulk: the Title and Its Weight**

The address *Lord over all authorities* renders *mālik al-mulk*, and the two words of the phrase are related to each other and to a family of terms: *malik*, a king; *mulk*, dominion or rule; *malakūt*, the inner sovereignty. The title appears at 67:1, where the Book states that all authority rests in His hands, and 25:2 describes Him as the one to whom the kingdom of the heavens and the earth belongs, who has never had offspring and no partner in governing.

The sūrah states the same claim at its end: *"To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. And Allah is Most Capable of everything"* (3:189). Setting that sentence beside this prayer shows how the Book approaches politics. Earthly rule is not ignored or dismissed; it is located inside a larger ownership, so that no throne is described as independent and no dynasty is described as permanent.

**"You Give Authority to Whoever You Please"**

The second clause turns the doctrine into history. Authority is granted and withdrawn by a will that is not accountable to the holders, and the Book illustrates the point with rulers of very different kinds. Saul is appointed over a people who complained that he lacked wealth and lineage, and the appointment is defended on grounds they had not considered: knowledge and stature (2:247). Nimrod argues with Abraham on the strength of his kingship and is silenced by a question about sunrise (2:258). Korah, who trusted in his own power, is swallowed by the earth with no helper left (28:81).

None of these narratives treats the ruler as the author of his own standing. The sūrah's prayer and the Book's histories agree: a crown is a loan with a term attached, and the term belongs to the lender.

**Honour and Disgrace, the Two Companions of Rank**

The prayer pairs honour and disgrace as it paired giving and removing, and the pairing is not a rhetorical flourish. In the culture the Qur'an was addressing, honour was the social currency on which safety, marriage, and livelihood depended, and disgrace was close to civic death. The Book's assertion is that both are conferred.

Earlier in the Qur'an the theme of honour is turned inward: those who take disbelievers as allies are asked whether they seek honour in that company, and the answer is that honour belongs to God — *"Do they seek honour through them? Indeed, all honour belongs to Allah entirely"* (4:139). The verse above gives the same truth the form of an address, so that a believer states it to God rather than merely holding it as an opinion. Rank, then, is a moment in a larger administration, and it can be transferred in a night.

**"All Good Is in Your Hands"**

The sentence adds a restriction that is easy to pass over: all *good* is in His hands. No parallel clause says that evil is in them, and the commentators drew conclusions from the omission. Whatever God decrees is good in itself, even when it arrives as loss, and what appears evil in the world is attributable to the acts of creatures or to the limited view of the observer.

The Book supports the restriction with a blunt statement about jurisdiction: *"He cannot be questioned about what He does, but they will ˹all˺ be questioned"* (21:23). That sentence is the reason the prayer can put the whole of political fortune into God's hands without embarrassment about the human cost. The Book does not explain every event; it locates the authority that governs events beyond the reach of cross-examination.

**"Most Capable of Everything" — the Ground of the Prayer**

The address closes with the attribute *qadīr*, capable of everything, and the sūrah repeats it at 3:29 and 3:189. Its function in this verse is to make the earlier clauses more than a description. It is one thing to say that God owns authority; it is another to say that He can move it, and the closing phrase states the second.

Without that attribute, the prayer would be a statement of resignation. With it, the prayer becomes a request that expects an answer, and the community praying it is told where to direct a petition about its own survival. Nothing in the sentence asks God to be generous to a particular side. It asks Him to do what He already does.

**What This Prayer Teaches a Minority**

The verses that follow will describe the community's real problems: the temptation to take powerful allies who do not share its faith, the danger of hidden disloyalty, and the fear of an accounting that reaches into the heart. The prayer above is placed before those verses for a reason.

A believer who has said out loud that authority, honour, and disgrace are all dispensed by God has already answered the central political question of his own life. He may still be prudent, but he cannot be fundamentally impressed by the people who hold power, and he cannot expect to hold it himself in any way that outlasts the giver's will. That is the frame inside which the sūrah's instructions about loyalty will be given.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:27

> You cause the night to pass into the day and the day into the night. You bring forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. And You provide for whoever You will without limit."

**Two Sentences About Time and Life**

The prayer continues, and it moves from authority to two things that every human being experiences and no human being controls: the alternation of night and day, and the passage between life and death. Both sentences are phrased as ongoing actions — you cause, you bring forth — rather than as past events.

The choice matters. A miracle can be dismissed as an exception that proves nothing about the ordinary; the alternation of darkness and light is so ordinary that nobody thinks to attribute it to anything. The Book presents it as an act of the same will that confers kingdoms in the verse before, which means the daily cycle and the fall of dynasties are being made into the same kind of event.

**The Merging of Night and Day**

The image in Arabic is of one period sliding into the next: the night drawn over the day and the day drawn over the night, with each stealing part of the other. The Book uses the same expression at 22:61, at 31:29 where the sun and moon are described as subservient and orbiting for a fixed term, and at 57:6, where the verse immediately adds that God knows what the heart conceals.

The progression of those passages is instructive. The physical observation belongs to everyone; the conclusion drawn from it is that the one who moves the hours also knows what happens inside them. Time in this Book is not a neutral container in which events occur. It is administered, which is why the Book so often connects the passing of days with the possibility of repentance: a person's remaining hours are borrowed from someone whose will governs the borrowing.

**Living From the Dead, Dead From the Living**

The second pair of clauses states a law that runs through the whole Book: God brings the living out of the dead and the dead out of the living. The commentaries took the first direction to include the sprouting of grain from seed and the birth of a child from a body, and the second to include the death of a living being and the consumption of the living by what grows from the soil.

The Book uses the same formula to answer the denial of resurrection: *"Indeed, Allah is the One Who causes seeds and fruit stones to sprout. He brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. That is Allah! How can you then be deluded ˹from the truth˺?"* (6:95), and again in a verse that closes with the resurrection itself: *"He brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. And He gives life to the earth after its death. And so will you be brought forth ˹from the grave˺"* (30:19). The argument is elegant: the thing being denied is happening in every field and every womb, and has been acknowledged by the denier every time he ate.

**"Without Limit" — the Phrase in Its Two Directions**

The prayer ends with provision *without limit*, *bi-ghayri ḥisāb*. The Arabic can mean that the gift is given without measure, and also that God gives without rendering an account to anyone. Both senses are present in the Book's usage. Provision without reckoning is promised to the generous at 24:38, to the believers at 40:40, and to the patient at 39:10, where the reward promised for patience is precisely the absence of a limit.

The phrase is a deliberate inversion of the way people think about resources. Human beings distribute what they have according to accounts kept — measures, entitlements, ledgers.
