The closing phrase is a superlative, and superlatives about God in this Book are usually statements about quality rather than predictions about outcomes. God is the best of planners in the sense that His arrangements cannot be defeated, cannot be anticipated, and cannot be improved upon.

The word also removes a misunderstanding the previous clauses might create. Saying that God "also planned" could suggest a deity who reacts to human schemes, arriving late with a countermeasure. The superlative says otherwise: the plans of creatures are the small, visible movements in a design they cannot see, and the Book's habit of speaking of God's planning in the same vocabulary as human plotting is a concession to human capacity for understanding, not a description of two equals at a board. What looks like a contest from below is an arrangement from above.

**What the Verse Says to a Frightened Community**

The sūrah is being recited to a small community that had been pursued, besieged, and slandered, and whose members were watching more powerful parties decide their future. In that position a report of a plot is bad news unless the report includes the second clause.

The verse supplies the second clause without any drama. It does not promise that the messenger will be delivered by a miracle in front of everyone, or that his enemies will be humiliated in public; it says that a plan was made and that the answer to it was made as well. A believer reading the verse is learning how to occupy his own history: to treat the plans of the powerful as real, to take them seriously enough to prepare, and to hold them inside a larger arrangement whose author is neither surprised nor outmatched.

**The Plot That Failed and What It Left Behind**

The conspiracy the verse names is one of the most consequential acts of planning in the history of religion. Its aim was to remove a man and it succeeded in removing him from the world, and the Book's account stands the aim on its head: the removal became the raising, and the raising became the beginning of a proclamation that travelled beyond the borders of the community that had rejected him.

The disciples' pledge of support in the previous verses and the plot in this one belong to the same story, and the sūrah places them side by side. A group of men stood up for a message; a group of men arranged to destroy its messenger; and the verse above, standing between the two, says which of the two arrangements was better made. The next verse opens with God addressing the messenger directly.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:55

> ˹Remember˺ when Allah said, "O Jesus! I will take you ˹in death˺ and raise you up to Myself. I will deliver you from those who disbelieve, and elevate your followers above the disbelievers until the Day of Judgment. Then to Me you will ˹all˺ return, and I will settle all your disputes

**God Speaking to His Servant**

The verse opens with a reminder of a conversation: when God said, O Jesus. The speaker is God and the addressee is the messenger, and the words that follow are a set of promises spoken at the moment the plot of the previous verse was in motion.

Placing the promises in God's own voice matters for how the passage is read. Everything here is a commitment made by the one who made the plan, and the reader is being shown the content of the countermeasure at the exact point where the sūrah has just told him that a countermeasure was made. The promises are delivered to the man in danger, and the sūrah records them in full.

**Mutawaffī: the Word That Carries the Dispute**

The first promise uses a word from the root that governs taking and completing, and the sūrah's translation renders it as taking to Himself. The commentators spent considerable effort on it, and their disagreement is worth setting out plainly. A large group held that the word ordinarily refers to death, that God takes a person to Himself at the end of his life, and that in this verse, in view of what the Book says about the crucifixion, it means God's removal of Jesus from the world rather than his death at the hands of enemies. Others held that the word does retain its ordinary sense here and that the verse describes God causing him to die. A third reading took it in the sense of sleep, comparing the verse that says God calls back souls by night (6:60) and the verse that says He takes the souls of people at their death and those who do not die during their sleep (39:42).

Each reading has a Qur'anic usage behind it. What is not in dispute is that the word places the whole matter in God's hands: whatever the taking is, the taker is the one being addressed, and no enemy appears in the sentence.

**"And Raise You Up to Myself"**

The second promise names an elevation, and the Book states the same event when it answers the boasts of the plotters: they did not kill him, and God raised him up to Himself (4:158). The two verses describe one act from two angles, one as the removal of a servant from a hostile world and the other as an ascent.

The phrase "to Myself" is the part that has drawn the most attention from the commentators. The Book uses it nowhere else of a human being at the end of a mission, and whatever it means in detail, it states a destination rather than a location. The promise to a man whose enemies were arranging his execution is that he will be brought near to the one who sent him, and the nearness is the answer to everything the plot intended.

**Purification From the Disbelievers**

The third promise is that God will clear him of those who disbelieved. The commentators gave the verb two senses that complement each other: freeing him from their power, and absolving him of the charges they laid against him. Both are needed if the verse is to answer the situation described.

The followers of Jesus had accused him of blasphemy and sought his death; the sūrah's readers knew what the community had said about his mother and about his birth. A promise that he would be freed from their grasp and cleared of their claims is the answer to a slander as well as to a threat, and the Book's later defences of Mary and of her son belong to the same act. What God promises in this clause is that the record will be straightened out, and the sūrah's narrative is part of how that happens.

**Elevating His Followers Above the Disbelievers**

The fourth promise concerns his followers, and the commentators offered distinctive readings. One takes it as a statement about the historical relation between the Christian community and the community that rejected Jesus, the former prevailing politically and culturally for centuries. A second takes the followers to be those who believed in him correctly rather than all who later claimed his name. A third reads the elevation as a matter of proof and argument: the case of the believers will stand above the case of the deniers.

The three readings are not mutually exclusive, and they converge on the Book's usual way of describing success. The Book does not promise its people victory in numbers; it promises that truth will not be extinguished, and it allows that promise to be fulfilled in the arguments people make, in the survival of the community, and in the verdict of the Hereafter, often in combinations that no single generation can observe.

**"Then to Me You Will All Return"**

The promises end with a return, and the return is addressed to everyone involved rather than only to the messenger: the plotters, the believers, the deniers, and the men who made promises of support in the earlier verses. The disputes are to be settled there.

The sūrah has already used this closing device, and it will use it again. A statement about God's handling of history acquires its finality from the return, since a world without it would leave the schemes of the powerful as the last word on how matters are arranged. What the verse gives its readers is a limit on every controversy: no dispute in this Book is left open at the end, and none of them is settled by the party that happens to hold power while it is running.

**The Return of Jesus in the Tradition**

The commentaries also record the belief, held across the classical authorities, that Jesus will return before the end of time, that he will fight the false messiah, and that he will rule according to the law of the final Prophet. The reports about this are numerous and their like is not found for other prophets, but the verse above and the passages that parallel it do not state the doctrine, and the reader should be careful to attribute it to the tradition that carries it rather than to this sentence.

What the verse does state is complete on its own. A messenger was taken, raised, cleared, and given followers who would prevail, and he returns to the one who sent him. The next verse describes what happens on that day to two groups.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:56

> As for those who disbelieve, I will subject them to a severe punishment in this life and the Hereafter, and they will have no helpers

**The Speech Continues**

The verse remains inside the speech that began in verse 55, and it turns from the messenger to the people who refused him. The promises given to the servant are followed by the consequences prepared for those who rejected him, and the sūrah keeps both halves of the announcement in the same voice and the same passage.

Reading them together prevents a mistake that a reader might make with either half alone. A promise of deliverance to the messenger could be mistaken for a reprieve for everyone who opposed him, and a warning about punishment could be mistaken for a description of God's ordinary disposition toward a community. The verse states the second half plainly, in the same tone, so that the two parties to the dispute hear the outcome that concerns them.

**Punishment in This Life and the Next**

The punishment is described as severe and as falling in two places: the life of the world and the Hereafter. The Book applies the two-world pattern to both reward and punishment in several passages: one verse speaks of those who are disgraced in this world and have a severe punishment in the next (2:114); this sūrah makes the same statement earlier in its own words (3:22); and the sūrah of the Bee speaks of the evil of this life and the punishment of the Hereafter as two parts of one outcome (16:30). The two arenas are described for the believers in corresponding terms, since the Book promises good in this world and a better reward in the next to those who act well (16:122; 39:10).

What the punishment in this life consists of is left unspecified here, and other passages fill in the range: military defeat and displacement, the loss of the standing a community relied on, and the internal divisions that follow when a doctrine outlives the ability to defend it. The commentators read the worldly part in more than one way: it can be the humiliation that follows a community's conduct in its own time, and it can be the losses and defeats through which a people is given a chance to reconsider, since the Book treats affliction as a warning that precedes a heavier reckoning. Either way, the sentence keeps the two outcomes connected. The important element for the verse's argument is that the reckoning is not postponed entirely to another world. The Book describes a God whose judgments operate inside history, and the sūrah has already offered the fate of the people of Pharaoh as the proof that they do.

**"They Will Have No Helpers"**

The closing clause is the one that gives the sentence its weight. The sūrah used the phrase earlier for the deeds of a particular group being wasted (3:22), and it recurs across the Book in warnings about the Day of Judgment. A helper in the vocabulary of the Qur'an is not a friend in the ordinary sense; he is the person whose support changes what happens to you.

The world these verses describe was organised around exactly that relationship. A person's security depended on having parties who would defend him, ransom him, or plead for him, and a community's standing depended on the allies it could call on. The verse removes the whole apparatus: no clan, no patron, no protector, no intercessor, and no arrangement that could be made in advance. What remains is a case heard by a judge no one can influence, on a matter that the accused party has already decided.

**Who the Verse Addresses**

The sentence is introduced in the previous verse's frame as being about those who disbelieved, within the dispute over Jesus that occupies this part of the sūrah. The commentators understood it as covering the people who actively rejected him, and they read the severity as proportionate to the evidence they had refused.

The Book does not treat all disbelief as equivalent, and neither do the commentators. The severity in this verse is attached to a rejection made in the presence of a messenger, a message, and signs that the rejecters themselves had witnessed. That is the situation the Book consistently describes as the point at which a community's case is closed, and the tone of the sentence matches the situation rather than expressing a general disposition.

**A Sentence That Leaves the Timing Open**

The verse states that the punishment will come in this world and the next, and it does not say in what proportion or at what moment either part falls. The omission is a feature of the Book's treatment of divine judgment, and it protects the reader from two errors.

The first error is to assume that a community's disasters in this world are the whole of its punishment, which would make every calamity a complete verdict and every prosperity a complete acquittal. The second is to assume that nothing happens until the Hereafter, which would make the world a place where conduct has no consequence. The verse's two-world formula keeps both channels open, and it leaves the distribution to the one who arranges it. A reader is therefore never in a position to announce that a particular group has received its full sentence or escaped it entirely.

**The Two Verses as a Pair**

The sentence before this one promised the messenger deliverance, clearing, and elevation; the sentence here promises his rejecters punishment and isolation. The symmetry is not vindictiveness; it is the sūrah's account of the same event described from both sides.

Understanding the pairing also clarifies the role of the community that is reading. The sūrah is not asking its audience to avenge the messenger, and it does not hand them a list of enemies. It asks them to recognise the framework, which the next verse completes by turning to the believers, and which explains why the Book so often places the dividing line at the response to a messenger rather than at any other difference among people. The verse after this one states the reward.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:57

> And as for those who believe and do good, they will be rewarded in full. And Allah does not like the wrongdoers."

**The Other Half of the Sentence**

The previous verse stated the end of those who disbelieved; this one states the end of those who believed and did good. The two form one sentence in the original sequence, and the sūrah gives the reward the second position, so that the passage closes on the group it hopes its readers will join.

The construction is familiar from the Book's treatment of the Day of Judgment, where the two outcomes are normally stated together. What deserves attention here is the order and the proportion. The warning occupies a full sentence about punishment in two worlds and the absence of helpers; the promise occupies a shorter sentence about full reward, and a clause about God's disposition that does not contain the word reward at all.

**"Rewarded in Full"**

The verb behind the phrase describes the payment of a wage in full, without deduction, and the Book applies it to the settlement of every soul's deeds on the Day of Judgment in several passages. Used here of believers, it carries an implication the reader should not miss: the reward is presented as a payment owed, an obligation the giver has taken upon himself.

The Book's language about reward goes further in other places, offering multiplication beyond measure, gifts without reckoning, and increases out of God's grace. Setting those verses beside this one shows how the Book holds two things together: what is owed is paid in full, and what is given exceeds what was owed. A servant who has done little and receives much is not being treated unfairly; he is dealing with a giver whose account of the transaction is generous rather than exact.

**Belief and Deeds Named Together**

The verse names two conditions, faith and good conduct, and the Book pairs them so consistently that separating them would produce a different religion. Faith without works appears in the Qur'an as a claim that has no consequence for the claimant, and works without faith appear as a structure built on a foundation the builder has refused.

The sūrah has already given the reader both warnings. It described the people whose deeds were wasted in two worlds because their acts were not grounded in the proper relationship to God (3:22), and it described the supplication of a community asking to be counted among the witnesses (3:53). This verse's pairing of belief and good actions is the positive statement of the same doctrine, and its brevity suggests that the Book regards the combination as obvious rather than subtle. What is promised is not to those who felt something or to those who did something, but to those in whom the two ran together.

**"Allah Does Not Like the Wrongdoers"**

The verse ends with a statement about God's disposition that seems, at first reading, to belong to the previous sentence. The wrongdoers are not mentioned in the clause about belief and good deeds, and the reason for the closing phrase lies in the structure of the whole passage.

The Book's convention is to name a divine attribute that explains the verdict just given. Here the verdict is twofold: disbelievers are punished, and believers who do good are rewarded in full. The attribute that covers both is God's disfavour toward wrongdoers, since the punishment of the deniers and the reward of the upright are both expressions of the same preference. A reader who understands the final clause aright understands the verse's claim about God's character: the distinction He draws among people is not arbitrary favour but a settled opposition to wrongdoing and a settled regard for what is right.

**A Reminder About Who Is Being Described**

The verse is often read as a general statement about the destiny of two classes of people, and it functions that way. In its immediate setting, though, it concerns the parties to a specific dispute: the community that rejected a prophet and the community that accepted him and acted on the acceptance. The word translated "wrongdoers" is not a synonym for "disbelievers" in every Qur'anic passage, and here it describes the members of the rejecting party whose conduct earned the description.

The distinction matters because the sūrah will go on, in the following verses, to make a very particular argument about what belief in Jesus requires. The reward described here is not offered to a category of people defined by their ancestry or their community affiliation. It is promised to those whose faith found expression in acts that the Book considers good, which is a condition that any reader of the verse can measure himself against.

**Why the Reward Is Described So Barely**

The warning in the previous verse carries details; the reward here carries almost none. The Book gives the believer a full payment and nothing else, and the silence is worth weighing against the elaborate descriptions of Paradise in other sūrahs, where gardens, rivers, spouses, and the pleasure of God are named.

The difference is one of rhetorical purpose rather than of importance. A passage arguing against people who had misread the destiny of a prophet does not need a portrait of Paradise; it needs the assurance that the accounting will be fair and that the two parties will be separated. The bareness also keeps the reader's attention where the sūrah has placed it, on the question of belief and conduct, rather than on the inventory of the reward. What the verse guarantees is enough: the payment is not partial, and it is owed.

**The Comfort Inside a Warning**

The passage as a whole is a warning addressed to a community under pressure, and its second half is the part that makes the warning bearable. A person told only that his enemies will be punished gains nothing; a person told that his own belief and conduct will be repaid in full has a reason to continue.

The Book's pastoral pattern, visible in this verse and throughout the sūrah, is to make the consequences of both roads clear and then to leave the reader facing a choice that has an obvious better option. The reward described here is deliberately unelaborated. What is emphasised is not the content of the reward but the fact that it is full, owed, and given by the one who does not like wrongdoing. The next verse turns from the destiny of the parties to the source of the account the reader has just received.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:58

> We recite ˹all˺ this to you ˹O Prophet˺ as one of the signs and ˹as˺ a wise reminder

**A Break in the Speech**

The verses before this one were God's address to Jesus; this one turns to the Prophet of the sūrah, who is told that the narrative he has just received is being recited to him. The change of addressee is unmistakable in the Arabic and it marks the passage off from what preceded it.

The Book uses this device to signal that a story has ended and its lesson is being handed over. What the Prophet has been given is not a legend to be repeated but an account with a purpose, and the purpose is named in the second half of the verse. The reader who has followed the dispute about Jesus and the plotting against him is now told what he has been reading and why.

**"We Recite It to You"**

The verb describes a recitation taking place in the present, and the Book uses it for the act by which revelation is delivered to the Prophet. The same verb appears at the openings of sūrahs where the revelation is being called to the reader's attention, and it places the Prophet in the position of a hearer rather than an author.

The construction has a doctrinal edge in a sūrah that has just been arguing with communities who possessed scriptures. A messenger is being told that the stories of the previous messengers are being recited to him by God, which means his knowledge of them is direct rather than inherited. Every narrative in the Book is presented as coming from the same source as the ones that came before, and the word "recite" keeps that claim alive at the level of the sentence.

**The Two Descriptions: Signs and a Wise Reminder**

The verse calls what it has recited two things. First, the material is a sign, and the Book uses that word for both the verses of revelation and the events of the natural world; both are described as things put in front of a person to be read. Second, the whole of it is a reminder that is wise, and the commentators noted that this phrase is used elsewhere as a name for the Qur'an, with some taking it to refer to the Preserved Tablet from which revelation comes down.

The two descriptions answer two purposes. A sign instructs by pointing at something beyond itself, and the disputes about Jesus, the plots, and the promises all point at the God who governs such matters. A reminder instructs by bringing back what is known, and the sūrah has been reminding its readers of truths their own traditions contained. Both descriptions apply to the passage the reader has just finished, and while the first of them requires him to think, the second requires him to remember.

**The Wisdom Claimed for the Reminder**

Calling the reminder wise is a claim about its composition as well as its content. The Book applies wisdom to the arrangement of revelation in other verses, and it rests the claim on the fit between what is said and the situation that requires it.

The passage as it stands supports the claim. A community troubled by the claims of a neighbouring religion is given a clear account of the prophet at the centre of the dispute, a statement of his own creed, the record of his supporters, the plot against him and its answer, and a statement of the two destinies. Nothing in it is extraneous, and the parts answer the questions in the order that a person's objections actually arise. The verse's closing description is thus not decoration; it is an argument that the reader can test against the material he has read.

**"One of the Signs" — the Identity of the Narrated Event**

Calling the narrative a sign places the story of Jesus in the same category as the natural phenomena the Book calls signs, and the comparison is deliberate. A sign in the Qur'anic sense is an event arranged to be read, and its value lies in what it points to rather than in itself.

The verses the reader has just finished contain several such events: a birth announced to a woman, a speaking child, a mission with proofs, a conspiracy, and a removal that defeated the conspiracy. Each could be read as an interesting occurrence or as a claim about the God who governs them, and the verse's first description settles which reading the Book intends. What has been recited is not history for its own sake. It is evidence, and it is placed in front of the reader with that status attached.

**What the Reader Is Meant to Do**

A reminder that is wise makes a demand on the person reminded, since it is possible to hear a reminder and leave it where it was found. The verse places the reader in the position of both the Prophet and the community: the story is recited, and it will be recited again in every generation that recites the sūrah.

This verse marks the end of the section that began with the announcement to Mary, and the two verses that follow it address the Prophet directly about what belief in Jesus requires. The sūrah moves from the past to the present, and from the story to the disputants who had raised the story as an argument. What follows is a statement about the truth of the whole account and a warning against doubting it, and then the challenge to the disputants. The verse above is the hinge, and its grammar says something about how the Book understands its own delivery: revelation is not a document handed over once but speech that continues to be recited, which is why the Book's vocabulary for it is dominated by words for speaking and hearing rather than for writing and reading.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:59

> Indeed, the example of Jesus in the sight of Allah is like that of Adam. He created him from dust, then said to him, "Be!" And he was

**The Comparison That Ends the Argument**

The verse answers the claim made about Jesus with a comparison, and the comparison is with another man. The Book does not respond to the argument about his birth by denying the miracle; it concedes the extraordinary birth and produces Adam, who had no father and no mother at all.

The logic carries the whole verse. A person who concludes from the virgin birth that Jesus must be divine has to explain why the same conclusion does not apply to the first human being, who came into existence with no parents whatsoever. The absence of a father is evidence of God's power rather than of the son's nature, and the case of Adam makes that plain without further reasoning.

**Why Adam Is the Decisive Case**

The choice of Adam rather than another figure is deliberate. Isaac, for instance, was born to parents one of whom was beyond the age of childbearing; his case would show God overcoming a natural obstacle. Adam's case is categorically different: no parents at all, an existence that began with dust and a command.

If being created without a human father is evidence of divinity, then Adam has the stronger claim to it, since he was created without any human ancestry. Since nobody makes that claim for Adam, the inference from the virgin birth collapses. The Book's argument is not that the miracle did not happen; it is that miracles of origin are statements about the one who performs them.

**Dust, Then the Command**

The creation of Adam is described in two stages: created from dust, then told "Be", and he was. The two elements belong together. Dust is the material, and it has no life in itself; the command is the act, and nothing in it requires the cooperation of what it addresses. God's speech is sufficient for anything to exist, and this verse applies that picture to the beginning of the human race so the reader can measure the nativity of Jesus against it.

**What "Likeness" Means Here**

The Arabic word for likeness describes a resemblance in a particular respect, and the commentators were careful about which respect is meant. Adam and Jesus share the relevant attributes: both came into existence without the ordinary means, both were created by God's command, and both are servants rather than sharers in their Lord's nature.

The comparison is not a claim of equivalence in every respect. The Book gives Adam a role in the story of humanity that it does not give to Jesus, and it gives Jesus a mission within Israel that Adam did not have. What the verse asserts is likeness in the matter at issue — the manner of their coming into being — and that single point of resemblance closes the argument about divinity.

**"Be" Throughout the Book**

The command appears wherever creation is described, and its repetitions form one of the Qur'an's most consistent themes. The Originator of the heavens and the earth, when He decrees a matter, tells it to be and it is (2:117); God's affair, when He wills something, is only to say to it "Be" and it is (36:82); He will say "Be" on the Day the trumpet is blown (6:73); the same words describe the giver of life and death when He decrees a matter (40:68); and His command, when He wills a thing, is a single word (16:40).

Placing this verse in that series shows that the creation of Jesus is not an isolated exception but an instance of the way God has always acted. Existence follows a command, and an unusual origin is a difference in circumstances, not in the kind of act.

**A Creature Cannot Be Its Own Maker**

The argument carries an implication the sūrah has already stated and will state again: a being brought into existence by a command cannot be the one who issues commands in his own right. The Book is explicit about this in the case of Jesus, whose own words are recorded as a confession of service — he speaks only what he is commanded, and he names a Lord over himself.

The comparison with Adam therefore does more than settle a local dispute. It sets the rule by which every claim about a created being is measured, and the rule applies to prophets, to angels, and to anything else that might be treated as more than a servant. Whatever came into existence was made, and whatever was made worships. The verses that follow declare the account true and address the disputants directly.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:60

> This is the truth from your Lord, so do not be one of those who doubt

**An Address to the Prophet**

The verse changes addressee and speaks to the Prophet about the account he has just received. What has been recited about Jesus — his birth, his mission, the comparison with Adam — is described as truth from his Lord, and the sentence closes with a prohibition against doubt.

The combination of a statement and a warning in one breath is characteristic of the Book's manner with its own messenger. He is not excused from the demand revelation makes on everyone who hears it; the Book's treatment of him contains warnings as well as commendations. A messenger told not to doubt is being held to the same standard he is delivering.

**"Truth From Your Lord"**

The phrase identifies both the content and its source: what has been recited is true, and it comes from the Lord of the person addressed. The construction recurs when the Book wants to end a discussion with a verdict rather than an argument. After the change of the direction of prayer and the disputes it produced, the sūrah of the Cow states that what has come to them is the truth from their Lord and that they should not be among the doubters. This sūrah applies the same formula at the close of the dispute about Jesus, and the effect is to lift the question out of the plane of debate and set it on the plane of revelation.

**What the Warning Presupposes**

The clause assumes that a person can hold the truth and still be in danger, which is a claim about conviction rather than about the evidence. If certainty were produced automatically by contact with revelation, a warning of this kind would have no object. The Book's position is that the truth is fixed and the person holding it is variable; its remedies are the repeated call to remember, the instruction to renew understanding, and the believers' prayer that their hearts not be allowed to swerve after guidance has come. This verse adds the direct address that names the danger while the material is still fresh.

**Doubt and How It Is Prevented**

The clause is usually read as an assurance that the Prophet would not entertain doubt rather than as a suspicion that he might, and the commentators understood it as a warning that also strengthens. The Book addresses its messenger in this way at several points where the surrounding denial was most relentless.

What the Book treats as dangerous is not the inquiry that wants an answer; it is the drift that follows prolonged exposure to hostile argument. A person who hears the same objection made confidently, day after day, may hold the answer less firmly without ever having been convinced by the objection. The prohibition answers that process by naming the source of the material rather than by supplying another argument: a truth that comes from one's Lord does not need to be won again in every conversation.

**Who "Those Who Doubt" Are**

The Arabic participle describes a person in a state, and the Book uses it both for those who waver and for those who stand outside belief. Here the reference is broad: the sūrah has just described a dispute in which a party refused an account of Jesus that the Book presents as clear. The phrase therefore covers a community that had knowledge of the story and chose the interpretation that suited it, as well as any reader who hears the account and hesitates.

**The Weight of a Single Clause**

The clause does a great deal of work for readers who come after the Prophet. Anyone can recite this account and then spend years where it is contradicted by crowds, by authorities, or by the persistence of a rival version. What the Book provides against that pressure is not a new proof in each generation but the identification of the material's origin. The believer's protection is the claim about where the story comes from, and that is what must be held when the argument cannot be settled in the room.

The sūrah has been building a picture of what conviction rests on: a Book revealed in truth, a testimony given by God, the angels, and the learned, and now an account of a prophet declared to be truth from the Lord. Certainty here is not a feeling a person generates; it is produced by evidence whose source is identified. That is why the next verse is a challenge to the disputants rather than a further explanation: the account is settled, and what remains is the terms on which the disagreement will be conducted.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:61

> Now, whoever disputes with you ˹O Prophet˺ concerning Jesus after full knowledge has come to you, say, "Come! Let us gather our children and your children, our women and your women, ourselves and yourselves—then let us sincerely invoke Allah's curse upon the liars."

**A Challenge Issued After the Argument**

The verse names its situation: someone disputes with the Prophet about Jesus, and the dispute continues *after full knowledge has come*. The Book has set out its account, drawn the comparison with Adam, and declared the whole to be truth; what remains is the party that will not accept it.

The response is not another round of proofs. The Prophet is told to propose a procedure that would end the matter: an invocation of God's curse on whichever party is lying. The Book resorts to this kind of appeal where the facts are no longer in question and the disagreement has become a matter of who will abandon a position. What is at stake is not information but the willingness to be wrong.

**"Come, Let Us Gather"**

The invitation is addressed to the disputants directly, and it includes their families: children, women, and the parties themselves. The commentators understood the detail as a test of sincerity. A man may argue a case with his intellectual commitment intact and his interests untouched; the presence of his family removes that insulation. To stake what one loves most on the truth of one's claim is a step taken only by a person who believes it. The proposal is a challenge to conviction, not a rhetorical flourish.

**The Mutual Curse and Its Logic**

Each side is to invoke God's curse upon the liars. The formula does not name a party, and that is what gives it force: each side asks that the falsehood be identified and its bearer placed under the curse. The form was recognised in the region, where an imprecation could settle a dispute no court could decide. The Book's use of it is not a concession to superstition; it is a recognition that some disagreements cannot be resolved by argument and must be referred to the One who knows. The proposal carries its own threat: whoever enters such a proceeding in bad faith has called down on himself the verdict he invited.

**The Delegation From Najrān**

The commentaries connect this verse to the Christians of Najrān who came to Madinah and disputed with the Prophet about Jesus, arguing that his having no human father made him the son of God. The accounts say the parties agreed to invoke the mutual curse the next day, that the Christians deliberated overnight, and that they concluded there was no advantage in proceeding. Their recorded reasoning took two forms: fear of what would follow if the Prophet was in fact truthful, and a political calculation about contending with a rising power. The narrative does not ask the reader to choose between the motives; it preserves a decision made by men who had the chance to settle the matter and declined it.

**Who Was Brought to the Meeting**

The accounts differ in detail but agree on the essentials. The Prophet came out for the appointment accompanied by his daughter Fāṭimah, her husband ʿAlī, and her two sons Ḥasan and Ḥusayn — so that the party matched the summons in each category the verse named: children, women, and selves. He brought no one else from a whole community, which the tradition reads as confidence in the outcome. What the record shows beneath any interpretation is a small family group standing at an appointment the other side did not keep. The scene became one of the most cited in later Islamic literature for the standing of the Prophet's household.

**The Refusal and the Treaty**

The refusal was followed by a settlement. The Najrān delegation remained Christian, concluded a treaty with the Muslim community, and undertook an annual payment, and the Prophet sent a trusted companion back with them at their request to act as arbiter. The outcome shows what the challenge was not: an instrument of forced conversion. A community that declined the challenge and kept its religion kept its religion, and the terms that followed were political and financial. The sūrah itself had already limited the Prophet's duty to delivering the message.

**What the Scene Establishes**

Strip the drama and one plain fact remains: when arguments were exhausted, an invitation was made to refer the entire disagreement to God, and it was declined. The Book preserves the proposal, the appointment, and the refusal without commentary. It marks the point at which the sūrah considers the discussion closed — the truth is stated once more in the next verse, and the address turns to the wider community.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:62

> Certainly, this is the true narrative, and there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Allah. And indeed, Allah ˹alone˺ is the Almighty, All-Wise

**A Verdict on the Account**

The verse names what the reader has just received: this is the true narrative. The word for "true" describes a report that conforms to what actually happened, and it is applied to the whole passage about Jesus, not to a single sentence of it. Al-Rāzī took the phrase to refer to the verses immediately before it, so that the account of Jesus and of the challenge that followed is declared accurate rather than legendary; al-Qurṭubī read it more broadly, as a statement about the accounts the Book relates. The readings are complementary: the passage at hand is the case under dispute, and the sentence declares the recited history to be the accurate one.

Placing the verdict here answers the challenge in the previous verse. The account is not one version among several that a community may hold; it is the report of the matter, and the disputants were invited to test precisely this.

**The Creed That Follows**

The declaration that comes next states God's unity, and its position completes the argument about Jesus. The question at issue was whether a created being could be divine; the answer is the sentence on which the whole sūrah has been built, repeated here in the frame of a verdict. The Book joins the two themes throughout: wherever it discusses what people say about a prophet, an angel, or a saint, it returns to the statement that there is no deity but God. This is not a change of subject but the sūrah's method — an overestimate of a creature is corrected by restating the position of the Creator.

**An Intensified Form of the Testimony**

The wording here is not the community's usual testimony of faith, which negates every deity and then affirms the one God. This verse begins with the particle of restriction, and al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī described the construction as a stronger form of the negation — no deity at all, without the possibility of an exception being smuggled in. The commentators dwelt on the detail because of where it stands: immediately after a dispute in which the "exception" — a partner to God in the person of Jesus — was exactly what one party wanted. The stronger form is fitted to the argument being conducted.

**The Two Names at the End**

The verse closes with Almighty and All-Wise, the pair the sūrah used at its opening. Each name answers one way the argument about Jesus could go wrong. Almighty addresses power: the miraculous birth is evidence of a capability no creature possesses, and the name keeps that capability with God. All-Wise addresses purpose: the manner of the birth was arranged, not accidental, and it served the ends revelation describes.

**The Creed as the Conclusion of a Story**

From the family of ʿImrān to this point the sūrah has narrated a household, a birth, a mission, a conspiracy, and a removal — and it closes the sequence with a statement about God that contains no narrative at all. That is a deliberate ending. The story of Jesus, as the Book tells it, does not terminate in a claim about Jesus; it terminates in a claim about the One who sent him. The reader is left with the same words the sūrah used before the story began.

**Where the Argument Goes Next**

The verse ends the dispute about Jesus with a declaration rather than a rebuttal, and it does not address the disputants again. What follows turns to the shared ancestor of the communities and to a new phase of the sūrah's engagement with the scriptured peoples: a test of the claims built on Abraham.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:63

> If they turn away, then surely Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of the corruptors

**The Refusal Named Once More**

The verse follows the declaration of God's oneness with a conditional: if they turn away, God knows the corruptors. This sūrah has now named the same refusal several times, and each time it has responded differently — once by stating the messenger's duty, once by naming the consequence in the language of divine disfavour, and here by referring the whole matter to knowledge.

The repetition traces an argument that has been made and declined. These are not people hearing a fresh conversation; they are people who have heard the recitation, the account of Jesus, and the creed, and have held their course. What this verse adds is a description of the refusal that is also a verdict — delivered as a fact about God rather than as a judgement passed by the messenger.

**Who Are Called Corruptors**

The Arabic word describes a person who spoils or corrupts, and the Book applies it to those who damage an order entrusted to them: people who break agreements, spread disorder, and ruin what they were given to keep. In this context the term fits the conduct the sūrah has been describing precisely. The people addressed possess scriptures; the charge against them is not that they lack revelation but that they handle it in a way that spoils it — mixing truth with falsehood, concealing what they know, and turning others away from a message they have reason to recognise. The word is more exact than a plain synonym for disbelief would have been.

**Knowledge as the Final Answer to Refusal**

The sentence does not threaten punishment, promise a reckoning, or issue another invitation. It states that God knows. The effect is to move the matter outside the reach of the dispute and leave the disputants where they stand. The Book uses this response at the end of several arguments, and its force lies in what it implies: a case that has been examined and refused does not need a new argument; it needs a judge, and the judge is already informed. The reader who has followed the sūrah from its opening declarations about what is hidden from God now meets that same knowledge applied to particular people who refused an account they were in a position to verify.

**What the Verse Does for the Messenger**

Read with the verses on either side, the sentence relieves the Prophet of a burden and clarifies his role. He has delivered the account, made the argument, offered the challenge, and stated the conclusion; the other party's refusal is no longer his responsibility — the sūrah has said as much in plain words earlier. The verse does not tell him to redouble his efforts or adjust his message for a better reception. It hands the matter over, in the form of a statement about God's knowledge rather than an instruction. His task continues, but on the footing that the outcome belongs elsewhere.

**A Conclusion That Opens the Next Section**

The sentence closes the narrative block about Jesus and opens the block about Abraham. The subject is the same in both: which community holds the better claim to a prophet of God. The transition is smooth because the ground has been prepared — the account has been declared true and God declared one; the sūrah now turns to the ancestor the disputants shared with the Muslim community and to the claim that he belonged to one of them. The refusers have just been described as people who spoil what they hold, and the coming verses show what they did with Abraham.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:64

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "O People of the Book! Let us come to common terms: that we will worship none but Allah, associate none with Him, nor take one another as lords instead of Allah." But if they turn away, then say, "Bear witness that we have submitted ˹to Allah alone˺."

**An Invitation, Not a Proclamation**

The sūrah turns from the dispute about Jesus to a direct address to the People of the Book, and it opens with an invitation: *let us come*. The form matters before the content does. An invitation belongs to parties setting out to reach an agreed position; it treats the other side as capable of accepting, and it places speaker and hearers inside the same sentence. Nothing in the verse abandons the claim the sūrah has been making about its own account. What it does is put the terms of agreement where both parties can recognise them.

**"Common Terms" and the Word Behind It**

The phrase translates a single Arabic word whose root sense is levelness and evenness — the root that also gives the expression for the level middle of a road. The commentators gave two readings that overlap more than they appear to. Some took the word to mean a just and fair one, equitable to both sides — al-Rāzī's gloss: come to a word in which there is fair treatment from each of us to the other. Others took it to describe what the two sides already share. The second reading fits the three items that follow, since each is a proposition the earlier scriptures themselves assert.

**The Three Propositions**

The invitation names three commitments: worship of none but God, association of nothing with Him, and refusal to take one another as lords instead of God. The three are one position stated from different angles. The first two concern God directly, and they are the ground of everything the sūrah has said about Jesus: a messenger whose birth was a wonder is not a partner in divinity. The third concerns human authority, and the commentators read it two ways — a prohibition on the veneration paid to religious leaders, and a prohibition on obeying them to the point of setting aside the truth. The Book states the same charge elsewhere in harsher form: it describes people who took their rabbis and monks as lords apart from God, along with the Messiah, though they were commanded to worship only one God (9:31). Here that criticism appears as an invitation to its remedy.

**Who Is Addressed**

The commentators differed: some restricted the address to the Jews of Madinah, some included both communities, and a third group confined it to the Christian delegation whose dispute occupies the earlier verses. The positions are not exclusive, since the terms apply wherever the conditions obtain, and the sūrah continues to address both communities afterwards. What matters is the level at which the invitation operates: it does not ask for a new book or the renunciation of an ancestry. It asks for a statement about God that the earlier scriptures themselves support — which is why it can be presented as a basis for agreement rather than a demand for surrender.

**"If They Turn Away, Bear Witness"**

The verse closes with a provision for refusal: the Prophet is to say, bear witness that we have submitted. The response to rejection is neither argument nor retaliation but a public statement of the speaker's own position. The formula echoes the disciples' request to be counted among the witnesses, and the word for submission is the one the sūrah attached to the religion itself (3:19) and the one Abraham and Jacob counselled their children to die upon (2:132). A community that has stated its case and been refused does not need to win the conversation; it needs to have said clearly what it holds. The witness is borne, the offer stays on record, and the matter is left.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:65

> O People of the Book! Why do you argue about Abraham, while the Torah and the Gospel were not revealed until long after him? Do you not understand

**A Question About Abraham**

The address to the People of the Book continues, and the question concerns their own claim: why argue about Abraham? The dispute in view is a competition over which community may claim him, and the Book's response is to point at the calendar.

The argument is as simple as it is decisive. Abraham lived before the Torah was given to Moses and before the Gospel was given to Jesus. The claim that he belonged to a community defined by either book cannot stand, because neither book existed in his lifetime. A person cannot be a member of a community whose defining scripture arrives centuries after his death.

**The Dates Both Sides Accept**

The commentators noted that the chronological point was one the disputants themselves accepted; the argument was never about whether Abraham predated Moses and Jesus, but about what conclusions could be drawn from ancestry and communal practice. The Book draws the conclusion the facts actually run: if two communities define themselves by later revelations, then the ancestor they argue over preceded both, and the dispute is exposed as an attempt to annex a figure from the past to a position he never held. The sūrah used a similar move with Jesus — comparing his creation to Adam's, which places both in a single category. Here it uses chronology, which places the disputed ancestor outside the terms of the dispute altogether.

**A Shared Ancestor, Denied to Both Claimants**

The argument cuts against both claimants at once, and it is meant to. The Jews of Madinah and the Christians of the region each had a narrative of belonging that ran through Abraham, and each found in him a warrant for its own standing. The Book refuses both moves with one stroke: it denies that the ancestor can be owned by a community whose distinguishing revelation came after him, and it does so without discarding him. Abraham remains central — named among the chosen families at the sūrah's beginning and discussed at length in the coming verses. What is rejected is the use of his name as a deed of ownership. The question "whose was he?" is set aside for the question "what was he?", which the next verses take up directly.

**The Logic of Priority**

The Book argues from Abraham's priority elsewhere as well, and the reasoning is always about the order in which God sent things. What came first cannot be a later community's property, and what came first sets the standard by which later claims are measured. That order is the sūrah's answer to communities that treated the antiquity of their traditions as proof of standing. Antiquity is not the issue; the sequence of revelation is — and in that sequence the pure worship of Abraham stands before the books that came after him.

**The Closing Question**

The verse ends: do you not understand? The Book uses such endings to place responsibility on the listener rather than supply another premise. The point is not obscure; a chronology the hearers accept has been set out, a conclusion drawn from it, and the hearer is left to recognise the implication. The question is not an insult — it asks for a step a person may simply decline to take, and the sūrah keeps addressing the same communities for many verses after this one. The coming verses supply the positive account of what Abraham was, in a form that lets the reader compare his own confession with the description as it is read out.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:66

> Here you are! You disputed about what you have ˹little˺ knowledge of, but why do you now argue about what you have no knowledge of? Allah knows and you do not know

**A Rebuke in Two Stages**

The verse is an observation about the disputants' own record: you argued about things you had some knowledge of, and now you argue about something you have no knowledge of at all. Two disagreements are set side by side, and the difference is in what the parties could bring to them.

The rebuke is severe but delivered gently, in the manner of a person pointing out an inconsistency rather than pronouncing a verdict. The first dispute concerned matters within the community's competence — its own scripture, its own laws, the points its own scholars differed over. The second concerns Abraham, who lived long before their books and about whom their traditions carried no direct information. The charge is that the habit of arguing has outlived the basis for it.

**What the Two Disputes Were**

The commentators identified the first dispute in several ways: arguments over their own scripture and its contested passages, over what their law permitted and forbade, or over whether the new revelation agreed with the earlier books. All the accounts describe an argument conducted about material the parties actually possessed — which is why the verse can say they had knowledge of it. The second dispute, over Abraham, is one no party could settle from evidence: he preceded their books by centuries, and neither community could produce a record of his practice that decided the question. The verse thus marks the difference between two kinds of argument — one examined from texts in hand, one conducted from inherited pride — and objects only to the second being treated as the first.

**"Allah Knows and You Do Not Know"**

The closing clause states the ground of the difference. Knowledge of the matter belongs to God; the disputants do not have it. The formula recurs in the Book where a dispute has reached the point at which the parties are asserting what they cannot establish, and its function is to close the argument by naming the only available authority. The hearer is left with the choice the sūrah has been building toward: accept the account revealed by the One who knows, or keep maintaining a position for which no evidence exists. The verse does not offer itself as one opinion among the disputants; it states the fact that makes the dispute decidable.

**Why Abraham Makes the Rebuke Stick**

Abraham is the right subject for this charge because he is the shared ancestor both communities prized, and the Book's own narrative about him arrives as revelation — which is exactly the point. Knowledge of Abraham must come from God, since the period is closed and no witness remains. A community that had received a book knew this about a figure who preceded its book. The charge is therefore about method as much as content: the disputants treated their own position as the measure of a matter only revelation could supply.

**Disputing After Knowledge: the Sūrah's Pattern**

The distinction this verse draws runs through the sūrah from its opening pages. It stated that the People of the Book did not dispute among themselves until knowledge had come to them, and then did so out of mutual envy (3:19); it warns the believers not to become like those who divided and differed after clear proofs had reached them (3:105); and the account of the first communities makes the same diagnosis — they disputed the scriptures only after the proofs arrived, out of jealousy (2:213). In each case the arrival of knowledge is what turns a disagreement into a charge. This verse applies the distinction at a smaller scale, between two arguments conducted by the same people, and the sūrah's repeated use of the pattern shows it treats a dispute conducted in the dark differently from one conducted in the light.

**What Can and Cannot Be Known**

The sentence leaves the reader a plain intellectual rule: the soundness of an argument depends on what the arguer can know. Where evidence is available, the Book presses for the inference; where it is not, the Book supplies revelation and expects it to be accepted as such. The next verse gives the positive account of Abraham — a series of negations followed by a description, which is how the Book usually corrects a claim it cannot accept.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:67

> Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian; he submitted in all uprightness and was not a polytheist

**Two Negations, Then an Affirmation**

The verse answers the question of what Abraham was, and it begins by excluding the two categories the disputants wanted him to occupy. Only after clearing both names does it say what he was.

The order is deliberate, the order of a legal finding that first removes the competing claims and then states the position. The Book uses this technique where an argument has become a competition over labels: it denies the labels and supplies a description. What is affirmed after the exclusions is not a faction but a disposition.

**Ḥanīf: Turning Away and Turning Toward**

The word rendered "in all uprightness" — *ḥanīf* — describes a person who inclines away from the worship of anything other than God. In the Book's usage the term is tied to Abraham specifically, and it carries the idea of a deliberate turn: away from the practices of a people gone astray, toward the One to whom worship belongs.

The Book pairs the term with Abraham repeatedly. The sūrah of the Cattle records the Prophet's declaration of following the creed of Abraham, the upright, who was not a polytheist (6:161); the sūrah of the Bee describes Abraham as an exemplar, obedient, upright, and no polytheist (16:120); and the sūrah of the Cow instructs the believers to say their creed is that of Abraham the upright (2:135). The recurrence shows the term functioning as the name of a religion that existed before the books — which is why it can serve as the common ground in this dispute.

**The Third Negation**

The clause denying that Abraham was a polytheist is not a repetition of the first two denials. A person could be neither Jewish nor Christian and still worship other gods; those were not the only options in the ancient world. The Book adds the clause because the argument concerns the oneness of God — the sūrah's subject for many verses now. Abraham's refusal of idols is the pattern the Book keeps returning to, and the verse identifies his religion with that refusal before anything else. The positive content of his faith is the combination: turning from false objects and facing the one God, and both halves of the movement are named.

**What the Verse Removes From the Dispute**

The exclusions answer both communities at once, and without insult. Neither is told its tradition is false; the verse simply declines to trace Abraham's religion to either. That restraint matters. The sūrah has just rebuked the two communities for arguing about the ancestor without knowledge, and it now supplies a description neither can claim as property. Abraham is removed from the contest over communal standing and placed where the Book consistently puts the prophets: in a category defined by conduct toward God rather than membership in a later community.

**A Portrait of a Man Who Stood Alone**

The three clauses also read as a portrait. Uprightness in this vocabulary is a direction taken by an individual, not a position inherited from a group, and the denial of polytheism is a statement about a person surrounded by it. Abraham's story in the Book is exactly that: a single figure arguing with his people, breaking the images, and being thrown into the fire for it. The verse's grammar is the grammar of that life.

**The Shape of the Argument**

The sūrah's case against the scriptured communities has been building to this point: Jesus was created as Adam was; the communities dispute about Abraham without information; Abraham belonged to neither of them. Each step removes a claim and narrows the field. What remains is the question of who can claim to be his descendant in spirit — which the next verse answers in terms of following, not descent. The description of Abraham is therefore also a criterion the sūrah applies to its own readers: the Muslim community is neither Jewish nor Christian, refuses all association with God, and takes as its founding model the man who did the same before any of the books existed.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:68

> Indeed, those who have the best claim to Abraham are his followers, this Prophet, and the believers. And Allah is the Guardian of those who believe

**Who Has a Claim on Abraham**

Having said what Abraham was not, the verse says who belongs to him. Three parties are named: those who followed him, this Prophet, and the believers. The order runs from Abraham's own contemporaries to the messenger of the sūrah and then to the community that accepted him.

The structure of the claim is the point. Belonging is defined by following, and the first category consists of the people who followed Abraham in his own time and after. Everything that follows is measured by the same standard, which is why the two later parties can be named in the same sentence. What is described is a continuous body of people united by a way rather than by blood.

**Follower Before Ancestor**

The disputes the sūrah has been answering turned on ancestry, and the verse replaces ancestry with adherence. The Arabic term describes those who come after a person on a road, taking his direction — the word the Book uses throughout for a community's relation to a prophet.

The replacement has consequences the sūrah's first readers would feel. A claim resting on descent can be checked in a genealogy; a claim resting on following can be checked in conduct. The Book consistently prefers the second test, and it does so without denying that the first exists — the Prophet's own descent from Abraham through Ishmael is part of the tradition. What the verse refuses is the notion that genealogy settles the matter. A descendant who does not follow the ancestor's religion has no claim under this criterion, and the Book makes the same point explicitly elsewhere when it denies that a covenant extends to wrongdoers (2:124).

**"This Prophet"**

The second party named is the Prophet of the sūrah, designated by the phrase the Qur'an uses for Muhammad in its arguments with the scriptured communities. The wording carries the claim the passage has been building: if following is the criterion, then the messenger who has brought the clearest statement of God's oneness and the rejection of all partners stands closest to the man who did the same in his own day. The claim is not that Abraham belonged to the later community — the previous verse explicitly denies that. It is that the religion of Abraham and the message of this Prophet are one, and the people of that religion are his people.

**The Believers Included**

The third party is the believing community, and its inclusion extends the claim beyond the messenger. No one in that community had Abraham's lineage or lived in his era, yet the verse names them among those with the best claim to him. That extension makes the verse a statement about the religion rather than about a personal succession. A community assembled from different tribes and backgrounds is told that its relation to the founding monotheist rests on what it holds — a doctrine that answers Abraham's own prayer, recorded in the Book, for a nation to be raised from his descendants that would submit to God. The sūrah's claim is that the answer is recognisable in the community's confession rather than in its ancestry.

**God as Guardian of the Believers**

The verse ends with God's relation to the believers: He is their guardian. The word is the same one the sūrah used when warning against taking disbelievers as guardians, and the placement within one sūrah is not accidental. The believers were told not to seek security from those outside their faith; the closing clause tells them whose protection they hold in exchange — stated as a fact about God's relation to them rather than as a prediction of events. The community's confidence does not rest on its size or its allies, and the verse supplies the alternative in a few words.

**A Definition That Reads Both Ways**

The three parties are not merely a list of groups; they are a criterion. Reading it, a believer learns that his standing rests on the same thing Abraham's did, and the sūrah leaves him to check whether that is where his confidence sits. The order of the list carries the lesson too: the followers come first because they set the standard, the messenger second as the living instance of the same way, and the believers last as the community being formed by it. A reader finds his own name in the third position and his standing tested by the first — the question the verse leaves with him is whether he belongs among the people who follow. The next verses describe those who had the opportunity and did something else.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:69

> Some of the People of the Book wish to mislead you ˹believers˺. They mislead none but themselves, yet they fail to perceive it

**A Wish Directed at the Believers**

The verse reports an intention rather than an act. Some among the People of the Book wish to lead the believers astray — the verb describes a desire to pull a person off a road — and the sūrah states the desire without describing any particular attempt. A community told that the pursuit of its members is hoped for by others has been warned about something no incident would necessarily reveal; the believers are told what is being thought about them behind the arguments.

**"They Mislead None but Themselves"**

The second half of the sentence reverses the direction of the harm. The wish is real, and the Book says its outcome falls on those who hold it. This reversal is stated repeatedly, with a variety of images: the hypocrites hope to deceive God and the believers while deceiving only themselves (2:9), and those who broke faith in the desert are told they wronged nobody but themselves (2:57). The claim is not that intended victims escape all harm — the verse exists to warn them. It is that the deeper damage of a corrupting act settles on the one who commits it.

**The Envy Behind the Wish**

The sūrah's companion account names the motive: a group of the People of the Book wish to turn the believers back to disbelief out of envy within their own souls, after the truth has become clear to them (2:109). The verse under discussion does not repeat the diagnosis, but the passages belong together, and the commentators read them as one report.

The identification of envy as the engine makes the wish intelligible. These groups had argued against the message on doctrinal grounds; the Book's account says the argument was not the reason for their resistance. It also sets a limit on discussion, since a disputant whose real concern is his community's standing cannot be persuaded by evidence about a prophet's standing. What the sūrah asks of its readers is that they understand the nature of the opposition they face.

**"They Fail to Perceive It"**

The verse closes by noting that these people do not notice what is happening to themselves. The Arabic describes an absence of awareness rather than of intelligence. The groups being described were learned, established, and in possession of scriptures; they understood the arguments they were making. What they lacked was the perception to see that the damage they arranged for others was landing on their own position — and that the standing and learning they trusted were exactly what their conduct was eroding. The warning is for them as much as for the believers, and the sūrah continues to address them in what follows.

**What the Verse Asks of the Believers**

The verse does not instruct the believers to withdraw from the other communities, and it authorises no hostility. It tells them what is wished for them and what the wishing costs the one who does it. Taken with the preceding verses — Abraham belongs to those who follow his way, and there are people who would like to detach the believers from what they hold — it produces the disposition the rest of the passage appeals to: hold the religion firmly, argue fairly where argument is offered, and know that the pressure on the community is intention rather than accident.

**Disclosure and Address Together**

Having told the believers who wishes them ill, the sūrah turns to the other party directly and puts questions to them in the verses that follow. This combination is characteristic of the Book's handling of opponents: the community is told what it faces, and the opposing party is told its conduct is visible and its reasons are being requested. Nothing is kept from either side, and no accusation is made that the accused is not invited to answer.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:70

> O People of the Book! Why do you reject the signs of Allah while you bear witness ˹to their truth˺

**A Question That Puts the Accused on the Spot**

The sūrah now addresses the People of the Book directly, and it opens with a question about conduct rather than belief: why reject the signs of God while you bear witness? The Book uses this interrogative form repeatedly with the scriptured communities, and its effect is to shift the discussion from a contest of doctrines to a matter of the addressees' own consistency. The question does not ask what they believe; it asks why, holding what they hold, they do what they do — a harder question to deflect, because the premise it rests on is supplied by the addressee, not the speaker.

**"While You Bear Witness" — the Heart of the Charge**

The witnessing clause is what makes the question incriminating, and the commentators gave it two readings, both damaging. Al-Ṭabarī read the verse as referring to the refusal of the Prophet's mission by Jews and Christians who, on the Muslim understanding, find his coming announced in their own books (7:157): people in a position to recognise what they reject. Al-Qurṭubī recorded the other reading: they bear witness to the truth brought by the earlier prophets, whose message agrees with what the Prophet teaches, so their rejection contradicts the testimony they already carry.

Either way the charge has the same structure: rejection here is not an absence of information but a decision made in the presence of it. The sūrah has made this point repeatedly — the dispute over Jesus continued *after knowledge had come*, and those whose hearts swerve follow the ambiguous while the clear verses stand before them (3:7). Here the witnesses are the defendants, which is what gives the sentence its severity.

**The Signs in View**

The word for signs covers both the verses of revelation and the events that accompany a prophet's mission, and the sūrah has just narrated a series of them: a birth without a father, a child who speaks, healings, the raising of the dead, and the removal of the messenger from the hands of his plotters. The question therefore points at a body of material, not a single proposition — and since the Book calls its own verses signs, the recitation the community has heard is part of the case against it. What is being rejected is not a foreign claim but what the hearers have been given.

**A Question Framed to Make the Addressee Supply the Verdict**

The verse's position matters. It follows the passage in which the Book declared Abraham neither Jew nor Christian and named his followers as those with the best claim on him. A community that prized Abraham and accepted that he preceded both books is now asked why it rejects what has come — and the implication, that this conduct departs from the pattern of the ancestor they invoke, is left for the hearer to supply himself. That is the strength of the interrogative mode: the accusation is carried by the listener's own premises rather than the speaker's assertion.

**What the Question Does for the Believers**

The verse also explains to the Muslim community the resistance they are meeting, without instructing retaliation. The believers have just been told that some wish to mislead them; now they are shown the shape of that opposition — a refusal made by people with grounds to know better. They are not asked to treat their neighbours as ignorant. The charge is that these communities have the information and act against it, and the Book addresses them openly rather than talking about them.

**A Formula the Sūrah Repeats**

Near its end the sūrah asks the same people an almost identical question: why do you turn those who believe away from the way of God, seeking to make it crooked, while you are witnesses? (3:99). The two verses form a pair some thirty verses apart, and the commentators read them together. The recurring formula is a question about consistency, addressed to people whose own tradition supplies the standard: the Book does not accuse them of following a different religion, but of failing to follow their own with integrity. That is the more serious charge, and it leaves the door open in the way the sūrah's continuing address to them shows.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:71

> O People of the Book! Why do you mix the truth with falsehood and hide the truth knowingly

**The Second Question**

The verse keeps the addressee and the interrogative form, and sharpens the charge. The first question concerned rejection; this one concerns the handling of truth and falsehood, and it names two acts: mixing the two, and concealing the truth while knowing it.

The pair is significant because one act is public and the other hidden. A person can hide a text, a reading, or a fact; he can also construct a presentation in which the true and the false are blended until the distinction no longer shows. The charge covers both operations, and its question-form asks the addressees to explain a method rather than deny a conclusion.

**The Image Behind "Mixing"**

The verb describes blending one thing into another until they cannot be separated — the ordinary Arabic word for confusion and for murky water. What the verse describes is not an honest mistake by a reader who cannot tell two meanings apart; it is the deliberate production of a compound in which true material carries false conclusions.

The sūrah of the Cow states the same charge in almost identical words: do not clothe truth with falsehood, nor conceal the truth while you know (2:42). The two verses are read together, and the commentators treated them as one practice — the characteristic practice of a community that wants to keep its position while retaining the authority of the revelation it holds. The truth is not denied; it is used as a vessel.

**Concealment and What Is Being Hidden**

The second act is concealment, and this sūrah describes its most elaborate form a few verses later: a group distorts the Book with their tongues so that what they say sounds like it is from it, and then attributes it to God knowingly (3:78). The classical commentators also developed the distinction between altering a text and altering its interpretation, and disagreed about which the Book accuses the earlier communities of. This verse does not need that dispute resolved: it charges the addressees with hiding something they know, which presupposes they still have access to it. A community that had genuinely lost the material could not be accused of concealing it. The verse assumes the truth is present among them and withheld.

**The Weight of "Knowingly"**

The sentence ends with the adverb that recurs in the Book's most serious accusations. Its addition changes the category of the offence from error to something else. The Book is consistently careful about the difference between acting in ignorance and acting against one's own knowledge, and its severest language is reserved for the second. That is why the question is put to the community rather than to individuals who might have been misinformed: the charge is corporate, a policy rather than a mistake.

**What the Charge Implies About the Scriptures**

A reader might expect a passage like this to deny that the other community possesses anything genuine. The verse does not. It accuses the community of concealing and mixing what it has — an accusation that makes sense only if what it has includes the truth. This is the sūrah's position throughout: the earlier scriptures are treated as genuine revelations, the Qur'an is described as confirming them, and the failure of the communities is placed in the handling rather than the holding. The charge concerns conduct, and conduct can be changed.

**A Rule for Any Custodian of a Text**

Read apart from the immediate controversy, the verse states a rule for anyone who holds a revelation.
