The two acts it forbids — blending the true with the false, and withholding what one knows — are the ordinary operations by which a religious community defends a position it can no longer support from its own sources. The sūrah's own community is not exempt from the warning; the Book's habit of recording such charges against another party is not a licence for the reader, but the earlier community's story told in advance. The verses that follow describe a stratagem the same community devised, so the sūrah is not naive about the likelihood of a change of heart — it continues to address them anyway, stating the accusation publicly and leaving the question standing.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:72

> A group among the People of the Book said ˹to one another˺, "Believe in what has been revealed to the believers in the morning and reject it in the evening, so they may abandon their faith

**A Stratagem Reported in Full**

The verse reports what a group among the People of the Book said to one another, and it reports it as a plan rather than an accusation. The members are not named; the sūrah records the scheme in their own words, so the reader meets a conspiracy rather than a charge laid by an adversary. A community that has been told what is said about it in private can no longer be surprised by the manoeuvre — the disclosure is part of the defence.

**In by Morning, Out by Evening**

The plan is described with precision: appear to accept the revelation in the morning, publicly retract it in the evening. The point is neither the acceptance, which is false, nor the retraction, which is intended. The point is the sequence, and the sequence is the weapon.

The reasoning is easy to reconstruct, and the commentators supplied it. When respected people adopt a position and then abandon it, observers draw conclusions about the position rather than the people. A message its own apparent converts have left within a day looks unstable, and doubt planted that way is cheaper than doubt earned by argument. The plan's target is not the Prophet's claim but the confidence of the ordinary believer watching the performance.

**What the Plan Assumes About the Believers**

The stratagem is applied psychology, and its assumptions are worth spelling out, because the sūrah's answer addresses them. The planners assumed the Muslims' conviction depended on appearances — on who was seen joining and staying — rather than on the content of what they had accepted; and that a young community, many of whose members had left families and standing to join, could be destabilised by the sight of important people coming and going. Both assumptions would hold in a community that had never been told what its foundation was — and the sūrah's response, in the surrounding verses, is to keep telling them. A person whose faith rests on an argument he has understood cannot be moved by a performance; the Book's remedy for the manoeuvre is the account of that argument.

**The Book's Reporting Style**

The sūrah introduces the quotation without commentary and lets it stand — a technique it uses elsewhere with the statements of opponents, letting the reader judge the plan by hearing it. There is a particular dignity in the restraint: the scheme appears in the plotters' own voice, no adjectives are added, and the verse neither gloats nor follows the quotation with a denunciation. The answer to the trick is delivered elsewhere, generally and without reference to the incident.

**How the Book Answers a Stratagem**

The sūrah does not tell the Prophet to expose the group publicly, and it does not instruct the believers to treat future retractions as tests of loyalty. What the passage as a whole does is continue stating the case for the message with the same arguments used before: a claim about revelation's truth is answered by the truth of revelation, and the community is equipped with reasons it can hold rather than suspicions it must guard. The next verse records a further element of the plan and the command given to the Prophet in response — neither a counter-conspiracy nor a public rebuke, but a statement about where guidance comes from.

**What the Record Gives Later Readers**

Read from outside, the manoeuvre identifies something true of religious communities generally: their confidence can be eroded by the visible conduct of prominent people, whether or not that conduct is sincere. The sūrah's answer is not to deny the vulnerability but to locate the community's certainty elsewhere — guidance is God's, bounty is in His hands, and He gives it to whom He wills, as the following verses say. What is defended is not a reputation to be managed but a position held by evidence and by gift, and neither of those is reachable by the tactics reported here. Any community whose members are watched by their neighbours will meet the same pressure, and the condition for withstanding it is the one the sūrah keeps supplying: knowledge of what one has accepted and of the ground on which it was accepted.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:73

> And only believe those who follow your religion." Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "Surely, ˹the only˺ true guidance is Allah's guidance." ˹They also said,˺ "Do not believe that someone will receive ˹revealed˺ knowledge similar to yours or argue against you before your Lord." Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "Indeed, all bounty is in the Hands of Allah—He grants it to whoever He wills. And Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing."

**The Second Clause of the Same Plan**

The verse continues the report of what the group said among themselves, and its second part concerns who deserves to be believed at all: only those who follow their own religion are to be accepted as credible — a rule that closes the circle of trust around the community that made it.

The arrangement of the two elements is instructive. The first part of the plan was tactical: perform acceptance, then withdraw. This part is structural: hold that anyone outside the community cannot be telling the truth about religion. Together they amount to a system in which the message can never be accepted on its merits and never on testimony, since the only credible witnesses are those who have already refused it.

**The Interjection From God**

The sūrah breaks into the quotation with an instruction to the Prophet: say, true guidance is God's guidance. The interruption splits the reported speech and answers it in the middle — a construction the commentators noted and resolved in several ways. However the syntax is settled, the content is decisive. The claim that truth is confined to one community is met by the statement that guidance belongs to God, which removes the question of credibility from the arena of communal authority and returns it to the One who guides.

**The Fear Behind the Second Remark**

The group's other remark is reported in the same breath: do not believe that anyone will be given revealed knowledge similar to yours, or that he will argue against you before your Lord. The reasoning is transparent, and it is not really a doctrine about revelation. It is a fear about standing: if someone outside the community can be given a book, then possessing a book no longer guarantees a community's position in the argument. The sūrah reports the fear because it explains the resistance better than any doctrinal objection could — and the answer given to it concerns God's freedom to give, not the merits of the recipients.

**"All Bounty Is in the Hands of Allah"**

The Prophet is told to say that bounty belongs to God, that He grants it to whom He wills, and that He is All-Bountiful and All-Knowing. The sentence answers the objection at its root by placing the distribution of revelation in the same category as the distribution of everything else — the sūrah's opening verses had already described God giving authority and honour to whom He pleases. What is asserted is that no community holds a veto over God's generosity: the community that received a revelation earlier cannot determine who receives one later. And if the giver is All-Knowing, the choice of recipient rests on His knowledge rather than on any community's qualifications.

**The Two Names at the End**

Each name answers part of the objection. Bounty explains why gifts are given at all — giving proceeds from generosity, not from the recipients' merit. Knowledge explains why the gifts reach the right recipients — the giver knows what each people can carry. The pair recurs at the ends of verses about provision, prophecy, and mercy, and its presence here ties the distribution of revelation to the ordinary providence by which the world is sustained. The same hand that supplies food and rain decides where a book appears — which makes the jealousy in the group's clause unintelligible as a religious position and recognisable as what the sūrah's companion verse calls it: envy.

**"A Group Among Them"**

The sūrah does not say how many people held the reported position; the phrase is a group among the People of the Book. That qualification, used throughout these accounts, prevents a report about a faction from becoming a verdict on a people. The Book's practice here is consistent: it names the conduct, describes the reasoning behind it, answers the reasoning, and leaves open the question of who else shares it. The reader closes the verse with a clear picture of a tactic and a clear statement of the principle that defeats it, and with no invitation to assess anyone not mentioned in the text.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:74

> He chooses whoever He wills to receive His mercy. And Allah is the Lord of infinite bounty

**A Sentence That Answers an Envy**

The verse follows the statement about bounty with a sentence about mercy, in the same key: God chooses for His mercy whom He wills, and He is the Lord of abundant bounty. Read after the reported fear of the previous verse — that someone else might be given revealed knowledge like theirs — the sentence explains what that objection had misunderstood. Bounty describes the generosity of the giver; mercy describes the relief that reaches the one who receives. The sūrah places them in sequence: mercy is what is chosen, and bounty is the source from which the choosing proceeds. What the group had feared as a threat is thereby classified as an act of mercy — a classification that removes any sense that anything has been taken from anyone.

**Chosen, Not Earned**

The verb describes selection, and it repeats the sūrah's pattern from the opening verses: authority is given to whom God wills; honour is His to confer; the families named at the sūrah's beginning were chosen rather than self-made. The recurrence is a running correction of the assumption behind the whole dispute. Every party argued from something it possessed — a lineage, a book, a history — and each was being told that the standing it relied on had been given rather than achieved. The sentence here is the general form of that correction, and its brevity makes it harder to argue with than a long demonstration would be.

**Who Is Chosen Here**

The commentators offered several identifications of the mercy's recipient: the Prophet himself, chosen for prophethood despite objections that the position should have remained elsewhere; the Qur'an; or the religion established through it. The readings are not far apart. In each, what is described is a conferral no community could award itself and none could withhold from another — which is the verse's point. The sūrah's own report of the objection, that a messenger could not be raised from among the unlettered, fits the first reading; the surrounding verses about the Book fit the second. What matters is the act: the choice belongs to God, and the object of the choice is a mercy, not a property.

**The Lord of Infinite Bounty**

The final description uses a word denoting greatness and abundance, which the Book applies to gifts that are not measured out — knowledge, provision, reward. It is always a statement about the scale of the source, not the quantity in a particular case. Its placement here answers the fear of the previous verse a second time: if bounty is infinite, then a gift given to one party is not subtracted from another. Those who worried that someone else might receive something like theirs were thinking from a limited store, and the verse names the giver as the Lord of a store without limit. That single word removes the ground on which the whole anxiety rested.

**What the Passage Says About Religious Envy**

Taken together, the verses diagnose a recurring condition of religious communities: the conviction that one's own standing depends on others being excluded from the same gifts. It produces exactly the conduct described earlier — concealment, mixing, stratagems aimed at outsiders' confidence. The Book's answer does not argue that the gifts are distributed fairly, and promises the envious nothing; it states that the distribution belongs to God and describes it as mercy. A reader who accepts that has removed from his own religion the assumption that its value depends on anyone else's deprivation — a service this verse performs for every community that recites it.

**Comfort and Caution in One Sentence**

For the believers who were warned that a group wished to mislead them, the sentence is reassurance about the ground they stand on: what they hold was given, and it can neither be earned away from them nor taken by the manoeuvres just described. For those who made the plan, it is a caution: the gifts they were guarding were never theirs to allocate. The Book states both readings without differentiating its audience and leaves the reader to work out which side of the sentence he stands on. And it is worth noticing the small thing that is easy to overlook: the mercy is something God chooses a person *for*, not something earned by rank, learning, or lineage. No one can inspect another's selection or certify his own — which is why the Book leaves its readers with the practice it has been describing rather than a status they can point to.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:75

> There are some among the People of the Book who, if entrusted with a stack of gold, will readily return it. Yet there are others who, if entrusted with a single coin, will not repay it unless you constantly demand it. This is because they say, "We are not accountable for ˹exploiting˺ the Gentiles." And ˹so˹ they attribute lies to Allah knowingly

**The Test of a Trust**

The verse describes a community by its behaviour with property, and a comparison makes the description exact: among the People of the Book are those who return a large deposit unpursued, and those who will not return a small sum unless the creditor stays at their door. The choice of subject is deliberate. A religion is most visible not in its arguments but in its handling of what belongs to other people, and the sūrah puts the test where no doctrine can obscure it.

**The Small Coin and the Stack of Gold**

The contrast is not between honesty and theft but between honesty that costs nothing and honesty that requires effort. Returning a great deposit costs nothing to one who never meant to keep it; returning a single coin to a man asking for it costs a decision. The two sums are far apart: the word for the stack of gold names a quantity many times a single coin, though al-Rāzī noted it is not a precise measure — the Book uses the same word for heaps of gold and for a great bride-gift (3:14; 4:20). The test is which sum a person will honour. The commentators also explained the entrusting in the widest sense: a loan, a purchase price, or property left for safekeeping — all claims the holder is obliged to satisfy. The second case adds a practical cruelty: the refusal is a policy of resistance that wears down the person owed, calculating that the effort of recovery will eventually exceed the value. The Book presents that as a religious matter, not a commercial one.

**"We Are Not Accountable for the Gentiles"**

The verse reports the justification the community offered, as the reason for the conduct rather than an excuse: the religion's obligations do not extend outside it, so an outsider's property may be treated differently. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī recorded the explanations: on one account some held that agreements with members of their own community who accepted the new message became void; on another, the attitude was tribal loyalty rather than law. The word the verse puts in their mouths means non-Jews, and the Book uses the same term elsewhere for those it calls unlettered (2:78; 3:20; 7:157–58; 62:2).

The claim deserves attention because it is the most dangerous position a religious community can reach. A rule of conduct that applies only inside a boundary turns the religion into a loyalty and dissolves the very obligations the scriptures were given to establish — the Book's question elsewhere, why do you believe in part of the scripture and reject the rest (2:85), applies to this position exactly.

**"They Attribute Lies to Allah Knowingly"**

The last clause attaches the conduct to a claim about God: the policy was justified by what God required, and the sūrah says what they attributed to Him was not His. This is the same charge made two verses earlier about concealing and mixing, worded the same way, with the adverb marking the deliberateness. The community did not merely reason wrongly about God; it invented a position and presented it as His — and the Book treats that as the gravest form of the offence this whole passage describes.

**Trusts in the Book's Vocabulary**

The vocabulary of trusts runs through the Book and covers property, agreements, duties of office, treatment of dependents, and the inheritance of revelation itself — the believers are commanded to return what is entrusted to those entitled to it. Placing property in that framework makes the verse about religion rather than commerce: a community that holds a book holds a trust, and a person's handling of a coin indicates what he will do with a text. It is one test at two scales, which is why the verse can move from a debt to a lie about God in three clauses — one habit of mind behind both.

**How the Verse Was Used: Ibn ʿAbbās's Correction**

The tradition preserved a report in which Muslims came to Ibn ʿAbbās and claimed, against a non-Muslim community living under treaty, that nothing prevented them from taking those people's property. He rejected the claim: no wealth may be taken from the treaty peoples beyond the indemnity they had undertaken to pay, the jizyah mentioned in the sūrah of Repentance (9:29). The report shows how the community's teachers applied this verse — a warning against reproducing on the other side the very misuse the verse condemns. Al-Qurṭubī attached a further report tracing a chain from resentment to betrayal, from betrayal to the loss of compassion, and from there to faith departing a person's heart.

**The Correction That Follows**

The verse ends with the false claim stated, and the next verse begins the answer: those who honour their trusts and are mindful of God are the ones God loves. The sūrah leaves no gap for argument — the claim about Gentiles is answered by a statement about God's love. The pair of verses teaches that a community's boundary does not bound its obligations, and that the obligations are where God's approval is decided. The sūrah will later make the diversity explicit, stating that the People of the Book are not all alike and naming an upright group among them (3:113) — the very conduct this verse has just illustrated in its first party.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:76

> Absolutely! Those who honour their trusts and shun evil—surely Allah loves those who are mindful ˹of Him˺

**The Answer to the Previous Claim**

The verse responds directly to the justification reported a line earlier — that obligations toward outsiders did not bind. The answer is not an argument about the clause; it is a statement about who enjoys God's approval. The Book often answers a false principle this way, by replacing it rather than refuting it: the disputed rule was about which people a community owed honest dealing, and the answer given is a description of the conduct God loves. Comparing the two sentences, the reader sees the clause set aside rather than engaged — and the ground of the comparison named in the closing phrase: mindfulness of God.

**What "Trusts" Covers**

The term rendered trusts covers more than a deposit for safekeeping: everything placed in a person's care — property, agreements, duties of office, the treatment of dependents, and a community's inheritance of revelation. Read in its widest sense, it explains why a question about commercial dealing is answered with a statement about divine approval. The coin and the book are both trusts, and the Book treats them the same way: what is entrusted remains the property of the one who entrusted it, and the holder is accountable for his handling of it. A community that had convinced itself that one class of trust did not bind it had, by that reasoning, loosened a principle reaching far beyond the case in front of it.

**Mindfulness as the Second Condition**

The verse names two things: honouring trusts and mindfulness of God. The second is not a decoration on the first. *Taqwā* in the Book's usage is the guardedness that keeps a person aware of his accountability — it is what makes fulfilling a trust a matter of conscience rather than convenience. The pairing recurs in the Book's portraits of the righteous and in the sūrah's earlier description of the people who inherit the Garden. The distinction the verse draws is precise: a person who fulfils a trust because he fears consequences performs the act; a person who does it because he is mindful of God has understood what the act is for. It is the second fulfilment that the verse attaches to God's love.

**God Loves the Mindful**

The closing clause states the reward in the vocabulary the sūrah has been using since the passage on love opened. Earlier, the Book told its readers that if they love God, following the Prophet would bring God's love in return; here the love is attached to a specific conduct — honest handling of what one holds. The Book's attributions of divine love, gathered together, are a list of conducts rather than statuses: God loves those who do good, those who are mindful, those who rely on Him, those who act justly. The community under discussion in this passage had no shortage of status and was in the middle of arguing about it; the verse ends by naming the conduct God's approval follows. In one sentence it both answers the false claim and describes what is asked in place of the system the claim represented.

**A Standard That Applies to the Reader**

The passage has been distinguishing people within the same community: the previous verse placed those who return a large deposit beside those who withhold a single coin; this verse states the principle that decides between them; and the sūrah will later state explicitly that the People of the Book are not all alike. The Book is not conducting a case against a people — it is describing a conduct and naming the standard that judges it, and the standard applies inside as well as outside the group.

The clause about trust is also one of those sentences that addresses every reader, since everyone holds something that belongs to someone else: property, a position, a confidence, a text — the measure is the same for all of them. What the passage does next is describe the parties who did the opposite, trading their covenant for a price, so that the reader can see both the standard and what departing from it looks like.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:77

> Indeed, those who trade Allah's covenant and their oaths for a fleeting gain will have no share in the Hereafter. Allah will neither speak to them, nor look at them, nor purify them on the Day of Judgment. And they will suffer a painful punishment

**A Trade Described in Three Parts**

The verse names the transaction, the price, and the loss. The merchants are those who sell God's covenant and their own oaths for a small gain, and the consequence is no share in the Hereafter. The proportions are deliberate: the thing traded away is a pledge made to God, and the thing received is described by a word that diminishes it.

The Book uses the language of commerce for such exchanges repeatedly — people are said to sell God's signs for a price, to sell the Book and their oaths, and the sūrah of the Cow describes those who exchange guidance for error. The consistency of the metaphor tells the reader how such conduct is assessed: not merely a failure but a purchase, judged by the value of what was given up against what was received.

**The Covenant and the Oaths**

The two things sold are named separately, and the distinction is worth marking. A covenant with God is the standing obligation of a community that has received a revelation and pledged itself to it; an oath is a particular promise made by a particular person, often in the course of a dispute. Together they cover the general and the particular: a community's failure is rarely a single lie — it is a practice of making promises it does not keep, which erodes the general pledge along with each specific one. The previous verse stated the positive requirement — those who honour their trusts and are mindful, God loves. What this verse adds is the mechanism: the pledges are traded, and traded *for something*, which means the seller has made a calculation about what his word is worth.

**"A Fleeting Gain"**

The price is described by a word for something small and passing, which the Book uses for worldly goods set against the Hereafter. This is where the verse's argument does its work: the exchange is not condemned because trading is wrong or because the parties wanted something, but because the price was inadequate to the thing sold — the seller misjudged the value. The Book's ethics of trust rest on a claim about relative worth, not merely a rule about promises: a person breaks his word when he has decided that the offer in front of him matters more than what he has pledged. The verse names that decision and states its cost.

**The Three Withholdings**

The consequence is stated in three negative clauses: God will not speak to them, will not look at them, and will not purify them on the Day of Judgment. The commentators treated the language carefully, since other passages describe God addressing the wicked on that day; their conclusion is that the clauses describe the withdrawal of favour rather than a literal impossibility. In a culture where being addressed, being looked upon with regard, and being treated as clean were the marks of acceptance, the sentence puts a person before his Lord in the position of an outcast — the complete reversal of the welcome the previous verse promised the trustworthy.

**Why the Trade Is Named Before the Punishment**

The order of the sentences is deliberate: what was done and what it cost come before what follows, and the sequence gives the punishment its intelligibility. Met alone, the three withholdings are severity without cause; met after a pledge sold for a small price, they are a proportion. The sūrah uses this arrangement throughout — it described the killing of prophets before announcing a painful punishment, and the same pattern appears here in miniature. Verse by verse, the Book teaches that its severity is always a response to something named in the text, never a display.

**A Warning With the Believers in View**

The immediate target is a group among the People of the Book, and the Book applies the same warning to its own community elsewhere, using the same language of trading a covenant for a small gain. The reader is given a description he can use to inspect his own practice: does one's word hold when keeping it is costly? The test is left where it can be applied — not in the dispute over which community has the better claim, but in whether the promise made yesterday is still honoured today. The following verses turn to a charge about speech itself.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:78

> There are some among them who distort the Book with their tongues to make you think this ˹distortion˺ is from the Book—but it is not what the Book says. They say, "It is from Allah"—but it is not from Allah. And ˹so˺ they attribute lies to Allah knowingly

**A Charge About Speech**

The verse describes a group among the People of the Book distorting the Book with their tongues so that what they say sounds as though it came from it — when it does not — and then attributing it to God, when it is not from God. The final clause names the act: attributing lies to God knowingly.

The subject is not the copying of a text but the recitation of one. The image is of men reading aloud and bending the words as they go, so that a listener without the text in front of him hears something the text does not contain. The charge concerns the space between what is written and what is said about it — a space every religious community occupies whenever it explains its scripture.

**The Tongue as the Instrument**

The phrase is exact. A distortion made with the tongue can be a change of wording, a shift of emphasis, or an explanation attached so closely to the reading that the hearer cannot separate the two. What all three share is that the original remains intact behind the performance, and the performer knows where the difference lies.

The Book's other account of the same conduct uses the language of writing: woe to those who write the book with their own hands and then say, this is from God, to sell it for a small price (2:79). The two verses supply the two channels by which a scripture can be made to say what it does not — the pen and the voice — and the charge is the same in each: the deliberate substitution of a community's position for the text it holds. The Book does not object to interpretation; it objects to the presentation of interpretation as revelation.

**"You Think It Is From the Book"**

The clause about the hearers is what makes the offence public. The performers are not deceiving themselves; they are managing the impression of a third party, and the verse names the intended effect. The commentators described occasions for such conduct, including recitations before audiences that could not read for themselves, with the meanings adjusted. What the accounts share is an asymmetry of knowledge: the reciter knows the Book and the audience does not. The Book treats that advantage as a trust, and this verse is one of the places where it says what happens when the trust is spent on the speaker's own position.

**The Two Denials, in Checking Order**

The verse states the act twice: it is not what the Book says, and it is not from God. The first denial concerns the text and the second the source, and they stand in the order a person would verify the claim: begin with the Book and ask whether it contains what is attributed to it; if not, the question of origin is settled. Both denials come without qualification from the One competent to settle what His Book contains, and the verdict is delivered without rehearsing the argument that produced it.

**Attributing Lies "Knowingly"**

The last words return to the adverb that has run through this whole passage. It is what separates the conduct described from an error of reading. The Book's consistency is notable: it never accuses the communities it criticises of ignorance; the recurring accusation is acting against knowledge. A person who misreads a text is to be corrected — the Book corrects its own readers with questions like "do you not understand?" A person who decides his community's standing is worth more than the accuracy of what he attributes to God has made a different kind of decision, and the sūrah reserves its sharpest language for that one.

**The Same Choice, Both Ends**

The charge is addressed to a group within the People of the Book, but the account is handed to a community that will recite it for centuries, and its usefulness depends on the reader recognising the conduct rather than the people. The offence is available to anyone who speaks about scripture with authority — which means it is available to the community reading the verse. What the Book provides against it is the text itself, preserved and recited in public, with explanations kept distinguishable from the text, and the repeated instruction not to conceal what one knows. The Book's portrait of the alternative comes later in the sūrah: the covenant taken from those given the Scripture, that they would make it known to the people and not hide it (3:187). The obligation to publish stands against the practice of bending, and the two verses show the same community at the two ends of the same choice.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:79

> It is not appropriate for someone who Allah has blessed with the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood to say to people, "Worship me instead of Allah." Rather, he would say, "Be devoted to the worship of your Lord ˹alone˺"—in accordance with what these prophets read in the Scripture and what they taught

**A Limit Set on the Gifts of God**

The verse states what is not fitting for a human being given scripture, wisdom, and prophethood: to say to people, be my worshippers rather than God's. The list ascends, and the point is that even the highest accumulation of divine gifts does not license a claim on worship.

The Book argues here from the nature of the office rather than the character of any individual. A prophet is constituted by the three gifts named — a revealed text, the capacity to use it rightly, and the commission to deliver it — and every one of them is a relationship of dependence. A man who has received them cannot turn them into a claim on others' worship without denying what they are. The sentence's grammar is impersonal ("it is not for any human being"), which makes it a rule rather than an accusation.

**The Command He Would Give Instead**

A person in that position would say: be devoted to the worship of your Lord — in accordance with what the prophets read in the Scripture and taught. The phrase about the prophets is worth attention: the command is not presented as a new religious instruction but as what the earlier books already contained. The verse's picture of a prophet is a teacher who directs people back to what has been given, which is the pattern the sūrah has drawn throughout — Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus each come with a message recognisable in its content, and the call to worship God alone appears in every one of their traditions.

**Devotion Instead of Deification**

The Arabic verb rendered "be devoted" belongs to the vocabulary of religious service, and the Book also uses its noun-form for those who devote themselves to teaching religion. The contrast the verse draws is between two invitations — *worship me* and *serve your Lord*. One treats the prophet as the destination; the other treats him as the pointer. The alternative offered is not modesty as a personal quality; it is the accurate description of the relation between a messenger and the Lord who sent him, and the verse presents it as the only thing a prophet in that position could say.

**Why the Verse Is in This Passage**

The preceding verses described a community attributing statements to God which He had not made, and reporting their views about obligations and the credibility of outsiders. This verse turns to the question underneath those manoeuvres: who is entitled to worship. The connection is close — a community that appoints itself guardian of who may be recognised tends toward placing its teachers where its religion reserves God's place, and the Book names that tendency elsewhere when it describes people who took their rabbis and monks as lords apart from God. The refutation here restores the relation between servant and Lord by quoting what a prophet himself would say before his people.

**The Historical Setting**

The commentaries connect the verse to questions put to the Prophet about whether he expected veneration, and to the wider controversy over how Jesus was regarded among the Christians. In both readings it is a categorical denial of any human claim on worship, however exalted. For the sūrah's first readers it served another purpose: a community formed around a prophet must sooner or later face the question of how far veneration may go, and the answer here is built into the definition of the office — the Prophet is a teacher who directs his people to God's exclusive worship, in accordance with what the previous prophets read and taught.

**"Devoted Ones": the Word Family and a Variant Reading**

A variant reading of this verse's final clause replaces the command with a description: the prophet says, be *rabbāniyyīn* — lords in the sense of people of devout learning — by what you have taught of the Book and what you have studied. Al-Ṭabarī explained the plural as naming the wise, knowledgeable, or devout; al-Qurṭubī narrowed it to the most eminent scholars, those who know what is lawful and forbidden. The same word appears in the sūrah of the Table, where the rabbis and scholars are described as judges by the Book of God entrusted to them (5:44, 63). A related form occurs earlier in this sūrah, where many *devoted ones* fought alongside their prophets without weakening (3:146) — showing the term moving between learning and devoted service. Al-Ṭabarī also recorded a reading of the final phrase as a prohibition — forbidding the speaker to claim knowledge of the Book he has not studied. On either reading, the standing attaches to what a person learns and does, not to what he is called.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:80

> And he would never ask you to take angels and prophets as lords. Would he ask you to disbelieve after you have submitted

**The Second Prohibition**

The verse continues the description of what a prophet would never ask, and it names the objects: angels and prophets as lords. The previous verse excluded the invitation to worship the speaker; this one excludes the invitation to worship other venerable beings, in the same construction. Together the two sentences cover the whole range of the error. A false teacher may place himself where a deity belongs, or direct his followers to intermediaries whose standing is real but whose status is inflated. The Book closes both routes with one sentence each, and the sūrah's wider argument has supplied the principle: whatever has been created — including angels and messengers — stands on the servant side of the line.

**Angels and Prophets Named Together**

The choice of categories is not arbitrary: they are the two classes of being the Book treats as closest to God, and the communities addressed had strong traditions about each. The angels are described as servants who carry out God's commands without disobedience, and the prophets as the most honoured of humanity. Naming both removes the possibility of a religion of elevated intermediaries — without denigrating the beings themselves. The Book elsewhere records a conversation in the Hereafter in which Jesus is asked whether he told people to take him and his mother as gods apart from God, and his answer is a declaration of his own servitude (5:116). The verse here is that answer stated in advance as a rule for every prophet and every angel.

**The Rhetorical Question**

The verse closes: would he bid you disbelieve after you have submitted? The question shows the attributed proposal to be incoherent on its own terms, since inviting worship of another is inviting unbelief in whatever language it is dressed. The Book's use of the question form differs from the earlier challenges to the People of Book: those asked opponents to explain their conduct; this one works inside the argument by showing that the conduct attributed to a prophet would contradict what a prophet is *for*. The implication is left for the reader: if a man has brought people to submission to God, he will not then ask them to place that submission elsewhere — and a report that he did so has misdescribed either him or what he said.

**Where the Verse Places the Prophets**

The sentence puts the messengers under the same prohibition that protects God's exclusive claim — a striking way of honouring them. They are shielded from being made objects of worship by a rule stated as a fact about their own conduct. The Book's treatment of its prophets is consistent on this: their honours are real, and the sūrah has enumerated them for Jesus at length — a word from God, honoured in two worlds, brought near, taught writing and wisdom, given signs. What those gifts never become is a basis for a share in divinity, and this verse supplies the reason in the form of what the prophet himself would refuse. The reader who has followed the narrative from the family of ʿImrān has seen the gifts and the refusal together, and they are not in tension.

**The Two Verses as One Argument**

With the verse before it, the passage forms a single statement about the limits of loyalty in religion: a prophet directs his people to God and no further; he offers neither himself nor others, and any teaching that does either is not from the prophets. The placement is precise — it follows the verses on the community that attributed false statements to God, and comes just before the covenant God made with the prophets, binding them to acknowledge the messenger who came after them. The sūrah is constructing a picture of one continuous mission whose whole purpose is the worship of God alone, and the two prohibitions are its boundary markers.

**A Test the Reader Can Carry**

The verse offers a portable criterion: any teaching claiming to come from God can be tested by asking where it directs the loyalty it produces. A teacher who accepts veneration for himself, or trains his people to depend on intermediaries rather than on God, has moved outside the pattern — and the criterion is stated in the prophets' own voice. The next verse gives the same argument the form of a covenant, turning the whole history of revelation into a single obligation.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:81

> ˹Remember˺ when Allah made a covenant with the prophets, ˹saying,˺ "Now that I have given you the Book and wisdom, if there comes to you a messenger confirming what you have, you must believe in him and support him." He added, "Do you affirm this covenant and accept this commitment?" They said, "Yes, we do." Allah said, "Then bear witness, and I too am a Witness."

**A Covenant Binding Every Prophet**

The verse reports an undertaking God made with the prophets as a class: given a book and wisdom, they were bound — when a messenger came confirming what they had — to believe in him and support him. The covenant is then ratified: God asks whether they affirm and accept the obligation, they answer that they do, and God calls them to witness while declaring Himself a witness too.

The scope gives the sentence its weight. This is not an agreement with one people at one time; it is a standing obligation attached to the office of prophethood itself. The Book states it as a documented undertaking, complete with offer, acceptance, and witnesses — and a covenant ratified and witnessed cannot be treated as a suggestion.

**What the Covenant Requires**

The two verbs are *believe* and *support*, covering the inner and outer response: the messengers are not merely to acknowledge a subsequent prophet but to stand with him — helping his cause and making it known. The obligation also passes to the followers of each prophet, since the prophets themselves would not fail in such a matter; what the covenant establishes is the standing rule of religious history, in which each messenger's community is answerable for how it receives the message that arrives after them.

**The Particle That Carries Two Readings**

The construction rendered "if there comes to you a messenger confirming what you have" was read in more than one way. Al-Ṭabarī recorded the reading on which the clause is an oath, God swearing by what He has given the prophets before binding them; on another reading it is a condition, stating what they must do should such a messenger arrive. The two arrive at the same substance, and the difference is grammatical rather than material: accepting a later messenger is part of what the earlier scriptures themselves require.

**Who Is Bound**

Al-Rāzī set out the options on the parties addressed. On one view the command is to the prophets themselves, obliged to affirm such a messenger if he appeared in their lifetimes; on another, the verse addresses the prophets' followers, since the messengers would never turn from what God sent while their communities might — al-Qurṭubī recorded this reading too. The question "do you affirm and accept" was read as God's speech to the prophets or the prophets' speech to their followers, and the command to bear witness as God's own. The community's teachers understood the messenger in view to be Muhammad in the first place, whose coming they found announced in the earlier books (7:157), while al-Rāzī noted that the covenant as worded covers any messenger arriving in the terms described.

**The Messenger in View**

Taking the covenant as pointing to the Prophet of this sūrah fits the sūrah's wording: a messenger *confirming* what the earlier messengers had is the formula the sūrah applied to the Qur'an at its opening. The covenant places the Prophet's mission inside the obligations of all the earlier missions rather than beside them. The communities holding the earlier books are told that their own prophets promised to acknowledge the one who fulfilled their message — which gives the sūrah's dispute over Abraham and Jesus a further dimension. The argument is not between one community's revelation and another's; it is between a covenant and the conduct of those who inherited its obligations.

**Why the Report Is Placed Here**

The verse stands after the passage on worship and before the verses on the consequences of turning back. It answers the question a reader might carry from the discussion of Abraham and the People of the Book: on what basis can a new messenger require recognition from communities that already possess scriptures? The answer: their own messengers were bound to this. The obligation is not a newcomer's demand on unwilling predecessors but the honouring of an agreement they accepted — and the sūrah states it before stating the consequences of refusal, so the reader meets the obligation before the warning.

**A History That Holds Together**

The verse's picture of religious history is a single successive mission in which each messenger knows he is not the last. The chapter has repeated the theme — books confirming one another, messengers acknowledging one another, Abraham's people defined by following rather than descent. What the covenant adds is the mechanism: the confirmation is not an accident of history but a term of the commission, agreed before any messenger began. The sentence ends with the witnesses — they bear witness, and God is a Witness — closing the transaction, and leaving what comes next: the fate of a community that turns back after such an agreement.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:82

> Whoever turns back after this, they will be the rebellious

**The Consequence of the Covenant**

The verse follows directly from the undertaking just reported: the prophets accepted an obligation, and God took witnesses to it. Whoever turns back after that agreement belongs among the rebellious. The sentence is short because the preceding verse has done its work — the reader has just seen a ratified covenant with witnesses on both sides, and what remains is what refusing a ratified agreement amounts to.

The word chosen for that condition is worth attention: it is not a word for doubt or error. It describes a departure from a state of obedience, and the Book uses it throughout for those who leave the way their own undertaking had set out.

**What Turning Back Means**

The Arabic describes a turning away and a reversal of course, and the same root covers apostasy, the refusal of a summons, and the conduct of a community that agrees to something and then abandons it. The word therefore describes conduct rather than a mental state, which fits the setting. The covenant was to believe in and support a messenger; turning back from it is not a failure to reach a conclusion but the abandonment of an undertaking. The Book's legal vocabulary is consistent: an agreement obliges the one who accepted it, and a breach is serious in proportion to the standing of the party who received the assurance. A covenant witnessed by God is the most serious kind, and the description of the violator follows from that fact rather than from any added accusation.

**The Rebels as a Category**

The Book applies the term to several groups: those who refuse what God has sent, those who set aside obligations they accepted, and those who turn away after seeing the truth. The constant element is something accepted earlier and then left. The Book does not use the word for people who never received a message, nor for those whose knowledge was too slight to oblige them. That is why the placement matters: the verse comes after a covenant described in detail — acceptance and witnesses reported — so the rebellion in view is the abandonment of a position formally taken up, not a departure from an unfamiliar one.

**Why the Verse Is Not Addressed to the Prophets Alone**

Strictly read, the covenant was made with the prophets, who by the Book's own account would not have turned back. The commentators resolved the difficulty by noting that the obligation passes to the communities that inherit each mission. The point is not technical. The Book treats religious history as a series of communities bound by what their messengers accepted on their behalf — which is why it can address the People of the Book about a covenant they did not personally negotiate. A community in the Book inherits obligations and is judged by its fidelity to them; this verse states the consequence for the heirs when the terms are abandoned.

**Severity With a Qualification**

The language is severe, and the severity is drawn from the agreement it follows, not from the persons of those who break it. That distinction matters, because the passage as a whole has been careful to distinguish members of the communities it addresses: the sūrah has already shown one member returning a deposit faithfully and another withholding a coin, and it will shortly say in explicit terms that they are not all alike. The verse states a rule about those who turn back; it does not convert the statement into a verdict on an entire people.

**A Sentence That Looks Forward**

The verse ends at the edge of a question: if a person had a covenant and abandoned it, what was he heading toward? The next verse takes the question up immediately, asking whether such people desire a way other than God's while everything in the heavens and the earth submits to Him. The second verse supplies the scale against which the abandonment is measured — the offence is defined by its object: not merely a broken promise between people, but a refusal of the arrangement in which everything that exists already participates. Nobody in this passage is described as having been defeated by an argument; the failure named is a departure from a position taken up with the parties' own agreement and God's testimony.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:83

> Do they desire a way other than Allah's—knowing that all those in the heavens and the earth submit to His Will, willingly or unwillingly, and to Him they will ˹all˺ be returned

**A Question About Desire**

The verse asks whether the people just described are looking for a way other than God's, and it phrases the question in terms of desire. What is at issue is not only what they do but what they want — and the Book's habit of naming desire as the engine of a doctrinal position is on display. The question also exposes the impossibility of what is wanted: everyone in the heavens and the earth submits to God's will, willingly or unwillingly, and all will be returned to Him. A way other than God's is not an alternative route but a contradiction of the only order there is, and the question is designed to be felt as unanswerable rather than answered.

**Submission Willing and Unwilling**

The phrase distinguishes two ways submission occurs, and the commentators found it difficult and fruitful. Some read the willing submission as the devout obedience of believers and angels, and the unwilling as the condition of those who submit to God's decrees in creation, death, and the outcome of events while refusing His commands. The Book uses the distinction elsewhere: everything in the heavens and the earth prostrates to God, willingly or unwillingly, as do their shadows morning and evening (13:15); and when God addresses the heaven and the earth, calling them to submit, they answer together that they submit willingly (41:11). This sūrah states the general case; other verses give the instances — the prostrating sun, moon, mountains, and trees belong to the same theme.

**Power, Not Piety**

The verse's argument is not that everyone obeys God's commands, which would be false. Its claim is that nobody escapes God's will — a statement about power, not about piety. The two adverbs exist to mark exactly the difference between being subject to a power and agreeing with it. The Book elsewhere describes people whose faith appears at the moment of punishment, when it can no longer help them, and states that belief at that point profits no one; submitting much earlier, while it still counted, is what distinguishes the two kinds of submission. The universe is not divided between those under God's rule and those outside it; it is divided between those who accept His rule and those governed by it against their preference.

**What the Argument Does Here**

The verse stands between the covenant and the passage about Abraham's inheritance, and its placement keeps the controversy within one frame. The disputants had been arguing over which community held the correct position, and the verse widens the frame when the discussion has turned factional: an argument about who has the correct revelation is set inside a statement about the submission of everything that exists, and the parties' claims look small. The next verse returns to the specifics and states the content of the faith the believers are to profess — in a form that includes the prophets both sides honoured.

**The Return at the End**

The sentence closes with the return to God, a favourite ending of the Book in passages about people confident of their standing. Its force here: a person looking for a way other than God's will find, at the end of his road, the One whose way he was avoiding. The verse describes no punishment and pronounces no verdict; it states a fact about the destination of all things and leaves the opening question lying against it — what does a seeker of an alternative way imagine he is seeking, given that everything returns to the same Lord?

**One Lord, Two Postures**

The commentators drew out the passage's further dimension: to submit willingly is the believer's condition; to submit unwillingly is the condition of the rest of creation — but both share the same Lord, and the Book says so in one sentence so that no claim of independence can obscure the difference. What distinguishes the two is not the fact of the relationship but the posture of the person within it. The refusal of a decree does not prevent the decree. What readers are offered is not the world as it should be but the world as it already is — the field inside which their own decisions take place and by which their weight is measured.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:84

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "We believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and his descendants; and what was given to Moses, Jesus, and other prophets from their Lord—we make no distinction between any of them, and to Him we ˹fully˺ submit."

**A Confession That Names Its Contents**

The verse instructs the Prophet to state what the community believes, and the statement begins with God and lists the revelations given to named prophets — from Abraham through his descendants to Moses and Jesus and the other prophets — ending with the declaration that no distinction is made among them, and a statement of submission.

The Book is giving its community a creed in the form of a list. The contents are not abstract propositions; they are the messengers and books the sūrah's disputes had been about, named one by one so the community's position on each is public. The confession answers the preceding arguments by including everything the other parties claimed — and refusing to rank it.

**Abraham's Sons in One Sentence**

The list runs Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the descendants. In a sūrah that has been arguing about who has the better claim to Abraham, the inclusion of Ishmael alongside Isaac settles the ancestral dispute in a stroke: the two sons whose descendants became separate peoples are named as recipients of revelation in one clause, with no hierarchy between them. The community confessing this creed is not choosing one branch of Abraham's house against another; it receives what was given to them all, and the confession's shape makes the choice unnecessary.

**Moses, Jesus, and the Rest**

The second half names Moses and Jesus and then generalises: and the other prophets from their Lord. The two names carry the two books the sūrah has discussed at length; the general phrase covers the remaining messengers without an inventory. The construction — nearly identical in the sūrah of the Cow — shows how the Book handles revelation's history in a creed: it names the figures its audience knows and places the whole history under one source. What is recited is not a general principle about prophets; the names are those of the particular messengers whose communities stood before the first hearers.

**"We Make No Distinction Between Any of Them"**

This clause is the creed's centre and the Book's standard formula for the relation between the missions. The distinction it forbids is not the recognition of differences between the laws given to different peoples, which the Book acknowledges elsewhere; it is the acceptance of some messengers and the rejection of others. The same formula appears in the Cow's parallel verse (2:136) and in the believers' declaration that they believe in God, His angels, His books, and His messengers without distinction (2:285). The Book states the reverse case with equal clarity: those who believe in some messengers and disbelieve in others — wishing to take a middle way — are described in the sūrah of Women, which declares that those who accept all the messengers and reject none will have their reward (4:150–152). The clause is the line between a faith that takes the whole message and one that takes what suits it.

**Submission as the Closing Clause**

The sentence ends not with the prophets but with the speakers: to Him we submit. The confession closes on the same word the sūrah has used repeatedly for the response God requires, placing the community in the same category as the messengers it has just named. That arrangement keeps the creed from becoming a list of honoured names: the prophets are named, and those who name them describe themselves with the word describing a prophet's own stance before God — which is what prevents the veneration of persons the earlier verses warned against.

**The Creed as an Answer**

The confession arrives after the covenant with the prophets, the turning back from it, and the universal submission — and each theme appears in it: the prophets whose covenant it honours, the refusal of partial acceptance the covenant bound them to, and the submission described as universal. The Book's practice of placing a creed after an argument has a use the reader can feel: a controversy leaves a community holding positions; a creed leaves it holding a statement it can recite — and this one is built so that reciting it is itself the answer to the disputes that prompted it.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:85

> Whoever seeks a way other than Islam, it will never be accepted from them, and in the Hereafter they will be among the losers

**A Statement About the Accepted Way**

The verse declares that whoever seeks a way other than *islām* will not have it accepted from him. The word is the same term the sūrah has used since early in its argument, and its meaning in the Book is submission to God rather than membership in a community. The sūrah itself supplies the evidence: the word's participle is applied to Abraham and Jacob counselling their children (2:132), to the disciples answering Jesus (3:52), and to the community receiving this revelation (3:19). The verse states the acceptance of a stance, not the registration of a name.

**The Wider Sense and the Narrower**

The commentators differed about the verse's scope, and both positions are worth setting out. One reading holds that after the coming of the Prophet of this sūrah no religion other than the one he brought will be accepted — the reading applied to other verses that make the same point. The wider reading, emphasised by many classical authorities, takes the verse's word to mean submission to God, the state of those who conformed to His will in every community that received a messenger. Both readings rest on the same sentence, and the second has the support of the Book's own usage. What the sūrah does not do is restrict the sentence to a community's name at the expense of everything it has said about Abraham, Ishmael, and the disciples — all described with the very word this verse uses. The reader is best served by holding the readings together: the way God accepts has always been submission, and it has been given its most complete and final statement in this revelation.

**The Word "Seeks" and Its Implication**

The verb describes a deliberate search — not an accident of birth or an inherited position. The verse concerns a person who has the option of the way God accepts and goes looking for a substitute. That is a narrow class, and the narrowness is the point. The sūrah's arguments have been directed at people who had the message in front of them, argued about God's messengers, and turned from a covenant they had accepted. Reading the verse as a verdict on the world's population requires the verb to mean something its sense does not carry, and the surrounding verses continue to address the communities before the Prophet as parties who may still respond.

**Where "In the Hereafter" Sits**

The verse ends by placing the outcome — being among the losers — in the Hereafter. The Book puts this theme at the end of several arguments with the People of the Book, because the dispute concerned what would be accepted from them at the end. Nothing in the sentence tells the community to enforce the criterion, and nothing in it describes a punishment in this life: it speaks of the accounting the whole sūrah has been describing since its opening verses.

**A Criterion Held Carefully**

The verse answers the confidence of communities that took their standing for granted: what will be accepted is not their standing but their submission. Readers through the centuries have applied the sentence in the opposite direction — a measure of how easily a criterion becomes a possession. The sūrah's own use of it warns anyone who treats the accepted way as a badge rather than a practice.

**How the Verse Stands With Its Neighbours**

The verse sits between a creed naming every prophet without distinction and a passage about people who believed and then turned back, and its position states the relation between the two: belief in the whole line of messengers is the content of the submission; abandoning that belief is the seeking of another way. The following verses pursue the second case and end with a provision for repentance, so the reader sees one argument rather than disconnected verdicts. The sūrah states the standard here; the next lines describe what happens to those who met it and left.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:86

> How will Allah guide a people who chose to disbelieve after they had believed, acknowledged the Messenger to be true, and received clear proofs? For Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people

**A Question About Guidance**

The verse asks how God could guide a people who chose disbelief after believing, acknowledged the messenger as truthful, and received clear proofs. The question is rhetorical in form and precise in content: it names three things the people had, and their presence is what makes the question unanswerable. Guidance is not described as withheld arbitrarily; it is withdrawn from a course abandoned in the presence of evidence. The three clauses move from initial faith, through the acknowledgement of the message's truth, to the proofs received — each step raising responsibility, and the question arriving after the third.

**What the Three Clauses Establish**

The first places the starting point: they had believed. The second reports an acknowledgement that the messenger was true — a conscious act, the commentators noted, rather than a verbal formula. The third names the clear proofs, the Book's word for the signs and evidences that accompany a message. Together they describe a situation in which nothing was lacking except the will to continue. A person who has believed, admitted the messenger's truth, and seen the proofs has no unanswered question left; the refusal is a decision made with every means of verification already held. The Book's accusations against the earlier communities follow this pattern: never a shortage of information.

**Who Is Being Described**

The commentators offered two identifications. Some took them as apostates who left the community after accepting the message, connecting the verse with a report about a group that departed for Makkah and later returned. Others took the passage as continuing the address to the People of the Book, who had received revelations and then refused the message confirming them. Both readings share the elements named: people who had believed — the People of the Book had believed in the revelation they received — and whose refusal arrived after knowledge. What matters for the reader is that the verse excludes the sympathetic explanation of ignorance and leaves the refusal standing as a choice.

**Why Guidance Is Tied to Conduct**

The closing statement — God does not guide a wrongdoing people — is the Book's standard way of expressing the connection between conduct and access to guidance. The connection runs in both directions: the sūrah itself stated the negative case, that those who persistently deviated had their hearts caused to deviate, as the Book says of the people of Moses (61:5). The Book's language of guidance describes a process, and its warnings are addressed to people who can still turn. What this verse states is what happens when the turning is unrepented: the guidance is not offered again in the same way, and the people described have used the means of knowledge to settle their decision.

**Why They Are Called Wrongdoers**

The description is chosen, not generic: the word is not a synonym for disbeliever as such, but for those whose acts wrong the truth they received. A person who accepted an obligation and set it aside has not merely failed to reach a conclusion; he has acted against something owed. The passage will go on to cursing and lasting punishment, and the reader who has followed the sequence is meant to see the severity as proportionate to a wrong committed with knowledge and in the presence of proof.

**The Door the Next Verses Leave Open**

The verses that follow state the punishment, and then provide for those who repent and mend their ways, with a declaration of forgiveness and mercy — a single sentence after the severity, the Book's usual arrangement. That arrangement keeps this verse from being a verdict on any living person: the descriptions of what happens to those who turn away are written to be read by those who have not yet turned away, and the sūrah will show the door open for anyone who returns. The severity and the offer belong to the same paragraph, and the reader who takes one without the other has misread the passage.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:87

> Their reward is that they will be condemned by Allah, the angels, and all of humanity

**A Reward That Is a Curse**

The verse states the compensation for the conduct just described, and it uses the vocabulary of cursing: the condemned are cursed by God, the angels, and all of humanity. The word for "reward" is the ordinary term for the return a person receives for his work — a choice that is ironic and precise. What is described is not an arbitrary infliction but the settled outcome of a course of conduct, and the Book insists on describing it as earned. The same word carries the believers' reward throughout the sūrah, and the parallel is deliberate: both parties receive what their conduct produced; the difference lies in what was done, not in how the account was kept.

**What the Curse Means**

The Book's language of cursing describes being driven away and removed from mercy, not the utterance of an insult. The commentators explained it as distance from God and exclusion from His mercy. The same triple formula — God, the angels, and humanity together — appears in the sūrah of the Cow for the state of those who disbelieve and die in that state (2:161), and its repetition across sūrahs shows the Book treating it as a fixed description of a final condition rather than an expression of anger that varies with circumstance.

**Why Three Parties Are Named**

Naming God, the angels, and humanity gives the sentence its scope. The condemnation is not one community's reaction to another's offence; it comes from the order of things — and the three parties mirror the hierarchy the sūrah used for the witnesses to God's unity: God, the angels, and those given knowledge. The verse turns that structure around. What is described is the whole of creation withdrawing its regard from a person whose conduct has placed him outside the mercy everything else participates in — and the universality is the point of the list.

**"All of Humanity"**

The phrase needs care, since it cannot mean every human being individually pronounces a malediction. The commentators explained it by what human beings will recognise on the Day of Judgment: a verdict everyone present concurs in, because the truth about what happened is then available to all and no interest distorts it. The Book's account of the final gathering repeatedly presents unanimity about facts where unanimity about conduct had been impossible in life.

**The Curse Spoken Aloud**

The Book supplies the scene this sentence names. Communities entering the Fire are said to curse their sister communities as each arrives (7:38), and those who followed false leaders are told they will disown and curse one another on the Day of Resurrection (29:25). What the passages show is the shape of the severed relation: allies blaming one another, followers cursing the leaders they obeyed. The verse names the outcome without the scene, and the scene shows what a condition entered in secret in this life becomes in the next — public speech.

**Placement and Purpose**

The passage opened by asking how a people who had believed, acknowledged the messenger, and seen the proofs could be guided after turning away. This verse states the consequence, and the next states its permanence. The cursing comes before the punishment, which reverses chronology deliberately: the reader meets the severance of every relation first, and then what that severance looks like in the Hereafter — the sūrah's habit of describing the inner reality of a condition before its outward form. The formula is stated in absolute terms at the close of a discussion about a community that possessed revelation and left it, and the reader who recites the verse is included in the process by which the judgment becomes known. The severity is part of what makes the conduct unlikely — and the sūrah does not end here: after the statement of permanence comes a provision for those who return.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:88

> They will be in Hell forever. Their punishment will not be lightened, nor will they be delayed ˹from it˺

**The Condition Described as Lasting**

The verse states two things about the people whose course the passage has traced: their condition will last, and the punishment will neither be eased nor postponed. The two clauses cover duration and intensity, and together they remove the two hopes a person naturally entertains about a penalty. The description is unusually bare — nothing about the nature of the punishment, nothing about a change in the arrangement. What the reader gets is a sentence about what will *not* happen: no lightening, no delay. The negative form is the Book's way of stating finality without describing machinery.

**"Will Not Be Lightened"**

The clause rules out the hope of a sentence that gradually becomes bearable. Some traditions describe degrees within punishment and intervals in its intensity, and the Book itself speaks of a Day whose length varies and of punishments in a range of images — but the clause here concerns people whose history included faith and knowledge, and its function is to close off the expectation of relief. A person who expects a severe penalty to soften eventually has a reason to postpone repentance; the Book removes that reason for the case it is describing. What the reader is told is not how the punishment will feel but that its terms will not be renegotiated.

**"Nor Will They Be Delayed"**

The second negation concerns time, and it removes the other common expectation: accepting that a penalty awaits while hoping it can be put off — by intercession, excuse, or interval. The Book denies this consistently across its descriptions of the Day: no soul can avail another, no ransom is accepted, and intercessors are not heard in the cases it describes. The effect is to make the accounting, in a certain sense, instantaneous — the person arrives and the matter is settled, with no gap between arrival and consequence.

**Why the Verse Is Placed Here**

The Book's pattern in treatments of final rejection is to state the settled condition before offering the way out, and the sūrah follows it: the permanence first, the provision for repentance after. The order makes the offer of return the reader's destination rather than an interruption. Reading through the passage, a person meets the sentence about what cannot be lightened and then the verse beginning with those who change course and mend what they spoiled — two moments in a life, not a contradiction. The sūrah has stated the truth about both outcomes and left the reader facing the choice; this verse is the last statement of the first outcome before the door is described.

**A Statement That Stops Rather Than Argues**

The verse contains no argument and no appeal. It does not urge the reader to avoid the condition or explain why the condition is just. It states what will be and stops — the Book's form where the case has already been made at length: a covenant accepted before witnesses, a people who turned away after believing and after acknowledging the messenger, and the guidance that could no longer reach them as before. What remains is the outcome, and the outcome is given without commentary. Nothing in the sūrah's treatment suggests the door remains open after death; what the two negations suppress is any hope *within* the framework described — and what they concentrate is attention on the one variable that remains: the conduct of a person still alive. The following verses describe what happens to those who return.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:89

> As for those who repent afterwards and mend their ways, then surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**The Exception That Interrupts the Passage**

The verse opens with a single word that turns the preceding sentences around. After the description of an unrelieved condition and a punishment neither lightened nor delayed, the Book admits an exception, and the exception covers those who repent and make amends. The interruption is deliberately abrupt: the reader has just been told what cannot be changed, and the next sentence begins with the word of exclusion — so the severity of the previous verses is measured against the door that opens here. The Book states the rule for those who persist and then the provision for those who turn, and neither cancels the other.

**Repentance and Making Amends**

Two conditions are named, and the second is what distinguishes this verse from a mere expression of regret. Repentance describes the return; making amends describes the repair of what the return left behind — in belief, in conduct, or in relations the person had cut. The Book pairs the two terms wherever it describes the acceptance of those who had gone far astray, and it never omits the second element: a person who returns owes something for the damage done while he was away, and the repair is part of the return, not an optional extra. The verse does not specify what the amendment consists of — fitting for a sentence that must cover different kinds of harm — it names the two things that must be present and stops.

**The Two Names at the End**

The verse closes with Forgiving and Merciful, the pairing that recurs at the end of sentences about the acceptance of the repentant. Forgiveness describes the covering and removal of the offence; mercy describes the kindness that accompanies it. The order matters: forgiveness is stated first, so the reader does not conclude that the return is accepted grudgingly or depends on the person's own adequacy. The pair is attached here to exactly the offences the previous verses detailed — the offer applies to the people whose conduct has been described at length.

**The Report the Commentaries Attached**

The classical commentaries connect the verse with a Companion who had left the community and then wrote to his family asking whether a return was still possible, and report that the verse was revealed in answer — an account whose chains of transmission the sūrah's editors note they have not examined. Whatever its status, the report describes the situation the verse addresses: a person who knows he has done something serious and wants to know whether the door is shut. The Book's answer does not ask how far he went or weigh the seriousness of the departure; it names the two conditions and the two divine names and leaves the matter there.

**Why the Exception Comes After, Not Before**

The placement is not careless. Placing the exception earlier would have softened the warning while the reader was still reading it; placing it after lets the warning stand in full weight and then shows the way out. A person told that the condition lasts and its terms will not be revised has reason to consider his situation — and the immediate offer supplies the direction to move in. The passage is not an invitation to speculate about the fate of the community described; it shows that the same door stands open to everyone still in a position to walk through it.

**What the Verse Does Not Do**

The sentence says nothing about how long the repentant must wait, what penalty he owes, or what the community's response should be.
