Those belong to the Book's legal passages and its instructions on reconciliation; this verse establishes the underlying fact they all rest on: the return is accepted, and the amendment is part of it. The vocabulary keeps both movements in one frame — the Book's word for repentance is a turning back, the same root used for the reversal of course in the preceding verses, so the person who left is described as turning, and the turning is measured against the distance travelled. The verses that follow describe the other case: the person who does not return but goes further in the direction he chose.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:90

> Indeed, those who disbelieve after having believed then increase in disbelief, their repentance will never be accepted. It is they who are astray

**A Second Case Stated After the First**

The previous verse admitted the repentance of those who turned back and repaired the damage. This verse names a group whose repentance will not be accepted, and the difference lies in what happens after the departure: these are people who disbelieved after believing and then *increased* in disbelief. The verse's picture is of a movement with a direction — not a single act reconsidered but a course that continues — and it ends by naming them as the ones astray.

**"Then Increase in Disbelief"**

The clause describes intensification rather than repetition, and the commentators explored what the increase consists of. Some explained it as persistence without any return; others as the accumulation of acts that follow the initial departure; others as the move from private refusal to public hostility against the message and its community. The sūrah's own passage makes the third reading natural, since the people described had been arguing, circulating objections, and turning others away. The departure has stopped being one person's business and has become a campaign — the person is not merely in a condition he might leave; he is working to make it permanent, which is why the refusal of his return reads as the description of a settled state rather than the rejection of an enquiry.

**When the Return Is Not Accepted**

The commentators offered a range of explanations. One holds that the sentence refers to repentance at the moment of death, when — as the Book says elsewhere — no return is accepted once the matter has passed beyond choice. Another holds that such a person's return would not be sincere, since he has already known the truth and turned from it deliberately, so the sentence describes what he is rather than what God does with him. A third holds that one who returns and then leaves again has emptied the earlier return of its effect. The readings are not mutually exclusive, and each is grounded in the sentence's grammar. What they agree on: the verse does not describe a person who wants to come back and is refused for no reason; it describes the completion of a determined course, stated in the language of finality.

**Two Applications That Converge**

The verse's context suggests the community of this revelation, but the commentators noted the description also fits the communities of the earlier scriptures, who believed in the message they received and then refused the messenger confirming it. In both cases a person has held a revelation, known its truth, and moved from acceptance to deepening refusal. The warning is addressed in the plural — a description of groups, not individuals — so the community now receiving the sūrah is included among the audiences rather than exempted. A reader on his own is not being told that private doubts have placed him in this category; what is described is a public course of action pursued with knowledge.

**The Ending That Names Them Astray**

The word for astray describes a person who has left a road and can no longer find it — the Book's language for wandering without a route, contrasted with guidance as knowing where to go. Placed at the end, it summarises the whole sequence: believed, left, went further, and now cannot find the road. The verse adds the element of disorientation to the preceding warnings, and the verse after it adds what such a person could still offer and what it would not buy.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:91

> Indeed, if each of those who disbelieve then die as disbelievers was to offer a ransom of enough gold to fill the whole world, it would never be accepted from them. It is they who will suffer a painful punishment, and they will have no helpers

**A Ransom the Size of the World**

The verse describes people who disbelieve and die in that state, and states that if one of them offered as ransom gold enough to fill the whole earth, it would not be accepted. The image is built to be impossible, and its function is to state finality in terms the reader can picture. The Book uses ransom language several times in its descriptions of the Day, and each time it denies the transaction; what this verse adds is the measure of the offer — not a sum beyond most people's reach but a quantity that would fill the world, removing any possibility of imagining an amount that would be enough.

**Why the Ransom Cannot Work**

The refusal is not that the price is too low. The Book's account of the final accounting has no mechanism by which a person's standing before God is transferred or purchased, because the relation that would make a purchase meaningful has already lapsed. The verse's own wording makes the point: these are people who disbelieved and remained in that condition until death — by then the subject of the transaction is gone. The Book places the accounting of a life in the life itself; the descriptions of the Day are accounts of what has been determined, not negotiations. The image of the world's gold does not set a maximum price; it closes the question of whether a price exists.

**Dying in the Condition**

The sentence specifies death in the state of disbelief, and the specification limits its scope. The passage has just accepted those who repent and make amends, and has described those who went further in refusal; this is the third case, the end of the trajectory. The Book attaches its final severities to the condition in which a person dies, not to acts considered in isolation — which is why these verses sit so naturally after the offer of the previous lines. Naming the death fixes the moment when the offer of return is no longer pending, and the moment is not under the reader's control.

**Painful Punishment, No Helpers**

The verse ends with two statements: a painful punishment, and no helpers. The second deepens the isolation the passage has been building since the cursing. The Book's accounts of the Day repeatedly strip away the supports a person counts on: near kindred are named as unable to help, intercession is denied for the cases described, the entreaties of the condemned go unanswered. Here the matter stands in its shortest form. From the refusal of a ransom the sentence moves to the absence of assistance — from what a person owns to whom he knows, the two things people rely on when they imagine their future.

**The Progression Across the Three Verses**

Read together, the last three verses describe three conditions, and their order is the logic of time rather than of rank: the first offers acceptance to those who return; the second states that no return is accepted from those who believed, left, and went further; the third describes those who die in the condition. Each verse mentions something to be offered — repentance, a return, a ransom — and the sequence shows what happens to the offer at each stage. The arrangement is designed for a reader still alive, who meets the last verse while the first still applies to him.

**The Restraint, and the Turn That Follows**

The verse says the punishment is painful and does not elaborate. In a passage arguing with communities that claimed religious standing, the restraint has a purpose: an elaborate scene would invite the reader to think about the mechanics of the penalty, while a single clause about pain and a clause about the absence of help direct attention to the seriousness of the state itself. The sūrah then turns at once to a different subject — the spending of what a person cherishes as the road to righteousness. The two halves of the sūrah's method stand in sequence: a passage about the consequences of refusal ends, and a passage about the practice of the believers begins, so the reader is not left with a warning unaccompanied by a command.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:92

> You will never achieve righteousness until you donate some of what you cherish. And whatever you give is certainly well known to Allah

**A Condition Attached to Righteousness**

The verse states that righteousness will not be attained until a person gives away something he values, and closes with the assurance that whatever is given, God knows it. The two halves answer the two questions a person asks about charity: how much, and who is watching.

The Book's word here is broader than the English suggests — it covers goodness, kindness, loyalty, sincerity, and obedience, and can also designate the reward of all these. Read in the wider sense, the verse says the whole of the good life, and its outcome, is blocked until a particular kind of giving is done, which sets the demand apart from a general recommendation of generosity.

**"Of What You Cherish"**

The qualification gives the command its edge. The Book could have said people must spend from their wealth; it says they must spend from what they love, which excludes disposing of what one is glad to be rid of. The same qualification appears in the sūrah of the Cow, where the believers are told to spend from the good things they have earned and warned against giving away the worthless portion; and the sūrah of the Man describes the righteous feeding the needy, the orphan, and the captive despite their own love for the food. The three passages are one instruction, and this verse states its condition in the most concentrated form: the test is the value of what leaves the hand.

**The Report of a Prized Garden**

The commentaries connect the verse with a Companion of Madinah who owned a tract of land he valued above all his property and who wanted to give it in the way of God — a report whose chains of transmission the sūrah's editors note they have not examined. It is a single instance of what the verse requires, and the commentators were careful not to restrict the verse to that instance: the grammar is general, and the first community is described as carrying the practice out too often for one story to exhaust it.

**No Fixed Measure**

The sentence sets no minimum, percentage, or category of property. It says only that the gift must be drawn from what the giver loves, which makes the measure personal rather than fixed. The Book's treatments of obligatory and voluntary giving do contain fixed amounts, and this verse is not one of them; its function is to state the principle the fixed obligations apply to. That is also why the requirement is tied to the attainment of righteousness rather than the avoidance of a penalty: the verse's language describes a journey whose destination is the practice itself.

**"Whatever You Give, God Knows It"**

The second sentence is the assurance, and it does two things: it tells the giver nothing is lost, and it removes the possibility of giving for appearances. The Book's instructions on charity carry this reminder repeatedly, set out at length in passages comparing the person who gives to be seen with the one who gives quietly. Here it is shortest: God knows. The phrase covers the small gift no one notices and the large gift the giver is attached to — and the motive, which no human observer can see. For a demand as exacting as the first sentence, the assurance keeps it from being a burden without a destination.

**The Ḥadīth With the Same Logic**

A widely transmitted report states that none of you truly believes until he loves for his brother what he loves for himself; it is recorded in the two Ṣaḥīḥ collections and graded authentic. Its logic is the verse's: both sentences locate the measure of a person's commitment inside his own attachment — the verse speaks of the property a person loves, the ḥadīth of the good he wants for himself. In each case the standard is what a person already values, not a quantity imposed from outside.

**Where the Verse Stands**

The verse follows a passage about communities that received a revelation and left it, and precedes a passage about the religious prohibitions the Israelites took on. The two surroundings are disputatious and historical; the verse in the middle is neither. Its concerns are entirely practical, and its position suggests what the sūrah means to leave with the reader after the disputes are stated: when the arguments about lineage, prohibitions, and revelations have been laid out, what remains is the conduct of the person reading — and the verse offers the one measurement that applies to all of it.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:93

> All food was lawful for the children of Israel, except what Israel made unlawful for himself before the Torah was revealed. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "Bring the Torah and read it, if your claims are true."

**A Statement About Food and a Challenge**

The verse states that all food was lawful for the children of Israel except what Israel forbade himself before the Torah was revealed, and then instructs the Prophet to call on his opponents to bring the Torah and recite it if their claims are true. Both halves answer a specific argument: the claim that the dietary restrictions of the Jewish law go back to Abraham. The verse replies by pointing at the history of the restrictions — and at the document the claimants themselves accept.

**Who Israel Was**

Israel is Jacob, son of Isaac — the third of the figures named in the sūrah's creed a few verses earlier. Calling him Israel rather than Jacob is deliberate: the people addressed are the tribes descended from him, and the name is the one their own tradition uses for their ancestor.

The claim about him is historical, and its importance lies in what it excludes. If the food laws the Israelites observed went back to a divine legislation given to Jacob, they would be part of revealed law. The verse says instead that he forbade certain foods *for himself*: the restriction originated with a person, and it predates the book the claimants treat as the source of their practice.

**The Narratives About the Forbidden Foods**

The commentators collected several explanations of what Jacob imposed on himself and why: an illness from which he sought relief, vowing to give up certain foods if he recovered; physicians advising him to avoid certain foods, which he then formally renounced; or an act of asceticism with no external cause. The narratives differ in their details, and the verse requires none of them. What all the accounts agree on — and what the argument depends on — is that the restriction was his own undertaking, made before the Torah; his descendants' practice inherited it, and the Torah later recorded those prohibitions among Israel's laws, which is why the practice and the book are consistent even though the practice came first.

**The Challenge to Bring the Torah**

The instruction to bring the Torah and recite it demands evidence from the opponents' own book — the Book's method wherever a dispute concerns a document both parties can examine. The structure is worth following: the Prophet is told not to appeal to his own revelation but to invite the other side to produce theirs, in the confidence that the text will support him. The Book repeats the technique in other passages — asking the People of the Book about what they have been taught, and telling the Prophet to demand proof for the claim that God has forbidden particular things, if they are truthful. The challenge is a test of honesty rather than of memory.

**One Argument, Three Verses**

Several commentators read this verse with the two that follow as a single unit answering the claim that Abraham was subject to the food laws: the laws did not exist in Abraham's time, and what existed was a restriction Jacob imposed on himself, which the Torah later recorded. The sūrah has already made the same point in another key, asking why the People of the Book argued about Abraham since the Torah and the Gospel were revealed only after him. The two passages use different evidence and reach the same conclusion — a fact about chronology, a document both parties accept, and a conclusion about who the ancestors of the faith actually were.

**Why the Stakes Are Large**

The dietary question looks narrow; its stakes in the passage are not. If the restrictions of the Jewish law had always been in force, then a religion not carrying them could not be the continuation of Abraham's way, and the claim with which the sūrah opened would be damaged. The verse's answer: legislation given to one people after their ancestor's time is not the measure of a faith that looks back to that ancestor. The Book's treatment elsewhere is consistent — it states that the restrictions were imposed on the Israelites as a consequence of their transgressions and came with the Torah rather than before it. What remains of Abraham's legacy is the orientation toward God that the next verse names.

**The Support Inside Their Own Tradition**

The commentators noticed a further support in the description of Jacob's injury in Genesis, where the Israelites are said to abstain from a particular cut of meat because of his lameness. The detail is incidental to Genesis, and it confirms what the verse claims: practices among the Israelites could originate in Jacob's own life and be remembered without being part of a revealed dietary code. The sūrah does not ask its opponents to abandon their tradition; it asks them to read it — and the reading does the work of the argument.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:94

> Then whoever still fabricates lies about Allah, they will be the ˹true˺ wrongdoers

**A Verdict on Those Who Invent**

The verse states that whoever fabricates a lie against God *after that* will be among the wrongdoers. The phrase ties the sentence to the challenge just made — the point after which the truth about the dietary laws has been made clear. The Book's phrase for the conduct is making something up and ascribing it to God, the most serious form of false speech in its vocabulary: not the inaccuracy of an interpretation but the invention of a divine command.

**The Lie in View**

The lie is specific. The previous verse answered the argument that Abraham had been subject to the food laws, with the sequence of the Torah's revelation as evidence. A person who continues asserting the earlier position after the timeline has been set out is claiming, in effect, that God legislated what He did not. The Book treats the ascription of a law to God as a claim on the highest authority available, and reserves its severest descriptions for those who make that claim on their own initiative.

**The Evidence Still in Their Hands**

The demonstration was not a private revelation the opponents had to take on trust: the Prophet was told to ask them to produce the Torah itself, a book they possessed and recited, and the argument rested on a document both parties could inspect. The verse describes the condition of a person holding such a document and continuing to state the opposite of what it shows. The sūrah will later describe a group among them twisting the scripture with their tongues so hearers take it for the Book itself (3:78) — the same conduct in a form that reaches the ear rather than the page. Their own scriptures are, throughout the Book's argument, the source of the case against them.

**Why the Corruption of Knowledge Ranks So High**

The description — wrongdoers — is the Book's general verdict on those who violate what is due, and its use here is instructive because the conduct is a corruption of knowledge rather than violence or theft. The Book places the corruption of religious knowledge among the gravest wrongs, and the reason is visible in its own logic: violence damages bodies, but a false attribution to God damages the way a community relates to its Lord for generations.

**Fabrication Elsewhere in the Book**

The same accusation appears in consistent settings: the curse on those who write a book with their own hands and then say it is from God (2:79), and the naming as worst of wrongdoers those who invent a lie about God or deny His verses after they reach them. The set of passages agrees on what is at stake. When a person claims a text or ruling comes from God, he has made a claim only God can make true — an offence against the source of the message, not only against the hearers. The sūrah's warnings about the covenant and the turning away from it belong to the same family: the message received, understood, and then misrepresented.

**"After That": Why the Timing Matters**

The phrase is not filler. It marks the point after which the truth has been made plain, and the Book consistently treats responsibility as growing with the clarity a person has received. The same principle appears in its statements about altering God's favour after it has come, about concealing what was revealed after it was made clear, and about those who turn back after guidance has been explained — given a respite rather than an excuse. This verse is the shortest of the set, and its structure is the same: the act is named, the prior clarity is stated, and the outcome follows in one clause. The offence is aggravated by knowledge, not excused by it.

**Where the Argument Turns**

The verse closes the short exchange about the food laws. What follows turns from argument to instruction: the next sentence summons the community to an allegiance rather than to a proof. The movement is characteristic — establish the facts about revelation's history, state what went wrong with those who held the earlier scriptures, then give the community its response. The transition is where the reader stops examining the opponents' position and starts examining his own.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:95

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "Allah has declared the truth. So follow the Way of Abraham, the upright—who was not a polytheist."

**A Declaration of Truth**

The verse instructs the Prophet to say that God has spoken the truth, and the statement follows the demonstration about the food laws and the verdict on the fabricators. Al-Ṭabarī explained it as confirming, in this place, what had been said about Jacob and about the food made unlawful before the Torah was revealed — the disputed matter of the preceding verses (3:93). The declaration therefore answers an argument rather than introducing a new subject: the Book has just told its opponents to bring the Torah and read it if their claims are true, and this sentence states that the account the sūrah has given is the accurate one.

The phrase also entered the community's practice: Muslims recite it when a passage of the Book has been read outside the formal prayer. A sentence spoken in a specific dispute became a general affirmation of the Book.

**Following the Way of Abraham**

The command that follows is to follow Abraham's way, and the choice is the point: the communities addressed had been claiming him, and the passage has shown that the practices they attributed to him did not belong to his lifetime. What remains of their claim when the attributions are removed is his way — which the verse names and then defines. He is called upright, the word for a person turned away from falsehood toward what is straight, and the description adds that he was not one of those who associated others with God. The positive and negative halves are the Book's standard way of identifying his position, as in the sūrah of the Cow's parallel command to follow the creed of Abraham the upright rather than the claims of the two older communities (2:135).

**Why "Upright" Matters**

The Arabic term describes a natural orientation toward truth, and the commentators understood it as the original disposition on which Abraham was formed. The Book uses it precisely where an ancestral claim is being settled — here, in the Cow's comparable command, and in the verse declaring that Abraham was neither Jew nor Christian but upright and submitting. Its function is to move the discussion from labels back to a stance before God: a community might dispute which of them Abraham belonged to, and the verse answers by naming the thing he actually was.

**A Legacy That Is a Disposition**

The Book describes Abraham with the vocabulary of submission elsewhere: God tells him to submit, and he answers that he has submitted to the Lord of all worlds; he and Jacob each counsel their sons to hold the same stance and not to die except in it. Read beside this verse, the legacy is a disposition rather than a set of rituals — a way of facing God. The sūrah's next move, to the prayer direction and the House of worship, belongs to the same argument about what may legitimately be called Abraham's practice.

**Two Verbs: Declaring and Following**

The sentence contains a statement and a command. In the first, God is described as having spoken the truth; in the second, the listener is told to follow a way. The pairing embodies the Book's treatment of knowledge as something that terminates in movement rather than agreement: a person may concede a proposition and remain where he is, and the construction leaves no room for that. That is also why the verse's content is the *way* of Abraham rather than the *family* of Abraham — a lineage can be claimed without any change in conduct; a way cannot. The command is phrased to the Prophet, and its content becomes the community's obligation by their following him, as with his other commands concerning belief and fidelity. The community reciting the sentence answers a dispute about ancestry with a commitment about conduct.

**The Hinge**

The argument about Abraham's law is now complete: the truth has been stated, the way remaining after the false attributions has been named, and the two community labels set aside. The sūrah then turns to the place where that way was established and the obligation attached to it — in the language of a house and a pilgrimage. This verse is the hinge between the two discussions, and its brevity is what makes the turn visible: the reader leaves a dispute about texts and arrives at a sanctuary and a practice, with Abraham named at both ends of the transition.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:96

> Surely the first House ˹of worship˺ established for humanity is the one at Bakkah—a blessed sanctuary and a guide for ˹all˺ people

**A House for All People**

The verse states that the first house established for humanity was the one at Bakkah, and describes it with two things: blessing and guidance for all peoples. The word for humanity is the widest available, and it carries the verse's meaning: the House was not founded for one tribe or lineage but as the first place of worship set up for the whole human race. That universal framing is why the sūrah can move from a dispute about ancestry to a discussion of pilgrimage — the House belongs to a wider public than the disputants had been assuming.

**Bakkah and Makkah**

Bakkah is the name used here, and the commentators identified it with Makkah, treating the difference as the common alternation between the sounds m and b. Some held that Bakkah designated the ground on which the House stands while Makkah referred to the settlement around it. Others connected the name with a root meaning to crowd, explained by the gathering of pilgrims and the concentration of worshippers. The explanations are not in competition: one is topographical, one etymological, and both attach the name to the feature the verse makes central — the assembly of people who come to it.

**Blessing and Guidance**

Blessing carries, in the commentators' note, the senses of growth and permanence — fitting for a place whose significance has outlasted the empires around it. The guidance is stated as being for all peoples, matching the universality of the opening. In the Book's usage a place does not guide by itself; it guides by directing those who come toward their Lord, and the next verse names the signs standing in it. The two words answer the two needs of a visitor: blessing for his situation in the world, guidance for his relation to God — the same pairing the Book uses for what the prophets brought.

**The First House and the Mosque of Jerusalem**

A well-known report gives the priority a concrete shape. Abū Dharr asked the Prophet which mosque was built first on earth and was told it was the Sacred Mosque; asked which came next, he was told the Aqṣā Mosque; and asked how long separated them, he was told forty years (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3366). The report establishes what the verse claims without requiring the House to be the first building of any kind, and its closing instruction — that prayer is to be performed wherever its time falls due — keeps the honour of the House from becoming a reason to neglect worship elsewhere.

**Before the Building Stood**

The verse speaks of a house being established, and the commentators distinguished the site from the stones. Several reported traditions tracing the Kaʿbah's foundation to the time of Adam — reports the commentaries relate from earlier authorities, and whose chains the sūrah's editors note they have not examined; on that reading the verse's priority refers to the ground plan and the place where worship was first appointed, not the masonry of any particular age. Abraham's role in the Book's account is then to raise foundations that already had a location: he and Ishmael raise the foundation of the House, praying as they work. The distinction keeps the verse's claim about humanity and worship intact, and explains why the sūrah can treat the House as older than the community that now faces it.

**The House in the Sūrah's Debate**

The verse's position is deliberate. The argument before it established that the practices attributed to Abraham did not belong to his lifetime, and that what remains of his legacy is the upright way toward God. The House enters as the visible form of that legacy: a community arguing over who inherited Abraham is shown a building whose foundation the Book attributes to him and whose purpose is the worship of God alone — and the description of it as blessing and guidance for humanity reopens the question of who actually honours what he built. Al-Rāzī's account of the guidance imagines the House drawing people of exalted aspiration, with some portion of humanity facing it in prayer at every hour — whatever a reader makes of the imagery, the verse keeps the House subordinate to its Lord, and the next verse attaches to it an obligation the Book ties to ability rather than rank.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:97

> In it are clear signs and the standing-place of Abraham. Whoever enters it should be safe. Pilgrimage to this House is an obligation by Allah upon whoever is able among the people. And whoever disbelieves, then surely Allah is not in need of ˹any of His˺ creation

**Signs, Sanctuary, Obligation**

The verse names signs in the House, specifies one of them as the standing-place of Abraham, states that whoever enters it is safe, and lays the pilgrimage on those able to make the journey. The order matters: the signs establish the House's standing, the safety describes what it offers those who come, and the obligation follows from both. A reader shown the House's universal purpose in the previous verse now sees what stands in it and what is required because of it.

**What the Signs Are**

The commentators offered several identifications: many took the signs to be the standing-place itself, some connecting it with the footprints said to be preserved there; others read the signs as the evidences that this structure is the one Abraham built and that God has honoured it. The syntax supports the second reading — the standing-place is named *after* the signs rather than identified with them, suggesting the signs are the marks of the House's origin and standing. Both readings point one way: the evidence in the House is evidence about Abraham, answering the previous dispute with something his opponents could see.

**The Sanctuary and Its Limits**

The statement that whoever enters the House is safe was read by some as describing the past, since the precinct around the Kaʿbah served in pre-Islamic Arabia as a refuge no pursuer could violate. The commentators then discussed whether the safety continued, reporting a range of positions: that the sanctuary no longer shields a person from prosecution, though punishment is not carried out within it; a strict regime under which a fugitive may not trade, speak, lodge, or be fed, leaving him no option but to leave and face his case; that the verse commands the community to make the place secure, which people may fail to do; and a further reading, from several authorities, taking the safety as deliverance from the Fire for the one who enters to perform the rites.

**Makkah's Inviolability in the Prophet's Words**

The report of the conquest gives the city's standing in the Prophet's own words. Ibn ʿAbbās related that on the day Makkah was taken, the Prophet said God had made the town a sanctuary: its thorn bushes were not to be cut, its game not chased, and anything dropped in it not to be picked up except by one announcing it publicly (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1587). The wording supplies what the verse's short sentence leaves open: the safety is a status conferred by God, imposing duties on those who live there and visit — not only a privilege for those who need protection. What God has made inviolable obliges the people inside it to treat it as such.

**The Obligation of Pilgrimage**

Pilgrimage to the House is a duty owed to God by those among humanity who can find a way, and the qualification of ability is central. The commentators explained it as covering physical capacity and the means to travel and provide for dependents during the absence. The obligation attaches to the House rather than the surrounding city, and it is the duty of the individual, not of a representative. The Book's other pilgrimage passages specify the months, the rites, and the conduct required, and record the summons Abraham was given to call humanity to the journey; this verse stands at the head of them, stating why the journey exists — a House established for humanity, and an able worshipper who comes to it.

**"A Duty Owed to God"**

The phrasing places the pilgrimage among obligations owed to God, not among people's rights: no human being is wronged by its omission the way a creditor is wronged by an unpaid debt. What is at stake is a person's standing before his Lord. The commentators differed about whether this verse or the passage in the sūrah of the Cow instituted the pilgrimage — a question of the order of revelation, not of the obligation itself.

**The Closing Clause**

The verse ends with God's freedom from need of all creation, so that whoever disbelieves harms nothing but himself. The disbelief in view was taken either as the denial that pilgrimage is obligatory or as general rejection of God and the final accounting. The clause protects the passage from a misunderstanding its rest might invite: the House is blessed and the duty is real, but neither creates a need in God that human beings satisfy by coming. The Book returns to this point whenever it lays down a costly obligation — which is why the sūrah can move from a house on a journey to the people who withhold their testimony about it without altering its tone.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:98

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "O People of the Book! Why do you deny the revelations of Allah, when Allah is a Witness to what you do?"

**A Rebuke Addressed by Name**

The verse instructs the Prophet to address the People of the Book directly and ask why they reject God's revelations. The address is courteous in form and severe in content — the Book's characteristic way with a community that holds a scripture. The command to *say* the words puts them in the messenger's mouth rather than in a narrative about him, so the reader hears the rebuke as speech; the Book uses the formula repeatedly in this sūrah, building the sense of an argument conducted in public with the community listening to both sides.

**What Is Being Denied**

The term used is the Book's ordinary word for God's signs, covering both the verses of revelation and the evidences establishing them — and here the two senses converge, since the revelation being rejected claimed confirmation from what the People of the Book already possessed. The commentators understood the denial as directed at the messenger and his message, which the Book treats as denial of the sender. That is why the question is a rebuke rather than a request for information: the answer is known to the ones being asked. The Book states elsewhere that they recognise the messenger as they recognise their own children, while a group among them hides what they know (2:146).

**The Witness Clause**

The sentence does not stop at the question; it adds that God is a witness to what they do — and the clause gives the verse its force. A person may deny something and be unknown to everyone but himself; the verse states that the record is being kept. The Book closes its rebukes with statements of God's knowledge so often that the pattern is a feature of its style. The effect is to remove the two consolations available to a person conducting such an argument: that the denial is private, and that its motive is hidden. The matter is not between them and the messenger; the account of their conduct sits with the One whose revelations they reject.

**Recognition and Concealment**

The sūrah has been developing a distinction between not knowing and not acknowledging, and this verse depends on it. A community that received the earlier scriptures was in a position to test the claim made to it, and the argument throughout assumes the materials were in its hands. That is why the harshest descriptions are reserved for concealment: the group that mixes truth with falsehood and hides the truth knowingly (3:71), and those who hide the clear proofs after they have been made clear for humanity, whom the Book condemns — along with all who condemn (2:159). This verse is the shortest form of the same charge, and it leaves the accused a question he cannot answer without either admitting the recognition or denying the record.

**Why the Book Asks Rather Than Declares**

The question form recurs throughout the sūrah's dealings with the People of the Book — several verses ask the same community *why* they do or deny something, with the formula held nearly constant and the subject changing. A declaration would tell the reader the verdict; a question makes him supply it, in language he cannot dispute without denying what his own knowledge tells him. The method is that of a trial in which the accused is invited to speak, and the invitation is extended until the silence becomes the answer. The passage has turned from the argument about Abraham and the House to this series of direct addresses: the evidence is presented, the claims of ancestry answered, the obligations of the House stated — what remains is the response of the people who had been arguing, and the Book asks them about it to their faces.

**A Warning With Two Audiences**

The verse names a community of the Prophet's own time, and its abiding lesson concerns a type of conduct rather than a group of persons: a community possessing a revelation is asked why it denies a further revelation its own book anticipated. The question is addressed to the heirs of a message as much as to the first hearers — the sūrah has already warned its own community about turning back after receiving proof, and the same warning runs beneath this rebuke. What the Book asks is not whether the recipients are learned but whether they are truthful with what they hold. The next verse presses the interrogation further, asking why they turn believers away from God's path and seek to make it crooked — the description the Book uses elsewhere of those who obstruct the way and desire it crooked (7:45; 11:19; 14:3) — while they are witnesses.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:99

> Say, "O People of the Book! Why do you turn the believers away from the Way of Allah—striving to make it ˹appear˺ crooked, while you are witnesses ˹to its truth˺? And Allah is never unaware of what you do."

**From Denial to Obstruction**

The previous verse asked the People of the Book about their denial of God's revelations. This verse asks about something else: what they do to the people who believe. The charge names an act, a purpose, and a state of knowledge — they turn believers away from God's way, work to make that way look crooked, and are themselves witnesses to its truth. What is described is not a private conviction but a campaign directed at the believing community.

**Turning Away: Distortion, Not Force**

The commentators discussed whether the turning is physical or figurative, and their explanations favour the second. The Book does not accuse these people of standing in the road; it accuses them of misdirection — presenting the way as something other than it is, so that those who hear them begin to doubt. The act is one of distortion, and its target is the believers' confidence. The commentators added that when these people were asked directly, they denied that the messenger was foretold in their books — the very denial the previous verse addressed. The two verses thus describe one behaviour from two sides: withholding testimony, and working to unsettle those who accepted it.

**Making the Way Appear Crooked**

The phrase recurs in the Book in a set of verses with a striking profile: in each, the people described hinder others from God's path, seek to make it appear crooked, and disbelieve in the Hereafter (7:45; 11:19; 14:3). One passage places them among those who prefer this life over the next and have gone far astray; another groups them with those who bar the way and deny the final accounting. The repetition shows the Book treating the obstruction of others as a settled pattern rather than an isolated mistake — and the sūrah's version is the more serious, because the accused here know the truth of what they are bending.

**"While You Are Witnesses"**

The witnessing clause is the part the commentators explained most often. Some took it as a reference to the messenger's presence among them — a community living in the same city as the Prophet could observe him. Others took it as a reference to their own scriptures, which contained what the messenger taught, so their witness came from the books they recited. Both readings converge on one point: the knowledge was not hidden from them. The sūrah has used the language of witnessing in its account of the covenant, and its sharpest edge here is the combination of the two facts placed side by side — they know, and they work against what they know.

**A Wrong Done to Others**

The verse's placement after the passage on the House and the pilgrimage gives the accusation a wider significance than a scholarly dispute. The believers are being kept from a way — the same way the sūrah has traced from Abraham through the prophets. That is why the Book treats misleading others as more serious than private refusal: a person who rejects a message damages himself; a person who draws others away damages them as well, and the Book's vocabulary places the offence among the wrongs that corrupt a community. The sūrah has already warned its own community about turning back; this verse shows the mechanism by which such a turning is produced from outside.

**The Closing Clause and the Passage's End**

The sentence ends: God is never unaware of what they do. The wording differs slightly from the previous verse's witness clause, and the difference matters — the witness establishes the record; the absence of unawareness guarantees the record will be used. The two clauses work as a pair across the two verses, the second placed where the harm has spread to others. The statements about God's knowledge that close such passages are not decoration; they are the answer to the calculation a person makes when he believes the consequences of his conduct will not reach him.

The verses that follow turn from the People of the Book to the believers, and the change of address marks the close of this stage of the argument. The result is a warning running in both directions: the earlier community is told its knowledge increases its responsibility, and the believers are told in the next verse what would happen if they followed the others' guidance. The passage ends not with a verdict on the opponents but with an instruction to the believers — the sūrah's consistent practice after stating a case.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:100

> O believers! If you were to yield to a group of those who were given the Scripture, they would turn you back from belief to disbelief

**The Address Turns to the Believers**

The three preceding verses asked the People of the Book why they denied the revelations, turned the believers from the path, and what they intended by it. This verse turns to the believers themselves and warns them about the consequences of listening. The change of address is the sūrah's method at the close of an argument: once a party's conduct has been described, the Book instructs its own community on how to respond. The instruction here concerns obedience rather than belief — not how to refute an argument, but what follows from complying with the guidance of a particular group.

**"A Group" — the Refusal to Generalise**

The word for those in view means a section or party, not an entire people. The choice matches the sūrah's care elsewhere in this passage: it states that the People of the Book are not all alike, and that some among them are upright, reciting God's revelations through the night while prostrating (3:113); that some among them are faithful while most are rebellious (3:110); and, near its end, it praises those who believe in what was revealed to the believers and to them, and do not trade God's revelations for a small price (3:199). A warning directed at a group is consistent with the sūrah's own distinctions — the reader is protected from turning a judgment about conduct into a verdict about a people.

**The Incident the Commentaries Record**

The commentators connect this part of the sūrah with an occasion in Madinah: a man from among the Jews of the city came upon a gathering of Muslims from the tribes of Aws and Khazraj — enemies before Islam — and began recalling their old wars and insults, until the gathering grew heated and the former enemies nearly came to blows. The Prophet came out and asked whether they would return to the practices of the days of ignorance while he was among them, and while God had honoured them with Islam and reconciled their hearts; at that they laid down their weapons, embraced one another, and went with him. The commentaries relate the report without a chain the sūrah's editors have examined, but the incident illustrates exactly the mechanism the verse warns about: a community made brothers can be pulled apart through the memories that once divided it.

**What "Yielding" Produces**

The sentence states a consequence, not an accusation: if the believers follow that group's direction, they will be turned back from belief to disbelief — a process with stages rather than a single decision. The sūrah states the same outcome near its end in blunter terms: if the believers obey those who disbelieve, they will be dragged back and become losers (3:149). The image of dragging matches the sense here — the end of the road is reached by following rather than arguing. A community is not talked out of its faith in one exchange; it is guided away in a series of small compliances, and the verse names the end of the road so the first step can be recognised for what it is.

**Envy as the Plain Motive**

The Book supplies the motive in another passage: many among the People of the Book wish they could turn the believers back to disbelief out of envy, after the truth has become clear to them (2:109). The wording removes the possibility of mistaking the behaviour for honest disagreement. Naming the motive does two things: it warns the believers about the source of the pressure they will feel, and it prevents them from treating the dispute as an intellectual puzzle. The Book's counsel in the verse following that one is to pardon and bear with them until God brings about His decision — a policy for living alongside neighbours whose intentions have been stated, not a licence for retaliation.

**The Remedy and the Balance**

The Book's answer to the danger of being drawn apart comes a few verses later: hold firmly to the rope of God all together, do not be divided, and remember the favour by which you were enemies and became brothers (3:103). The remedy is the opposite of the quarrel-stirrer's work: he revived old enmities; the Book commands the community to remember the reconciliation and guard it. The warning is followed by the question of the next verse — how could you disbelieve while God's revelations are recited to you and His messenger is among you? — and together the two verses locate the community's safety in what it has been given rather than in its own strength. The passage teaches care about whom one follows, without becoming unjust about whom one judges.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:101

> How can you disbelieve when Allah's revelations are recited to you and His Messenger is in your midst? Whoever holds firmly to Allah is surely guided to the Straight Path

**A Question That Names Two Proofs**

The verse asks how disbelief is possible when two things are present: God's revelations being recited, and the Messenger in the community's midst. The question follows a warning to the believers about pressure they would face, and the pairing is the point. A recited text can be examined and argued with; a living messenger can be watched and questioned. Where both are available, the excuse of insufficient evidence has no room left. Al-Rāzī put it plainly: with the recitation and the Prophet present, the people had every proof they needed to hold them back from rejection.

**The Recitation as Evidence**

The Book's challenge to doubters rests on the character of the recitation, not only its content. Three separate passages invite sceptics to produce a comparable composition, and one escalates the demand through stages, ending with the observation that even if humanity and the jinn combined their efforts they could not produce its like (17:88). The invitation to match it is the strongest form of the argument, because it is testable by anyone who knows the language. This verse assumes that force while it is being conducted: the recitation was happening in the community, verse by verse, day by day, and the people addressed were hearing it as it came down.

**The Messenger in Their Midst**

The second proof is a person. The argument does not depend on the messenger's words alone; it points to his presence among the people arguing with him. The community lived alongside the Prophet, saw his conduct, and listened to him outside the moments of revelation — and a substantial body of tradition records how they assessed him, his manner and behaviour being treated by the Companions as part of the evidence for his mission, transmitted onward by the generation that followed. The verse's phrasing makes the point in the simplest way: after all of that, how could anyone turn away?

**Holding Fast to God**

The second sentence states the alternative to doubt: whoever holds firmly to God is guided to a straight path. The Arabic image is of a person gripping something so as not to fall, and the commentators identified the object in two ways: al-Ṭabarī read it as holding fast to God's religion and obedience to Him; al-Qurṭubī read it as holding fast to the Book itself. The two agree in practice, since the Book is where the religion is set out. The same image appears later in this sūrah in the command to hold firmly *together* (3:103), and the recurrence ties individual steadfastness to the community's common ground.

**Two Readings of the Closing Clause**

The last clause can be understood in two ways. On one, the person who holds fast is guided as a consequence of his act: holding leads him to the path. On the other — al-Rāzī set out both — the act of holding fast is itself the guidance, the description of a state rather than a reward following a deed: guidance as something God places in the person who clings to Him, fitting the Book's usage elsewhere, where guidance is described as a gift given to those who respond. On either reading the result is the same: the path is reached by holding on to the One who established it, and the verse closes by naming the destination rather than the effort.

**The Straight Path, Recurring**

The phrase is not new here. It stands at the centre of the prayer that opens the Book, appears at the beginning of this sūrah, and returns near its end in the petition that God not let the heart swerve after He has guided it. Its reappearance after a warning about being drawn from the faith shows what the sūrah means by it: the path is straight, and it can be left — and the risk is not obscure, since the previous verse named the very group that would attempt the drawing away. The verse supplies the remedy in a single clause, and its brevity is deliberate: the alternative to being led off is to hold on, and the One being held is named.

**What the Verse Adds to the Warning**

The warning before it told the believers what would happen if they followed a particular group; this verse answers with a question and a promise. The question removes the excuse of confusion; the promise supplies the alternative course. A community living with the recitation and the messenger is told it does not lack the means to remain — the pattern the sūrah repeats: a danger named, then the ground of confidence restated. And the verse's edge endures for every generation reciting it: the recitation and the prophetic example are available to later readers in transmitted form, and the question retains its force — what reason remains for turning away when the message is being read and the model of its first community is known?
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:102

> O believers! Be mindful of Allah in the way He deserves, and do not die except in ˹a state of full˺ submission ˹to Him˺

**A Command With a Measure Built In**

The verse calls the believers to be mindful of God *as He deserves*, and then tells them not to die except in submission. The first command carries a measure, and the measure is the Arabic word for a right — what is due. Al-Ṭabarī explained the phrase as a mindfulness in which God is obeyed and not defied, remembered and not forgotten, thanked and not denied. The formulation answers the question a person naturally asks when told to give God what He deserves: the answer is not a quantity but a way of living. The same root appears in the Book's rebuke that people have not shown God His proper reverence — a criticism attached to those who denied He had revealed anything to a human being (6:91).

**The Command That Seemed Too Heavy**

The community's first reaction is part of the verse's history. Reports record that the Companions found the command difficult and were troubled by it, since no one could claim to have given God everything He deserves — and some then suggested it was lightened by the verse telling the believers to be mindful of God as far as they are able (64:16). The reasoning is not that the earlier command was cancelled but that the two verses explain each other: the same Book states that God does not require of any soul more than it can bear (2:286), so a command to give God His due cannot mean a demand beyond human capacity. Al-Rāzī drew the conclusion directly: to be mindful of God as He deserves is to be mindful of Him to the extent of one's ability, because the Book has ruled out the impossible.

**The Discussion of Whether the Command Was Eased**

The reports about the community's anxiety connect the verse with a wider discussion of commands that appear to exceed human capacity, and the commentators treated the question carefully: some argued one verse abrogated the other; others replied that commands concerning God's own attributes and the Hereafter are not subject to abrogation, and the two statements are complementary. Al-Rāzī's resolution fits both texts without appeal to cancellation — the due is measured by capacity, because no soul is tasked beyond what it can bear. The anxiety the reports describe is itself instructive: the first generation treated the verse with the seriousness the demand deserves, rather than assuming a general command costs nothing.

**The Two Commands as a Pair**

The second half — do not die except in submission — works with the first. The first concerns the whole of life; the second concerns the moment it ends. The Book uses the same combination in the advice Abraham and Jacob gave their children, where the dying clause appears in almost identical wording after the statement that God chose the faith for them (2:132). In both passages the point is not to arrange a particular death but to live in a way that keeps the ending what it should be. A person has no power over the moment or manner of his death; the command is directed at the part he controls.

**Why Death Is Mentioned**

A command about the state in which one dies is a command about persistence, since nobody can guarantee the end of his life. The sūrah has just warned the community against being drawn back from belief, and the warning gains urgency from being attached to death: between guidance and the end of life lies a distance whose length nobody knows, and the second command fixes the direction to be maintained across however many days it contains. The wording also places the whole matter under God — the One who demands the state of submission is the One who determines when it is sealed.

**A Phrase the Community Lives With**

The sentence about dying in submission became a phrase Muslims repeat and hear in the funeral prayer and at moments of loss. What it asks for is durable rather than dramatic: a mindfulness measured by capacity, sustained over years, and maintained until the end. The sūrah's passage continues with the command to hold together and the reminder of what God did for a divided people — instructions that belong with this one, since a community cannot maintain the state the verse describes while it is coming apart.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:103

> And hold firmly to the rope of Allah and do not be divided. Remember Allah's favour upon you when you were enemies, then He united your hearts, so you—by His grace—became brothers. And you were at the brink of a fiery pit and He saved you from it. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to you, so that you may be ˹rightly˺ guided

**What the Rope of God Means**

The verse commands the believers to hold firmly to the rope of God *together* and not to be divided, then reminds them of what God did for them. The image has been explained in several ways, and the explanations are not far apart. Many commentators preferred the community of believers itself, since the sentence goes on to forbid division; others took the rope to be the Book, and some traced the image to a report describing the Qur'an as a rope stretching from heaven to earth. A further reading, noted by Ibn Kathīr, understands a covenant or pact — the same word appears later in this sūrah for the treaties by which a people may be protected.

**"All Together" Is Part of the Command**

The verse does not say only that the rope must be held; it says the believers must hold it together — one act described from two sides, the second requiring the first. The Book's treatment of unity is not a general commendation of harmony: it is a command attached to a specific object, so that agreement is required *about* something rather than for its own sake, and the object is the revelation and the religion it establishes. That is why the same sūrah can forbid division here and, in its next verses, command a group to call others to what is good: unity in the Book's sense is not the absence of activity but the shared direction of it.

**Remembering What God Did**

The verse turns from command to memory, naming three things: the enmity that existed before, the joining of hearts that followed, and the brink of a pit of fire from which the believers were delivered. The enmity refers to the tribal wars of the Arabs before Islam — particularly the long feud between the two Madinan tribes of Aws and Khazraj, who had fought for years and were separated only by the arrival of the Prophet and the faith he brought. The Book states elsewhere that the joining of hearts was God's work and could not have been bought with all the wealth in the earth (8:63) — which makes the memory a statement about divine action rather than diplomacy.

**The Brink of a Pit of Fire**

The image is drawn from the community's condition before Islam, and the commentators explained the deliverance as guidance that arrived before death — a person on the brink who has not yet fallen is separated from the pit only by the time he has left. The metaphor gives the memory its force: a community drawn back from the edge by the message it now holds has a concrete reason to remain where it was placed. The sūrah has just been warning the believers about being pulled away; the reminder of the rescue is the ground of the warning's force.

**The Book's Standing Command Against Division**

The command not to divide is repeated across the Book with a fixed pattern. It was given to Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus, together with the instruction to establish the faith and make no divisions in it (42:13); another passage states that those given the Scripture did not split into sects until knowledge came to them, out of mutual envy (42:14); and the same order — knowledge first, then division — appears again where the People of the Book are said to have differed only after the clear proof reached them (98:4). The sūrah's own version follows the same logic: the community has received what it received, and the warning is against coming apart in the presence of the truth rather than in ignorance of it.

**Brotherhood as the Fruit of the Favour**

The reminder ends with the community described as brothers, and the Book builds that description into a rule elsewhere: the believers are a single brotherhood, so make peace between your brothers, with mindfulness of God named as the condition for receiving mercy (49:10). The sequence here matches: the joining of hearts comes first, brotherhood is the name of the result, and what the community is asked to do is not create the bond but stop dividing what God joined. Hence the phrasing of the command — the effort required is not the manufacture of unity but the refusal to break it.

**A Verse the Community Reads About Itself**

The verse has served as the community's charter on division and a reminder of what the alternative cost, and its mention of the pit gives the instruction a history rather than an abstraction. The closing sentence — God makes His revelations clear to you so that you may be guided — places the instruction within the Book's general purpose: this is not a rule imposed on people who never saw its value, but a policy stated for a community that had already experienced both conditions and knew which one it was rescued from. The verses that follow set out what the community is to do with the unity it has been given.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:104

> Let there be a group among you who call ˹others˺ to goodness, encourage what is good, and forbid what is evil—it is they who will be successful

**Three Tasks for a Group Within the Community**

The verse asks that a group among the believers call others to what is good, encourage what is right, and forbid what is wrong — and it names such people as the successful. The sentence describes a function rather than an office. Calling to good is the general invitation; encouraging the right and forbidding the wrong are the two particular practices that give the invitation its content in daily life. The Book repeats the pair in several passages — sometimes describing the community as a whole, sometimes as an obligation — and this sūrah uses it again a few lines later of the believers among the People of the Book (3:110).

**The Report That Sets Out How It Is Done**

A well-known report from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī orders that whoever sees something abominable should correct it by his hand; where he lacks the strength for that, by his tongue; and where he lacks the strength even for that, in his heart — which the report calls the least of faith (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 49a). The detail matters for the verse's interpretation: the means are ranked by capability, and the heart is the last resort for someone who can do nothing more. The sequence also shows the practice is not aimed at producing conflict — where the hand is able, it is used; where it would cause greater harm, the tongue or the heart remains. What the report forbids is indifference; what it requires is the strongest response a person can responsibly make.

**What the Two Key Words Carry**

The word for "right" means what is known and recognised — which is why it covers both the morally good and what a community has agreed to treat as decent. Its opposite means what is disowned or denied, carrying the same double sense of the morally evil and the socially repudiated. The choice of these two words, rather than a pair from purely legal vocabulary, tells the reader where the practice begins: with what a person already recognises, and with the sense of disgust wrongdoing produces in a healthy conscience — only then rising to formal instruction. A person who has lost the ability to be repulsed by wrong has nothing left to work with when asked to correct others.

**Who the Group Is**

The phrase about a group has been read two ways: as a distinct segment of the community taking the task on itself, or as a statement of what the community should be, addressed to the whole body. The case for a segment rests on the observation that not everyone can carry the practice out — it requires knowledge and the standing to speak, and the community is not composed only of those who can influence others. The case for the wider reading rests on the community being described elsewhere in the sūrah as a whole as enjoining right and forbidding wrong (3:110). The positions meet when the duty is understood as binding on the community as a whole and satisfied when a qualified part of it acts.

**Knowledge as the Prerequisite**

Because the practice requires distinguishing right from wrong, the commentators connected it with knowledge: a person who does not know what the law requires may end up forbidding what God has permitted, so the obligation rests most heavily on those equipped to judge. The Book's passage on the distribution of religious learning belongs to this discussion — it describes a party from each group going out to gain understanding of religion and returning to warn their people (9:122). The two verses describe one system: expertise is developed in some and used for the benefit of all, and the community is expected to arrange itself so the function does not lapse.

**Success Named in the Book's Vocabulary**

The verse closes by calling such people the successful — the same word that rings through the call to prayer, where success is placed beside the summons itself. In the Book's vocabulary success is not wealth or standing; it is the outcome of the final accounting. The placement is deliberate in a passage about public duty: a person who corrects wrongdoing exposes himself to resentment, and the Book names the reward at the end of the instruction so the effort is not measured by its reception. The success is attached to the practice rather than its results, since the practice is what lies within human control.

**How the Verse Has Been Used**

The command has shaped the community's debates about the limits of correction, the right to speak to those in power, and the balance between personal and shared responsibility. Those discussions are consequences of the verse rather than its content, and the text supplies their criterion: the practice is directed at what is recognised as evil, carried out by those able to do it, and aimed at what is good. The next verses continue with the memory of what the community was saved from, so the duty described here sits inside a passage about the community's common life rather than standing alone as an instruction to individuals.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:105

> And do not be like those who split ˹into sects˺ and differed after clear proofs had come to them. It is they who will suffer a tremendous punishment

**A Command Framed as a Comparison**

The verse tells the believers not to be like those who divided and differed after clear proofs reached them, and closes by stating the severe punishment awaiting such people. The command is expressed through a comparison rather than a prohibition, and the form gives the instruction its shape: what is forbidden is not division in the abstract but the imitation of a specific history. The Book holds up a community's conduct and tells its readers to avoid resembling it — a form of warning stronger than a rule, because it supplies the example.

**Who Divided and Differed**

The commentators identified the people in view as the communities that received the earlier scriptures, and the Book describes the pattern in several places with the same details. The sūrah of the Cow states that no one disputed the Scriptures except the very people who had received them, and that the dispute began after the clear proofs had come to them, out of jealousy (2:213). This sūrah said the same earlier: those given the Scripture did not dispute among themselves out of mutual envy until knowledge came to them (3:19). The Book is consistent about the sequence — the proof arrived, and the division followed it. That is what makes the conduct so grave: it is not the disagreement of people still searching.

**The Weight of "After Clear Proofs"**

The phrase carries the whole accusation. A community that has received an explanation has lost the excuse of not knowing, and its divisions are then matters of preference rather than evidence. The Book returns to this point whenever it describes the communities of the Book, treating the arrival of knowledge as the moment responsibility increases — not the moment quarrelling becomes tolerable. The verse takes that material and turns it into a warning for a community that has just been given its own proofs, so the historical example becomes a mirror rather than a stranger's story.

**Division as a Category**

The theme recurs with a fixed vocabulary: those who divided their faith and split into sects have nothing to do with the Prophet, and their affair rests with God, who will inform them of what they did (6:159); the same people are described with each party rejoicing in what it holds (30:32). Alongside those descriptions stands the command given to Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus to establish the religion and make no divisions in it (42:13). The Book treats unity as a requirement of the religion itself and division as a departure from it — this passage belongs to that treatment, not to a general preference for agreement.

**A Warning for the Believers Themselves**

The verse is addressed to the believers, and its position is significant: it follows the reminder of the community's own rescue from enmity and the command to hold together, and precedes the description of what awaited those who took the other path. The warning is not aimed only at the past. The believers are told the same thing could happen among them — and the Book records the prediction elsewhere in plain terms, saying the community would divide as earlier peoples had. What the verse establishes is the standard by which such a development is judged, and it removes any sense that a religious community's divisions are a neutral feature of its history.

**Two Verbs: Splitting and Differing**

The verse uses two words for one development, describing different aspects: the first names the act of separating, a body becoming parts; the second names the disagreement that follows and fills the distance between them. The order matters for how the prohibition works. The Book does not condemn disagreement as such in this sentence; it condemns the breaking apart that turns disagreement into division. Its standard is a community that keeps its argument inside a shared ground — the historical example is of people who stopped sharing one. The distinction explains why the Book can command the community both to refuse division and to correct one another, and why the presence of different views within a single body is not what the sentence describes.

**The Punishment Stated Without Detail**

The closing statement attaches a tremendous punishment to those described, and the Book gives it without elaboration — its practice at the close of such warnings. What the reader receives is the plain fact that the matter is not small, and the sentence leaves him with the choice the passage has been building toward: a community can hold to what it has been given, or reproduce the history it has just been shown. The verses that follow describe the outcome of both courses.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:106

> On that Day some faces will be bright while others gloomy. To the gloomy-faced it will be said, "Did you disbelieve after having believed? So taste the punishment for your disbelief."

**A Day That Shows on the Face**

The verse describes the Day of Judgment as the day when some faces become bright and others dark, and reports what is said to the second group. The image comes from everyday experience, where the face carries news of the inner state before any word is spoken. Arabic uses the darkened face for shame, distress, and suppressed anger, and the Book employs the idiom in this world before applying it to the next: one passage describes a man whose face glooms when he is given news of the daughter he attributes to God, suppressing his rage as he does so (43:17). The verse transfers that familiar reaction to the day when nothing is concealed.

**The Faces That Brighten**

The bright face is the Book's image for joy, relief, and approval, and the commentators added the spiritual sense: the light on those faces is the light of faith made visible. The Book describes one group as receiving the finest reward and more, with neither gloom nor disgrace covering their faces (10:26) — and the quiet detail is the two negations, which name the two things a person dreads most in public: sadness and humiliation. The verse divides humanity into two conditions with no third category, the Book's usual way of describing the final state.

**The Faces That Darken**

The dark face belongs to the people the Book elsewhere calls liars against God: on the Day of Judgment those who lied about God will be seen with gloomy faces, and the passage then asks whether the Fire is not the home of the arrogant (39:60). The connection between the face and the accusation is exact: what the dark faces display is the truth their owners denied for a lifetime — the moment the denial is no longer available. The verse does not describe the mechanism; it reports the condition, and the next words supply the speech addressed to the people in it.

**The Implied Words**

The clause about what is said to the dark-faced has no introductory verb in the text, and the commentators supplied it: *it will be said to them*. The grammatical point is small but shapes how the verse is heard — what the people receive is not a punishment imposed without explanation but a question, and the question is the first part of what they face. The Book's final scenes repeatedly include this element of address: the condemned are spoken to, their conduct named before them, and only then does the sentence follow — which is why the question comes before the command to taste.

**"Did You Disbelieve After Having Believed?"**

The question is put to people who once accepted the message, and the commentators identified them in several ways. The most natural, on the sūrah's own argument, is the people the preceding verses warned about being drawn away from belief, since the warning and the outcome belong to the same passage. Others read it as addressed to those who broke the primordial acknowledgement — the moment the Book describes when humanity testified that God is its Lord (7:172). A further reading applies it to hypocrites, whose attestation of faith was contradicted by their actions. The three readings converge on the verse's phrase, which names faith as something the people had and then lost.

**Later Applications**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī recorded that the words about disbelieving after belief were applied by some commentators to the groups that formed within the community's later history, among them the Khawārij, who emerged within a generation of the Prophet's death. The identification extends the sentence beyond its first audience without changing its sense, since the pattern it describes — a departure from a position once held — is the same. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī also report that the Prophet was shown something of the Hereafter in which people he knew were driven away from him, and that he was told he did not know what they would do after him. The accounts place the sentence within the warnings the community carried about itself.

**Why Faces**

The Book describes human beings in the Hereafter through the condition of their faces more often than through any other part of the body, and the choice is not arbitrary: the face is the one part a person cannot hide and the first thing another person reads. Passages describe faces humbled, radiant, and turned down — each conveying a state faster than any list of deeds could. Here the device carries the whole contrast between the two groups: the reader is shown what the two lives produced rather than being told about it. The verse's speech is addressed to the people whose faces have already answered the question — and the punishment they are told to taste is paired with the offence itself, in the Book's consistent practice: the tasting describes direct, personal experience, and what they experience is the disbelief they chose.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:107

> As for the bright-faced, they will be in Allah's mercy, where they will remain forever

**Mercy Described as a Place**

The verse states that the bright-faced will be in God's mercy, and the phrasing treats mercy as an environment rather than a transaction. What they enter is not a payment but a condition in which they remain. In the Book's usage the word for mercy here covers the kindness that surrounds a person and the provision that keeps him — the verse does not say God was merciful to them once; it places them inside the mercy and leaves them there. The sūrah's earlier verses used the same vocabulary when the well-grounded in knowledge asked for mercy from God's presence while praying that their hearts be kept firm — the two passages belong to one body of language.

**One Group Spoken To, the Other Described**
