The previous verse reported words spoken to the dark-faced; this verse describes the other group without reporting any words at all — the quietest feature of the pair. The condemned receive a question and a command, their state described in terms of what they are told; the blessed receive no speech, and are simply said to be in mercy. The Book often withholds the reward's content where it wants the reader to understand the reward as a state of favour rather than a quantity of goods, and the absence of an address here is one of those effects. Speech in the Book's judgment scenes is a form of accounting: the condemned are questioned, reminded, and sentenced in words. The blessed meet no accusation and are granted no dialogue — what is reported about them is a location, not a verdict. The difference is the difference between a case being made and a case being closed.

**Permanence**

The verse states that they remain where they are, and the formula recurs throughout the Book's descriptions of Paradise. This sūrah uses it for those who do good, who receive forgiveness and gardens with rivers flowing beneath, staying there forever (3:136). The Book attaches the same word of permanence to the punishment of those who persist in rejection — which is why the pairing in this passage is exact and deliberate: both outcomes are described as lasting, and the sūrah gives both descriptions in the same sequence so the reader meets them together.

**The Light on the Face**

The commentators read the brightness as the visible sign of the inner light of faith — which explains why the two conditions are described with imagery of light and darkness rather than a list of pleasures and pains. If the face displays the state of the heart, then the Day of Judgment is the day the display becomes accurate, and the difference between the two groups is the difference between what they carried within them. The sūrah has been describing hearts that swerve and hearts that are kept; this verse shows the end of that distinction in the simplest visual terms available.

**Who Is Described**

The bright-faced are those who end their lives in the condition the passage has been urging, and the commentators connected them with the believers the sūrah has addressed since its opening. The identification matters for the passage's balance: the preceding verse warned the believers against repeating the splitting of those who received proofs, and this verse states the outcome for those who do not — placed inside the divine mercy without the details of their deeds being rehearsed, since the passage has already set out what those deeds are: holding together, and calling others to what is good.

**The Two Outcomes Held Together**

The Book does not divide the two groups across separate chapters, and here they stand in consecutive verses — a working part of the sūrah's method. A community being warned about division is shown, on one side, what division produced for those who chose it, and on the other, what awaits the people who hold to what they received. The verse has also served the community as a statement of what it hopes for, and its wording keeps the hope in the right place: what the blessed possess is not a rank they achieved but a mercy they were placed in. A community told to give God His due and hold together is not left with the impression that its standing depends on the perfection of its performance — at the end of a passage full of warnings, it is shown a mercy that surrounds those who kept faith.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:108

> These are Allah's revelations We recite to you ˹O Prophet˺ in truth. And Allah desires no injustice to ˹His˺ creation

**The Revelations Described as Recited**

The verse states that what has been set out in the preceding passage consists of God's revelations recited in truth, and adds that God desires no injustice for any of His creation. Both sentences concern the message and the One who sent it. The formula appears elsewhere in nearly identical words, paired there with the address to the Prophet that he is truly one of the messengers (2:252) — the pairing showing what the formula does: it identifies the material as revelation and locates the one reciting it within the sequence of those sent by God. Here the addressee's name is omitted, the kind of variation the Book uses in repeated formulas, because the passage's attention is on the message and its reliability rather than the person receiving it.

**Truth as a Property of the Recitation**

The truth is not offered as a claim to be tested later. The passage has presented commands, warnings, historical examples, and the descriptions of two outcomes, and the sentence declares the whole of it true. The Book makes such declarations at the close of passages that have answered objections at length, and the effect is to place the reader's response inside a settled frame: the matter has been explained, the objections addressed, and the revelation stated to be what it claims.

**The Statement About Injustice**

The Arabic word for injustice describes the placing of something outside its proper place, and the commentators offered explanations that fit the wording. Al-Rāzī read the sentence as a denial that God desires human beings to commit wrong. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī connected it with the verse that follows and read it as a statement that God has no need to wrong anyone, since everything belongs to Him and His power is complete. A third explanation takes it as the plain statement that God does not in fact wrong anyone. Each denies injustice; they differ over whether the denial concerns God's will for human acts or God's own conduct.

**The Question in Theology**

The sentence became one of the points at which the schools of theology worked out their positions on God's relation to good and evil. One school held that wrong is by definition a trespass on what belongs to another — and since everything belongs to God, nothing He does can be described as wrong. Another held that justice and injustice are objective features of the world that human beings can recognise, and that God is always just by that standard. The schools agreed about the conclusion the verse states and differed about how to explain it. The verse itself states the denial and leaves the argument to those who came after it — and the sentence that follows supplies the fact on which every version of the argument rests: whatever is in the heavens and the earth belongs to God, and all matters are returned to Him. The one who judges owns everything he judges and holds everything the judgment concerns.

**Two Kinds of Command**

Some theologians distinguished between what God asks of human beings and what He brings into existence — the command of obligation and the command of creation. On that distinction, a person can disobey what God has asked of him, and cannot prevent what God has decided to bring about; the wrong that occurs in the world belongs to the creature's choice rather than the creator's desire. The sūrah's passage has been built on exactly this understanding of human responsibility, and this verse states the divine side of it.

**How the Verse Closes the Passage**

The verse gathers the commands, the examples, the two outcomes, and the warning against division under one statement: these are God's revelations, recited in truth, from a Lord who desires no injustice for anyone. Its two sentences answer two different anxieties, and the passage needs both — the first answers whether what has been recited can be trusted; the second whether the Lord who sent it will deal justly with those who receive it. Together they close the argument: neither doubt about the revelation nor fear about the Judge remains available as an excuse. The next verse adds the final statement about ownership and the return of all matters, and then the sūrah turns to the community's identity.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:109

> To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. And to Allah ˹all˺ matters will be returned ˹for judgment˺

**Ownership and the Return of Matters**

The verse states that everything in the heavens and the earth belongs to God, and that all matters will be returned to Him for judgment — a statement of possession followed by a statement of jurisdiction. The Book pairs them often: whatever is in the heavens and the earth belongs to God, who is the Self-Sufficient and Praiseworthy (31:26) — ownership that carries no benefit for the owner and no limitation on Him; and God will gather humanity on a day about which there is no doubt, after which the question is asked whose word could be more truthful than God's (4:87).

**Why the Formula Settles Disputes**

The ownership sentence is the Book's standard reply to arguments about authority. Throughout this sūrah the disputes have concerned lineage, scripture, and standing, each party claiming a position the others contested. The reply is not to adjudicate the claims one by one but to state who owns the terms: if everything belongs to God, no community owns a claim on Him, and no lineage carries a right against Him. The sūrah has already used this reasoning in calling God a witness over the conduct of the People of the Book and never unaware of what they do; the sentence here generalises the point to all creation.

**"All Matters Are Returned"**

The clause appears in several contexts — one is a passage about the battlefield, where the return of matters to God is given as the reason an outcome was arranged as it was (8:44). The phrase covers both the disposal of events in this world and the final accounting: nothing that occurs falls outside the jurisdiction of the One to whom matters return. Its function here is to add time to ownership — what belongs to God also comes back to Him, and the reckoning is the moment when the ownership becomes visible.

**What It Means for This Sūrah's Parties**

The communities addressed had claimed Abraham, held scriptures, and disputed about which of them stood where. The verse places all of that inside a statement that leaves nobody outside God's ownership. The dispute about who possesses the truth matters — the sūrah has pursued it at length — and what the ownership sentence supplies is the fact that the dispute itself stands before a judge whose authority nobody granted and nobody can withdraw. The communities may argue as long as they like; the matter will return to its owner.

**The Comfort the Sentence Contains**

The Book states the return of matters as reassurance as often as warning, and both uses are present here. For people who have been wronged and cannot obtain justice, the clause means no case is lost by being left unresolved in this world. The sūrah has just described communities that divide after receiving proofs and groups working to draw the believers from their faith — wrongs whose consequences are hard to measure, and the sentence asserts the measurement will be carried out. The Book's consolations are frequently of this kind, addressed to people who cannot see the outcome they are owed.

**Ownership and Human Trust**

The statements about divine ownership do not remove human responsibility for the world; the Book holds the two together elsewhere, describing human beings as placed on the earth to act within it and answerable for what they do with what they have been given. What belongs to God is entrusted to people for a time, and the return of matters is the accounting for the trust. That is the framework in which the sūrah's commands about wealth, unity, and calling others to good make sense: each concerns the use of something never owned outright.

**A Sentence Repeated at Points of Decision**

The Book places the statement that matters return to God where human judgment fails or a dispute cannot be settled in this world — in the account of a battle whose outcome nobody could have predicted, and at the close of passages about communities that refused their messengers. The recurrence gives the sentence a practical role: a reader who has exhausted what he can do about a wrong is told where the matter goes next. The verse ends a section that began with the address to the believers about being drawn from their faith and ran through unity, division, and the two outcomes — and the sentences that follow carry the reader from the terms of the argument to a statement about the community's identity.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:110

> You are the best community ever raised for humanity—you encourage good, forbid evil, and believe in Allah. Had the People of the Book believed, it would have been better for them. Some of them are faithful, but most are rebellious

**The Best Community Raised for Humanity**

The verse addresses the believers as the best community brought forth for humanity, then names the practices that define the description: encouraging what is good, forbidding what is wrong, and believing in God. The rank is stated first, its content immediately after.

Commentators read the verb in more than one way. Some read it as a destiny settled in advance — the community told what it has been written to be, and expected to live up to it; others read it as what the community becomes through the practices named. The same title appears in the sūrah of the Cow, where the community is described as made upright so that it may bear witness over humanity, with the messenger a witness over it (2:143) — a role that explains why the description of practices follows the title, since a witness must be visible and its conduct is part of what it witnesses to.

**Why the Practices Come Before Belief in the List**

The order is worth noting: enjoining and forbidding are named before belief. Ibn Kathīr drew the conclusion that the community is called best because of what it does for others — the concern of enjoining good and forbidding wrong. The three items are not ranked against each other; together they describe a community defined by what it does as much as by what it holds. The sūrah has just commanded these very practices (3:104), and this verse states the result as the community's identity — an argument made in the form of a life rather than a claim.

**"Had the People of the Book Believed"**

The verse then states that if the People of the Book had believed, it would have been better for them — a statement about benefit, not blame: belief is described as what would have improved their condition. Belief here means acceptance of the message that came to the Prophet, since the verse speaks of a community that already held a scripture, and the sūrah's arguments with them have been precisely about acknowledging that message. The sentence states the consequence in the mildest terms available — which gives the next clause its force.

**"Most Are Rebellious"**

The word rendered rebellious is used in the Book for abandoning an obligation or stepping outside a limit, and the commentators noted it is contrasted here with belief rather than with disbelief. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī read the term as describing the People of the Book's failure to be faithful to their own scriptures, which — in the Muslim reading — point toward the final message. The choice is exact: it names a breaking of trust with what a person already holds, not a simple rejection of what he has not held. The accusation is of a community that had something to be faithful to, and mostly was not.

**Believers Named Among Them**

The statement that some among them are faithful is confirmed elsewhere in the sūrah: they are not all alike, and an upright group recites God's revelations through the night while prostrating (3:113); the closing passages speak of those among them who believe in God, in what was revealed to the believers, and in what was revealed to them (3:199). The Book's own categories prevent readers from converting a judgment about majority conduct into a verdict on a whole people — and the tradition records individuals of the People of the Book who accepted the message and were counted among the community's most respected members.

**Three Readings of the Verb**

Al-Rāzī set out what follows from each reading. Read in the past, the words describe something already written: the community was named the best of peoples in the register the Book mentions at the end of the sūrah of the Constellations — a Preserved Tablet (85:22) — and the sentence calls on the community to live up to its name. Read as addressed to the community once their lives are over, the words describe them in the Garden, looking back at what they were in the world. Read as a verb of becoming, the title is no fixed inheritance at all: a community becomes the best of peoples by doing the work the sentence names. The three readings differ about when the sentence is spoken and agree about the condition attached to it — none turns the title into a possession, and each leaves the community's conduct in its own hands.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:111

> They can never inflict harm on you, except a little annoyance. But if they meet you in battle, they will flee and they will have no helpers

**What the Opposition Can Actually Do**

The verse tells the believers that the people in question cannot harm them beyond a limited irritation, and that if it comes to fighting they will retreat and find no help. The claim is carefully measured. The Book does not say the enemies are harmless — the same passage has described efforts to turn the believers from their faith and warned them against complying. What it names is the boundary: what the opposition can produce is annoyance; what it cannot produce is the destruction of a community that holds to its message.

**The Annoyance Named**

The word for annoyance covers insult, mockery, and the kind of speech that wears a community down without defeating it — the commentators included blasphemous claims about God among the things it describes. The sūrah returns to the theme a few verses later, telling the believers that patience and mindfulness will prevent the schemes of their opponents from harming them at all (3:120). The two verses fit together: one describes the category of harm that remains possible, the other the conduct that neutralises even that. The Book names the residual risk rather than promising absolute immunity, which keeps the exhortation to patience meaningful.

**The Prediction About Fighting**

The second sentence predicts armed confrontation: if the opponents meet the believers in battle, they will turn and flee, with no helpers. The prediction matches another passage stating that the same people would certainly flee if fought, leaving the disbelievers no help (59:12). The sūrah has described one battle in which an outnumbered community was supported, and will shortly describe another in which the believers were tested; the prediction here is not that every encounter ends in victory — the sūrah later describes a defeat in detail — but that the opposition cannot make the message fail. What a hostile group can do to individuals is real and is described plainly; what it can do to the message is nothing.

**"No Helpers" as a Fixed Description**

The phrase is one of the Book's fixed descriptions for those cut off, and its recurrence is deliberate: it is used of people whose deeds are wasted in both worlds (3:22), of those subjected to punishment in both lives (3:56), and of those whom God has condemned, for whom no helper exists (4:52). In the Book's vocabulary having no helpers describes a condition, not an event — the state of people who have placed themselves outside the protection others enjoy. The verse applies it to a group that had been trying to draw the believers from their faith, and the sentence lets the reader see the end of that effort.

**Reassurance Framed for the Believers**

The sentence is framed for the believers, not their opponents: it tells a community under pressure what the pressure amounts to, immediately after the previous verse described that community's rank among the nations. The pairing matters — a community told it is the best raised for humanity is at once told what its opponents can and cannot do, so the rank is not turned into an expectation of ease. The sūrah's pattern is to give the believers both their standing and the limits of their trial, and this verse supplies the second half. The limit is not a reason to relax: the irritation is real, and the sūrah's response to it is patience rather than confrontation — expect it, refuse to be governed by it, and continue.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:112

> They will be stricken with disgrace wherever they go, unless they are protected by a covenant with Allah or a treaty with the people. They have invited the displeasure of Allah and have been branded with misery for rejecting Allah's revelations and murdering ˹His˺ prophets unjustly. This is ˹a fair reward˺ for their disobedience and violations

**A Condition Described as Lasting Abasement**

The verse states that the people in view will be struck with humiliation wherever they are found, unless protected by a covenant from God or a treaty with people, and it explains the condition by naming their offences. The Book describes the same condition in an earlier passage about the Israelites, struck with disgrace and misery for rejecting God's signs and unjustly killing the prophets (2:61). The wording of the two verses is close, and the repetition shows the Book treating this state as the working out of a long-standing pattern rather than an isolated event.

**The Two Means of Protection**

The exception names two things that lift the condition: a covenant from God and a treaty with people. The commentators explained the first as protection from a divine obligation — whether through accepting the religion or through the pledge a Muslim ruler grants — and the second as an agreement with a community or a state. Ibn Kathīr read the same phrase in the command to hold firmly to the rope of God (3:103) as a reference to a pact, the sense it carries here. The sentence therefore acknowledges that a people can live in safety among others, and locates that safety in a formal relationship rather than in strength.

**The Reading That Connects the Verse With the Tax**

Commentators of later centuries connected the abasement with the indemnity paid by non-Muslim communities under Muslim rule and the protected status that accompanied it. Al-Ṭabarī took a different view of the same phrase, arguing the protection cannot be what removes the abasement, because the abasement and the protection are presented as alternatives in the sentence itself. The disagreement shows the commentators distinguishing between the text's statement and the legal arrangements later societies built around it.

**The Offences Named**

The verse gives two reasons for the condition: rejecting God's signs, and killing the prophets without right. Both are acts committed by communities that had received messengers, and the Book attaches them to the history of the Israelites in particular. The phrase about killing without justification marks a crime with no excuse available: the prophets were sent to their own people, and the Book's account of their rejection includes violence in several cases. The accusation is not addressed to a foreign enemy; it is addressed to the descendants of those who received the message — the footing on which the sūrah has argued with them throughout.

**The Closing Word: Disobedience and Transgression**

The verse describes the outcome as a recompense for disobedience and for going beyond limits — the failure to do what was required, and the decision to exceed what was permitted. The pair covers both leaving a duty and invading a prohibition, and the Book uses it to state that a people's condition is earned rather than imposed. That is the point here: the abasement is not a gratuitous humiliation of one community by another but the result of a course of conduct the passage has been tracing.

**Why the Verse Does Not Authorise Contempt**

The sentence describes what happens to a people whose conduct has been as described; it does not instruct the believers to humiliate anyone. The distinction is essential, and the sūrah makes it in the very next verse: the People of the Book are not all alike. A community reading this passage is being told how to understand the condition of the people around it, and the explanation is moral rather than racial. The Book's statements about believers among the People of the Book — kept close to this one, and developed in the verses that follow — prevent the description from hardening into a verdict on a group defined by descent. A reader who stops at the abasement has read half of the passage: the judgment comes first, and the qualification follows immediately.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:113

> Yet they are not all alike: there are some among the People of the Book who are upright, who recite Allah's revelations throughout the night, prostrating ˹in prayer˺

**The Qualification That Prevents a Blanket Judgment**

The verse opens by stating that the People of the Book are not all alike, and describes one part of them: an upright community reciting God's revelations through the night while prostrating. The sentence follows the previous verse's account of a people struck with humiliation, and the Book's judgment is qualified immediately after being stated. Some commentators understood the qualification as a distinction within the People of the Book itself; others read it as a comparison between the believers and the other communities. Both agree on the practical point: the verse forbids the reader to close the account on a whole people.

**What "Upright" Carries**

The word describing the group means standing, with the senses of firmness, justice, and the standing posture of prayer. Al-Ṭabarī noted its association with fairness, and others explained the upright as those who maintain the limits God has set and do not distort what they hold. The word's double sense fits the verse's content, since the same group is then described praying through the night: what is described is a portion of a community that remained straight in conduct and constant in worship — the reason the blanket judgment cannot be applied to everyone.

**Night Worship in the Book's Vocabulary**

The Book returns often to praying in the dark: people who abandon their beds to call on their Lord in hope and fear, giving from what they have been provided (32:16); the righteous described in this very sūrah as patient, truthful, obedient, and seeking forgiveness before dawn (3:17). The command to rise in the last part of the night for additional prayer comes in the sūrah of the Night Journey (17:79), with the command to recite the Book in measured tones in the same context. The group described here is performing a practice the Book commends to its own community — the passage is acknowledging the practice wherever it is found.

**Why the Night Is Named**

The night carries a significance beyond the hour. Prayer in the dark is hidden from people, and the Book's descriptions of it repeatedly pair hope with fear — the state of a person alone with his Lord. A practice no one sees cannot be performed for display, so the description of a community reciting revelation in the small hours is a description of sincerity as much as devotion. That is why the mention matters at this point in the argument: after verses about communities that fought the truth or were drawn from it, the Book names people whose worship was conducted where only God could see it.

**The Reports About the Occasion**

The commentaries record several occasions for the verse's revelation: believers waiting in the mosque for the Prophet to lead the night prayer, and his telling them that no people of any other religion were remembering God at that hour besides them; groups from Najrān, from Abyssinia, and from Byzantine territory who followed the religion of Jesus and affirmed the truth of the message; a people who prayed between sunset and the end of twilight. The commentaries relate these reports without chains the sūrah's editors have examined. Whatever the occasion, the description is general in form — the Book is naming a type of conduct, not one gathering.

**Between Two Judgments**

The verse stands between the statement that most of the People of the Book were false to what they held and the detailed description of the upright group in the verses that follow. Its position gives the passage its shape: after the case against the People of the Book has been developed at length, the verse interrupts it to name a part of them the case does not cover. The Book's habit of holding both facts in view at once is why the passage can be read without producing contempt for a people as such.

**A Standard, Not Just a Report**

The group is identified by conduct — standing firm, reciting the revelation, prostrating at night — and nothing in the description depends on lineage or history. That makes the verse usable as a standard: the community receiving the sūrah is given the marks of the people it is told to respect and recognise among others, and those marks are practices its own revelation commands. The pairing of recitation and prostration describes a single act of worship — the recitation is the content of the standing and the prostration its completion — so the group is engaged in exactly the night prayer the receiving community is itself instructed to perform, which is why the description can serve as recognition rather than only report.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:114

> They believe in Allah and the Last Day, encourage good and forbid evil, and race with one another in doing good. They are ˹truly˺ among the righteous

**Four Marks of the Upright Group**

The verse continues the description of the group named in the previous verse and gives four elements: belief in God and the Last Day, encouraging what is good, forbidding what is wrong, and racing one another in good deeds — closing by placing them among the righteous. The list answers the question the previous verse left open about who the upright are: they are identified by what they hold and what they do, in language as general as the Book's allows — faith in God and the final accounting, and conduct directed at both good and evil in the world.

**Why Belief Is Paired With the Last Day**

The pairing of God and the Last Day is the Book's shortest creed and one of its most frequent formulas: when the Book wants to describe belief in a way that covers both the present and the future, it names these two. The reason is the difference the two make to conduct — belief in God establishes who is being obeyed; belief in the Last Day establishes that obedience has a destination and no deed is lost. The Book uses the pair when promising reward: whoever believes in God and the Last Day and acts rightly will have his reward with his Lord, without fear or grief (2:62), and the sūrah of the Table repeats the same formula in the same terms (5:69).

**Practices the Sūrah Has Already Required**

The two middle items are the ones the sūrah commanded of the believers: a group that calls to good, enjoins right and forbids wrong (3:104), and the same words used of the community as a whole (3:110). The vocabulary is not community-specific — the Book applies it to the Muslim community as its own obligation and to the upright among the People of the Book as their description, which shows the terms name conduct rather than membership. What the sūrah presents is the recognition of the same conduct in another community, stated as a fact rather than a concession.

**"Racing With One Another in Good"**

The final element is the language of competition. The Book uses the same verb for Zachariah's family, who raced one another in good deeds, calling on God in hope and fear and humbling themselves before Him (21:90). The image is deliberate: two people running toward the same goal is a picture of shared effort rather than rivalry for a prize. What the verse adds to the qualities of the upright is motion — the good they do is not a settled possession but an activity that draws them forward.

**What "Among the Righteous" Adds**

The closing phrase is a verdict, given without the qualifications that accompany the descriptions of the other groups in this passage. The Book's habit is to conclude such descriptions with a statement of standing, and the next verse confirms the same standing by promising that no good they do will be denied.

**A Standard in Three Dimensions**

The verse covers doctrine, ethics, and effort in one list, and the sūrah's own instructions run through all three. None is sufficient alone in the Book's account: a person may hold correct beliefs and remain inactive, so the belief is paired with practices requiring him to intervene in the world around him; he may act without excelling, so the verse adds the motion of racing. The description of the upright group is a full picture, placed in front of a community that has just been given the same instructions.

**Who These Verses Describe**

The identification of those praised here required two decisions from the commentators: who the upright community of the previous verse is, and how the earlier statement that some of the People of the Book believe is to be taken. On one reading the upright community consists of the People of the Book themselves, and this verse continues the description; on another it refers to the Muslims, and the passage moves from the community to the group it has been discussing. The content resembles the earlier statements about those who believe in God and the Last Day and do right (2:62; 5:69), and the same questions of identity arise in each place. What the verse names is a set of qualities rather than a group — the qualities the sūrah has been commending throughout.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:115

> They will never be denied the reward for any good they have done. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of those mindful ˹of Him˺

**A Promise That No Good Is Lost**

The verse closes the three-verse description of the upright group with an assurance about their reward: whatever good they do will not be denied them, and God has full knowledge of those mindful of Him. The assurance is stated in the strongest form the language provides — the verb of denial carries the sense of refusing something that is due. The Book uses the same idea elsewhere, assuring people that their charity and vows are known to God and nothing given has gone unrecorded (2:270).

**What the Word for Denial Implies**

The word shares its root with the Book's word for ingratitude — the refusal to acknowledge a benefit received. Applying it to the reward means the good done by such people will not be treated as if it had never happened. The choice is precise in a passage about people whose standing was doubted: the sūrah has just described a group whose worship and conduct the receiving community might have been tempted to discount, and the sentence removes the possibility of discount. What is promised is not a reward of a particular size but the acknowledgement that the deed occurred and belongs to the person who did it.

**The Reassurance for Converts**

The commentators — Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī among them — connected the verse with the situation of people from among the People of the Book who had accepted the message and were taunted by their former coreligionists with the claim that everything they had done before was now worthless. The wording fits: the sentence tells such people that the good they performed in their former community is not lost, without making the reward depend on the label they once carried. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī recorded a reading of the verb in the second person — a direct address to the community: what *you* do will not be denied. On either reading the verse answers a fear, and the fear belongs to people who had left one community for another and were uncertain what had become of the good they had done. The answer: God does not discard what people have done well.

**God's Knowledge of the Mindful**

The second sentence shifts from the reward to the knowledge of the One who gives it — and it explains how the first can be true of deeds nobody witnessed. The group described in the preceding verses prayed at night and worked quietly at their good; the deeds no one saw are recorded by the One who saw them, and the standard for the reward is the mindfulness that accompanied the act rather than its visibility.

**Reward as Acknowledgement, Not Arithmetic**

The verse sets no scale or measure, placing the reward in the same sentence as God's knowledge. The Book's other statements about recompense often multiply or specify; this one simply says nothing is refused. That suits the passage's purpose — settling a question about standing rather than publishing a tariff. The emphasis stays on the fact of the accounting, not its size, and the criterion named in the final clause is the Book's consistent position on the value of acts: a deed's worth is tied to the state of the person who performs it, and that state is known to God alone.

**How the Description Closes**

The three verses about the upright group end here, and the sequence is worth noticing: worship first, then beliefs and practices, then the assurance that the reward is secure — from the observable to the promised, the Book's usual direction in such passages. Nothing in the description is drawn from claims the group makes about itself; everything is conduct, and the outcome is stated by God, attached to the conduct rather than to the community. The passage's larger balance — warnings followed by commands, judgments followed by qualifications, descriptions of conduct closed with statements of reward and knowledge — is the sūrah's method in miniature, and the verses that follow take up wealth and children and the fate of those who disbelieve.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:116

> Indeed, neither the wealth nor children of the disbelievers will be of any benefit to them against Allah. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever

**The Sentence That Comes Back**

The verse restates what the sūrah said near its beginning: the wealth and children of those who disbelieve will not benefit them against God, and such people will be companions of the Fire. The earlier statement came before the long argument about the covenant, the House, and the People of the Book; this one comes after it. A repetition in the Book is a structural signal: the reader who carried the first sentence through the intervening passage now meets it again with everything learned in between — and the second occurrence shows the intervening material was an explanation of how the condition arises rather than a change of subject.

**What Lies Between the Two Statements**

The distance between the sentences is filled with the sūrah's discussion of communities that received revelations, disputed about their ancestors, contracted covenants, and divided — some concerning the People of the Book, some the believers, all concerning people who possessed things they trusted. Read with that in mind, the repeated sentence gains a specific sense: the wealth and children it names are not only money and offspring — the passage has been treating standing, lineage, and scripture as possessions of the same kind. What the verse denies is that any of them interposes between a person and his Lord, and the intervening argument has supplied the evidence for the denial.

**The Delusion the Book Diagnoses**

The Book gives the psychology of the condition elsewhere: a person who turns away when the revelations are recited does so because he has been given wealth and children (68:14); and the Prophet is told not to be impressed by the wealth and children of such people, because God's purpose with those gifts is a trial ending in death in disbelief (9:85). The verses here describe a settled pattern, and the rest of the Book diagnoses it: a person measuring his safety by what he holds, mistaking the abundance he was given for an endorsement.

**Why Children Are Named With Wealth**

The two items appear together because they cover both of a person's horizons: wealth is what he has; children are what continues him. The Book's verdict is not a condemnation of either — it describes them as the adornment of the present life (18:46) and as a test in which many are tried (64:15). What is denied is their use as a defence: a person may love his children and possess his wealth and still answer for his conduct, and the belief that these things can stand in his place is the error that ends badly.

**"Against Allah" and What Benefit Means**

The phrase sets the terms of the denial: nothing here says wealth is useless in ordinary affairs; it says wealth cannot be used against the outcome the passage has been describing. That is why the verse stands here rather than only at the sūrah's beginning — the reader has now seen the ending, and the sentence about benefit is a statement about that ending. The Book's habit is to deny a thing's usefulness precisely where its usefulness has been trusted, and the sūrah has spent a long passage showing what people trust.

**From Fuel to Residents**

The earlier statement described such people as *fuel* of the Fire, naming what they become; this verse calls them its *residents* and states they will remain, naming where they live and for how long. The change of image is deliberate, and the two belong together — fuel describes the person's relation to the fire; residence describes his relation to the place. The Book uses them in the order of a claim and its elaboration, the more vivid image first and the fuller description second.

**A Condition, Not a Birth**

The verse speaks of those who disbelieve, and the sūrah has used the category carefully: it distinguished the People of the Book who rejected the message from those among them who believed, and refused to treat a whole community as one thing. The description is of a condition, not of a birth — what the Book condemns is the state in which a person dies, and the same Book has just described a group within the very communities under criticism as upright and among the righteous. A reader who applies the verse to a people rather than a condition has read past the qualification placed two verses earlier. The verses that follow take the same two possessions and describe what happens to what such people spend in this world — the next step in dismantling the securities the passage has named.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:117

> The good they do in this worldly life is like the harvest of an evil people struck by a bitter wind, destroying it ˹completely˺. Allah never wronged them, but they wronged themselves

**A Parable About Outlay Without Return**

The verse compares what such people spend in this life to a crop destroyed by a bitter wind, and ends by stating that God did not wrong them — they wronged themselves. The image is agricultural, and its subject is labour that produces nothing. The parable does not deny the labour occurred: a harvest is the result of work, and the verse describes the destruction of something that had grown. What it denies is the survival of the result — stated in an image any farmer in the audience would have recognised.

**Wind in the Book's Picture of Effort**

The Book uses wind in several passages about the fate of deeds, with variations carrying different emphases. One describes a garden of palms, vines, and every kind of fruit, whose owner grows old with young dependents while a fire-laden whirlwind strikes and burns the whole of it (2:266). Another compares the deeds of those who disbelieve to ashes blown away by a fierce wind on a stormy day, so nothing they earned remains available to them (14:18). Elsewhere wind is the bearer of good news ahead of rain and a means by which provision reaches people. The two uses belong to a single doctrine: the wind does not discriminate — what it destroys is destroyed because of where it blew, not because wind is cruel.

**Which Good Deeds Are in View**

The spending named is the kind performed for reputation, obligation, or the maintenance of standing, and the commentators applied the image to such outlays. The description does not make the acts invisible; it says their fruit does not reach the place where fruit matters — a statement about the relation of the deed to its giver, not about the deed's size. The Book's treatment of sincere and insincere giving takes the same line, and its opening denial that the disbelievers' wealth profits them at all is the same doctrine in another form.

**"A People Who Wronged Themselves"**

The crop in the parable belongs to people who wronged themselves, and the destruction is attached to that condition — placing the cause inside the owners rather than in the weather. A person's losses are not arbitrary, and the Book allows no doctrine of blind fate: the wind that destroys the harvest blows on a field whose people are described as wrongdoers. The Book's parables are generally constructed this way, with the moral stated inside the picture rather than added afterwards.

**The Denial of Divine Injustice**

The verse states directly that God did not wrong them — the same in substance as the statement two verses back that God desires no injustice for His creation. The insistence is needed because of the verse's subject: a person who has worked hard and lost everything is the readiest audience for the idea that he was treated unfairly, and the Book's answer is that what was lost had already been forfeited by the state of the one who planted. The denial is an explanation rather than a defence: it tells the reader where to look for the cause. The phrase that people wrong themselves runs through the Book — the sūrah uses it again of those who remember God after wrongdoing and ask forgiveness — and in every case the wrong is directed at the self: the primary victim of a person's misconduct is his own account.

**The Limit of the Parable**

The parable concerns the fate of what a person spends in this present life, and the Book's promises about the Hereafter are not contradicted by it: the verses immediately before this one promised the upright that no good of theirs will be denied, and the two statements stand together in the same passage. The difference is the state of the person who acts. The Book's consistent position is that deeds are weighed with the giver, so an identical act performed from faith and from display has different results — the parable states one side of that position, the promise about the upright the other, and the reader is expected to hold both.

**Why the Verse Follows the Previous One**

The previous verse denied that wealth and children can stand between a person and God; this verse describes what happens to what such people give away. The order dismantles the securities in sequence — first the possessions a person keeps, then the resources he parts with — and in each case the outcome is stated without reference to the amount. The conclusion the passage is building: what saves a person is not the size of what he holds or the extent of what he gives, but the state in which he stands before his Lord.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:118

> O believers! Do not associate closely with others who would not miss a chance to harm you. Their only desire is to see you suffer. Their prejudice has become evident from what they say—and what their hearts hide is far worse. We have made Our revelations clear to you, if only you understood

**The Word Behind Intimacy**

The verse forbids the believers to take certain people as intimates, and the word it uses for that relationship is the one for the inner lining of a garment — the layer of cloth closest to the body and hidden from view. It describes confidants, the people to whom a person tells what he tells no one else. The choice of word tells the reader what is being regulated: not dealings, speech, or neighbourliness, but trust — the kind of closeness that exposes a person's inner affairs to someone else. The prohibition concerns the disposal of confidences, not the avoidance of a community.

**The Advice the Commentaries Took**

The classical commentaries read the verse as a caution about relying on outsiders in matters where a disclosure could harm the community: Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī mention contracts, correspondence, and the conduct of important affairs, and al-Qurṭubī adds a complaint from his own lifetime about the employment of Jews and Christians as scribes and secretaries — a remark about his society rather than a rule of the verse. What the commentaries preserve is the practical sense of the instruction: a community at odds with a group should not place the details of its own affairs in that group's hands, since the verse's own reason is that such people wish the community harm.

**The Limits of the Instruction**

The verse names a particular kind of relationship, and the Book's wider instructions show what it does not forbid. The same sūrah has just described upright people among the People of the Book and promised their good will not be lost; and it commands justice and kind treatment toward those who do not fight the believers or drive them from their homes. Read within the Book, the verse is a rule for the protection of the community, not a permission for hostility: what it forbids is extending inner trust to people whose attitude the preceding verses have described, and what it leaves in place is everything else relations between people ordinarily consist of.

**"They Would Not Spare You Any Corruption"**

The second sentence states that such people will not miss a chance to harm, and that their wish is for the believers to suffer — a disposition rather than an incident. The Book attaches the same disposition to the hypocrites elsewhere, whose speech changes with their company: they tell the believers they believe, and when alone with their own leaders they say they were only mocking (2:14). The verse assumes that condition as the reason for its instruction and describes what it produces when the opportunity arises.

**Hatred That Has Reached the Tongue**

The verse says hatred has appeared from their mouths while their hearts conceal more — speech treated as the visible edge of something larger, with the hidden part measured by what has already escaped. The Book uses the same approach in the sūrah of the Table, describing people who say they believe with their tongues while their hearts are in disbelief (5:41). The point in both places: a person's public statements are the least of what he carries, and the instruction here is drawn from that fact — the community is being told what to expect from what it can already hear. The clause about the hidden hostility also links this verse to the next, which continues with what such people say in private; and the statements of God's knowledge that close such verses are the answer to a community that cannot see what is being planned against it: what the believers cannot penetrate is not hidden from the One who settles accounts.

**"If Only You Understood"**

The verse ends with a call to understanding — the Book's way of saying the instruction is obvious once its grounds are considered. The sūrah uses the formula where a plain inference is available and has been missed. Here the inference concerns the mismatch between what the community was doing and what it was dealing with: extending intimacy to people whose intentions had been made plain is named as a failure to understand the situation rather than an abstract moral lapse — a reading confirmed by the verses that follow, which describe how such people react to the community's fortunes.

**Who Is Described**

The commentators differed about whether the people in view were the hypocrites of Madinah or a faction among the People of the Book, and the passage's context supports both readings. The identification does not change the instruction: in either case the verse deals with people inside the community's reach, whose hostility is partly hidden and partly visible, and whose closeness to the believers' affairs is a risk. That is why the verse is addressed to the believers rather than to the people it describes, and why it speaks about trust rather than enmity.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:119

> Here you are! You love them but they do not love you, and you believe in all Scriptures. When they meet you they say, "We believe." But when alone, they bite their fingertips in rage. Say, ˹O Prophet˺, "˹May you˺ die of your rage!" Surely Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart

**Love That Is Not Returned**

The verse addresses the believers as people who love those who do not love them back, and the commentators set out several ways of understanding the love in view: the affection believers are expected to hold for all people as fellow creatures of God; the wish that others be guided, which is a form of good will; or the absence of any wish that their opponents fall into difficulty. The three readings share one feature — the believers' stance is generous, contrasted with an attitude that is not. The sentence is not a complaint but a description of an asymmetry, and the passage goes on to state how to live with it.

**"You Believe in All the Scriptures"**

The verse names the believers' acceptance of every revelation as part of the contrast: the Muslims affirmed the scriptures that came before — including those held by the communities opposing them — while those communities rejected the message that came last. The sūrah of the Cow describes the believers as accepting God, His angels, His books, and His messengers without making distinctions among them. The point here is the imbalance the acceptance produces: a community that believed in everything is opposed by people who believe in part, and the sentence makes that disproportion part of the accusation.

**Biting the Fingertips in Rage**

The verse describes what such people do away from the believers: they bite their fingertips in rage — an idiom for fury with nowhere to go, the reaction of people whose opposition has achieved nothing. The Book records the same double speech elsewhere: such people tell the believers they believe, then tell their own associates they were only mocking (2:14). Here the reaction is put in the body rather than in words — a physical image conveying a rage visible to onlookers and useless to the one who feels it.

**The Command to Say "Die in Your Rage"**

The Prophet is instructed to tell them to die of their rage, and the commentators — al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī among them — read the phrase as naming the cause and its outcome rather than as a request for their death: their rage is produced by the success of the community they oppose, and that success is not going to stop. What the sentence tells the reader is that the intensity of an adversary's feeling is not a measure of his prospects.

**The Knowledge of What Hearts Conceal**

The sentence ends: God knows best what lies hidden in hearts — the clause returning to the theme of the previous verse. The gap between what people say and what they hold is this passage's subject, and the verse closes it with a reminder of who has access to the hidden side. The sūrah repeats the formula at several points here, and its function is consistent: the believers cannot know what is being planned against them and are not required to; what they must do is act on what has been made known to them, assured that the unknown part is not ungoverned.

**The Stance the Verse Teaches**

The passage before this one issued warnings and instructions; this verse speaks about the community's own affections, with an honesty unusual in a text concerned with conduct. The community is told it loves people who do not love it — a description of a painful situation, not a moral failing. The Book names the situation exactly so that the instruction to be careful about intimacy sits alongside the recognition that the believers' disposition was generous; both facts stand in the same passage and neither cancels the other. The Book does not ask the community to stop loving people in order to protect itself. It asks for a distinction between the affections a believer holds for others and the disclosures he makes to them: a person may wish good for those who oppose him and still decline to place his affairs in their hands — keeping good will and confidences in separate places, a harder discipline than either hostility or trust.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:120

> When you ˹believers˺ are touched with good, they grieve; but when you are afflicted with evil, they rejoice. ˹Yet,˺ if you are patient and mindful ˹of Allah˺, their schemes will not harm you in the least. Surely Allah is Fully Aware of what they do

**The Portrait of a Settled Enmity**

The verse reports that when good comes to the believers their opponents grieve, and when harm comes to them the opponents rejoice — then gives the community the condition under which such reactions will not harm it. Ibn Kathīr drew attention to what the pair reveals: someone may oppose a community out of interest or disagreement and still regret its hardship; the people described do the opposite. That another's good news produces grief is a measure of how deep the hostility runs — these are not presented as rivals but as people whose own condition is affected by the fate of a community they do not belong to.

**Moses' Plea in the Same Key**

The Book records the same sentiment in the words of Moses, who pleaded with God not to let his enemies rejoice over his misfortune and not to count him among the wrongdoers (7:150) — the scene one of distress, after his people had taken a calf in his absence. The parallel shows how the Book treats such feelings: being the object of an enemy's satisfaction is a real pain, and the Book records it without reproach. What this verse adds is the instruction that goes beyond the feeling — a community cannot prevent an opponent's reaction, and it is told what to do with it.

**The Condition: Patience and Mindfulness**

Two things neutralise the schemes: patient endurance and mindfulness of God — the two words that recur in the Book's instructions to communities under pressure, and which this sūrah has already used in its command to be mindful of God as He deserves. Patience in the Book's usage is not passive resignation; it describes holding to a course under conditions that make holding difficult — the same word used for fasting, for standing firm in battle, and for enduring harm without retaliation. The promise is attached to that kind of resolve combined with the awareness of God that keeps conduct within the Book's limits.

**What "Their Schemes Will Not Harm You" Means**

The claim recalls the passage's earlier statement that such people could inflict nothing more than annoyance (3:111) — the two statements stand at the two ends of the same argument. The opposition's plans are real, and the sūrah has described their content: turning the community from its faith, stirring old quarrels, making the path appear crooked. What the verse denies is their ability to achieve their purpose, and the denial is conditional on the believers' own conduct: the limit on the harm is in the hands of the community it threatens.

**The Setting**

The verses that follow describe the battle of Uḥud, and this passage was recited in a community that had already fought one battle and was about to face another. The reactions described are those of the community's opponents in Madinah and Makkah. The setting explains the practical tone: a community under pressure needs something it can do, and the verse gives it patience and mindfulness rather than information about its enemies' intentions. The Book's guidance in such passages is consistently about the conduct of the believer and consistently silent about the details of the plot.

**God's Awareness, and the Counsel Left**

The verse closes: God is fully aware of what they do — the formula that ends several verses in this passage, and the Book's answer to the gap between what a community can see and what is being done against it. It adds no new instruction; it changes the frame in which the instruction is carried out — the believers are told to be patient, and told that their opponents' actions are not unobserved. The Book's habit of ending warnings about opposition with this statement guards against two errors: underestimating the hostility, and treating it as a matter over which nobody has authority. The verse gives the community two things to do and one thing to know — patience and mindfulness are the actions; the awareness of God is the knowledge that makes them possible. And the next section tests the counsel: the community about to be shown a day on which it lost its footing is the one told that patience and mindfulness protect — the connection is deliberate, and the reader arrives at the battle with this verse still in hand.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:121

> ˹Remember, O Prophet,˺ when you left your home in the early morning to position the believers in the battlefield. And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**A Morning Recalled by Command**

The verse opens with a command to remember, and what is to be remembered is a specific morning: the Prophet leaving his household at daybreak to assign the believers their positions on the battlefield. The sentence preserves a piece of military history inside the revelation itself. Most commentators place the scene at the battle of Uḥud — some read it as the battle of the Trench — the reasoning for Uḥud resting on the events the sūrah describes in the verses that follow, and the details here fitting the day on which the Prophet placed his forces outside the city. The Book's purpose is instruction rather than history, and the details it records are the ones the passage will use.

**The Consultation Before the Fighting**

The historians describe a disagreement over strategy before the battle. The Prophet consulted the community: a majority advised remaining inside Madinah and using the city's fortifications; another group pressed to go out and meet the enemy. He decided to march out — and when some of those who had urged caution saw the decision, they asked him to stay; he replied that a prophet who had put on his armour should not take it off again. The scene shows the community's internal life at a moment of danger, and the sūrah's mention of the morning belongs to the same stage: the positioning of the army is presented as an act of leadership the revelation itself records.

**The Withdrawal of a Portion of the Force**

The historians also report that the leader of the hypocrites in Madinah withdrew before the fighting with a large part of the force — usually given as about three hundred of nearly a thousand — on the grounds that his advice had not been followed. The historical and tafsīr sources relate the report of ʿAbdullāh ibn Ubayy's withdrawal without a chain the sūrah's editors have examined. The withdrawal is the background to the verse's attention to positioning: a force that has already lost part of its strength is a force whose commander must place the remainder with care.

**Positioning the Believers**

The verse does not describe the arrangement, and other sources fill in the detail — the most important concerning the archers the Prophet placed on a hill to protect the army's rear. A narration from al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3039) reports that the Prophet appointed ʿAbdullāh ibn Jubayr over fifty archers and told them to hold their position — not to move even if they saw the enemy defeated and fleeing — until he sent for them. The instruction and its precise conditions became the crux of the day's events, and the sūrah's account of what follows turns on what happened on that hill.

**God as All-Hearing and All-Knowing**

The closing names frame everything the passage will describe: what was said in the consultation, what was intended in the withdrawal, and what each man carried in his heart on the march out were all heard and known. The names also answer the anxiety of the moment — a community that has just discovered part of its force has abandoned it needs to know the matter is not beyond its Lord's knowledge, and the verse places the whole day inside that knowledge before the first engagement is described.

**Why the Verse Addresses the Prophet, and Why the Morning Matters**

The command to remember is addressed to the Prophet, in the form common to passages recalling his own actions — and what is remembered is not something that happened to him but something he did. The effect is to place the community's history within the revelation: the Book does not narrate his decisions from a distance but addresses him about his own morning, and the reciting community hears its own story told to its own leader. The detail of the early departure carries the whole setting: the army left Madinah at dawn and took up positions at the foot of the mountain, the city behind it and the open ground in front — outside its own defences by its own decision, after the argument about strategy had been settled. What the revelation records is the morning the decision took effect: a leader on the ground at first light, placing his men, with the outcome unknown to everyone present. The battle ended in losses, and the sūrah treats the outcome as instruction — showing the positions assigned, the orders given, and the reasons they were not kept: a case study in the obedience that success requires.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:122

> ˹Remember˺ when two groups among you ˹believers˺ were about to cower, then Allah reassured them. So in Allah let the believers put their trust

**Two Groups Named in the Tradition**

The verse recalls a moment when two groups among the believers nearly lost their resolve, adds that God was their protector, and commands the believers to place their trust in Him. The community remembered which groups were meant: a narration from Jābir ibn ʿAbdullāh identifies them as the two Madinan tribes of Banū Ḥārithah and Banū Salimah, and adds a detail with the flavour of the first generation's way of reading the Book — Jābir said he would not have wished the verse had not been sent down, precisely because of the clause naming God as the protector of the two groups. The report is in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (4051) and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2505).

**What "About to Cower" Means**

The verb describes an inclination rather than an act: the two groups considered faltering, and the Book records the thought without describing it as a deed — keeping the accusation at the level of temptation. The Book is consistently careful about this distinction, reporting the inner intentions of people whose outward conduct did not follow, and treating the withdrawal from a resolve as different from the abandonment of a duty. What the two groups carried was a heaviness that could have turned into action; the verse names the condition without condemning the men who felt it. The Arabic term describes a slackening of resolve, the moment determination weakens before any action has been taken — the vocabulary of an inner movement rather than a visible failure.

**God as Their Protector**

The answer to the hesitation is a statement about protection, using the word for a guardian or ally — the same word the sūrah used in the account of the covenant, carrying the sense of a stronger party taking responsibility for a weaker one. The two groups did not overcome their hesitation by their own strength; the sentence attributes their steadiness to God. The protection is stated as a fact about the past, and it explains why the hesitation never became a departure — and grammatically the clause can also be read as present, a statement of what is always true of such people. Al-Rāzī noted that reading and drew from it a sign of mercy: God is described as the protector of the two groups even as the verse reports the weakness they were struggling with. On either reading the danger came from within and the remedy from outside.

**The Command to Trust**

The verse ends by commanding the believers to put their trust in God — attached to the recollection rather than offered as a general principle. The Book's word for trust describes reliance on another after taking whatever steps are available, and the passage contains both elements: the Prophet had positioned his forces and given his orders, and the community had done what it could; what remained was the part beyond its control — the point at which the instruction applies. Trust is taught as the companion of preparation, not a substitute for it.

**The Book's Honesty About Its Community**

The verse records a moment of weakness among the believers, and the Book records such moments repeatedly — the community's fear at Badr, its hesitation before the battle of the Trench, its afflictions in between — never presenting the first generation as a body of people who were never afraid. The honesty serves the sūrah's purpose: a later reader who finds himself afraid is told that the people whose example he is meant to follow also found themselves afraid, and that what distinguished them was the outcome rather than the absence of the feeling. That is why Jābir's remark is so telling: the community valued the record of its own wavering, because the verse attached God's protection to it. What the verse honours is not the absence of fear but the refusal to be governed by it — and the protection attributed to God is the reason the refusal was possible.

**Placement Before the Test**

This is the second time in the passage the believers are told to rely on God, and the repetition is deliberate: the sūrah is preparing its readers for an account of a battle that went wrong. The instruction comes before the account, so the reader encounters the failures of the day with the verse about trust already in hand — taught the habit in advance of the test. The next verse supplies the reminder of a victory the same community had already been given.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:123

> Indeed, Allah made you victorious at Badr when you were ˹vastly˺ outnumbered. So be mindful of Allah, perhaps you will be grateful

**A Victory Recalled Before a Trial**

The verse reminds the believers that God gave them victory at Badr when they were few, and tells them to be mindful of God so that they may be grateful. The reminder stands between the recollection of the march to Uḥud and the promises of angelic aid that follow — and the position is the point: the sūrah is about to describe a day of loss, and it first recalls a day of victory in which the same community was vastly outnumbered. A reader who holds the two together learns that neither outcome was determined by numbers, and the instruction at the end of the verse tells him what does determine it.

**The Disproportion at Badr**

The sūrah has already described the two armies at Badr, stating that the believers saw their enemy at twice their number (3:13). The report of the Prophet's supplication that day, recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (1763), describes him calling on God with the words that if the small band with him were destroyed, none would remain to worship God on the earth. The community's later memory placed its own numbers at three hundred and thirteen against roughly a thousand of the enemy; the verse's phrase about their being few matches that memory, and its use of the plural verb addresses the whole community rather than the Prophet alone. What happened at Badr is presented as something the believers did and something God gave.

**The Victory Attributed to the Giver**

The verse's construction places God as the agent and the community as the recipients. Attribution in the Book is not a denial of the community's part — the believers marched, fought, and held their ground, and the Book says so elsewhere. What the sentence refuses is the inference that their own effort accounted for the outcome, and its few words make the point in the plainest possible way.

**The Pivot to Mindfulness**

The second half turns the recollection into an instruction: be mindful of God, so that you may be grateful. The connection is the verse's own logic — a person who lives with awareness of God recognises where his successes come from. The same pairing occurred in the sūrah's command to be mindful of God as He deserves (3:102), and in the passages tying God's help to the community's state. The Book treats gratitude as a practice rather than a feeling that occurs on its own, and attaches the practice to the awareness that produces it: the word for gratitude carries the sense of acknowledging a benefit, and its opposite is the refusal to recognise what has been received. The community's future conduct is made the measure of its gratitude — not an emotion at the memory of a victory but a life shaped by the awareness that the victory was given. Hence the command to be mindful comes first: the awareness is the root, and the gratitude is its fruit.

**Why the Earlier Victory Is Named**

The sūrah's use of Badr here is not nostalgia; it is the provision of a case the community could check against its own experience. The men addressed had been at Badr, many had been at Uḥud as well, and the two events were the material from which the sūrah's lessons are drawn. A community that has won against the odds and then lost when the conditions were met differently is in a position to understand a lesson about obedience and help that no abstract instruction could supply. And the sūrah refers to Badr twice: first as a sign, with the armies seen at double strength (3:13), and here as a reminder of help already given. The two uses serve different purposes — one belongs to the opening argument about the fate of those who reject the message; the other to the passage preparing the community for another battle. The Book's habit of returning to a single event with a new application shows how its history functions: the same fact can be evidence in one argument and encouragement in another.

**The Verse's Role**

What follows — the promise of angelic support, the divine purpose behind the alternation of days, the testing of the community — all rests on the premise this verse supplies. The reader is being taught to read his own history as the Book reads the community's: defeats examined for their causes, victories attributed to their giver, both used to establish a way of living. The verse's brevity is what allows it to serve as the hinge between the recollection of the march and the promises that follow.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:124

> ˹Remember, O Prophet,˺ when you said to the believers, "Is it not enough that your Lord will send down a reinforcement of three thousand angels for your aid?"

**Words Spoken to Steady a Community**

The verse recalls the Prophet's address to the believers, asking whether it was not enough that their Lord would send down three thousand angels to their aid — a question put to people facing a force larger than their own. The form is reassurance rather than announcement: the Prophet is not reporting intelligence about the enemy but reminding his community of the resources belonging to the Lord they serve. By preserving the words in the revelation, the Book keeps the community's encouragement available to every later generation, who face their own versions of the same anxiety. The verse also shows the Prophet's method in a crisis: he told the people what their Lord had undertaken to do, and left the conduct of the battle to them.

**The Form of the Question**

The question begins "is it not enough" — the Book's construction for addressing a doubt that has not been spoken. The community had been told the size of the force it must face, and the question supplies the answer to the unspoken fear. The rhetorical question is a gentler instruction than a command: it does not accuse the believers of anything; it invites them to consider the adequacy of what has been promised, with the answer supplied by the hearer himself. That the verse records the question rather than the answer suggests what the community's response was understood to be.

**Why Three Thousand**

The Book does not explain the number; its significance lies in the contrast with the community's own strength. The sūrah's other account of angelic support at Badr states that the believers cried out to their Lord for help and were answered with the promise of a thousand angels sent in succession (8:9) — a promise to a force of three hundred and thirteen. Here a promise of three thousand is given to a community that had recently been through defeat. The escalation matches the situation: the number is not a calculation of what a battle requires but evidence of the generosity of the One who gives, stated in the register the Book keeps throughout — the magnitude of the promised help answering the smallness of the community that receives it.

**Badr or Uḥud?**

Al-Rāzī set out the disagreement over when these words were spoken: if the promise belongs to Badr, the sentence joins the passage recalling the community's weakness before the fighting and the help that followed; if to Uḥud, the account runs on from the verse describing the Prophet leaving his household on the morning of the battle. The commentators divided over how the angels' help was understood as well. Most held that the angels fought at Badr, where the Book's own account describes the rain, the sleep that came over the believers, and the hosts that were not seen (8:9–12); some reports describe the participation in physical terms, with strange deaths in the enemy ranks and unknown fighters on the field. Others read the help as the strengthening of the believers' hearts, the sharpening of their senses, and the courage their belief in the promise gave them — al-Rāzī's treatment of the question. On the accepted view the angels did not fight at Uḥud; their mention there is understood as moral support, and al-Ṭabarī notes that God does not need angels to help the believers. If the promise is given for the sake of the hearts, the question of how the angels participated is secondary to what the announcement produced in the community that heard it.

**What the Aid Means in the Book's Framework**

The mention of angels in a battle context must be read with the Book's own statements about the source of victory, stated in the verses immediately following: the angels are described as sent for the sake of the believers' hearts, and the outcome is credited to God alone. The two statements protect each other — a community told about the aid could begin to expect victory without effort; a community told only about its duty could forget that the outcome is not its own to determine. The verse shows the Prophet performing the task the Book assigns to leaders in difficulty: keeping the community's resolve intact while the danger is stated openly. The next verse gives the same promise its condition.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:125

> Most certainly, if you ˹believers˺ are firm and mindful ˹of Allah˺ and the enemy launches a sudden attack on you, Allah will reinforce you with five thousand angels designated ˹for battle˺

**A Promise With Its Condition Attached**

The verse states that if the believers hold firm and remain mindful of God, and the enemy attacks suddenly, God will reinforce them with five thousand angels bearing distinguishing marks. The condition is stated before the promise, and the order is the verse's teaching. The condition has two parts — patient firmness and awareness of God — both already asked of the community in this passage, and both belonging to the believers' own conduct rather than to anything knowable about the enemy.
