What the verse does is attach the promised help to a state within the believers' control. The repetition is the passage's method of teaching: a quality needed in a moment of sudden danger has to be habitual beforehand, and the Book states it at every point in the narrative where the need appears — the command to be mindful as He deserves (3:102), the promise that patience and mindfulness prevent harm (3:120), and now the condition on the aid itself. By the time the reader reaches the battle, he has been told three times what the community needed to bring to it.

**The Increase in the Number**

The previous verse recalled a promise of three thousand angels; this one speaks of five thousand. The increase answers the added condition and the situation described — an assault launched without warning. An attack that can be seen coming can be prepared for; a surprise attack removes the time for preparation and consultation. The scenario makes the promise applicable to the moment when a community has nothing left but its own state — and purity of intention and firmness of purpose cannot be improvised. The Book attaches its instructions to that moment because it is the moment that reveals what a person carries.

**"Marked" Angels**

The word describing the angels comes from a root meaning to mark or distinguish, and the commentators explained it in two connected ways: some took it as angels bearing signs by which they were known; others mentioned horses with distinctive markings, the word being used of animals marked for identification. Both explanations agree on the practical sense: the promised force is described as prepared and identifiable rather than an indistinct multitude. The Book's account of the earlier promise speaks of angels sent in succession, and the two descriptions together convey a body of aid arriving in order. The precision of the language is part of the encouragement — a vague promise of help does very little for someone facing a surprise attack.

**Whether the Promise Was Fulfilled**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī record the question whether the promise of five thousand was ever carried out, since the previous sentence had tied the reinforcement to the community's firmness and awareness of God, and some held the conditions were not met and the reinforcement was never sent. The discussion shows how carefully the commentators treated the details, and it leaves the reader with the condition the verse attached rather than an account of what appeared in the sky. The sūrah's own next verses settle the purpose of the whole arrangement.

**What the Promised Aid Stands For**

The descriptions of angelic support in this passage are not given as military intelligence but so that a community facing a larger force understands the scale of the power standing behind the promise. That is why the numbers grow as the conditions become harder: the message is not that five thousand is a sufficient force for a particular engagement, but that the resources available to God are beyond anything the community could estimate. And the angels are agents, not replacements — the Book's accounts of such aid consistently place the outcome's source in God and present the aid as encouragement for the believers' hearts, as the next verse states explicitly. The promises sit inside a narrative whose ending the community already knows — a battle that ended in losses — and promised help that does not prevent a defeat tells the reader that the help was real and the conditions unmet. The reader is being prepared to examine the community's conduct rather than the balance of forces.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:126

> Allah ordained this ˹reinforcement˺ only as good news for you and reassurance for your hearts. And victory comes only from Allah—the Almighty, All-Wise—

**The Purpose Behind the Promise**

The verse states that the reinforcement was intended as good news and as reassurance for the believers' hearts, and ends by attributing victory to God alone. The sentence explains why the promise of angels was given rather than describing what the angels did. The same explanation appears in the other account of the battle, where the aid is called a sign of victory and a reassurance, followed by the statement that help comes only from God (8:10). The Book's consistent position is that the language of aid addresses the inner state of the people who receive it.

**Good News as a Category**

The Book uses the vocabulary of good tidings for the messages messengers bring and for the promises attached to faith; here the term applies to the announcement of support, and the announcement itself is described as done for the believers' sake. That framing keeps the promise from being a piece of information about the unseen: what the community receives is not a report to be verified but an encouragement to be acted upon. A community told that help is coming is expected to be steadied by the telling — which is the purpose named in the verse.

**Reassurance for the Hearts**

The phrase used for the promise's purpose is about the settling of the heart, a concern characteristic of this sūrah: its early verses described hearts that swerve and hearts that are kept, and its prayer for the community is that their hearts not be made to deviate after being guided. The promised reinforcement belongs to the same concern. What the community needed on the day was not merely a favourable balance of forces but the capacity to stand, and the Book locates that capacity in the heart — treating the promise as a means of producing an inner condition, and the inner condition as the thing that matters most.

**Victory From God Alone**

The clause that victory belongs to God alone is the sentence's centre. The Book states it elsewhere in its most direct form: if God helps the believers, none can defeat them; if He withholds help, nobody else can supply it (3:160). The Book's accounts of battles are written so the attribution is never in doubt: the community marched and fought, and the outcome is credited to God — the two facts stated side by side without embarrassment. What the verse denies is the inference that the means produce the result, and the denial comes immediately after the means are named.

**The Two Names at the End**

The closing names — the Almighty, the All-Wise — do specific work in a context of difficulty: the first establishes that the power to act is present; the second that the timing and manner of the action are governed by knowledge rather than the pressure of events. A community facing a stronger enemy needs both facts: power alone would leave it wondering whether God will use it on its behalf; wisdom alone whether anything can be done. The verse supplies the two together, and the sentence that follows states the outcome the wisdom intends.

**Means and Outcomes**

The verse distinguishes between the means by which a community is strengthened and the power that determines the result — a practical distinction. A community that confuses the two will either neglect the means, expecting help without effort, or rely on the means, expecting the result from its own preparation. The Book's instructions keep both in view, and this verse states the relationship in a single sentence: the community's reason for confidence is located neither in its own strength nor in the promised aid, but in the One who gives both. The passage's lesson is thus complete before the narrative continues — the aid was for the sake of the community's hearts, and the outcome of the fighting was never in the community's hands.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:127

> to destroy a group of the disbelievers and humble the rest, causing them to withdraw in disappointment

**A Purpose Stated in Verbs**

The verse sets out what the arrangement of aid was meant to achieve: the cutting off of a faction among the disbelievers, the humiliation of the rest, and their withdrawal in disappointment. Some commentators connected the purpose with Badr, where a portion of the enemy's leadership was destroyed; others with Uḥud, where the enemy failed to achieve what it came for. Al-Ṭabarī recorded both positions, and the difference turns on which battle the promises of aid are understood to accompany. Either way the sentence names the results the fighting produced and attributes their arrangement to God rather than to the two armies — and what the community is meant to take is that the outcome served a purpose it could not see while the day was unfolding.

**"Cutting Off" a Faction**

The verb describes severing a part from a whole — the Book uses the same root for cutting off a thing's continuation — and the commentators applied it to the leaders and prominent fighters who were killed. The choice of word is precise: it does not say the enemy was annihilated; it says a portion was severed, which is what a battle does to a force that suffers heavy losses among its commanders. The Book describes the effect in the terms of the battlefield rather than of total destruction.

**The Humiliation of the Rest**

The second outcome is humiliation — the Book's vocabulary for a defeat that does not destroy. A humbled force is one made to see the limit of its power, and the verse places that outcome alongside the cutting off of a part. The pairing suggests two different ends for two different groups: some are removed from the field altogether, and others are left to return with the knowledge of what happened. The verse's final clause describes the withdrawal with the word for being turned back without achieving the aim — a retreat that is also a failure, which the commentators explained as the community's enemies going home without the victory they had come for. The description fits Uḥud exactly: the opposing force was able to inflict losses but not to take the city or destroy the community. The Book describes the day from the standpoint of what the enemy failed to obtain.

**The Historical Shape of the Two Battles**

The two battles the sūrah describes ended in opposite ways on the surface: at Badr the believers, though few, broke the opposing force and killed a number of its leaders; at Uḥud the believers suffered casualties including prominent Companions, and the enemy withdrew without pressing its advantage. The verse's two clauses fit both events — a part of the enemy's strength was cut off at Badr, and the enemy went home disappointed after Uḥud. The Book describes the same purpose across two days that felt very different to the community that lived through them, and that is part of what the passage is teaching: the sūrah never presents the losses at Uḥud as a reversal of the promise recorded before them, but as part of the same arrangement, in which the community's own conduct and the divine purpose both had their place.

**Purpose Stated After the Promise**

The order of the verses is deliberate: the promise of aid first, then its purpose for the believers' hearts, and only then the effect on the enemy. The community's own condition is placed before the fortunes of its opponents — what matters first is the state of the people being addressed, and the outcome of the conflict is described as a consequence of the divine purpose rather than as the subject of the passage. The Book states the purposes of events so that its readers understand history as intended rather than random — without removing human action from the account: the enemy came with its own purpose and bore the consequences; the community prepared, fought, and suffered; what the verse adds is the dimension the participants could not see. The verses that follow turn from the promises and their purposes to the Prophet's own position in the matter, and then to the account of the testing — the purpose clause here being the bridge between what the aid was for and the events the account is about to explain.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:128

> You ˹O Prophet˺ have no say in the matter. It is up to Allah to turn to them in mercy or punish them, for indeed they are wrongdoers

**A Limit Placed on the Messenger**

The verse tells the Prophet that he has no share in the decision: the matter belongs to God, who may relent toward the people in question or punish them, since they are wrongdoers. The sentence defines the boundary of the messenger's role at a moment when his feelings were most engaged. The Book states the same limit elsewhere in different words — his duty is to convey, and guidance is not his to give — and this verse adds the specific situation of a messenger harmed by the people he is calling, told that the outcome of their case is not his to decide.

**The Occasion Reported in the Commentaries**

The commentaries connect the verse with the day of Uḥud: al-Ṭabarī records the report that the Prophet was wounded, one of his front teeth chipped or broken, his face bleeding from the fighting — and that he said, as he bled, how could a people prosper who injured their prophet while he was calling them to their Lord, with this verse then sent down. (The reports of the wounding and the words are related by the commentaries without a chain the sūrah's editors have examined.) Another account connects the verse with supplications the Prophet made in prayer against certain opponents, reporting that he stopped after it was revealed; and Ibn Kathīr notes that some of those named in that account later accepted the message — which is the point the verse makes: the outcome for any person is open until the end. The two versions differ in the details of the occasion rather than the content of the verse.

**Mercy and Punishment Reserved to God**

The sentence names two possibilities and places both in God's hands: the people described had done harm to the community and the messenger, and the response may be either forgiveness or punishment, with the decision resting where it belongs. The Book holds this position consistently: God's mercy encompasses everything, while punishment falls where He wills (7:156); and those who have gone to extremes against themselves are commanded not to despair of God's mercy, for He forgives all sins (39:53). This verse applies the same framework to a specific group.

**The Prophet's Conduct After the Verse**

The reports about the Prophet's prayers are the practical side of the verse: a messenger told the matter is not his to decide no longer asks for a particular outcome against individuals, and the tradition records the change in his practice. The instruction does not forbid feeling — the Qur'an records his distress elsewhere and never presents him as indifferent to harm he suffered. What the verse does is separate the feeling from the authority to settle the case, and the separation allowed him to continue calling the same people to the same message.

**Who the Wrongdoers Are**

The verse ends by naming the people wrongdoers — the Book's term for those who have injured a right, whether it belongs to God or to people. The description is stated as the reason the matter requires a decision rather than an immediate outcome, and it leaves the possibility of return open: a wrongdoer can cease to be one, and the Book's accounts of these very opponents include people who later accepted the message and became part of the community. The verse's placement at the end of the battle passage is a reminder that the account of the conflict is not the account of its ending.

**What the Verse Teaches About Authority**

The instruction to the messenger establishes a principle for everyone who carries his message: the one who conveys is not the one who judges. A person certain of the truth and harmed by those who reject it will want the matter settled in his own favour, and the verse removes the settlement from his hands — what remains is the work of conveying, which is the part he was given. The Book's teaching on the point is a set of parallel passages: the Prophet is not responsible for the guidance of the people, for God guides whom He wills (2:272); if they turn away, his duty is only to convey (3:20); and he will not guide whom he loves, since guidance is God's to give (28:56). Together they mark the boundary of a messenger's work: he delivers the message and cares about the outcome, and the outcome is not in his hands — a point made sharper by the verse's arrival at the moment when the Prophet's grief was greatest.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:129

> To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. He forgives whoever He wills, and punishes whoever He wills. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Ownership Stated Before the Decision**

The verse states that everything in the heavens and the earth belongs to God, then that He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills, closing with the two names of forgiveness and mercy. The order places the right to decide inside the fact of ownership. The same opening formula appeared earlier in this sūrah at the close of the discussion of two outcomes, and its return is deliberate: the sentence removes the case from the hands of every party and places it with the Owner of everything — framing the promises of aid, the battle, and the verdict on its participants between two statements about who owns what exists and who decides what happens to it.

**Why the Decision Belongs to the Owner**

A sentence about forgiveness and punishment would raise the question of authority if it stood alone; the verse answers the question before it arises by naming God as the owner of all that exists. If a person owns nothing, no person has a claim against another that could bind the One who owns both. The Book uses the point in its disputes with communities that claimed special standing, and here to settle a matter about which the messenger himself was told he had no say.

**The Two Verbs and Their Conditions**

The verse names forgiveness and punishment as what God does as He wills, with His will mentioned before either — a formulation that has occupied the commentators because it appears to place the decision beyond conditions. The Book's other statements supply the conditions elsewhere: it states that God does not forgive the association of others with Him while forgiving anything less for whoever He wills, and it commands those who have sinned not to despair of His mercy. The sentences are complementary: one establishes that the decision belongs to God alone; the others describe how it is made. This verse states the principle in its most general form, which is why it can stand alongside passages describing the outcome for particular kinds of people without contradiction.

**The Names That Close the Verse**

The sentence ends with the names Forgiving and Merciful, and their presence after the mention of punishment is the verse's quiet summary of its own balance. The Book habitually closes statements about judgment with these names, and the effect is not to soften the warning but to state the disposition from which the whole arrangement proceeds: a reader told the case is not in his hands still needs to know toward what the One who holds it is inclined, and the two names supply the answer.

**Neither Despairing nor Presuming**

The verse occupies the middle ground between two errors, and the Book addresses both elsewhere: it forbids those who have wronged themselves to despair of God's mercy, telling them He forgives all sins (39:53); and it warns those who presume on forgiveness while persisting in their conduct. The verse states the fact on which both warnings rest — the decision is God's. A person who understands the sentence will not conclude his outcome is fixed by anything in his own control, nor that it is arbitrary: he is given the knowledge that the matter rests with a Forgiver who is also a Punisher, and that both descriptions are true.

**What the Sentence Does for the Passage**

The preceding passage dealt with a battle and the fate of its opposing parties; this sentence takes that material out of the community's hands and places it with God — the necessary conclusion: a community that had suffered losses, and been told its enemies' outcome would disappoint them, needed the reminder that the final settlement belongs elsewhere. For an individual reader, the verse states where the account will be settled and who holds it — the Book's teaching about repentance is not contradicted by the sentence but grounded in it. The practical result is the removal of two things: the burden of judging others, which is not a person's to carry, and the illusion of controlling one's outcome by anything other than the conduct the Book describes. What remains is the conduct — and the verses that follow turn from the statement about God to instructions about money, patience, and forgiveness, so that the commands are received as something other than arbitrary rules: a community told who owns everything and who decides every case is in a position to hear them in that light.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:130

> O believers! Do not consume interest, multiplying it many times over. And be mindful of Allah, so you may prosper

**A Command About Money in a Passage About War**

The verse forbids the believers to take interest in multiplied form and closes with the command to be mindful of God so that they may prosper — and it follows a passage about battles, aid, and the fate of opponents. The placement is characteristic of the sūrah: having described matters of life and death, it turns to transactions, and the turn makes the point that the community's conduct in the market is part of the same religion as its conduct in the field. The juxtaposition is deliberate — integrity in dealings belongs to the same religion as courage in battle.

**What the Term Covers**

The Arabic word for interest describes an addition charged on a loan, and the Book's treatment of the practice is worked out at length in the sūrah of the Cow: God has permitted trade and forbidden interest (2:275); the believers are commanded to give up what remains outstanding of it; and those who persist are warned that they are at war with God and His Messenger (2:278–279) — language of a seriousness unmatched in the Book's treatment of economic matters. This verse supplies the command in the context of the community's life, with the framework already established.

**"Multiplying It Many Times Over"**

The phrase describes a debt that grows by doubling when it is not repaid — a practice that left borrowers in a position they could not escape. The commentators discussed whether the qualifier limits the prohibition to the compounded form or describes the practice as it was commonly known. Most classical scholars held the second: the prohibition covers any guaranteed addition for the mere passage of time, and the Book's later verses in the sūrah of the Cow state the rule in general terms — the description here records a shape the practice took rather than a boundary of the prohibition.

**Interest and Charity Set Against Each Other**

The Book makes the prohibition a matter of principle rather than amounts: whatever is lent seeking interest does not increase with God, while whatever is given as charity seeking His pleasure multiplies its reward (30:39); and directly — God makes interest fruitless and makes charity fruitful (2:276). The pair treats the two uses of wealth as producing opposite results, measured not in the world's terms but in the accounting the sūrah has been describing throughout.

**"So That You May Prosper"**

The closing clause attaches the command to the word for success — the same word that opens the call to prayer and that the sūrah used of the group calling others to good. The Book's promise of prosperity is not attached to accumulation. The choice of word answers the appeal of the practice it forbids: a person takes interest because it looks like gain, and the sentence tells him that gain is not the success the Book means.

**Why the Sūrah Raises It Here**

What follows this verse continues with commands about spending, patience, and the pursuit of forgiveness — the conduct of a community just come through a testing. The connection between the battle narrative and the rules about money is not incidental: the community's ability to function depended on its internal dealings, and a practice concentrating wealth in lenders' hands at debtors' expense would have divided the very community the sūrah has been trying to hold together. The command belongs to the same project as the commands about unity and patience. The prohibition's effect on the early community was substantial, since credit arrangements of this kind were common in the region; the Book's method was to state the rule, describe the two alternatives, and leave the community to organise its affairs — with the closing command to be mindful of God placing the whole matter under the awareness the sūrah has been asking of its readers throughout.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:131

> Guard yourselves against the Fire prepared for the disbelievers

**A Command to Guard Oneself**

The verse commands the believers to guard themselves against the Fire, using the word the Book employs throughout for the awareness that keeps a person within limits — carrying the senses of fear, care, and standing watch. The same root provides the quality named in the following verse as the condition for the Garden, so the command and the promise use a single vocabulary: the safeguard recommended is the awareness of God, and the reward is attached to the same awareness. What the verse adds is the object of the guarding — a specific destination — and the verb's form makes the guarding an act the believer performs, not one performed on his behalf.

**The Fire Described as Prepared**

The sentence states that the Fire has been prepared and names those for whom the preparation is made. The Book uses the same image earlier: a blaze fuelled by people and stones, prepared for the disbelievers (2:24); and it addresses the believers directly in another passage, telling them to protect themselves and their families from a fire whose fuel is people and stones, over which are appointed angels who are severe and powerful, who do not disobey God in what He commands them, and who do what they are ordered (66:6). The image of preparation suggests something made ready in advance — the Book's way of removing any sense that the destination is a metaphor for consequences in general.

**Prepared for the Disbelievers, Addressed to the Believers**

The apparent tension — a warning about the disbelievers issued to the believers — is explained by the sūrah's method: the community is told what the destination of a certain course of conduct is, and told in time to avoid taking it. The warning stands between two instructions, the prohibition of interest before it and the command to obey God and the Messenger after it, where the Book usually places such warnings — and the community reading it is given the choice the passage is designed to present.

**Fuel, and What Guarding Consists Of**

The Book describes the Fire through its fuel in several passages, and the descriptions are concrete rather than informational: they are moral images. What fuels a fire is what is put into it — the destination is fed by the conduct that leads to it. And the command to guard specifies no technique; the surrounding verses supply the content: giving what a person values, calling others to what is good, holding together as a community, obeying God and His Messenger. The warning is not a separate instruction to be carried out on its own but a reminder of the stakes attached to the instructions around it.

**The Verse at the Turn**

The verse stands where the sūrah turns from the battle narrative and its promises toward a series of moral instructions about wealth and conduct, and its placement gives those instructions their frame: a community told about the fate of its enemies and the losses of its own day is now told what kind of conduct leads where. The warning is short because the material around it is not — the verses that follow describe the character of the people for whom the Garden is prepared, and this sentence provides the contrast against which that description is read.

**A Warning the Community Recites About Itself**

The verse has been recited by Muslims in every generation as part of the sūrah, which means it has always functioned as a warning addressed to the community that reads it, not as information about someone else: the distinction between the two destinations is not fixed by the label a person carries. The Book describes the Fire in plain terms and does not present it as a figure of speech — it names its fuel, its preparation, and its guardians, placing the descriptions in passages addressed to the believers as often as to anyone else, and the tradition follows the Book in repeating the warnings. Nothing in the passage suggests the warning is meant to drive people to despair; the verses that follow immediately describe the alternative and the character of those who take it, and the two destinations are set out with the same directness.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:132

> Obey Allah and the Messenger, so you may be shown mercy

**Two Obediences in One Command**

The verse commands obedience to God and to the Messenger, attaching a promise of mercy to the command — one of the Book's most frequent constructions. The logic of the pairing is that the messenger conveys what God has commanded, so obedience to the message is obedience to the sender; the Book states elsewhere that whoever obeys the messenger has obeyed God. The sūrah gives the connection from the other side as well, telling the Prophet to say that those who love God should follow him, and that God will then love them and forgive their sins (3:31). The two verses describe a single relationship with two faces: love toward God, obedience toward the one He sent.

**What Obedience Covers**

The command to obey the Messenger appears throughout the Book in a variety of contexts — settling disputes, submitting to judgments, participating in the community's undertakings — and is not restricted to a single area of life. The battle narrative in this sūrah supplies the illustration of what disobedience costs: the archers who left their position contrary to their orders left the army's rear exposed, and the losses of the day followed. The Book records the episode because it demonstrates in a single day what this verse states in principle, and the community that lived through the events understood the connection. The sūrah also describes the structure obedience serves: the Prophet is told to consult his companions in matters, and once he has decided, to put his trust in God (3:159) — consultation, decision, and an obedience that no longer reopens the question. The community's health depended on that structure, and the losses of Uḥud were the price of the break in it.

**"So That You May Be Shown Mercy"**

The promise attached to the command is mercy, a word this sūrah uses at points where the community's failures are being addressed. The choice of terms is significant: a command accompanied by a promise of wages invites calculation; a command accompanied by the hope of mercy places the result in God's hands. The Book uses the second form when addressing communities whose record includes failures — the situation of this community throughout the passage. And the aim stated at the end is not victory or standing but God's mercy: the Book teaches elsewhere, in a form leaving no doubt, that God does not need the believers' obedience and that they are the ones in need. The promise of mercy here is the same idea in a softer key.

**Between Warning and Promise**

The verse stands between the command to guard against the Fire and the command to hasten toward forgiveness and the Garden — the Book's standard sequence of danger, instruction, and hope. The reader is shown the danger, told what to do about it, and shown the destination of those who comply. This verse contributes the command, and its brevity is deliberate: the community has already been given the material and the reminder; what remains is the response. The instruction is short, the promise attached to it is large, and the verses that follow describe the character of those who receive it.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:133

> And hasten towards forgiveness from your Lord and a Paradise as vast as the heavens and the earth, prepared for those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**A Command to Hasten**

The verse tells the believers to move quickly toward forgiveness from their Lord and toward a Garden described as wide as the heavens and the earth, prepared for those mindful of God — the verb being the one the Book employs for racing. The same command appears in almost the same form in another sūrah, where the believers are told to vie with one another for forgiveness from their Lord and for a Garden as vast as the heavens and the earth, prepared for those who believe in God and His messengers (57:21). The Book's repetition of the instruction shows how it treats the pursuit of the Hereafter: a task requiring speed, not a conclusion reachable at leisure.

**Forgiveness Stated Before the Garden**

The order of the two objects is significant: forgiveness is named before the Garden, and what the race reaches first is the pardon that opens the way to the reward. The Book's descriptions of the Hereafter consistently treat forgiveness as the entry and the Garden as what follows — the sūrah has already named God forgiving and merciful, and the passage will shortly describe repentance in detail. The order here is the frame for that description.

**A Garden as Wide as the Heavens and the Earth**

The description conveys magnitude without giving measurements, and the commentators discussed how to take it — some as a literal description of the Garden's extent, others as an image of abundance. The wider point is that the place is proportioned to the generosity of the One who gives it: the Book nowhere describes the Garden as a reward balancing the deeds performed for it; it describes it as vast, and attaches its preparation to the quality of the people rather than the quantity of their works. The commentaries record a question about the verse put by an emissary of the Byzantine emperor — where the Fire could be if the Garden is as wide as the heavens and the earth — and an answer attributed to the Prophet: where does the night go when the day comes? The commentaries relate the report without a chain the sūrah's editors have examined; its point, wherever it is placed, is that the two destinations do not divide a shared space.

**Prepared for the Mindful**

The Garden is prepared for those mindful of God — the same word used in the previous verse for guarding against the Fire. The Book thus describes both destinations with reference to the same quality, and the passage then explains the quality by describing the behaviour of the people who have it: giving in ease and hardship, restraining anger, pardoning, remembering God after a fault. This verse states the category; the verses that follow give the content.

**Hope Placed After Warning**

The verses around this one move from the warning about the Fire, through the instruction to obey, to this command to seek forgiveness — the sequence giving the passage its balance: the danger stated, the response described, the destination placed in view. The arrangement reflects the Book's treatment of its own warnings: a threat without an alternative produces despair; an alternative without the statement of the danger produces complacency. The passage supplies both in consecutive sentences, and the community reading it is expected to hold both in view at once.

**The Urgency of the Verb**

Hastening implies that the opportunity is not permanent — the Book's language of urgency in such passages is directed at people who have the capacity to act and may not always have it. It tells people to spend from what they have been given before death arrives, and warns against the moment when a person asks for a delay that will not be granted. What the verse asks for is not an emotional state but a course of conduct begun without delay, and the urgency is a practical judgement about the shortness of the interval in which the pursuit is possible: the Book treats delay as a decision like any other, and the verb places that decision in front of the reader at the moment he hears it. The reader who reaches the end of the passage has been given the destination, its extent, the condition for entering it, and a description of the people who satisfy the condition in their daily lives.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:134

> ˹They are˺ those who donate in prosperity and adversity, control their anger, and pardon others. And Allah loves the good-doers

**The Portrait of the People Described**

The verse continues the description of the people for whom the Garden is prepared, giving three practices: giving in ease and hardship, restraining anger, and pardoning others — and ends by stating that God loves those who do good. The three items cover the three situations in which character is tested: the first concerns wealth, the second provocation, and the third power over someone who has done harm. Each is a place where most people fail in a predictable way, and the verse names the practice that constitutes success in each.

**"In Ease and in Hardship"**

The pair received two explanations. Al-Ṭabarī read the terms as describing circumstances: the verse praises the person who gives both in a time of plenty and when there is little to give. Al-Rāzī recorded a second reading in which the pairing describes the giver's own state rather than his means: whether content or distressed, whether the giving pleases him or costs him something, he still spends. The second is the more demanding reading, and the Book's other descriptions support it — the people praised earlier in this sūrah are described as charitable without qualification (3:17), and the Book elsewhere praises those who give out of what they value while warning against giving only the worthless portion. The two readings cover the same ground: what is asked for is not generosity in one mood but a practice that survives the change of circumstances.

**Restraining Anger**

The word used here for anger describes the internal movement of resentment, and one commentator distinguished it from the term for anger expressed in words or actions — the first being the feeling, the second the feeling made visible. The distinction matters for what the verse praises: the people described are not said to be free of the feeling; they are said to hold it back. The Book's standard is that a person governs what he does with what he feels, and the verse places that government among the marks of those for whom the Garden is prepared.

**A Report About Strength**

The Prophet's definition of strength takes up the same theme. A narration from Abū Hurayrah reports him saying that the strong person is not the one who overpowers others, but the one who controls himself while in a state of anger (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6114; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2609). The report gives the verse's instruction the weight of a redefinition: a community that measured strength by the ability to dominate is told that the quality it admires belongs to the person who governs himself.

**Pardoning Those Within One's Power**

The last practice is pardoning, and the commentators noted that the term applies most naturally to those over whom a person has authority: it is easy to forgive someone who cannot be called to account; the verse praises the forgiveness offered by someone who could have taken his due. The Book states the same ethic elsewhere — the righteous are those who, when angered, forgive (42:37); whoever pardons and seeks reconciliation will have his reward with God (42:40); and the believers are told to repel evil with what is better, with the result that the person between whom and them there was enmity may become a close friend (41:34).

**"Allah Loves the Good-Doers"**

The closing sentence is the highest form of praise the Book offers, and its placement at the end of a list of difficult practices is deliberate: the community is told the behaviour described is not merely advisable but beloved, and the sentence supplies the motivation the preceding items require. The Book attaches that love to particular qualities in different passages; here it is attached to the combination the verse has just described.

**Three Tests, One Community**

Read together with the previous verse, the sentence supplies the content of the phrase "those mindful of God": the people for whom the Garden is prepared are those whose dealings with money, provocation, and power have a particular shape — that is how the sūrah defines its terms, showing the conduct of the people who possess it rather than leaving the quality an abstraction. And the three tests belong together for a reason: a community in which resources are shared in hard times, tempers are governed, and offences are pardoned is a community that can survive its internal strains — a concern the sūrah has carried since its commands about unity. The description is of a functioning community as much as of individuals, and the next verse completes the portrait by describing how such people handle their own failures.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:135

> ˹They are˺ those who, upon committing an evil deed or wronging themselves, remember Allah and seek forgiveness and do not knowingly persist in sin—and who forgives sins except Allah

**How Such People Handle Failure**

The verse completes the portrait of the people described in the two verses before it, and it deals with the one thing the earlier description left out: what happens when they do wrong. The answer comes in a sequence — an act, a remembering, a request, and a refusal to continue — the third being the step that defines the state. The Book presents repentance in this practical shape rather than as an emotional state, and the sentence is one of its most complete statements of what the return to God involves.

**The Two Ways of Doing Wrong**

The verse names two things that can be committed, and the commentators distinguished them: the first term covers a shameful and serious deed; the second is broader, covering any wrong a person does — including the ones that seem small — described as an injury done to oneself. The distinction removes the excuse available to a person who regards his offence as minor: whether the wrong is grave or slight, the verse describes what the people for whom the Garden is prepared do about it, and the response is the same in both cases.

**Remembering God as the Turning Point**

The step between the act and the request for forgiveness is the remembrance of God, and its position in the sentence is the key to how the Book understands the process: what produces repentance is not a calculation of consequences but an awareness of the One against whom the wrong was done. The commentators described that remembrance in three ways — recalling God's anger and the punishment that follows sin, living with the awareness that God is watching, and pronouncing the divine name. The three belong together in the practice the verse recommends, and they are the natural fruit of the mindfulness the sūrah has been commanding throughout.

**The Question in the Middle**

The sentence interrupts its own description with a question: who forgives sins except God? The question is rhetorical, and its function is to remove the possibility that the person might seek the remedy elsewhere or despair of finding it. The Book's statements support it on both sides: God does not forgive the association of others with Him but forgives anything less for whoever He wills (4:48); and those who have exceeded all limits against themselves are told not to lose hope in God's mercy, for He forgives all sins (39:53). The question stands between the two and confirms both — locating the remedy with the One who set the terms, and ruling out any other source of pardon.

**"Who Do Not Knowingly Persist"**

The final clause carries the weight of the definition: the refusal to persist is what separates repentance from a cycle of offence and regret. Al-Ṭabarī explained persistence as continuing in an act knowingly — whether the knowledge is of its prohibition or of its character as disobedience. The qualifier is significant because it excludes the person who falls into a fault and regrets it: the people described are not those who never wrong themselves — the sentence opens by acknowledging that they do — they are the people who, having done so, do not settle into it. Al-Rāzī drew from the verse a principle the community's teachers repeated: no sin is great once forgiveness is sought for it, and no sin is small if committed deliberately and persistently — the outcome attaches to the direction a person is facing rather than the size of what he has done.

**The Report of the Man Who Kissed the Woman**

Some commentaries connect the verse with a report about a man who bought dates from a woman, embraced and kissed her, and then came to the Prophet regretting what he had done. The report illustrates the situation the sentence describes, and the sūrah's answer to it is the description of the people who remember God, ask forgiveness, and do not continue.

**The Outcome of Such Repentance**

The Book's other statements describe what the return produces: those who repent, believe, and do right will have their evil deeds changed into good deeds, God being forgiving and merciful (25:70); and whoever commits evil or wrongs himself and then seeks God's forgiveness will find Him forgiving and merciful (4:110). The sentences describe the same movement this verse outlines and add the assurance that the return is met rather than merely required — the person who makes it is not left where he was. With this verse the description of the people for whom the Garden is prepared is complete: they give in both conditions, govern their anger, pardon those in their power, and when they fail they turn back without delay — a portrait defined without any reference to status, lineage, or achievement, and by what they do with their property, their tempers, their authority, and their mistakes.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:136

> Their reward is forgiveness from their Lord and Gardens under which rivers flow, staying there forever. How excellent is the reward for those who work ˹righteousness˺

**The Reward Stated as a Pair**

The verse states the reward of the people described in the two verses before it, naming two things: forgiveness from their Lord, and Gardens with rivers running beneath them. The pairing matches the description that has just ended — forgiveness addresses what they did wrong; the Gardens address what they did well — and the two together make up the full settlement the Book promises to people whose record includes both.

**The Rivers Beneath the Gardens**

The phrase is one of the Book's most frequent descriptions of the Hereafter, and the image gathers what a Garden needs in the lands from which the first audience heard the message: water that never fails. The Book uses the picture to describe a place of continuous provision rather than an estate with fixed borders; elsewhere the description is expanded with details of greeting and speech — the people of the Garden welcomed with peace, their closing words praise of God, Lord of all worlds (10:10).

**Abiding Therein**

The statement that they remain there is part of every extended description of the Garden, and it answers the fear that shadows human hopes about good things: that they end. The Book contrasts the permanence of the next life with the passing character of this one to order its instruction about what deserves effort — a reward that could be lost would not settle the account of people who gave up what they had for it. When the believers are admitted, the description states that they stay by the will of their Lord (14:23).

**Forgiveness Named First**

The order of the reward follows the order of the previous verse, which ended with the request for forgiveness and the refusal to persist: the reward begins where the repentance ended. The arrangement makes the mechanics clear the way the Book usually does — the Garden is not entered by an unbroken record but by people whose faults have been forgiven, and the description in this passage never claims the believers are without faults; it claims they do something about them.

**The Word for the Workers**

The closing exclamation speaks of the reward of the workers — a noun describing people who do something rather than people who hold a position. Some commentators used the choice as evidence that the reward involves what a person does as well as the grace that makes his work possible, and the verse supports both sides: the people described act, yet the first item of their reward is forgiveness, which no action earns as a wage. The tradition holds both in the same way: the report that no one enters the Garden by his deeds alone — and that the most beloved conduct is what is done consistently, even if little, the Prophet including himself in the rule — is recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6464) and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2818). Read with this verse, the report does not cancel the description of the workers; it explains the order in which the two stand: the deeds are the road, and the mercy is what meets the traveller at the end of it.

**The Exclamation That Closes**

The final sentence is not a statement of fact about the Gardens but a word of praise inviting agreement. The same form appears at the point where the reward is named elsewhere — the righteous in the Hereafter declaring that God has fulfilled what He promised them, and that excellent indeed is the reward of the workers (39:74). The Book puts such sentences at the end of a description to leave the listener with the value of what has been described, and this verse does so in a single clause.

**The Unit Completed**

The passage that began with the warning about the Fire and the command to hasten toward forgiveness ends with a portrait of the people who receive what they seek: the command, the conduct, and the outcome. What makes the instance notable is the placement of the description of wrongdoing inside the portrait of the righteous — the Book's ideal community is not presented as a company of people who never fail but as a company of people who turn back when they do, and the reward stated here belongs to that company.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:137

> Similar situations came to pass before you, so travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers

**The Word Behind "Wonts"**

The verse tells the community that patterns of conduct have passed away before it, and directs people to travel and consider how those who denied the truth ended. The word rendered "wonts" is the plural of a noun covering an established practice — a way of doing things set up and followed. The Book uses the term both for the practices of earlier peoples and for the way God dealt with them, so the word carries both halves of the lesson at once: there are habits people establish generation after generation, and there is a pattern in how those habits end. The verse asks its listeners to look at both — and claims a regularity rather than a random sequence in which the powerful win and the weak lose, inviting its listeners to examine the record and test the claim.

**A Command to Journey and Consider**

The instruction to travel the earth is part of the Book's method of teaching through observation: the listener is not asked to accept a report about the past but to look at what the past has left behind and reason from the evidence. The end to be considered is the outcome of denial — and the word used for "end" is not a simple marker of time; it carries the sense of a result, an outcome, the effect an act has on its doer. The Book uses it with that weight when telling people to consider what happened to those who were warned, those who denied, those who worked corruption: conduct produces a consequence, and the consequence can be seen in the record. Reading history this way turns it into instruction.

**The Peoples the First Audience Knew**

The commentators identified the peoples the earliest listeners knew best — ʿĀd and Thamūd, whose stories the Book relates at length, and whose territories and remains were within reach or memory of the Arabian audience. Al-Ṭabarī drew the general lesson: the wealth and power these peoples accumulated did not stand between them and their fate. The Book tells their stories repeatedly because they are close enough in time and place to function as evidence rather than distant legend, and this verse turns the familiarity into an argument.

**Hearing Versus Seeing**

Some commentators observed that the knowledge this verse commands can be acquired without the journey, simply by learning what happened to those peoples — while adding that hearing is weaker than seeing. The Book's instruction still prefers the direct encounter when available: the traveller who stands among ruins where a prosperous people once lived receives the lesson in a form argument alone rarely produces, and the sūrah's audience was surrounded by such sites.

**The Word's Later History**

The word in this verse is the same word Muslims later used for the Prophet's practice — the body of his sayings and actions transmitted in the reports — and for the recommended conduct that follows his example. That usage grew out of the meaning present in the verse: an established way that people follow. The verse itself speaks of many wonts, good and bad, and the tradition later invested the term with the highest of them all.

**A Report About Establishing a Practice**

A report from Jarīr ibn ʿAbdullāh records the Prophet's statement that whoever establishes a good practice in Islam has its reward and the reward of those who act on it after him, without anything being taken from their rewards — and that whoever establishes an evil practice carries its burden and the burden of those who act on it after him (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1017). The report explains what is at stake in the word the verse uses: practices outlive the people who begin them, so the account of a person who sets a pattern is not closed when his own life ends. The verse's command to consider the wonts of earlier peoples and the report's language about establishing practices belong to the same idea.

**After the Battle**

Some commentators connected the verse with Uḥud, reading it as a statement that the enemies of the believers would meet a fate like that of the enemies of earlier messengers: the community that had just lost seventy of its men was being told to look at the wider pattern. The consolation is not that the loss did not matter; it is that the community's story belongs to a sequence that has been running through history, in which denial eventually reaches its end. The instruction to travel and look gives the claim its evidence, and the verses that follow state the conclusion in the community's own situation.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:138

> This is an insight to humanity—a guide and a lesson to the God-fearing

**The Word Behind "Exposition"**

The verse describes what has been sent down as an exposition for mankind — a noun the tradition counts among the names of the Book itself, whose root carries the sense of making a matter clear and separating it from confusion. The choice of the word at this point is deliberate: the community has just been told to travel and consider how earlier peoples ended, and has been given a description of its own conduct in the verses about giving, anger, and forgiveness; the verse now states what the material it has been receiving does — it makes things clear.

**What "This" Points To**

The commentators disagreed in a small and instructive way about the scope of the demonstrative. One view holds that it refers to the whole Book, so the sentence is a statement about revelation as such; another holds that it refers to what has just been said in the preceding verses — the battle, the warnings, the description of the righteous. Both readings fit the sūrah's movement: the verses around this one have moved from an account of a battle to its lessons, and the sentence states that the material, wherever its limits are drawn, belongs to the same purpose. The disagreement itself shows how closely the commentators read the connections between consecutive verses.

**Addressed to Mankind**

The phrase gives the revelation a scope no community can claim for itself: the Book describes itself as a reminder and clarification for people generally, addressing its arguments to human beings as such. The universal framing has a practical consequence here — the community that had lost a battle is told that what it carries is not its own private possession but a message with a claim on everyone, the same understanding the sūrah states when it describes the community as one brought forth for the benefit of humanity (3:110).

**Guidance and Exhortation**

The verse adds that the revelation is a guidance and an exhortation for those mindful of God, the second word referring to speech that moves a person to act rather than merely informing him. The pairing appears elsewhere in the Book for the earlier scriptures as well — the Gospel is described in the same terms, a guidance and an exhortation for the reverent — the Book treating its own message and the messages before it as one continuous work of clarification. And the term covers the kind of speech that warns and encourages at once, which the Book uses for the prophets' preaching: its way of persuading goes beyond facts and commands, appealing to the consequences of conduct, the record of history, and the character of God.

**Why the God-Fearing Are Named**

The narrowing in the verse's second half is not a restriction of the message's scope but a statement about where its effect appears: the exposition is for all people; the guidance and the exhortation reach those who approach it with the disposition the Book names. The pattern is consistent across the Book — the same message clarifies for everyone and guides those who receive it with the awareness it asks for; the two audiences are distinguished by their response rather than by anything in the message.

**The Verse's Work in the Passage**

The verse stands between the command to consider the ends of earlier peoples and the two commands that follow — not to weaken and not to grieve — and it explains why the community should be able to absorb the lesson of its own losses. The losses at Uḥud were severe enough to invite the conclusion that the community had been abandoned; the verse answers that conclusion in advance by stating what the message the community carries actually is. The Book's own presence among them was the reason for confidence, and the next verses build on that ground. Sentences of this kind — in which the revelation describes its own purposes — recur throughout the Book and teach those who recite it what to expect from it: a reader who takes this verse seriously approaches the text expecting clarification, direction, and the kind of warning that moves conduct, and the sūrah supplies all three in the passage that follows.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:139

> Do not falter or grieve, for you will have the upper hand, if you are ˹true˺ believers

**Two Commands in One Sentence**

The verse forbids two things at once and then gives the reason: the community is told not to weaken and not to grieve, because its position is one of ascendancy — given the condition named at the end. The pairing is not accidental: after a defeat, weakness and grief belong together — the first the practical loss of confidence, the second the emotional form of the same loss. The verse addresses both, and the reason supplied treats them at their common root.

**The Word Behind "Falter"**

The verb carries the sense of becoming weak, faint, or diminished, and one commentator glossed it as the believers not considering themselves weak — so the prohibition is directed as much at self-assessment as at outward conduct. The Book uses the same root when it tells the believers in another sūrah not to lose heart and not to call for peace while they have the upper hand. Both instructions concern the estimate a community forms of itself, because that estimate shapes what it attempts: a community that decides it is finished behaves accordingly, and the Book addresses the decision where it is made.

**What the Grief Was About**

Al-Ṭabarī read the prohibition of grief as directed at mourning over the battle's losses — the men killed and the ground given up on the day of Uḥud. The grief addressed is not sorrow in general but sorrow settling into the conclusion that the cause is lost. The Book does not forbid the community to feel the deaths of its people, and it does not treat the memory of a defeat as a subject to avoid; what it forbids is the conclusion, and the next clause explains why the conclusion is wrong.

**The Promise of Ascendancy**

The reason given is a statement about outcome: the believers will be on top — the word denoting elevation — if the condition is met. The commentators explained the elevation as victory over the community's enemies, or as the superiority of the community's final state over theirs; the second reading holds true in every period, since the fighting does not always go the community's way. The Book's promises in this sūrah show the same care about outcomes: what is guaranteed is not the result of every engagement but the standing of the community with its Lord — the distinction the community most needed to learn. The same pattern appears elsewhere: if the believers are mindful of God, He will grant them a criterion to distinguish right from wrong, wipe out their misdeeds, and forgive them; and if they support God's cause, He will support them and make their footing firm. The promise of help is tied to the community's own condition and stated in terms of standing and firmness, not guaranteed victory in every engagement.

**The Conditional Clause**

The verse ends: if you are believers. Some read the particle as *since*, making the sentence a statement that ascendancy follows from the community's faith rather than waiting on a further condition. Both readings place the weight on the quality named at the end: the promise rests on faith and the conduct that accompanies it — not on numbers, equipment, or the possession of a city.

**After the Wound**

The verse follows the description of the community's moral condition and precedes the direct reference to the injury the believers received; it functions as the interpretation of the battle before the battle is mentioned again. The sūrah's method is to state the community's position before the event, so the event is heard in light of the position — the same order the Book uses with earlier peoples — and it makes the loss easier to place. The sentence remains useful long after the battle that occasioned it: every generation faces situations in which the visible facts suggest the cause has been abandoned, and the verse supplies the response in a line short enough to carry in memory.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:140

> If you have suffered injuries ˹at Uḥud˺, they suffered similarly ˹at Badr˺. We alternate these days ˹of victory and defeat˺ among people so that Allah may reveal the ˹true˺ believers, choose martyrs from among you—and Allah does not like the wrongdoers—

**The Comparison Stated Plainly**

The verse sets the community's wound beside the wound of its opponents and observes that they had already suffered something like it — and the Book does not soften the loss or present the day as anything other than a defeat. The comparison serves a purpose running through the whole passage: the believers are in no position to conclude God singled them out for punishment, because their enemies had been bloodied too, and recently enough that everyone remembering Badr would know it. What happened was a test within a contest both sides were undergoing.

**Days Handed Out in Turns**

The image for the sequence of events is days distributed in turns — the word describing the alternation of fortune between one party and another: a day belongs to one side, then to the other, and neither holds it permanently. Al-Ṭabarī explained it through the Arabs' proverb: one day for you and one day against you; al-Rāzī drew the general point that the affairs of the world rotate and no advantage is permanent. The tradition understands the turns as arranged deliberately, so that the success of one side and the reverse of another are both instruments rather than accidents — which offers the community a way of reading a battle it lost without concluding that God's help had failed. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī added the explanation that God's support is given to the believers when they hold to their duty, and that when they fail their enemies gain the advantage over them — the reversal itself a form of instruction. The sentence refuses both conclusions a person might draw from a defeat: that the cause has been abandoned, and that the outcome of a battle settles the standing of those who fought it.

**"So That Allah May Know"**

The purpose clause states that the turns come so that God may know (or mark out) those who believe — an expression the tradition of interpretation explains as knowledge made evident rather than knowledge newly acquired. God's knowledge of a person's faith does not change; the moment of trial makes the faith visible to the community and to the person himself. Al-Zamakhsharī noted the grammar: the clause joins what precedes it through an implied phrase, reading either as God acted in order to know, or God will know by acting — one of the places where the language of human experience describes what is beyond it. The trial produces something God recognises in His servants; the words for the refining and the losses describe how that recognition is reached.

**Choosing Martyrs**

The next purpose is that God may take martyrs from among the community. The word carries both the general sense of one who testifies and the special sense the community gave it — one who dies in the way of God — and both work here: the men who fell at Uḥud had borne testimony by their conduct and their deaths, and the tradition came to honour them under that name. The verse presents the loss from the side the community could not see from the field: the people who died were not merely subtracted from the ranks; they were selected.

**The Boundary Marker at the End**

The closing statement — God does not love the wrongdoers — serves as the passage's boundary marker: the turns of fortune are not morally neutral for those caught in them. The clause distinguishes the community's losses from the outcome of those who fight against the truth, and prevents the reader from concluding the Book is indifferent to which side a person supports when the days turn either way. And the verse closes off the two conclusions available to those who watched the day unfold: that the dead were being punished, and that the survivors had been abandoned. What it offers instead is a way of holding two facts together: the wound was real and deserved by no individual in particular, and the loss happened within an arrangement whose purposes could not be seen from the field — the Book's usual mode of consolation in the battle material. The command and its reason are meant to be recited together, and the verses that follow describe what the testing is for.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:141

> and distinguish the ˹true˺ believers and destroy the disbelievers

**The Purpose Clause Continued**

The verse carries on the sentence that began in the previous one, supplying the other side of the same arrangement: the purifying of those who believe, and the reducing of those who deny. The two verbs describe one operation from different angles — what is lost is gone, and what remains has been clarified.

**Assaying**

The word rendered "purify" belongs to the language of refining metal — the separation of impurities from what is valuable. Al-Ṭabarī explained it as the trials through which patience is tested, which fits the Book's use of the image elsewhere: the community should not think it will be left alone without being tested, that those who claim belief will be left untested, or that entry into the Garden comes without the struggle and endurance this sūrah describes. The image of the refiner's fire suits a passage written for people who had just come through one.

**Blight**

The word rendered "blight" describes reduction and shortage — a diminishing so thorough that nothing of the thing can be seen; al-Rāzī explained it in those terms, and the same root appears in this sūrah's statement that God destroys interest and causes charity to grow (2:276). The pairing of the two verbs is the point: one group is refined and the other reduced, and both outcomes are attributed to the same arrangement of days. The language avoids both suggestions — that Uḥud produced a result outside God's purposes, and that the two groups were being treated alike.

**Two Outcomes for Two Groups**

The clause assigns a different result to each party, and the sūrah has been building toward the distinction since its account of the battle began: the community's losses were not the same in kind as its enemies' losses, even on the same field. The difference lies in what each group was being fitted for — the believers separated from what might be mistaken for faith; their enemies reduced in a matter that would eventually leave them with nothing. The sūrah's earlier verses about the fate of those who deny the truth prepared the reader for the second outcome.

**Why Victory Is Not Simply Given**

Al-Qurṭubī and others stated the principle that runs through these verses: God supports the believers, and when they disobey or lose their footing, their enemies gain the upper hand over them. Al-Rāzī put it in the same terms, observing that God does not support the deniers as such — He permits their success as a means of testing the believers through loss. This explains the sūrah's whole treatment of Uḥud: the community had been ordered to hold a position, had argued about the decision, and had left the position for the sake of spoils; the result came through the ordinary working of events, described as a test rather than as the withdrawal of a promise.

**The Two Battles in the Commentators' Reading**

The reference to a people who had suffered a like wound was read as pointing to the enemies defeated at Badr the year before: the wound at Uḥud answered an earlier wound, and the same community was present in both. The reading gives the verse its historical anchor and explains the alternation of days — the proverb's rhythm used as a teacher, with both battles belonging to the lesson. And the statement that God may know the believers recurs in the next verse as a question — whether the community imagined it would enter the Garden without God knowing those among them who strove and who were patient. The knowledge in question is displayed rather than learned, the idiom being that of ordinary speech: to say one knows no good in a person is to say he is not good. The commentators connected the discussion with the earlier verse about the community being a middle nation, established so that it might be witnesses (2:143), and the construction keeps the reader from reading the phrase as information God acquires.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:142

> Do you think you will enter Paradise without Allah proving which of you ˹truly˺ struggled ˹for His cause˺ and patiently endured

**The Question Put to the Community**

The verse asks whether the believers imagined they would enter the Garden without God knowing who among them had striven and who had been patient. The question form exposes an assumption rather than merely contradicting it. The assumption is natural for a community told it was the best brought forth for humanity — and the verse does not deny the community's standing; it insists the standing is connected to the conduct the Book has been describing. Entry into the Garden is not a verdict on a group but the outcome of what its members do.

**Striving and Patience Named Together**

The two qualities are the ones the sūrah has been testing since its account of Uḥud began: striving covers the effort a person makes, including the effort of fighting; patience covers the endurance of what the effort costs. The Book habitually pairs them in passages about trial — the community is told that God will test it until the truth about those who strive and remain steadfast becomes known, and that entry into the Garden depends on proving which of its members struggled and endured. Neither is an alternative to the other: effort without endurance collapses at the first hardship; endurance without effort is passive.

**The Wording About Knowledge**

Al-Rāzī addressed the wording directly: the verse implies no incompleteness in God's knowledge. Its purpose is to state the condition on which the Garden is entered, and the knowledge it mentions is the kind demonstrated rather than acquired. Al-Zamakhsharī illustrated the idiom from ordinary speech — saying one knows no good in a person amounts to saying he is not good. The sentence states what will be evident about the community, not what God will discover; the same explanation applies to the clauses about knowledge in the surrounding verses.

**Two Readings of the Second Clause**

Some read the second clause as a separate condition, requiring both striving and patience as distinct qualifications; others as a consequence, so that the sense is God knowing those who strove in such a way as to know those who were patient. Both describe the same reality: patience is the form striving takes when the effort continues past the point where it stops being rewarding, and the two are so closely connected in the Book's usage that they are frequently named in a single breath. The difference concerns grammar rather than substance.

**Al-Rāzī on the Two Loves**

Al-Rāzī's comment on this verse is one of his best-known passages: the love of this world and happiness in the Hereafter cannot coexist — as one increases, the other diminishes, because the heart's occupation with worldly concerns leaves no room for what the next life requires. He adds that not everyone who affirms the religion is truthful, and the difference appears when a person's attachments are tested. Love, in his description, is what does not diminish when difficulty comes and does not increase merely because desires are satisfied. The verse's demand for patience is, on this reading, a test of whether the affirmation of faith was love or only agreement.

**The Principle Across the Book**

The Book states the same teaching in several places: people should not think they will be left alone once they say they believe, without being put to the test; and earlier communities are described as afflicted with hardship and shaken until the messenger and those with him asked when God's help would come. Read together, these passages form a consistent teaching about how the community's claim is verified — and the community at Madinah had just been given a demonstration of it in the space of a single afternoon. The question had a specific force for the first hearers: they had suffered losses after a period of confidence, and the verse asks whether they had expected the Garden as an automatic conclusion from their membership. The answer is visible in the verses around it: the community had among its members people who stood firm and people who wavered, and the battle had made the difference plain. The verse names the two qualities that separated them, in a form none of the listeners could answer in the affirmative — and the Garden stands at the beginning of the sentence and the two qualities at the end, the distance between them being the road the community is told to travel.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:143

> You certainly wished ˹for the opportunity˺ for martyrdom before encountering it, now you have seen it with your own eyes

**Who the Verse Addresses**

Al-Ṭabarī reports that the verse addresses believers who had not taken part in the battle of Badr and had wished for an opportunity to fight the idolaters in the way of God. The verse reminds them that the wish they expressed was now answered in the hardest possible form, without any softening of the contrast between the hope and the event. The address is therefore not to cowards being taunted but to people who had asked for something and then met it — the sūrah's method of holding the community to its own words, in two short sentences whose brevity gives them their force.

**Longing for Death, and What It Meant**

The commentators explained the longing as a longing for the fighting that could lead to death: the wish was for the honour and the test, not for extinction, and the Book's language reflects how such wishes were expressed by people expecting a decisive engagement. That understanding guards the verse from a misunderstanding that has sometimes attached to it — the Book does not praise the desire to die as such; it addresses the gap between what a person declares when the danger is distant and how he behaves when the danger arrives.

**The Emphatic Closing Phrase**

One reading takes the closing phrase as emphasis comparable to telling a person he heard something with his own ears: the community did not merely hear about death or see it from far off — the matter was placed before its eyes. The Book uses such insistence when it wants a point undeniable: the listeners could not claim afterward that they had been spared the test they asked for. Al-Qurṭubī records other ways of taking the phrase: that the community saw death with an untrammelled vision, no barrier between itself and the fact of it; or that the words carry an implied question — the community had seen the fighting face to face, so why did it fall back? The second reading connects the verse directly with the rout the sūrah describes later: not only a reminder about a wish but an inquiry into the conduct of the day, the question left unanswered in the verse so the next verse can supply the remedy.

**The Retreat at Uḥud**

The sūrah's later verses refer to those who turned back on their heels, and the commentators connected the passages: during the fighting the ranks broke, and some of the believers left their positions and withdrew. The verse's closing words therefore function as a mirror held up to a specific afternoon: the community that had wished for a chance to prove itself had its chance, and the record of what it did with it is preserved in the sūrah rather than passed over. The Book's willingness to preserve such material is part of what makes its account of the community's formation credible — and it does not treat the wish itself as hypocrisy: the verse acknowledges the wish as something genuinely felt, while insisting on the difference between it and the event. A wish expressed in safety is not the same act as a decision made in danger; both are real, but one costs nothing and the other costs everything.

**Wishing for Death Elsewhere in the Book**

The Book addresses the subject elsewhere, and in those passages the wish is treated as a claim a person is invited to substantiate: those who assert that the next life is theirs alone are told to wish for death if their claim is true; and those who spoke about their fallen brothers are told to try to avoid death themselves if what they say is true.
