The pattern is consistent: a claim about one's standing is tested by whether the person will accept its consequences. The verse before the reader belongs to that family, and its application to the community at Madinah was immediate.

**The Verse's Function**

The verse stands between the question about the Garden and the announcement that the Prophet is only a messenger, providing the concrete memory the community needed in order to hear both properly. The sequence gives the passage its emotional shape: the community is asked what it expected, reminded of what it asked for, and then told the truth about the messenger it followed — an examination of the community's assumptions, from which the sūrah proceeds to the instructions that follow, turning the community's attention from the memory of the field to the conduct of its ordinary affairs.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:144

> Muḥammad is no more than a messenger; other messengers have gone before him. If he were to die or to be killed, would you regress into disbelief? Those who do so will not harm Allah whatsoever. And Allah will reward those who are grateful

**The Name in the Sentence**

The verse names the Prophet directly and states that he is nothing more than a messenger — other messengers having passed away before him. The sūrah uses his name only a few times, and the use here is part of the sentence's teaching. The Book elsewhere insists on the same distinction between messenger and message, describing Muhammad as not the father of any of the men among his people but the messenger of God and the seal of the prophets; this verse works in the same direction by placing him within a line of messengers who came and went.

**Messengers Who Have Passed Away**

The statement places the Prophet within a sequence, and the Book states the mortality of the messengers in several places — asking whether, if the Prophet were to die, his people would live forever, and declaring that everything perishes except God Himself, together with the declaration that every soul will taste death. The tradition placed those verses alongside this one as a single teaching: the Book prepares its community for the death of the one it loves most, and this verse is the clearest of the preparations, asking the question in the community's own voice and answering it in the same breath.

**The Question About Turning Back**

The verse asks whether the community would turn back on its heels if the Prophet died or was killed — the image describing apostasy in the physical terms of a retreat. The question is not unanswerable rhetoric: the sūrah has just described a day when parts of the community did fall back, so the possibility raised had already been demonstrated in a lesser form. The word describes the movement of a person reversing his direction, used for the abandonment of a commitment rather than a momentary hesitation — the principle that the day's events made visible.

**To Whom the Harm Would Fall**

The verse states that whoever turns back does not harm God in the least — relocating the consequences of apostasy entirely onto the person who turns, the cause unaffected. The Book repeats the teaching where it considers the community's failure: God has no need of anyone's obedience, and the worlds are not improved by the conduct of those who abandon the truth. The statement removes the last excuse a person might use to justify leaving when the leader is gone — and the final clause directs attention instead to the thankful, whose gratitude is the opposite of turning back: a person who acknowledges what he has been given will not abandon the giver when circumstances change.

**The Rumour at Uḥud**

The comments on the verse record the crisis that occasioned it: during the fighting the Prophet was badly wounded, and word spread that he had been killed — unsettling parts of the community and raising the morale of its opponents. The reports describe a companion calling out that if Muhammad had died, the Lord of Muhammad does not die, urging the people to live and die as he did; and hypocrites taunting the believers that a true prophet would not have been killed, pressing them to abandon the religion. The verse answered all of it in a few lines, and the reports of the day note how quickly the community's composure was restored once the Prophet was seen alive and the line was held again.

**The Day the Prophet Died**

Years later, when the Prophet did die, the community's response showed what the verse had taught: ʿUmar refused the news and addressed the people as though the Prophet would not die; Abū Bakr mounted the pulpit, recited this verse, and said that whoever worshipped Muhammad should know that Muhammad had died, and whoever worshipped God should know that God is alive and does not die (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1241, 1242). ʿUmar later said it was as though he had never read the verse before that day. The tradition preserved the incident because it shows the verse carrying the community through the moment it had been prepared for — and the lesson has continued in every generation: a community that identifies its cause with the presence of a particular individual will collapse when that individual is gone. The sūrah provides the correction in advance, and the closing clause about the thankful describes the people who take it.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:145

> No soul can ever die without Allah's Will at the destined time. Those who desire worldly gain, We will let them have it, and those who desire heavenly reward, We will grant it to them. And We will reward those who are grateful

**Death and Its Appointed Term**

The verse states that no soul dies except by the permission of God and at a term that has been fixed — following the announcement of the Prophet's mortality with the principle that governs every death, the two verses belonging together. The Book states the teaching in many places: every soul will taste death; everything perishes except God. This sentence adds the element of appointment — the time of death is set, and no human precaution changes it. Al-Ṭabarī noted that the phrase for the written term works with an implied verb: God has decreed a term that is fixed. The same doctrine appears in the sūrah of the Iron, where nothing befalls a person — on earth or in themselves — without being in a record before it is brought about. The purpose of such statements is consistently practical: they remove from conduct the calculations fear and greed produce, and the verses that follow apply that removal to the case of fighting.

**Two Desires and Two Outcomes**

The verse contrasts those who desire the reward of this world with those who desire the reward of the next, and states that each will be given what he seeks. The Book makes the same contrast elsewhere in more pointed wording: whoever desires the harvest of the Hereafter is given increase, while whoever desires only the harvest of this world is given some of it but has no share in the Hereafter; and whoever desires the fleeting world alone is hastened toward what is willed for him and consigned to the Fire. The contrast here is stated with a similar economy, addressing the motives in play when the fighting began.

**The Spoils and the Wider Meaning**

Al-Rāzī read the reference to worldly reward as pointing to people whose motive in the fighting was spoil, glory, and reputation; al-Qurṭubī read the sentence more broadly, as a statement about human motives in general. The wider reading suits the verse's place: the community had left a position for the sake of what could be gathered on the field, and the clause names the kind of desire that produced that decision without accusing anyone in particular. The general form is deliberate — it gives the community a way to examine its own motives whenever it takes up a cause.

**Courage, Fear, and What They Change**

Al-Rāzī drew from the verse a lesson about fear: a person's anxiety and cowardice do not preserve him, and the term of his life is not in his hands — the conclusion being that a person should act for what is good without letting the fear of death govern him. The argument is precisely the verse's: if the appointment is fixed, calculation of the risk cannot extend a single life, and the only variable left is the quality of what a person does with the time he has. The Book's commands about fighting rest on this reasoning throughout.

**The Hypocrites' Argument Silenced**

The commentary tradition noted that the verse closes an argument the hypocrites were using: that the men who died at Uḥud would not have died if they had stayed home — used to discourage the believers from further fighting. The Book answers the argument directly elsewhere, telling the men who said it about their brothers to try to avoid death themselves if what they said was true. The principle here is the deeper answer: the term is fixed, and remaining behind provides no more protection than going out. The two statements together remove the calculation from the community's thinking.

**A Report About Three Men**

The tradition preserves a report that bears on the verse's contrast of motives. Abū Hurayrah related that among the first whose cases are decided on the Day of Judgment is a man who died as a martyr: he is made to recount his blessings and admits them, and is told that he fought so that it might be said he was brave — and it was said (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1905a). The same report describes a man who acquired knowledge and recited the Book, and a man who was given wealth and spent it, each told his motive was the reputation he gained rather than the pleasure of God. The report makes the verse's distinction concrete: the same act can belong to either category according to the intention behind it.

**The Closing Promise**

The verse ends by promising reward to the thankful — the same clause that closed the previous verse, tying the two together and identifying the quality that carries a person through both the fear of death and the temptation of gain. The Book's treatment of gratitude elsewhere describes it as the perception of the source of what one has, and promises increase to those who give thanks; here the word has a specific force: the people who acknowledge that their lives and their cause belong to God are the ones whose conduct holds steady when the days turn against them — and the sūrah closes the account of the battle's motives on that note.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:146

> ˹Imagine˺ how many devotees fought along with their prophets and never faltered despite whatever ˹losses˺ they suffered in the cause of Allah, nor did they weaken or give in! Allah loves those who persevere

**A Question That Measures the Community's Case**

The verse opens with the Book's idiom for abundance — how many there have been of a certain kind — which is not a request for a number but a way of saying the instance before the reader is one of many. The community at Madinah had just suffered a defeat, and the verse places that experience within a long record: the listeners are shown that their situation is not unprecedented and that the conduct expected of them has been expected of others before them. The same figure of speech carries encouragement elsewhere: when the companions of Saul looked at the army of Goliath and lost heart, the ones certain of meeting God answered that a small force has often overcome a great one by God's will — and the verse closes by naming God's company with the steadfast (2:249).

**The Two Readings of the Verb**

The verb in the opening clause can be read as fighting or as being killed. On the second reading the sentence says that many devoted men stood alongside a prophet who was killed — al-Ṭabarī's preferred reading, since it fits the plain sense of the preceding verses about the Prophet's mortality. Both readings support the same lesson: whether the prophet fought and his companions fought with him, or the prophet was killed and his companions held their ground, the sentence presents people who continued in their duty while the outcome was in doubt.

**The Word for the Devoted Men**

The noun describing the companions of the prophets has been given several explanations: some connected it with the word translated "sages" earlier in this sūrah (3:79), describing the men by their knowledge and devotion; others took it as a reference to large groups, one report giving ten thousand as the number the singular form denotes. The variety reflects the word's rarity; the sense that emerges from all of them is a body of people marked by seriousness and endurance rather than by any particular office. The verse places such people beside the prophets as the human material of a mission that has always required them — not the leaders of the mission but the ordinary people who carry it: the prophets deliver the message; the devoted men absorb the losses, hold their positions, and continue.

**Three Things They Did Not Do**

The verse names three responses the men did not have: they did not falter because of what befell them, they did not weaken, and they did not demean themselves. The three words describe a single moral posture from different angles — the first the initial reaction to a shock, the second the exhaustion that follows continued hardship, the third the submission to humiliation that comes when a person believes the cause has failed. The Book's descriptions of steadfastness usually concentrate on one of these; this verse covers all three, which is why it is so often quoted where a community is recovering from a loss.

**What Befell Them in the Way of God**

The phrase covers injury, loss of property, and death, and describes these as taking place within the activities of the religion rather than outside them — the cost of a struggle placed inside the definition of the struggle itself. The community at Uḥud had lost men dear to it, and the sūrah does not present those losses as a sign of abandonment; it presents them as what befell people in the way of God — exactly what the earlier companions of the prophets endured without faltering.

**God Loves the Patient**

The closing declaration gives the behaviour of those men its meaning: God loves the patient. The Book's attachment of divine love to particular qualities is one of its ways of teaching its values — patience, generosity, justice, and trust each attract that love — and here the endurance of a community under attack is placed in that category. What a community experiences as loss is described as the occasion of a love no loss can remove. The next verse reports the prayer such people made.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:147

> And all they said was, "Our Lord! Forgive our sins and excesses, make our steps firm, and grant us victory over the disbelieving people."

**The Restriction in the Sentence**

The verse reports that the only words of these people were a prayer — and the restriction is the point. Faced with a defeat, the devoted men did not debate, did not cast blame, and did not announce what the community should have done differently; they asked God for what they needed. The Book notices such restraint and records it as part of the description of the people it praises: the community that had just come through Uḥud is shown what the right response to a setback looks like in the form of speech — a model short enough to be memorised and repeated by anyone.

**The Two Kinds of Sin**

The prayer asks forgiveness of sins, and then of excess in one's affairs. Al-Qurṭubī suggested the first term covers the smaller faults and the second the greater, so that together the clauses leave nothing outside the request. The distinction, if accepted, reflects a pastoral concern running through the Book: a person who has made a serious mistake and a person who has accumulated minor ones both need to ask, and both need to know the wording covers their case. The word for excess covers waste and going beyond limits, and the verse attaches it to the whole range of a person's affairs rather than one department of life — the prayer acknowledges not only particular offences but a general tendency that needs correcting, teaching the community how to speak about its failures without either minimising them or treating them as beyond remedy.

**"Make Our Steps Firm"**

The third petition uses the image of standing and walking on the ground, and the Book uses the same image when it promises the believers that if they support God's cause He will support them and steady their footing. The request has a particular fitness here: the community's failure at Uḥud was a failure of footing, and the men whose prayer is recorded ask for the remedy in the words that describe the problem. The Book presents such prayers as the place where people take the weakness they have seen in themselves and ask for what would correct it.

**Help Against a Named Opponent**

The final request is for help against the people who deny — the opposition named rather than deliverance asked for in general terms. The community that prays it is in a contest, and it asks for support in the contest: without bitterness, and without asking for anything beyond success in the struggle. The request is for help, not revenge — the difference marks the attitude the sūrah commends to a community under attack.

**Prayer in the Middle of Trial**

The verse places the prayer in the middle of the fighting rather than after it: the men described in the previous verse were in the situation the prayer addresses, and their response was to ask rather than complain. The Book presents this as the natural conduct of people whose trust is in God — no hours of preparation, no particular ritual; a short supplication made while events were unfolding. The community reciting the passage learns that the correct use of speech in a crisis is to ask God for what one needs.

**The Order of the Petitions**

The order is instructive: forgiveness first, then steadiness, then the outcome of the struggle. A community whose own faults have been addressed can stand; a community that is standing can be helped. The sequence also describes the priorities the Book assumes: what is asked first is the repairing of the relation with God, and the request for victory comes last — the community's own condition is the first thing to set right when its affairs have gone wrong. The Book records the teaching in the form of a prayer rather than a list of instructions, and the prayer's compactness has made it one of the most used in the community's devotions — standing in a family of recorded prayers: the believers who say they have believed and ask forgiveness and protection from the Fire; the prayer of Adam and his wife in the sūrah of the Heights, acknowledging they have wronged themselves and that without forgiveness and mercy they will be among the losers; and the longer prayer asking God not to hold the community to account for forgetfulness and error and to grant victory over those who deny.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:148

> So Allah gave them the reward of this world and the excellent reward of the Hereafter. For Allah loves the good-doers

**Both Rewards Given**

The verse reports the result of the conduct and the prayer described in the two verses before it: God gave those people the reward of this world and the best reward of the Hereafter. The Book does not usually promise both to those who seek only one — it tells those who want the harvest of the next life that it will be increased for them, and those who want only the present one that they will receive some of it while having no share in the next. This sentence belongs to a different group, and the community at Madinah was being shown what its position could yield: with God are the rewards of this world and the next, both in His hands rather than in the hands of circumstances.

**The Reward of This World**

Al-Ṭabarī explained the reward of this world as victory, the spoils of war, and stability in the community's affairs — the concrete goods a community needs in order to continue: success in its engagements, the means to sustain itself, and a settled condition in which its work can go forward. The prayer in the preceding verse asked for steadiness and help; the answer given here describes what arrives when such a request is granted.

**The Best Reward of the Hereafter**

The second half uses the form that marks excellence — the Hereafter described as the better, or the most beautiful, of the two. The arrangement does not diminish the first reward; it orders the two: what is received in this world is genuine and worth having, and what is stored in the next is better. The sentence must have carried particular weight for people who had just lost property and family.

**The Good-Doers**

The verse closes by naming those whom God loves, using the term for people who do good and do it well — the same term the Book applies to those who spend in His way, to those who restrain their anger and pardon, and to those who keep their covenants. Its frequency suggests the Book uses it as a broad category for the conduct it commends, and the sūrah has already used it in the passage about the people for whom the Garden is prepared. The verse joins the steadfastness of the preceding verses with the generosity and restraint described earlier in the sūrah, placing the whole range of such conduct under the declaration of God's love.

**Whom God Loves**

The Book attaches its love to particular qualities in a set of passages the commentators gathered: it names the reverent, the patient, the just, those who act equitably — and the good-doers most often. The variety is significant: the Book is not describing an attitude that appears in one kind of person but a set of qualities its people are expected to hold together in their dealings — worship, endurance, trade, judgment, the settling of disputes. This verse names the broadest of the categories, and the sūrah's readers are meant to see their own conduct inside it.

**The Sequence Completed**

Read as a unit, the three verses describe a movement: people who stand firm under loss, a prayer that asks for forgiveness and firmness, and a God who gives both the goods of this life and the better things of the next. The arrangement gives the community a way of reading its own experience: the losses at Uḥud were real, and the sūrah does not promise recovery of every loss in the same currency; what it promises is that the conduct described is not wasted, and that the reward reaches into the situation the community is in as well as into the one that follows.

**For the First Hearers, and for Later Ones**

The first listeners had recently seen their dead carried back from a field they had expected to leave in better order, and the verse told them their endurance had a result, and the result would be given in full. The Book's practice in such passages is to state the outcome before the history has arrived at it: the community would go on to see victories and setbacks in turn, and the sentence teaches it to read both within a promise that the two rewards belong to the same God, and that the conduct earning them is the conduct described in the verses immediately before. The qualities required — steadiness, an honest reckoning with one's own faults, and the firm hope that God will steady what is unsteady — are presented without being tied to the specific events that occasioned them, which is why the pattern has travelled so well: the battle of Uḥud is the setting, and the instruction concerns the ordinary life of a community that has to keep going after something has gone badly wrong.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:149

> O believers! If you yield to the disbelievers, they will drag you back to disbelief—and you will become losers

**The Address and Its Warning**

The verse addresses the believers directly and warns them against obeying those who deny, with the consequence stated in physical terms: they would be turned back on their heels and end up as losers — a certainty in the form the Book uses for imminent dangers. The community had come through a period in which advice was plentiful and its sources mixed: the sūrah has described a party among the People of the Book wishing to mislead the believers, and the conduct of the hypocrites during the fighting. The verse gathers such advice into one category and addresses the habit of listening to it.

**Who the Warning Concerns**

Al-Ṭabarī recorded that the phrase about those who deny was understood to refer to the enemies of the believers among all the groups of the time: the People of the Book, the hypocrites, and the idolaters. The breadth is part of the verse's value — it warns against no single party or occasion but against a habit of mind in which the community takes its direction from people whose interests oppose its own. The Book's later passages make the same point about the company a person keeps and the counsel he acts on.

**What "Obedience" Covers**

The obedience named is not restricted to formal submission; it covers the practical response of a community that follows advice and adopts a course because it was recommended by people who do not share its commitments. The sūrah warned about such counsel earlier, describing a group who wished the believers would abandon the way they had received (3:100); this verse sharpens the point — the danger is not only that bad advice might be followed once, but that following it repeatedly would move the community back to where it started.

**Turned Back on the Heels**

The image is the same one the sūrah used a few verses earlier when it asked whether the community would abandon its religion if the Prophet died; al-Ṭabarī explained it here as a return to disbelief after faith. The shared image links the two dangers: apostasy raised first as a response to the death of a leader, and again as a response to outside influence — the same movement away from the ground the community stands on, with the second the more likely in ordinary circumstances. The theme of obedience also links the verse to the community's failure at Uḥud: the account of the battle showed what happens when a community departs from its instructions, and this verse names the source of such departures as the counsel of those who deny — the danger after the battle being the same as the danger during it, in a different form.

**The Word for Losing**

The closing word describes people who are lost or ruined, and the Book uses the root for both: to be destroyed and to lose what one has. The senses run together in a single condition, since the person who abandons the truth loses both his standing with God and what he had built. The Book uses the word of those who will regret their conduct on the Day of Judgment and of those whose efforts in this life come to nothing; here it applies to a community that reverses its course and loses the position it was given.

**What the Verse Asks — and Does Not Ask**

The warning serves the sūrah's larger purpose — the unity and steadiness of the community since the command to hold fast to God's rope together: a community pulled in several directions by outside advice cannot act with the coherence the mission requires. The instruction is not a rejection of dealing with people of other faiths — the sūrah contains passages treating such dealings with fairness and forbearance; the warning concerns direction and authority: the community is to take its course from its own revelation, and the counsel of those who oppose its purpose is not a legitimate source for that course. Nor is it a prohibition on speaking with outsiders or hearing what they say — the point of decision is not the sound of the advice but the identity of the source. The Book's later instruction on consultation inside the community, and its insistence that decisions rest with the believers and their messenger, are the positive form of this warning; and the next verse gives the community its alternative in a single line.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:150

> But no! Allah is your Guardian, and He is the best Helper

**The Correction**

The verse begins with the particle that sets aside what has just been said and puts something else in its place: the warning concerned dependence on those who deny, and this sentence supplies the alternative. The construction gives the passage a sharp shape — a danger, then the remedy in one line. The Book often answers a warning with a statement about God in this way, leaving the community holding a truth rather than only a prohibition.

**The Word for Guardian**

The noun rendered guardian covers several related senses: an owner, a patron, a protector, the one who takes charge of a matter on behalf of another. The Book applies it to God here and elsewhere in describing the community's relation to its Lord: one passage states that God is the patron of the believers while those who deny have no patron; another describes God as the guardian of the believers — an excellent guardian and an excellent helper. The vocabulary places what the community is being offered in place of outside counsel.

**The Best of Helpers**

The second half states that God is the best of helpers — a construction the Book uses for the attributes it wants its people to rely on. The comparison is not with other candidates who might help adequately; it states that no helper matches the One named. The Book says the same elsewhere: God knows the believers' enemies best, and He is sufficient as a guardian and as a helper; and it asks whether the community does not know that the kingdom of the heavens and the earth belongs to God and that it has no guardian or helper besides Him. This verse gathers the same teaching into six words.

**Seeking Help From No Other**

Al-Ṭabarī stated the practical contents: the verse directs the community to seek refuge with God and teaches it to seek help from no other — an instruction not limited to the crisis that occasioned it. The Book's teaching runs through its instructions about prayer, hardship, and fear: ask God for what is needed, rely on Him in what is undertaken, expect help from Him — because no other source has the same claim or capacity, and a community that turns to another source for protection is relying on something that cannot replace what it left.

**A Short Verse With Weight**

The verse is among the shortest in the passage, and the brevity suits its function: it closes the section dealing with the aftermath of Uḥud, supplying the conclusion the section has been building toward. After the account of the loss, the warnings about apostasy, and the reminder that death comes at an appointed term, the community is given a sentence it can carry: its Guardian is God, and no helper is better. The sūrah adds no qualifications, and the brevity is part of what makes it memorable.

**What the Community Needed to Hear**

The believers at Madinah were in a position that invited them to look for support in several directions: enemies nearby, allies whose commitment was uncertain, and a group inside the city whose loyalty had been exposed during the fighting. The verse tells them the security they were looking for would not come from a rearrangement of alliances. The Book does not forbid the making of treaties — the sūrah refers elsewhere to covenants the Prophet had with other groups; what the sentence establishes is the order of reliance: the community's protection is from God, and any arrangement it enters operates within that protection rather than replacing it. And the guardianship carries obligations as well as assurances: a community under a patron follows the direction the patron gives — the passage as a whole has been describing what that direction is — and the Book warns against assuming protection continues regardless. The verse states the relationship at its simplest, and the community is expected to hear both sides: the security offered, and the loyalty it assumes.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:151

> We will cast horror into the hearts of the disbelievers for associating ˹false gods˺ with Allah—a practice He has never authorized. The Fire will be their home—what an evil place for the wrongdoers to stay

**The Terror Cast Into Hearts**

The verse announces that terror will be cast into the hearts of those who deny, in a form presenting the event as settled. The Book describes this casting of horror among the opponents of the prophets in several passages with consistent wording: the instruction given to the angels on the day of Badr, in which God states He will cast horror into the hearts of those who disbelieve; the expulsion of a group from their homes with horror placed in their hearts, so that they destroyed their own houses with their own hands; and the same treatment of those who supported an alliance against the community. This sentence belongs to that family.

**The Reason Given**

The verse supplies the reason: they associated partners with God for which no authority had been sent down — the central charge of the sūrah's argument with its opponents, with the added element of warrant. The Book's objections to association are frequently accompanied by this observation: the practice rests on no divine authorisation. The point is not merely that the practice is wrong but that its defenders cannot produce the grounds on which it would rest — the verse states the charge as a refusal to accept a claim never supported. The term for authority covers proof, warrant, and the power accompanying a legitimate claim; the Book uses it for the evidence the prophets brought and in its recurring demand that those who associate partners with God bring their proof.

**Their Refuge Is the Fire**

The sentence then states where those who deny will end: their refuge is the Fire — the word describing a place to which a person retreats and in which he remains, foreclosing the hope of escape. The sūrah uses the same term for the reward of the reverent, whose refuge is the Garden: the two destinations are set out with the same vocabulary, and the reader learns to read them as the two ends of the same road. The closing declaration — an evil abode for the wrongdoers — uses the Book's term for those who deny the truth and those whose conduct damages the rights of others, usages the sūrah has connected throughout: the opponents of the community, the people who associate partners with God, and those who falsify revelation are placed in a single category, and the sentence about their abode stands as the conclusion of the whole.

**The Report From the Return to Makkah**

The commentators connect the verse with an event after Uḥud: the idolaters, on their way back to Makkah, regretted not finishing the community off and resolved to return and complete what they had begun. The reports state that God then cast terror into them, and they lost their resolve and continued home. The report gives the verse a concrete occasion: the army that had just defeated the believers was turned back without another engagement, and the sūrah attributes the outcome to the terror placed in their hearts rather than to any change in the community's strength.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse does not tell the community its enemies would cease to threaten it — the sūrah's other passages acknowledge the hostility it continued to face. What it establishes is where the decisive factor lies when the community is defended: the Book describes the fear of its opponents as one of the means by which the community is protected, treating that fear as an arrangement rather than an accident. The community's task is to hold its position and keep its conduct straight; the removal of the threat is left with the One who casts dread and grants safety. And the placement matters: the verse follows immediately after the statement that God is the believers' Guardian and best helper — the community is given the claim about protection and then an instance of it from its own recent memory, leaving the believers a basis for confidence that does not depend on their own condition after the battle.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:152

> Indeed, Allah fulfilled His promise to you when you ˹initially˺ swept them away by His Will, then your courage weakened and you disputed about the command and disobeyed, after Allah had brought victory within your reach. Some of you were after worldly gain while others desired a heavenly reward. He denied you victory over them as a test, yet He has pardoned you. And Allah is Gracious to the believers

**The Promise Fulfilled at the Start**

The verse opens by affirming that God was true to His promise, anchoring the statement in the first phase of the fighting, when the believers were overcoming their opponents by His permission. The commentators connect the sentence with the early events of the day, when the enemy's standard-bearers were killed and the tide ran entirely in the community's favour. The promise referred to is the help the Book had given the believers, and the testimony here is that the help arrived exactly as stated — before the failure set in.

**Three Failures Named in Order**

The verse then lists what happened, and the sequence is precise: the community lost heart, quarrelled among itself about the matter, and disobeyed. The order moves from an inner condition to an open act, describing how a collapse actually proceeds: a weakening of resolve first, then a division about what should be done, then the decision to do something other than what had been ordered. The sūrah presents the three not as separate incidents but as links in a single chain — and that is how the commentators treat them when explaining the loss of the day.

**The Word for Losing Heart**

The first failure is rendered as losing heart — the word describing a slackening of resolve, a collapse of firmness. The same root appeared in the sūrah's earlier command not to falter after the losses. The connection is instructive: the sūrah first forbids the condition and then records the occasion on which it occurred, and the community reciting the passage hears the instruction and the history in the same sequence. What is described here is not a failure of the sūrah to warn in time; it is the event the warning was designed to prevent.

**The Quarrel About the Matter**

The quarrel is explained by the commentators as a dispute among the men posted on the high ground: some wished to remain at their positions in accordance with their orders, while others argued for going down to secure a share of what was being gathered on the field. Al-Ṭabarī reports the disagreement was between those determined to hold their place and those who pressed to leave it. The episode shows the community's discipline breaking from inside, in a matter where the instructions had been explicit — and the placement of the quarrel between the weakening of resolve and the act of disobedience shows how the three belong together.

**Two Desires in One Community**

The verse states that among the community were people who desired this world and people who desired the next, and offers the distinction as the explanation of the quarrel: the assembly on the field was not of one mind about what the day was for. Al-Ṭabarī records that Ibn Masʿūd said he had not known, before this verse was sent down, that any of the Prophet's companions could be described as desiring this world — conveying the seriousness with which the first community received the diagnosis. The verse does not divide the community into two parties of fixed membership; it states that the two motives were present among people fighting side by side.

**Turned Aside in Order to Be Tested**

The sentence continues by stating that God turned the community away from its opponents in order to test it — the same arrangement as in the account of Badr, where each army was made to appear small in the eyes of the other for the purpose of establishing what had been decreed. The verse refuses the reading that the community lost by accident: the turning aside is an arranged test, and the failure it exposed is precisely what the test was for. The wording keeps the community's responsibility intact while placing the outcome inside a purpose, the two ideas held in a single clause.

**The Pardon and the Bounty**

The verse then states that God pardoned the community — the pardon announced before the passage has finished describing the fault, though the conduct was grave, since disobeying an order in the middle of a battle endangers the whole force. The Book's pattern is to name the failure and the forgiveness together, even in the case of a defeat: the community is not left to conclude that its position has been forfeited; it is told the offence has been dealt with, and the correction that follows concerns its conduct rather than its standing. The closing statement — God is gracious to the believers — uses the word for generosity, favour, and the giving of more than is deserved, and its position at the end of a verse about failure is the point: the Book's descriptions of God's dealings with the believers frequently end this way, with the generosity named after the account of human weakness. What the community is meant to take is that its losses did not come from a shortage of divine favour — and that the favour had already produced the pardon announced in the same sentence.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:153

> ˹Remember˺ when you were running far away ˹in panic˺—not looking at anyone—while the Messenger was calling to you from behind! So Allah rewarded your disobedience with distress upon distress. Now, do not grieve over the victory you were denied or the injury you suffered. And Allah is All-Aware of what you do

**The Scene Reconstructed**

The verse describes the battlefield in the hour of the collapse: the believers moving away, refusing to look at anyone, while the Messenger of God called to them from behind — a picture assembled from three details, each adding to the account. The flight is presented without softening, and the sūrah reports it as a fact of its own history: the Book's willingness to describe the believers at their weakest moment is part of what gives the passage its authority, and the verse is addressed to the people who lived through the day.

**The Verb and the Terrain**

The verb describing the movement has the basic sense of going up, and al-Ṭabarī records a difference of opinion about its form: some held it means to travel along level ground, fitting the account of the men coming down from the high ground to the field; others took it as flight up the slopes of the mountain. Both readings describe the same disorder — on one, the community streams across open ground with the enemy behind it; on the other, it is scattered up the hillside. The disagreement is a reminder that the commentators worked with detailed reports of the terrain and positions, and cared about getting the picture right before drawing lessons from it.

**Looking at No One**

The phrase about not looking at anyone describes a person so absorbed in his own flight that he has no attention left for those around him — the commentators explain it as not caring about anyone else. The image is the opposite of the solidarity the sūrah has been urging since its command to hold fast together: the men on the field were not looking to one another, and the verse lets the picture carry the point without adding a reproach. What the community is shown is what its division looks like from the outside, at the moment it mattered most.

**The Call From Behind**

The reports describe the Prophet calling after the retreating men — an address to the servants of God, repeated, with the call to come back to him — and the verse records the call as coming from the rear, where the Messenger had remained with those who stood firm. The detail is among the most affecting in the sūrah: the person the community had come out to defend was calling it back while it fled, and the reports place him among a small group holding the position. The verse mentions the call in a subordinate clause and leaves the image to do its work.

**Distress Upon Distress**

The verse states that God recompensed the disobedience with one sorrow upon another, and the commentators offer several accounts of the two sorrows: on one reading, the grief of having caused the Prophet grief, and the loss suffered afterward; on another, the loss of the battle and the loss of the spoils, followed by the enemy's rejoicing; on a further reading, the defeat and then the fear that the enemy would return to finish what it had begun. Al-Rāzī adds that the retreat itself was a form of sorrow, since a person who runs away feels his own weakness. The variety reflects the density of the experience, and the verse's phrase holds all of them: the day delivered one grief after another, and the community was made to feel the weight of the whole.

**The Purpose Stated**

The verse attaches a purpose to the sorrow: so that the community would not grieve over what escaped it or what befell it — the loss of advantage and the loss of life both to be held in a scale that does not make them the measure of everything. Al-Rāzī explained the clause as a means of loosening the community's attachment to the goods of this world, so that it would neither be saddened by losing them nor gladdened by gaining them. Read this way, the sorrow of the day was itself an instruction, and the community's education in detachment came through losing what it had hoped to keep.

**The Discipline of Fear, and the Closing Awareness**

The account of the collapse treats the fear that produced it as a condition to be governed: the Book's commands about courage rest on the same ground as its commands about patience — the person who knows the outcome is in God's hands is not ruled by the prospect of loss. The community at Uḥud had been given a demonstration of what fear does to a body of people, and the sūrah preserves the record so later generations could learn from it: the lesson is not that the believers were cowards but that a failure of steadiness in one place spreads through everything that depends on it — and the verse supplies the sequence in which that happened. The closing statement that God is aware of everything the community does is not a threat appended to a narrative but the ground of the account: the Book knows what happened on the field, and what was in the people who ran and in the Messenger who called — and the community that recites the verse is told its conduct is seen by the One who pardoned it.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:154

> Then after distress, He sent down serenity in the form of drowsiness overcoming some of you, while others were disturbed by evil thoughts about Allah—the thoughts of ˹pre-Islamic˺ ignorance. They ask, "Do we have a say in the matter?" Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "All matters are destined by Allah." They conceal in their hearts what they do not reveal to you. They say ˹to themselves˺, "If we had any say in the matter, none of us would have come to die here." Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "Even if you were to remain in your homes, those among you who were destined to be killed would have met the same fate." Through this, Allah tests what is within you and purifies what is in your hearts. And Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart

**Security Sent Down After Sorrow**

The verse reports that after the sorrow God sent down security in the form of a sleepiness that settled over one company of the believers. The parallel with the earlier battles is exact: the account of Badr describes drowsiness overcoming the believers as a gift of serenity, together with rain sent down to purify them and steady their feet. The Book treats the ability to rest in the middle of a crisis as a sign of trust, and the account of Uḥud records the same gift arriving after the worst of the day had passed. The reports about the field describe believers falling asleep where they stood, with weapons slipping from their hands — a detail the tradition preserved because the state was so remarkable. What the verse presents is a physical mercy with a moral edge: the people whose nerves had been broken by the day were given rest, and the rest was sent by the same God whose help they had doubted.

**What the Sleep Signified**

Al-Rāzī discusses the phenomenon at length and reads the sleep as evidence that God was with the community, reporting a statement attributed to Ibn Masʿūd: sleeping during battle indicates trust in God and detachment from the world, while sleeping during the prayer indicates the opposite — because the community's situation in each case is different.

**The Company That Remained Anxious**

Beside the sleeping group the verse sets another company, anxious about itself and unable to rest. The two groups lay on the same field under the same conditions, and the difference between them is the subject of the sentence — the sūrah's way of distinguishing the community's members by what the crisis brought out of them. The verse does not name the second group directly, but the description that follows identifies it: people worrying about their own safety and about the outcome of a decision they had opposed, their anxiety expressing itself in the way they spoke about God.

**Thinking About God What Is Not True**

The anxious company is described as thinking about God thoughts that are not true — called the thinking of the Age of Ignorance, the Book's name for the period before the message arrived, and the comparison is severe. The commentators explain the thought as the belief that if the community had really been on God's side it would not have met such a setback; al-Rāzī develops the point at length, warning against judging the will of God by a person's own assessment of what is pleasant or painful. The verse identifies the error as a way of thinking about God, not a simple mistake about tactics.

**The Question About the Decision**

The anxious company is reported asking whether they had any say in the matter, and the answer is that the decision belongs entirely to God. The commentators differ about who asked — one view assigning the question to the hypocrites, another allowing that sincere believers might have asked it in bewilderment. However assigned, the answer stands on its own: the outcome of the expedition was never within the community's control, and the sentence removes the basis for the complaint that had begun to circulate. Al-Rāzī's comment on the verse includes a discussion of providence arguing that people should not claim to deduce the will of God from their own evaluations: human beings do not possess all the measures needed to judge whether an event is good or harmful, and success or failure on a battlefield is one of the clearest examples. A person's limited view of the consequences disqualifies him from reading the event as a verdict — a lesson applying beyond the case of the battle.

**The Claim Made in Secret, and the Answer**

The anxious group kept to themselves a claim they did not disclose: that if the decision had been theirs, none of them would have been killed there. The answer removes the premise: had the community stayed in its houses, those destined to be slain would have gone out to the places where they were to die. The sentence is one of the sūrah's clearest statements of the fixed term, matching the verse declaring that no soul dies except by God's permission at an appointed time — the location of a person's death is not the variable that determines it.

**The Purpose of the Test**

The verse closes by stating that the events were arranged so that God might test what was in the community's hearts and purify what was in their breasts — the test being not for the purpose of gaining information but of making the truth visible. The Book knows what a person conceals, and the events at Uḥud made the contents of those breasts visible to the community itself; the sūrah treats that exposure as one of the day's purposes, and what follows in the passage is the community's opportunity to act on what it has been shown about itself.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:155

> Indeed, those ˹believers˺ who fled on the day when the two armies met were made to slip by Satan because of their misdeeds. But Allah has pardoned them. Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing

**The Day the Two Hosts Met**

The verse refers to the day the two armies met — Uḥud, in the commentators' identification — and addresses those who turned away during the fighting. The retreat described followed the departure of the archers from the high ground, when the enemy's cavalry was able to work around the community's position. The verse does not repeat the details; it names the participants and then states what the community is to understand about what they did and how God dealt with them.

**Satan Made Them Slip**

The turning away is attributed to a slip caused by Satan, and the Book uses the same image in its account of the first human failure: in the story of Adam and his wife, Satan caused them to slip from the state they were in; and another passage warns of what follows if people slip after the clear proofs have come to them. The image describes a loss of footing rather than an intention to abandon the faith, keeping the offence in its proper scale: the act was real and had consequences, and the community had been warned against exactly this — while the sentence identifies the agent that caused the slip, in a way that leaves room for the pardon stated at the end.

**The Cause of the Slip**

The clause about the cause has been read in two ways. One takes it to mean Satan caused them to slip *in part* of what they had done — the failure confined to certain matters rather than general. The other treats the phrase as stating they slipped *because of* what they had previously done, the earlier offence opening the way to this one. Al-Rāzī records both and draws the lesson that one error tends to lead to another. The readings describe different aspects of the same event, and both agree that the community's own conduct had prepared the ground on which the slip occurred.

**Who Turned Away**

The commentators were not unanimous about the identity of those described. Al-Ṭabarī records several views: that they were believers who ran from the fighting; that they were people who had remained behind in the city; and that the group includes both those who fled toward the town and those who climbed the mountain. Another report names specific individuals among the community, with reasons given for their flight including fear and the rumour that the Prophet had been killed. The absence of agreement is itself instructive: the verse addresses a class of conduct rather than a list of names, describing what happened in terms that apply to anyone who does the same thing.

**Why the Reports About Individuals Matter**

The reports naming particular people — including men of standing — have been read by the commentators as evidence of the scope of God's forgiveness: the people who turned away that day were pardoned, and the pardon is stated in the same verse that records their failure. The commentators connect the case with the Book's account of Adam, whose repentance was accepted after his error (2:37). The comparison supports the passage's larger purpose — teaching a community recovering from failure that its mistakes do not have to define it, and that the remedy has been provided.

**The Pardon Without Delay**

The pardon is mentioned in the same sentence as the sin: the sūrah creates no period in which the community is left to wonder whether it has been forgiven. The arrangement matches the pattern of the passage — the disobedience, the quarrel, the flight, and the secrecy of the anxious group have each been accompanied by a statement that God pardoned. The effect is to keep the community's attention on the correction that follows rather than on a debt it cannot discharge.

**The Two Names That Close**

The verse ends with two names: the Forgiving and the Clement. The first describes the readiness to forgive; the second — forbearing, unhurried — describes the way the pardon is granted: not as an immediate reaction to a fault but as the settled disposition of the One who forgives. The pairing is used elsewhere of God's dealings with communities that have failed, and the community that had fled from a battle is told it has to do with a Lord whose forbearance gave it time to return. The verse teaches that a failure after faith can be pardoned — without removing the seriousness of the failure: the community is not told its flight was trivial; it is told what caused it and how God responded. That combination is the heart of the sūrah's treatment of Uḥud: a community had to be able to continue after a day that exposed its weaknesses, and the Book gives it both the diagnosis and the pardon — the verses that follow turning from the battle to the lessons to be carried out of it.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:156

> O believers! Do not be like the unfaithful who say about their brothers who travel throughout the land or engage in battle, "If they had stayed with us, they would not have died or been killed." Allah makes such thinking a cause of agony in their hearts. It is Allah who gives life and causes death. And Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

**The Prohibition and the Model Rejected**

The verse addresses the believers and forbids them to resemble those who deny in what they say about their dead — the comparison being the point: the community is shown a way of speaking about loss and told it does not belong to people of faith. The Book often teaches by such contrasts, and the instruction here is about speech rather than belief: what the community is told not to imitate is a habit of recording outcomes as though human decisions had controlled them, presented as characteristic of those who have not accepted the message. Al-Zamakhsharī noted the force of the phrase about the brethren: the words were spoken *on account of* them — because of what happened to those men, not as an abstract case. The grief was real, and the remark was the shape it took; the verse is a lesson about how grief expresses itself. The Book does not reproach the sorrow; it reproaches the analysis that turns the dead into victims of their own decision — the community is taught to hold its losses without adopting a framework in which caution would have preserved anyone.

**Who the Speakers Were**

The commentators identified the speakers in three ways: the disbelievers taken as a body, the hypocrites taken as a body, or ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy in particular, whose conduct earlier in the sūrah the passage has described (al-Qurṭubī; al-Rāzī). The Book does not settle which is intended, and the omission suits how the sūrah has handled similar remarks: the conduct is named, and the reader is left to recognise it in his own circle.

**Regret Made in the Hearts**

The verse states that God made such talk a source of regret in the hearts of those who said it, and the commentators offered several explanations. One holds the regret belongs to the speakers, a fitting recompense for words meant to discourage others; al-Rāzī noted the regret may also come on the Day of Judgment for having said such things at all. Another reading holds the purpose was to create doubt in the hearts of weaker believers, so the community's faith would be tested by the remarks it heard. The verse leaves room for both, and the difference concerns who is being corrected rather than what the correction is about.

**God Gives Life and Causes Death**

The sentence answering the claim is a statement of divine authority over life and death — the same statement the passage has made twice already: no soul dies except by God's permission at an appointed term, and those destined for death would have gone out to the places where they were to die. The repetition is deliberate: the community had just heard the fatalistic argument, and the Book answers it by returning to the fact it had already established — what was first a reminder about a fixed term becomes here a response to a claim about the dead. Al-Rāzī records a further, spiritual explanation: God gives life to the hearts of those close to Him and causes the hearts of His enemies to die — widening the verse's application without displacing its plain sense: the One who controls the physical outcome also controls the inner outcome, both governed by a single authority, and the claim made by the speakers was a claim about that authority.

**The Closing Statement About Seeing**

The verse ends by stating that God sees what the community does — returning to the theme that appeared at the end of the passage about the retreat and placing the community's conduct under the same observation. The Book's statements about its knowledge are frequently placed at the end of an instruction, and the placement gives them practical force: the community's speech about its dead, its conduct on the battlefield, and its treatment of the people who failed are all seen by the same God, and no part of its behaviour is outside the account. The prohibition also comes before the premise is answered: the sentences that follow turn to those slain in God's way, and the answer to the claim about staying behind is supplied there rather than in this verse.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:157

> Should you be martyred or die in the cause of Allah, then His forgiveness and mercy are far better than whatever ˹wealth˺ those ˹who stay behind˺ accumulate

**A Preference Stated in Plain Terms**

The verse states that if the believers are killed or die in the way of God, forgiveness and mercy from God are better than anything those who stay behind amass — a comparison between two collections, asking the community to weigh them. The Book uses this comparative form when it wants two things placed side by side in the reader's mind: the wealth the community's enemies were gathering was the visible measure of success in the world, and the verse puts God's forgiveness and mercy against it and states the second is greater. The sentence does not deny the existence of the first; it denies its weight.

**The Two Ways of Dying**

The verse names both dying in battle and dying in the ordinary course of events, and repeats the pairing in the following verse. The community had just watched men die at the hands of an enemy, and had also lost people in ways that had nothing to do with the fighting. The phrasing removes the distinction between the two in terms of the promise: what qualifies a death for the mercy described is not the manner of it but the cause in which the person was engaged — the community is taught to stop grading its losses by how much violence attended them.

**The Qualifier "In the Way of God"**

The promise attaches to a person who dies in the way of God, and the qualifier is the important element: the Book uses the phrase for conduct undertaken for God's sake, in its instructions about wealth and fighting alike. The community that lost people on the field at Uḥud was engaged in precisely that cause, and the verse tells it what the deaths of those men amounted to. The claim is not that death is good; it is that the cause in which a person dies determines what his death has earned — and the reward named here is forgiveness and mercy.

**What "Amassing" Covers**

Ibn Kathīr read the word for what is gathered as covering wealth in the narrow sense and, more broadly, the accumulation of the world's vanities — widening the contrast the verse draws. The comparison is stated as a fact rather than a recommendation: the believer is not told to despise what others collect, only to measure it against what is promised to those who die in God's cause. The verse answers the fear that the deaths of the community's members were a loss, and the fuller account of the state of the slain comes a dozen verses later, where the Book states they are alive with their Lord and provided for (3:169) — the two passages read together as a single answer to the same anxiety.

**Forgiveness Named Before Mercy**

The two nouns stand in an order — forgiveness first, mercy second — reflecting the movement a person makes from his own record to his final state: the faults are forgiven, and the mercy follows. The Book pairs the two often, including in this sūrah's passages about the people whose reward is forgiveness from their Lord. The community reading the verse in the aftermath of a defeat hears the two words attached to the men it had lost, and the sentence gives it a way to speak about them that does not depend on the fortunes of the day.

**The Reply to the Talk About Staying Home**

The verse answers the argument reported in the preceding sentence, in which a person's death was attributed to his decision to go out. The reply is not that the decision was insignificant, but that the outcome of a life consecrated to God is not on that scale. The community was being offered a way of thinking in which caution was the highest value, and the verse replaces it with a comparison of a different unit entirely: what is at stake is not whether a person returns safely but whether what he has done for God is met with forgiveness and mercy — and the sentence states the second is worth more than everything the other side gathers. The Book expands the promise in the verses that follow, telling the community not to consider those killed in the way of God as dead, and describing those who believed and emigrated and struggled with their wealth and their lives as greater in rank with God. The comparison is not a consolation offered after the fact; it is a judgement about value the community is asked to adopt in advance, so that the losses it meets do not shake the estimate on which its conduct depends.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:158

> Whether you die or are martyred—all of you will be gathered before Allah

**The Gathering That Follows Death**

The verse states that whether the believers are killed or die, they will be gathered to God — taking the two ways of dying the previous verse named and giving both a single destination. The Book uses the vocabulary of gathering and return for death in many places, and the choice shapes how the community thinks about what happens at the end of a life: a person who is returned to someone is not extinguished; he moves into the presence of the One who sent him, and the sentence treats the two kinds of death as the same departure.

**The Book's Recurring Statement**

The Book repeats the statement about the return to God in passages about death: one states that every soul will taste death, that humanity is tested with good and with evil, and that all will be returned to God; another states the same sequence in fewer words — every soul will taste death, and then to God all will be returned. This sūrah makes the same teaching its own way in a later verse, stating that every soul will taste death and that the full reward will be given on the Day of Judgment. The claim here belongs to the Book's basic instruction about human destiny, restated at the point where the community needed to hear it most clearly.

**Why the Sentence Repeats the Earlier One**

The verse begins in almost the same words as the verse before it, and the repetition is one of the sūrah's methods: the community had heard that forgiveness and mercy are better than what the enemies of God amass; it is now told where the departed have gone. The Book's repetitions are not idle — each rests on the same truth and draws a different consequence from it, and this one supplies the fact that makes the comparison in the previous sentence reasonable: mercy and forgiveness can be compared with wealth only if the person who dies has not simply ceased to exist. The language of returning, being gathered, and going to meet God each carries the sense of movement toward a person rather than movement into nothing — vocabulary that shapes the community's attitude at funerals and in grief.

**With the Earlier Warning About the Appointed Term**

The sūrah has already stated that no soul dies except by God's permission at a decreed term; this verse complements that statement by naming what follows the appointed end. Together the two sentences leave no room for the fatalistic argument the community had been hearing: the first removes the possibility that human decisions control the timing of a death; the second states that the timing is not the end of the matter — the Book's treatment of death in this passage answers the argument from both directions.

**The Reward That Awaits**

The Book describes the state of those who die in the way of God in several places, and the descriptions are consistently of life rather than loss: those killed in God's cause should not be thought of as dead, since they are alive with their Lord and provided for; whoever leaves his home for the cause of God and dies in the course of it has his reward secured with God. Read with the verse before this one, the sentences form a single teaching: the departure is real and the destination is known. The community had seen its dead carried away, and the verse tells it where those men were going; the sentence does not describe what awaits them in detail — the following verses supply more, and the community's confidence rests on the direction of the movement rather than a picture of its stages.

**A Sentence the Community Carries**

The verse is short enough to be held in memory, and the community has used it in its burial practices and its consolations — a brevity deliberate in the Book, since a sentence to be spoken at the graveside needs to be carried easily. What the sentence provides is a fact rather than a sentiment: it does not tell the mourner how to feel; it tells him where the person has gone. The community's grief is not denied by the verse, and the sentence gives it a direction to face — which is what a grieving community most needs from its revelation, a direction being more useful than an argument. The two verses also mark the passage's shift from the failures of the battle to the destiny of those who die in God's cause: a community shown its faults also needs to know that its dead are not casualties of those faults, and the Book supplies the assurance before it resumes its instructions — the verses that follow turning to the Prophet's own gentleness and the community's practice of consultation.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:159

> It is out of Allah's mercy that you ˹O Prophet˺ have been lenient with them. Had you been cruel or hard-hearted, they would have certainly abandoned you. So pardon them, ask Allah's forgiveness for them, and consult with them in ˹conducting˺ matters. Once you make a decision, put your trust in Allah. Surely Allah loves those who trust in Him

**The Gentleness Attributed to Mercy**

The verse states that it was by a mercy from God that the Prophet was gentle with the community, placing his treatment of the men who had disobeyed within the working of divine mercy. Al-Ṭabarī identified the people concerned as the companions and followers who had fallen short during the battle — so the sentence is not only a general observation about his character but a statement about how he dealt with the specific failure the sūrah has just described. The Prophet had every ground for harshness after the day's events; the verse names what he showed instead.

**What Would Have Happened Otherwise**

Had the Prophet been severe and hard-hearted, the community would have scattered from around him: the word for severity describes a manner harsh in its treatment of people; the hardness of heart describes one unmoved by their condition. The observation is practical wisdom about leadership, stated as a counterfactual: had the man who was disobeyed responded with contempt, the community would have broken apart at the moment it needed to hold together. The verse gives the alternative its full weight, then records what actually happened.

**Three Instructions**

The verse issues three instructions — pardon them, ask forgiveness for them, consult them in the matter — moving from the wrongs committed against the Prophet, to the wrongs committed against God, to the business the community shared. Al-Rāzī explained the division: the first concerns what the people had done to the Prophet himself; the second, what they had done against God; and the third reaches beyond fault altogether and returns the community to the work it was assembled for — a mark of how completely the failure was to be left behind.

**Consultation**

The instruction to consult arrives after the community's most conspicuous failure, and the sūrah had described the consultation that preceded the battle: the Prophet asked the Muslims of Madinah whether to remain in the city or march out, and the decision to go out prevailed. The outcome did not lead to the abandonment of the practice — the Prophet is told to seek the community's judgment in the very kind of matter that had just gone wrong: a defeat is not a reason to abandon a sound method. The commentators restricted the command to matters of war and ordinary affairs, since revelation governed the rest; al-Qurṭubī and others record — and reject — the view that consultation was superfluous because the Prophet received revelation, noting that he consulted his contemporaries, men and women alike, on many matters. One report has the Prophet saying that the people knew more about the affairs of this world than he did — the consultation substantive rather than a formality. Al-Rāzī suggested the practice was intended to establish consultation as a settled practice of the community after him; and the Book names consultation among the marks of the believers elsewhere, describing people who respond to their Lord, establish the prayer, conduct their affairs by mutual consultation, and give from what they have been provided. The community is not to be governed by the impulse of a single person, however well placed; and the sūrah places the command in the middle of the battle passage — the consultative practice survived the test of the day that exposed everything else.

**Resolve and Trust**

The final instruction concerns what happens after consultation ends: once decided, the Prophet is told to place his trust in God — a reliance that does not rest on the quality of the deliberation. Al-Qurṭubī explained the sequence as a decision made after consultation, followed by trust in God rather than in the process. The Book states elsewhere that whoever places his trust in God will find Him sufficient, and the verse is among the Book's clearest statements of the combination: the human process, and the reliance that accompanies it. The closing declaration — God loves those who trust — gives the instruction its motivation: trust is not merely a practical attitude but one of the qualities the Book attaches to divine love, and the passage's series of instructions — obey, stand firm, consult, rely — closes with the last of these bringing a person under the love of God.

**The Gentleness in the Book's Larger Portrait**

The gentleness described here is a theme the Book states about the Prophet elsewhere: the sūrah of Repentance describes a messenger from among the community, grieved by their hardship, anxious over them, gracious and merciful to the believers (9:128); the sūrah of the Heights gives the command in its shortest form — hold to graciousness, enjoin what is right, and turn away from those who act ignorantly (7:199). Al-Rāzī quotes a saying attributed to ʿAlī on the same subject: the community had been idolaters, and if the religion had come to them all at once, with its obligations all at once, the burden would have been too heavy — the Prophet instead called them to a single word and led them on, word after word, until the religion was complete. The gentleness of this verse is not a concession granted after the battle but the manner in which the message was carried, and the instructions that follow are given in the same register.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:160

> If Allah helps you, none can defeat you. But if He denies you help, then who else can help you? So in Allah let the believers put their trust

**The Two Conditions Stated Together**

The verse sets the help of God against His withholding of help, and states the consequence of each: if God helps the community, nothing can overcome it; if He abandons it, no one else can take His place. The second clause carries the weight — the community at that moment was calculating its strength in numbers, allies, and the quality of its position, and the verse reduces the calculation to a single term: the help it needed was not the kind that could be secured by arrangement.

**The Truth of the First Clause**

The first clause is stated as an absolute: when God helps, none can overcome. The Book's promises of this kind are always connected in the surrounding passages to the community's own conduct, and this verse does not suspend the connection: the community had been helped at Badr with everything the two clauses describe, and had been shown at Uḥud what happens when the connection is broken. The sentence does not promise that every engagement ends in victory; it states who controls the outcome, and leaves the community's responsibilities where the earlier verses placed them.

**The Reading That Sees the Two Battles**

The commentators frequently read the two clauses as a description of the community's recent history: God helped the believers at Badr, and withheld His help at Uḥud — the verse then a statement of the difference between the two days rather than a conditional promise. For the first listeners, who had lived through both days within a year, the reading gives the sentence particular force: the sūrah had already explained the change in terms of the community's own conduct, and this verse states the result in terms of help received or withheld, without softening the contrast.

**The Closing Question's Two Readings**

The phrase rendered "after Him" can be read two ways: as referring to time — who could help once God has withheld His help — or with the pronoun referring to God Himself — who could help apart from Him. Al-Rāzī records the second, which harmonises the verse with the sūrah's declaration that God is the believers' Guardian and best helper. On either reading the question is unanswerable, and the sentence's function is to leave the community no alternative to reliance on God. The Book states the same thought elsewhere: it asks whether those invoked besides God could undo a harm if He willed it, or withhold a mercy if He willed that; and it reports the believers' declaration that nothing will befall them except what God has destined, that He is their protector, and that in Him they trust. This verse states the principle in the form of questions, and the community is left to answer them.

**The Command to Trust**

The verse closes by instructing the believers to place their trust in God — the practical conclusion of the two questions. Trust in the Book's usage is not the abandonment of effort: the sūrah has just commanded consultation, preparation, and firmness. Trust is what accompanies a person's work once he has done what he can; the Book names it among the marks of the believers in a passage describing hearts trembling at the remembrance of God and faith increasing when His revelations are recited. The community's task is to act and then leave the outcome with the One who holds it.

**A Verse Recited Throughout Life**

The sentence has been carried by Muslims in every generation as a statement of where reliance belongs, used in circumstances far from a battlefield — its usefulness in ordinary life coming from its structure: it disposes of the question of who controls outcomes before any particular difficulty is described.
