The community's teachers placed this verse at the head of their instructions about trust because it gives the doctrine in a form recitable in a single breath, which is what a person needs in a moment of fear.

**The Close of the Passage's Movement**

The verse closes the passage that began with the rumour of the Prophet's death: the community has been told that the messenger would die, that death comes at an appointed time, that the day's failures were pardoned, and that its dead were gathered to God — and is now told where help comes from. The sequence is the sūrah's characteristic movement, from crisis to lessons to a statement about God that reorders the community's understanding of its position. And the sentence stands as the last in the chapter's chain of statements about help: promised with conditions (3:124–125), granted when the community was weak (3:123), attributed to God alone (3:126), fulfilled and apparently withdrawn in a test (3:152). By the time the reader arrives here, help has been promised, granted, conditioned, and withdrawn — and the two clauses stand apart from all of it as a statement about agency itself: assistance is not a resource that appears and disappears but an act of a single will, and the second clause names the consequence for anyone who looks elsewhere. The sentence anchors the community's reading of its own history, whether victory or defeat.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:161

> It is not appropriate for a prophet to illegally withhold spoils of war. And whoever does so, it will be held against them on the Day of Judgment. Then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged

**The Statement About the Prophet**

The verse declares that it is not for any prophet to defraud — a defence of the Prophet's integrity in a matter where it had been questioned. The sūrah addresses the subject in the aftermath of a battle, when property had changed hands and accusations were circulating: the commentators connect the verse with the distribution of the spoils and with reports that an item of value had gone missing, after which some suggested the Prophet himself might have taken it. Al-Ṭabarī's preferred reading is that the verse was revealed simply to inform people that such a thing does not happen with a prophet.

**What the Verb Covers**

The verb is used specifically for taking part of what should be shared out, and the commentators treated the verse as dealing with the spoils above all: some held it concerned a valuable piece of cloth lost after one of the battles; others, the failure to distribute spoils equitably. The discussions show how seriously the community treated the smallest misappropriation of common property — and the verse's scope was then extended to cover every kind of embezzlement and misuse of a trust, since the principle forbidding a prophet to take what is not his must apply even more plainly to anyone lower in station.

**The Different Occasions Reported**

Beyond the reports about the spoils, some connected the verse with the archers who left their positions and were given no share of the spoils they had been trying to secure; others with expeditions whose spoils were distributed before the fighters returned. Al-Ṭabarī records a further view that the verse has nothing to do with spoils at all, forbidding the Prophet instead to withhold any part of the revelation entrusted to him. The variety does not affect the verse's meaning, since each occasion concerns a trust and the taking of what is not one's own — and the fact that so many were proposed shows how directly the sentence spoke to the community's situation, and how many of its problems touched the same principle.

**What the Fraudster Brings on the Day of Judgment**

Whoever defrauds will bring what he took on the Day of Resurrection — a concrete image: the misappropriated thing accompanies its taker into the presence of God. The commentators quote reports describing people arriving that day with the objects they had wrongfully taken hanging from their necks, matching the sūrah's later verse about the miserly, who will be collared on the Day of Resurrection by what they hoarded. The imagery gives property a weight that follows its possessor, and the sentence is designed to make a person think about what he will be carrying.

**The Payment Made in Full**

The verse then states that every soul will be paid in full for what it has earned and will not be wronged — widening the view from the specific offence to the general settlement. The Book uses this formula at the close of its instructions about property because the fear of loss and the desire for gain shape more of human conduct than most people admit: the account will be accurate and complete, and the phrase about no one being wronged answers the anxiety that a person might lose what he deserves.

**The Reports About Public Trusts**

The commentators connect the verse with reports about officials collecting alms who returned saying part of the property was the community's and part had been given to them as a gift. The Prophet's response, according to those reports, was to ask whether the gift would have come to the man had he stayed in his father's or mother's house. The question exposes the practice as a concealed payment for a public office; the verse's principle reaches such cases directly, and the tradition has used it as the ground for its rules about gifts to officials, just as against the taking of anything that belongs to the common fund.

**What the Verse Teaches About Leadership**

The sentence begins with the word for *any* prophet — the office itself excludes such conduct: the person who conveys God's message is entrusted with the message and the community's affairs, and the verse removes him from any suspicion of serving his own interest. The community that hears the sentence is told what to expect from its leaders and what to refuse to believe of them without grounds: the sūrah corrects a rumour and establishes a principle at once — and the next verse contrasts two figures so the principle can be seen in human terms.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:162

> Are those who seek Allah's pleasure like those who deserve Allah's wrath? Hell is their home. What an evil destination

**Two Figures Side by Side**

The verse asks whether the person who pursues God's contentment is like the person who earns a burden of God's anger, whose refuge is the Fire. The question expects a negative answer, and the sentence is built to make the difference visible. The Book uses such comparisons in several places, and the commentators gathered the passages where the righteous and the wicked are set against each other in the same form — sentences that are not arguments in the technical sense: they present two conditions and leave the reader to judge whether they could possibly be the same.

**The One Who Pursues Contentment**

The phrase describes a person whose aim is the pleasure of God, and the Book attaches that aim to specific conduct: keeping a trust, speaking the truth, spending from what one has, holding to an agreement. The verb carries the sense of seeking and following after what is desired, making the pursuit continuous rather than a single decision — not one who happened to please God on an occasion, but one in the business of seeking that pleasure. The sūrah has just described what such conduct looked like on the field where the community's trust was tested.

**The One Who Earns a Burden of Anger**

The second figure is described as returning with anger from God laid upon him — the word for what he earns describing something borne, a load carried away. The Book uses the same expression of those among the earlier communities who earned anger. The pairing is exact in structure and total in contrast: one person goes toward something he seeks; the other comes away with something he brought upon himself. The verse places the two directions in one sentence without adding any argument, the answer left to the reader's judgement.

**The Destination Named**

The verse states that the second figure's refuge is the Fire and calls it an evil journey's end: the word for refuge describes the place a person ends up in and settles into; the word for the journey's end describes what an undertaking finally produces. The two terms describe a movement completed — the person who spends his life in pursuit of what is other than God arrives somewhere, and the verse names the destination in the imagery the sūrah has used through this passage. The exclamation closing the sentence is one of the Book's standard ways of marking a matter as beyond dispute.

**The Contrast as the Book's Method**

The Book's teaching by contrast appears throughout this sūrah — the two companies on the field, the two desires in the community, the two faces on the Day of Resurrection — and this verse gathers those contrasts into a single question. The method suits a community that had just seen both kinds of people among its own members: the comparison is not between the community and its enemies alone but between two possible directions for a human life, both shown at close range within a few days. The Book's language about anger adds weight: the person seeking God's contentment works toward an aim that can be achieved; the person who has earned anger carries an outcome that follows from what he has done — the two presented not as feelings but as results as different as the destinations named.

**What the Question Leaves to the Next Verse**

The verse states the contrast and stops, the implied decision left to the reader — the Book's way of ending arguments by presenting the two paths and letting the plain sense do the work. The next verse answers the question's premise directly: the two groups are not equal in rank with God. And the verse describes a life in terms of what it pursues — the word for contentment covering the approval a person seeks in what he does, giving shape to the ordinary details of his dealings. The community at Madinah had just been through an examination of exactly this kind: the spoils, the positions on the hill, the question of who took what — an afternoon in which aims had shown themselves, and the verse setting the two aims side by side was written for people who had seen both at work.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:163

> They ˹each˺ have varying degrees in the sight of Allah. And Allah is All-Seeing of what they do

**The Statement of Rank**

The verse states that the two groups are ranked in degrees with God, closing with the statement that God sees what they do — answering the previous verse's question by denying the equality it had proposed. The commentators read the ranking in two ways: al-Ṭabarī observed that the two groups stand at different levels in this world, since their conduct and the conditions it produces differ; al-Rāzī read the verse as a statement about the next life, where the punished occupy different levels of suffering just as the people of the Garden occupy different levels of delight.

**Degrees and the Book's Teaching About Reward**

The Book states repeatedly that people will be dealt with according to what they have done, and the language of degrees is one of its ways of expressing the principle: each will be assigned ranks according to his deeds, and God will pay every soul in full for what it has earned. The sūrah of the Cattle states the principle plainly — for all there are degrees according to what they did, with the reminder that God is not unaware of what they do (6:132); the sūrah of the Sand Dunes states that all will be ranked according to what they did, so that they may be repaid in full, with no one wronged (46:19). The vocabulary covers reward and punishment alike, showing the Book does not divide humanity into two flat categories — and the difference people see now is a shadow of the difference that will be assigned later.

**Why Degrees Rather Than a Single Division**

The idea has a practical dimension: if the outcome were a single division, a person who had secured his place would have no reason to improve it, and a person who had made one mistake no reason to recover. The statement that the ranks differ maintains both incentives, matching the Book's frequent exhortation to compete in good works. And the phrase that the ranks are *with God* places the scale of measurement outside the community's own estimates — the same construction the Book uses in commending those who strive with their wealth and their lives as greater in rank in the sight of God. The phrase matters because human communities construct hierarchies from wealth, descent, and reputation; the verse states that the ranking that counts is held elsewhere, and the sūrah has been demonstrating the difference between the two scales throughout its account of the community's history.

**The Statement That God Sees What They Do**

The closing sentence is one the sūrah has used at several points, and the repetition connects the passage's theme of divine observation to the subject of ranks: the God who ranks the two groups is the God who sees what each does. The Book's statements about knowledge are frequently attached to instructions and warnings in this way, making the sentence practical rather than abstract: no conduct is invisible, and the scale is applied by the One who has full information about the lives it is applied to.

**The Contrast With Human Judgement**

The sūrah's account of the battle included the community's own arguments about who had acted correctly. This verse places the assessment of conduct in God's hands and does not invite the community to settle the question for itself: the Book's consistent teaching is that the judgement belongs to God, and the community is warned elsewhere against claiming purity for itself — the sentence leaves the community free to attend to its own conduct rather than compare itself with others. The commentators also noted the reading on which the sentence is a general affirmation that reality is arranged in levels, connected with the Book's descriptions of gradations among creatures — a claim about the order of creation and the One who ordered it, which extends the sentence's range beyond the immediate contrast without displacing its primary application.

**The Verse's Function**

The verse is short and stands between the comparison of the two figures and the reminder of the favour God has shown the believers; its brevity suits its function — closing the comparison without elaborating it. The passage moves from the question about trust and defrauding, to the two destinies, and then to the favour of the message itself; this sentence marks the end of the contrast and prepares the shift to gratitude, leaving the community with the reminder of the scale on which its conduct will be weighed.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:164

> Indeed, Allah has done the believers a ˹great˺ favour by raising a messenger from among them—reciting to them His revelations, purifying them, and teaching them the Book and wisdom. For indeed they had previously been clearly astray

**The Favour Named at the Outset**

The verse states that God conferred a favour on the believers by sending them a messenger from among themselves, placing the Prophet's coming at the head of everything the community had received — the word for favour covering a gift and an act of kindness, used by the Book for its weightiest blessings. The choice of term matters after the passage just ended: the community had been shown its failures in detail, and the verse now directs its attention to the gift that made its position what it was.

**A Messenger From Among Themselves**

The phrase describing the messenger as one of the community's own was what its opponents found hardest to accept: the Book reports the objections of those who asked whether they should follow a human being like themselves, and the reasoning behind their refusal in several passages. The verse presents the very fact they objected to as the favour: a messenger drawn from the community could speak its language, know its conditions, and share its life — and the Book elsewhere describes the Prophet as grieved by the believers' hardship and merciful toward them. The gift consisted partly in the closeness of the messenger to the people he was sent to; the verse makes the cause of the opponents' objection into the reason for the believers' gratitude.

**Reciting, Purifying, Teaching**

The sentence names the messenger's works in order. First, recitation of God's signs — the delivery of the revelation in the form it was received, the community's first relationship with the message being through hearing it; the sūrah of the Congregation uses the same sequence in describing the messenger raised among the unlettered people, and the recitation comes first because everything else rests on it. Then purification and instruction: the word for purification describes the cleansing of a person from what defiles him in conduct and character; the word for wisdom describes the understanding that accompanies knowledge. The order is instructive — the Book's arrival changes the people it addresses before it informs them, and the tradition reads the sequence as the moral and intellectual formation the message produced in a community that had possessed neither.

**The Book and Wisdom Together**

The pairing appears in several passages about the messenger's task: the prayer of Abraham for a messenger who would recite the signs, teach the Book and wisdom, and purify the people; and the description in the sūrah of the Cow of the messenger sent in nearly the same words. The recurrence links the mission of Muhammad with the supplication of the patriarch from whom the community traced its descent — the Book presents his coming as the answer to a prayer recorded in its own earlier passages, and this verse states the fulfilment as a favour to the believers.

**"In Clear Error Before"**

The verse closes by describing the community's condition before the messenger came: in clear error — the Book's word for straying, a confusion from which a person cannot find his way without help. The description is not a reproach but a measure of the change: the sūrah had already referred to the community's history before the message, and the sentence gives the contrast its full extent. The community's standing, whatever its lapses, was the result of a transformation it had not produced for itself, and the verse names the source.

**What the Verse Asks**

The opening words about favour set the tone for the community's response, which the sūrah develops in the verses that follow: gratitude for the message and obedience to the messenger are the same thing in the Book's presentation, and the sentence establishes the ground on which the community's obligations rest. The surrounding passage has dealt with failures, losses, and required conduct; the sentence about the messenger's coming places the whole account within a benefit already received — and the effect is to make the obligations that follow read as the natural response to a gift rather than burdens imposed on a failing people. The favour is stated in the past tense, as something already given, and the community's task is to live in a way that answers it.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:165

> Why is it when you suffered casualties ˹at Uḥud˺—although you had made your enemy suffer twice as much ˹at Badr˺—you protested, "How could this be?" Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "It is because of your disobedience." Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything

**The Question Asked After the Defeat**

The verse addresses the community about the affliction that had befallen it, noting that it had already inflicted a wound twice as large on its opponents, and reports the question people were asking: how did this come about? The Book reports the question and supplies the answer in its own words — the askers were trying to reconcile their losses with the promise of help, and the verse takes up the difficulty directly rather than leaving it unanswered. The reports explain that the question arose from the rout during the battle, and was sometimes phrased with an implication: if this is a true prophet, why did the defeat take place? The verse treats the question as a serious difficulty and answers it without impugning the questioner.

**The Arithmetic Behind "Twice as Much"**

The commentators worked out the comparison. Al-Ṭabarī reports that the believers lost seventy men at Uḥud, while at Badr they killed about seventy of the idolaters and took seventy more as prisoners. Al-Qurṭubī added that the prisoners belong in the total because their lives were at the community's disposal — had they been executed, the number would have reached a hundred and forty. Another explanation: the community had routed its opponents twice — once at Badr, once in the early fighting at Uḥud — while being routed only once. The differences concern how the accounting is done; the verse's point survives all of them: the community's losses were smaller than those it had inflicted.

**The Answer Given**

The affliction came from the community itself, and the answer is stated as a command to the Prophet to say it. The Book repeats the principle elsewhere — whatever good befalls a person is from God, and whatever evil befalls him is from himself — and the sentence is one of the most direct statements of responsibility in the Book. The verse does not attribute the defeat to the enemy's strength or to fate; it attributes it to the conduct of the people who suffered it.

**The Conduct That Brought the Loss**

The commentators identify the conduct in ways that all point to the same events: some held the community was afflicted because it ransomed the prisoners of Badr rather than settling the matter there, obtaining worldly wealth in exchange for a military advantage; others held the cause was the disobedience of the archers — leaving their positions, pursuing the spoils, or fleeing later in the day. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record these views without settling on one. What they share is that the loss followed from decisions the community had made, and the verse's answer holds all of them.

**Why the Answer Was Necessary**

A community promised help and then defeated had two ways of explaining what happened: the promise had failed, or the community had failed — and the verse states the second. The first conclusion would have destroyed the community's confidence in its message; the second preserved the message and directed attention to the community's own conduct, the only one of the two factors it could do something about. The sūrah's whole treatment of the battle follows this line, and this verse states it in the plainest available terms. The verse has been read by later generations as a general principle: a community's setbacks cannot be blamed on its enemies alone, and the Book directs it to examine its own conduct first — the question asked after Uḥud being one every generation asks after its own losses.

**The Closing Declaration**

The verse closes by stating that God is powerful over all things, placing the community's conduct and its consequences within the scope of that power — the declaration the Book uses at the end of passages about mercy and punishment alike. It prepares for the verses that follow, which describe what happened to those who stood firm and to those who fell: the losses and the victories alike are held inside the same power, and the declaration prevents either from being read as an accident.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:166

> So what you suffered on the day the two armies met was by Allah's Will, so that He might distinguish the ˹true˺ believers

**The Wound Attributed to God's Permission**

The verse states that what befell the community on the day the two hosts met happened by God's permission, and adds that the event served the purpose of making the believers known — the sūrah's summary of the battle's place in the divine arrangement. The wording sits beside the answer given a verse earlier, that the affliction came from the community itself. The two statements are not in competition: the Book holds them together, and the commentators treat the pair as the complete account of the field — the community's conduct produced the loss, and the loss occurred within a permission God had granted. The sentence also guards against reading the defeat as abandonment: what happened took place within a permission, not outside the knowledge of God.

**Reconciling Permission With Responsibility**

The Book states elsewhere that whatever good comes to a person is from God and whatever evil comes to him is from himself, and it uses the same division in this sūrah. The two halves describe different aspects of a single event — the good granted; the harm the consequence of conduct. The commentators applied the distinction carefully, since a community hearing only the first half drifts toward fatalism and one hearing only the second toward despair. This verse supplies the first half for a community already given the second, and the pairing is the Book's usual way of teaching its people to hold both truths at once.

**What Permission Means**

The term describes the granting of leave, stating that the event happened within the scope of God's will rather than outside it — the Book using the term for many kinds of event, from the arrival of rain to the delivery of a message. One passage states that no calamity or blessing occurs on the earth or in a person's own self without being recorded before it is brought into being; this verse makes the same claim about the day of fighting: the community's losses were not an interruption of the arrangement but a part of it, and the permission under which they occurred was not withdrawn afterward.

**The Purpose Stated**

The clause about knowing the believers uses the expression the sūrah applied to the battle earlier, and the commentators explained it the same way: the knowledge described is the kind that becomes evident, not the kind acquired. The community had learned from the day who among its members would hold a position under fire and who would not; the verse states that this disclosure was one of the purposes the event served. What the community received, along with its losses, was a clearer understanding of itself — and the sūrah treats the understanding as part of what the day was for. The arrangement of the two clauses makes the disclosure a purpose of the wound rather than an incidental result: the community that understood the sentence would stop reading its losses as evidence of abandonment and start reading them as information about itself — the reading the rest of the passage supports.

**The Day the Two Hosts Met**

The phrase naming the two hosts is the sūrah's standard way of referring to Uḥud, appearing in the passage about those who turned away. The wording keeps both sides of the engagement in view and presents the battle as a meeting of two bodies rather than a disaster that struck the community alone — keeping readers from reading the day as an isolated misfortune: what happened was a battle between two gathered forces, and the fact that one suffered more than it expected is stated in the same passage that explains why.

**The Consolation, and the Sequence**

The sentence is not a reproach. The verse immediately before it told the community where its affliction came from, and this sentence places the same event within a purpose God had willed — the sūrah's method of consolation after the battle: the community is not allowed to excuse itself, and not allowed to conclude the loss was meaningless. The next verse completes the statement by naming the other group the day had exposed, giving the community the full picture of what its wounds accomplished. The verse stands at the head of a short passage running through the statements about the hypocrites, the challenge to those who claimed the dead could have been saved, and the declaration about the martyrs — supplying the frame for all three, the order moving from the general arrangement to the particular conduct.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:167

> and expose the hypocrites. When it was said to them, "Come fight in the cause of Allah or ˹at least˺ defend yourselves," they replied, "If we had known there was fighting, we would have definitely gone with you." They were closer to disbelief than to belief on that day—for saying with their mouths what was not in their hearts. Allah is All-Knowing of what they hide

**The Second Group Named**

The verse continues the purpose stated in the verse before it and names the hypocrites as the other group the day exposed — the construction treating the two groups as outcomes of the same event, the sūrah having distinguished them since its account of the march began. The pairing shows what a trial does in a community: the same hardship that showed who among the believers would stand also showed who had never been committed, and the verse states both results in consecutive sentences without softening either.

**The Call to Come and Fight**

The verse reports the appeal: come, fight in the way of God or defend. The commentators explain the second alternative in several ways: some read it as a call to fight in self-defence if they would not fight for God's cause, so they would at least not be counted among the cowards; al-Ṭabarī understood it as a call to remain on the field and form ranks, even if no fighting followed, so the community's numbers would appear greater to the enemy. All the readings describe the same intention: the community needed bodies in its line, and the group was being asked for the minimum.

**The Excuse They Offered**

The answer recorded is: had we known fighting, we would have followed you. The commentators connect the exchange with the withdrawal of the leader of the hypocrites and about three hundred others before the fighting began — the event the sūrah mentioned in its account of the army's departure, which thinned the community's ranks at the moment when numbers mattered most (al-Qurṭubī; al-Rāzī; the commentaries give the number without a chain the sūrah's editors have examined). The excuse's content is a claim about their own judgement rather than their loyalty: they presented their departure as a correct assessment of the situation, and the verse records the claim so the community could see the reasoning for what it was. Al-Rāzī considered two ways of taking the statement: either they genuinely believed no fighting would take place and expected only a standoff, or they expected that if anything happened it would be a massacre rather than a battle, and their departure was prompted by the certainty of defeat. Both readings describe an attitude the verse calls hypocritical rather than sincere: the group joined the expedition and then left it on the basis of a private calculation.

**"Closer to Disbelief Than to Belief"**

The verse states that on that day they were closer to disbelief than to belief, and al-Rāzī draws a careful lesson from the wording: the Book does not call a person who has pronounced the testimony of faith a disbeliever — even here it describes the group as nearer to one than the other, not assigned to disbelief outright. Other commentators took the sentence to mean the group's outward conduct was nearer to disbelief while their inward state was disbelief altogether. The two readings differ over how the sentence should be applied, and the discipline the first recommends has been influential in the community's treatment of those who profess the faith while falling short of it.

**Saying With Their Mouths What Was Not in Their Hearts**

The phrase is the Book's standard description of hypocrisy: it appears in this sūrah's warning against taking confidants from outside the community, and in the sūrahs dealing with the groups in Madinah who watched the community's fortunes before committing themselves. The Book's descriptions of such people are consistent — they speak the language of the community while their loyalties remain available to whoever seems to be winning. The verse places the group's conduct at Uḥud in that category, and the report of their answer provides the evidence.

**God Knows What They Conceal**

The closing statement returns to the theme running through the passage: the community could see the withdrawal and hear the excuse; the verse states that the full contents of the matter were known to God. The Book attaches this reminder to its descriptions of hypocrites in many places, removing the possibility that a person might manage his appearance while keeping his conduct out of account — the assessment of the day's events is not limited to what was visible on the field.

**Conduct and Commentary, One Portrait**

The verse's naming of the group connects two points in the narrative: the reader is shown the men leaving, hears the excuse they gave, and then watches them judge the outcome of a battle they took no part in — the sūrah's attention returning to the same people in the verses that follow, where their words about the dead are taken up and answered, and their condition is stated once more among those who failed on the day. The treatment is severe and also explanatory: the group's reasoning is set out so the community could recognise it in future.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:168

> Those who sat at home, saying about their brothers, "Had they listened to us, they would not have been killed." Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ "Try not to die if what you say is true!"

**The Argument Restated**

The verse reports a group saying of their brothers, while they themselves sat at home, that had those brothers obeyed them they would not have been killed — the same claim the sūrah answered earlier in its account of the people who spoke about those who had travelled or campaigned. The repetition is deliberate: the community was told not to resemble the speakers of that sentence; the claim is now reported a second time and answered with a challenge. The Book's repetitions of this kind mark the matters needing the most attention, and the argument about the dead was evidently one of them.

**The Description of the Speakers**

The verse describes the speakers as sitting at home while they spoke about their brothers — the participle marking their position throughout: they were not among those who had gone out. The commentators identified them as the hypocrites, some connecting the verse with the leader of that group and his companions. The comment on the passage also notes that the brothers in question could be blood relatives as well as brothers in the faith — the grief of the community at Uḥud touched many households — so the claim was being made about people close to the speakers as well as about fellow believers.

**Why the Counterfactual Fails**

Al-Rāzī's comment explains the logical problem: the speakers asserted what would have happened under circumstances that did not occur — in effect claiming knowledge of what God would have done had things been otherwise. The sūrah had already answered such claims when it stated that those destined to be killed would have gone out to the places where they were to die even if the community had stayed in its houses. The claim that obedience to the speakers' advice would have preserved the dead assumes the appointed term can be moved, and the Book denies that assumption. The Book uses the same method elsewhere: it tells people that death will overtake them wherever they are, even in fortified towers (4:78). The attempt to control an outcome by avoiding a place or a decision is an illusion, since the term is set and the location does not change it.

**The Challenge Issued in Reply**

The answer takes the form of a challenge: ward off death from yourselves, if what you say is true. The Book uses this form when it wants a claim recognised for what it is, and the challenge is addressed to people sitting safely at home. The demand is impossible, and its impossibility is the argument: the men who claimed a different decision would have saved their brothers are told to demonstrate their power over death in their own case first. The Book's method in such sentences is to make the implication of a position visible, and the group's claim collapses as soon as it is stated in the challenge's terms. The community reciting the passage hears the claim and the answer together, and the listeners are expected to recognise the form of the argument when they meet it again — the verse supplying the reply in a form short enough to remember.

**The Connection With the Earlier Warning**

The passage that forbade the believers to imitate those who spoke in this way had already stated that God gives life and causes death; this verse returns to the same subject from the other side, addressing the people whose words the community was told not to adopt. The connection gives the passage its unity: the first statement warned the community about the sentiment; this verse shows the sentiment answered directly; and the verses that follow give the community the truth about the state of those who had died — the sūrah's usual order, from instruction to argument to consolation.

**Refutation Before Consolation**

The verse prepares the ground for the declaration about the martyrs that follows: the community had been hearing arguments about whether the dead had died needlessly, and the answer comes first as a challenge, then as a statement about where the dead now are — the counterfactual and the reality placed next to each other, the answer to the challenge given as a fact about the dead rather than a debate about probability. The order is effective for people in grief: the Book refutes the claim before it consoles, and the refutation removes the framework in which the community's losses would have looked like a waste. What remains, when the argument has been dealt with, is the sentence about the living — given in the next verse.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:169

> Never think of those martyred in the cause of Allah as dead. In fact, they are alive with their Lord, well provided for—

**The Prohibition of Thinking Them Dead**

The verse forbids the believers to regard those killed in the way of God as dead, and states what they are instead — addressing the community's grief directly and answering the arguments the preceding verses reported. The wording is a prohibition rather than a description, and the commentators read it as directed at the way the community was speaking about its losses: the men who died on the field were being discussed in the terms the hypocrites had supplied, and the verse replaces those terms with its own account of their state.

**The Word for Those Who Were Killed**

The verse uses the verb for being killed — a death by violence — and applies the phrase about the way of God to it. The community had just buried men who died in exactly that manner, and the verse does not avoid the word for what happened to them. The Book's willingness to name the violence is part of the consolation's honesty: the sentence does not tell the community the men had not been killed; it tells them not to consider those killed in God's cause as dead. The distinction between the manner of the death and the state of the dead is the whole content of the instruction.

**Alive With Their Lord**

The commentators discussed what kind of life is meant, and the Book's companion passage gives the teaching its shape: do not say of those killed in God's cause that they are dead — they are alive, though you do not perceive it. The life is real and not perceptible to the community that remains, and the phrase "with their Lord" locates it in a state the Book describes without setting out its stages. The community is not told how to picture the state; it is told the fact that governs how it should speak. The clause also explains why the instruction here is phrased as a prohibition — the community's senses reported only the burial, and the Book's teaching concerns the limits of what people can observe: speak on the basis of what you have been told, not of what you can see.

**Well Provided For**

The word describes the supply that sustains a living thing, and its form indicates a continuing condition rather than a completed act. The Book uses the same term for its provision in this life, and the commentators read the sentence as stating that the men who died in God's cause have been given something better than what they left — the community at Madinah had lost men who had been supporting households, and the verse tells it that the dead have not been left without provision.

**The Book's Other Statements About the Martyrs**

The Book returns to their state in several places: those who emigrate for God's cause and are then killed or die will be given a good provision, God being the best of providers; those killed in His cause will not have their deeds rendered void; and the company of the prophets, the truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous are named as those with whom the obedient will be gathered. This verse is the shortest of the statements, and the others fill in its content. The sentence is also one of the Book's clearest statements that death is not the end of a person's existence — a transition from one state to another rather than a cancellation, the dead placed in the care of their Lord, and the sharpest form of the general teaching that every soul tastes death and returns to God, applied here to a case the community was grieving.

**Al-Rāzī: Alive Now, Not Merely Later**

Al-Rāzī considered and rejected the reading that the verse means the slain will be alive later, in the Hereafter: everyone will be alive in the Hereafter, so the sentence would say nothing distinctive, whereas the statements that the slain are provided for and rejoice in those who have not yet joined them show that something is being said about their state now — alive at that moment, in proximity to their Lord. The verse and its companion in the sūrah of the Cow were used extensively in the community's theology for the position that souls are alive between physical death and the resurrection. The traditions describe that stage in several ways, and the verses leave the question of where the dead are unanswered — which is why the commentators described the matter with caution and left the detail to God.

**Why the Statement Follows the Argument**

The declaration about the martyrs comes after the sūrah has dealt with the claim that the dead could have been saved, and the placement is part of its meaning: the community was told to stop making the argument about whether the men should have gone out, and is now told where they are — a refusal of the wrong question followed by the statement of the right fact. The losses are not explained away, and the status of the dead is not left to the estimation of the survivors. The next verse continues the description of their state and adds that they are aware of those who remain behind.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:170

> rejoicing in Allah's bounties and being delighted for those yet to join them. There will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**The Joy Attributed to the Martyrs**

The verse continues the description of the men killed in the way of God, stating that they exult in what God has given them of His bounty — the participle carrying on from the previous sentence to give a picture of an ongoing condition. The Book's descriptions of the Hereafter often include the joy of those who have arrived, and the joy is always presented as a response to what God has given rather than a possession: the men are not said to be enjoying what they earned; they are said to be rejoicing over what has come to them from another's generosity.

**The Word for Bounty**

The noun describes favour and generosity, and it appeared in this sūrah at the close of the battle passage, where God is described as gracious toward the believers — the recurrence tying the reward of the martyrs to the same generosity named for the community as a whole. The choice of term keeps the description from becoming a calculation: the Book does not present the state of the dead as the payment of a wage; it presents them as recipients of a gift, and the joy described is that of people who have received far more than their deeds could have secured.

**Delighted for Those Who Have Not Joined Them**

The verse adds a second joy: gladness for those who have not yet joined them from among the believers who remain behind. The commentators explained it in a range of ways — glad at the prospect of their brothers arriving where they are; glad for the safety of those still alive; taking pleasure in knowing the survivors will not be harmed by what frightened them. The explanations differ in detail and agree about the direction: the men who died are not absorbed in their own condition; their attention extends to the community they left, and the verse makes their concern for those behind them part of their state. The tradition attached to the passage also records that the slain ask to be sent back to the world so that they may die again in God's way — the clearest evidence of the state the verse describes, since a person content with what he has received would not ask to exchange it for a repetition of the experience that brought him there.

**No Fear, No Grief**

The closing statement uses the pair that appears throughout the Book as the description of those secure with God — and it answers the two anxieties the community at Madinah was living with. The two terms cover the future and the past: fear is the apprehension of what is coming; grief is the weight of what has already happened. The verse announces that the men in question have been delivered from both. The commentators discussed whether the clause applies to the martyrs or to the believers still alive: some read it as belonging wholly to the martyrs; others as the condition promised to surviving believers who follow the same path. The readings are not far apart — the position at the end of a verse about the martyrs favours the first, and the community that heard it would have taken the second as well. The Book's promises in such passages are frequently read in both directions.

**The Book's Uses of the Pair**

The promise that no fear will come upon a group and that they will not grieve appears in the Book's descriptions of the believers, of those who follow God's guidance, and of the people of the earlier scriptures who believed and did right. Its frequency means the community reciting this passage had met the phrase before — and the application here is the point: what the Book promises to believers in general is stated for the particular people whose deaths the community was mourning.

**What the Passage Achieves**

From the placement of the day in the divine permission to this description of the dead, the passage is arranged to answer the questions a community asks after a loss: the verses deny that the dead died by mistake, refuse the argument that different conduct would have saved them, and then state where they are. The consolation is not a denial of the loss — the community had lost men it could not replace, and the verses do not say otherwise. What they give is a description of the state of the dead that makes the loss bearable without making it smaller, and the community is expected to carry both the grief and the description. The sūrah's statements about those killed in God's cause make up a short body of teaching — alive, with their Lord, provided for, glad, free of fear and grief, aware of those who remain — and the community's teaching has used them ever since. The verses that follow return to the condition of the believers still alive, and the account of the martyrs supplies the fact they needed in order to continue their work.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:171

> They are joyful for receiving Allah's grace and bounty, and that Allah does not deny the reward of the believers

**The Continuation of the Description**

The verse continues the sentence about those killed in the way of God, adding a further element to the state described across the verses before it — alive with their Lord, provided for, glad, aware of those who remain, and free of fear and grief. This verse supplies the ground of their joy and the assurance that the reward of such people is not lost.

**Favour and Bounty Distinguished**

The two words are not synonyms: the first describes a good condition a person is given; the second describes favour beyond what is due — the generosity of a giver who is not paying a debt. The pair closes several of the Book's passages about divine giving, and the sūrah had already used the second term in describing God as gracious toward the believers. The men who died are said to rejoice in both — a settled condition combined with an ongoing generosity.

**The Negation of Loss**

The sentence states that God neglects not the reward of the believers, and the wording denies that the reward is either lost or reduced. The Book uses the same formula elsewhere — declaring that God does not discount the reward of those who do good, and repeating the phrasing at the end of a list of the hardships the believers endure. The community needed the sentence: the arguments it had been hearing suggested the men who died had thrown their lives away, and the verse answers by denying the premise of any wasted deed. The reward is in the keeping of a God who does not allow it to disappear.

**Why the Assurance Stands Here**

The assurance ends the account of the martyrs because the sūrah has been dealing with a community that had reason to feel its sacrifices were lost. The Book does not require the community to treat the loss as insignificant; it requires it to trust the account that will be settled later: the dead had left households behind, and the survivors had been told by their own critics that the losses had been avoidable. The Book's answer does not dispute the losses; it asserts the accounting — the reward placed in a register the community could not see, with nothing given in the way described in the passage forgotten. And the last clause speaks of the believers in general, drawing the community reading the passage into the same assurance given about its dead: what is not neglected for the martyrs is not neglected for anyone who follows their path — a statement to rely on in every situation in which effort seems to have produced nothing.

**The State Between Death and Resurrection**

The description of men who are alive, glad, and aware of the living gave the community's teachers much material, and the tradition used these verses in its teaching about the state of the soul after death and before the resurrection: the Book provides the fact in a few words and leaves the stages unstated, the two passages — this one and its companion in the sūrah of the Cow — establishing the reality of a state the living cannot observe.

**The Report About the Slain of Badr**

The commentary tradition records that after the battle of Badr the Prophet addressed the enemy's dead and asked whether they had found what their Lord had promised them to be true; when he was asked how the dead could hear, he replied that they heard him no worse than the living. The report is part of the tradition's evidence for the awareness of the departed, and the commentaries place it alongside these verses — the verses establishing the state of the believers who died in God's cause, the report belonging to the same subject.

**The Direction of the Passage**

The commentators noticed that the passage describes the dead as aware of those who have not yet joined them, and discussed how such awareness relates to time as the living measure it — the Book does not settle such questions, and its discussions have remained the work of scholars rather than doctrine: the verses state what the community needed to know and leave the rest, the pattern the Book follows consistently in its descriptions of the unseen. The verses that follow describe the community's answer to a call made while its wounds were still fresh, and the assurance stated here is part of the ground on which that answer was given — a promise not of visible results but of an account that will be complete.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:172

> ˹As for˺ those who responded to the call of Allah and His Messenger after their injury, those of them who did good and were mindful ˹of Allah˺ will have a great reward

**The Summons After the Wounds**

The verse describes those who responded to God and the Messenger after they had been wounded, promising a great reward to those among them who acted well and remained mindful — and the order is the point: the response came after the suffering. The incident is well known in the sūrah's history: on the day after the fighting, with the wounded still being tended and the dead not yet counted, the Prophet called the men who had marched the previous day to set out with him in pursuit of the Makkan army.

**The Call and the March to Ḥamrāʾ al-Asad**

The reports describe the community assembling despite its condition — the Prophet himself among the wounded — and marching a few miles from Madinah to a place called Ḥamrāʾ al-Asad, on the road the enemy army had taken. The force was smaller than the one that had fought the day before and in worse condition, and the men who answered included those injured the day before. The verse's phrase about responding after being wounded therefore describes something specific: the community was not summoned at a time when enthusiasm was easy; it was summoned when the cost of the previous engagement was visible to everyone — and the verse records that the call was answered. The sūrah mentions the wounds before the response, and that order has shaped the way the passage has been read ever since.

**The Arrangement That Prevented Another Battle**

The reports describe the steps taken to prevent the Makkans from turning back toward Madinah: a man from a tribe with which the Prophet had a treaty was sent to tell them the Madinans were pursuing in far greater numbers than before, now including all who had stayed behind during the fighting; and the community lit a great many campfires at night within sight of the Makkan camp, so the enemy would judge its numbers from the fires. The Makkans abandoned their intention to return, and the sūrah reports the outcome in the verse that follows. The arrangement shows how much of the affair turned on what each side believed about the other.

**What the Phrase About Responding Means**

The phrase identifies the merit of the people described by naming the two directions of the summons: the response was not to a leader's preference but to a call carrying divine authority. The sūrah has used this vocabulary throughout its account of the campaign, and uses it here for the group that answered when the answer was difficult — the Book's descriptions of merit being frequently attached to the difficulty of the act rather than its scale. A march of a few miles is placed in that category because of the condition of the men who made it.

**The Reading That Points to the Lesser Badr**

A minority of commentators connected these verses with the appointment for a return engagement the two sides are said to have made after Uḥud — the meeting set for a year later at the site of Badr, where the believers went out at the appointed time, found no enemy, bought goods at a market, and returned. The reading changes the occasion but not the lesson: whether the community marched in pursuit of the army that had beaten it or out to a meeting its opponents failed to keep, the verses praise people who answered a summons when their own condition made compliance costly.

**The Condition Attached to the Reward**

The great reward is promised to those among them who were virtuous and mindful of God — the qualifier distinguishing within the group that went out. The sūrah has already shown that the community's ranks included people whose aims varied, and the sentence keeps the distinction in view: the requirement is the combination named repeatedly — good conduct and the awareness of God. The men who answered the call were not all alike in their motives, and the conditional form keeps the community from supposing that the march itself, apart from its motive, secured the promise.

**The Model the Verse Supplies**

The Book addresses the same situation in another passage, telling the believers not to falter in the pursuit of the enemy — their opponents also suffering, while the community had hopes the others did not have. The encouragement belongs with this account: the Book treats the decision to keep pressing an opponent as following from the community's hope, and the men described here made that decision with their wounds still fresh. The verse offers the community a picture of what recovery from a defeat looks like — the fighting ends, the losses are counted, and the community answers the next call: a body of people told the cause of their loss, who acted in accordance with the correction rather than with their fear. The next verses describe the report that reached them on the road and the reply they made to it.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:173

> Those who were warned, "Your enemies have mobilized their forces against you, so fear them," the warning only made them grow stronger in faith and they replied, "Allah ˹alone˺ is sufficient ˹as an aid˺ for us and ˹He˺ is the best Protector."

**The Warning That Reached Them**

The verse reports what people said to the community on the road: that a force had gathered against them, and that they should be afraid. The commentators identify the speakers in two ways — travellers who had passed the Makkan contingent and carried the news on, or the hypocrites who had been discouraging the community from fighting all along. The content is a report of the enemy's strength: the men receiving it were wounded, fewer in number than before, in no condition to meet a renewed attack — a report designed to produce the reaction the verse's next clause denies.

**The Reply Recorded in the Verse**

The community's reply was that God is enough for them and that He is the best guardian. The phrase has remained one of the prayers most used by Muslims in danger, and the Book contains it in more than one place: the Prophet is told to say that God is sufficient for him; the faithful are described as saying the same; and the same formula appears in other situations across the sūrahs. The reply's structure is the exact answer to the warning: the report said a force had gathered; the response named the One on whom the community relied. The sentence does not deny the report and does not discuss the numbers.

**The Effect of the Warning**

The verse states that the warning increased the community in faith — the statement reversing what the report was meant to achieve. Warnings of this kind work by narrowing a person's attention to the danger; here the effect was to turn the community's attention to its reliance. The Book describes similar reactions elsewhere, where the believers are said to grow firmer when tested. The sentence is praise of the way the report was received rather than of the report itself — one of the Book's regular teachings about how faith works: the same event produces fear in one person and confidence in another according to what each trusts. And the distinction between the reply as an increase in faith rather than a display of courage matters: the Book does not present their confidence as a calculation about the enemy's willingness to fight — the Makkans had been eager to return and the community knew it; its security rested on something other than an assessment of the balance of forces. The men had been asked to be afraid, and their answer shows what they had already decided to rely on before the question was put.

**The Deterrent and Its Results**

The reports describe how the threat was turned back: the message carried to the Makkans was that the Madinans were pursuing in far greater numbers than before, and the many campfires lit within sight of the enemy's camp supported the claim. The Makkans concluded another engagement would be unwise and abandoned their plan, and the believers returned without a battle — the enemy back toward its own city, the community home without further losses, the outcome the next verse describes in the language of favour and bounty. The verse records the community's reply at the moment of the warning; the fruits of the reply are described in the verse that follows.

**The Prayer in the Community's Practice**

The declaration that God suffices and is the best guardian has been used by Muslims at the outset of difficulties, and its frequent repetition in the Book has made it one of the most recognisable sentences of prayer in the community's life. This verse places it on the lips of a wounded company on the road from Uḥud, and the tradition's use of it has been shaped by that setting ever since.

**The Wider Application, and What Follows**

One reading of the passage draws a wider lesson: a person whose concern is with God should not be discouraged when people devoted to worldly interests unite to oppose him — their opposition a sign of the difference between the two orientations rather than of the man's error. The reading is applied to the interior life rather than the battlefield, and the commentators offered it without displacing the verse's plain sense; the Book's sentences about trust have been used in this way throughout the community's teaching. The verse closes the exchange without adding a threat of its own, and the next verse states the command that follows: the community had been asked to be afraid and had answered with a statement about sufficiency, and the sūrah treats that answer as the correct one — the source of the fear then identified, and the community directed to the fear that belongs to its faith.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:174

> So they returned with Allah's favours and grace, suffering no harm. For they sought to please Allah. And surely Allah is ˹the˺ Lord of infinite bounty

**The Return Described**

The verse states that the community returned with favour and bounty from God, that no harm had touched it, and that the men had pursued the contentment of God — describing the outcome of the expedition the previous verses introduced. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the community came back unharmed by the enemy, since no second battle took place: the sentence supplies the outcome of the decision reported in the previous verse, in the language of gift rather than of achievement. On the reading that connects the passage with the appointment at Badr a year later, the return was from that meeting, where no fighting occurred at all. Both readings describe a homecoming without losses.

**Favour and Bounty Distinguished**

Al-Rāzī drew a distinction between the two words: the favour may refer to goods of this world — the safety and provisions of the journey — while the bounty refers to the good of the Hereafter, the sentence covering both portions of what the Book promises to those who obey. The pair had appeared in the earlier description of the martyrs, and the repetition links the two groups: those who died in God's cause and those who came back safely from the pursuit are described with the same vocabulary of divine generosity, and the passage presents both outcomes as gifts.

**Pursuing the Contentment of God**

The phrase appeared earlier in the sūrah, where the verse contrasts the person who seeks God's pleasure with the one who earns anger — and the two verses are connected by the same words. The men who went out after Uḥud are described by their aim, and the aim is the one the sūrah named as the mark of the righteous. The Book's use of the same phrase in both places gives the passage a symmetry: the community instructed about trust and reward is shown in action, pursuing the same contentment the earlier verse described as the object of a good life.

**What "Untouched by Harm" Covers**

The phrase covers safety from the harm that had been feared, and the commentators read it as a statement about the community's condition rather than only its bodies: the men had gone out wounded and returned without further losses, and the sūrah notes the fact without elaborating it. The detail matters in the passage's economy — the community had been told it would be tested and that its conduct would determine the outcome, and the expedition after Uḥud was the first occasion on which it could apply what it had learned. The verse records that the application went well.

**The Name That Closes the Verse**

The sentence ends with the declaration that God is possessed of tremendous bounty — echoing the close of the earlier verse about the pardon the community had received. The same generosity that covered the failure at Uḥud is named here for the success of the pursuit, and the repetition gives the two moments the same ground: the community's pardon and its reward both come from a giver whose generosity is not measured by the merit of the recipients, and the sūrah's readers are expected to hold both statements together.

**The Confidence Restored, and the Pattern**

The two expeditions stand side by side in the sūrah: the march that ended in defeat, and the march that ended in safety and gain — the first having shaken the community's confidence, the second restoring it. The Book uses the pair to show that a community's condition is not fixed by a single day: the first outcome was explained in terms of the community's conduct and followed by a promise of pardon; the second outcome follows the corrected conduct and is presented as what such conduct produces. The reports describe the men setting out with wounds still fresh only days after the defeat, and the safe return gave the community a second memory to place beside the first. Read with the verses before it, the sentence describes a community that had been told its losses came from its own hands, had accepted the correction, had answered a call while still wounded, and had returned with what the Book calls favour and bounty — the sūrah's answer to the frustration of the earlier passage, showing that its position with God had not been forfeited by the defeat, and giving readers a model of recovery that does not depend on the specifics of the campaign. The next verses leave the fighting behind and take up the sūrah's wider arguments, the account of the expedition standing as the community's working example of what it had been taught.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:175

> That ˹warning˺ was only ˹from˺ Satan, trying to prompt you to fear his followers. So do not fear them; fear Me if you are ˹true˺ believers

**The Warning Traced to Its Source**

The verse identifies the fear stirred up in the community as the work of Satan, who frightens his friends — the report that reached the community on the road had been presented as information about the enemy, and the verse states what was behind it. The commentators read the sentence as a statement about how the warning worked rather than only who delivered it: al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī explain that Satan inspires the disbelievers to frighten the believers, so that the community's resolve fails before its opponents have done anything. Naming the source is the Book's way of reducing a threat to its true size — identifying the influence behind the report rather than disputing its details.

**Satan's Friends**

The phrase identifies those whose cause the fear serves. The Book uses the expression in its description of the people who follow their impulses and forget the remembrance of God, describing them as a party that is bound to lose. The verse attaches the party to the fear it was promoting: the community is told that the people whose threats it was dreading were acting as agents of an influence with no claim on the outcome — and the phrase shows whose cause the community's fear would have served, since the threats came from people whose cause the Book had already described as lost. The commentators note that the Book applies the term to human beings as well as to the unseen creature, allowing the verse to be read as applying to the disbelievers and hypocrites themselves — the sūrah's earlier passage about confidants describes men whose speech carries the same suggestion. The usage teaches readers to judge conduct by its character rather than by the nature of the actor: the influence works through human speech, and the sentence does not require the community to identify who was whispering in order to resist it.

**The Command Not to Fear Them**

The verse forbids the community to be afraid of the group whose threats had been reported — the command's object being the fear itself rather than the danger, as the Book's instructions about fear usually are. The community's situation gave the fear a foothold: its men were wounded, its ranks thinned, its opponents known to be eager for a second engagement — and the Book does not tell the community the danger was imaginary. It tells it that the fear of the enemy is not the fear it should be governed by, and the next clause supplies the alternative.

**The Command to Fear God**

The alternative is the fear of God, and the Book uses the same instruction in other passages: God is more deserving of the community's fear if its members are believers; the people entrusted with the scripture are told not to fear the people but to fear God; the community is told to be mindful of God as He is due. The command places the two fears side by side, and the teaching in all these passages is that a person cannot be governed by both: what the community is told to fear is the One whose judgement matters and whose protection is effective, and the sentence treats the fear of anything else as a misplacement of the same instinct. The conditional close — if you are believers — gives the sentence its weight: the clause does not suggest the community's faith was in doubt; it sets out the practical content of the claim, for a person who fears his enemies more than God has given his fear to something other than the One he professes to worship.

**Fear as a Discipline**

The Book treats the fear of God as one of the governors of conduct, using the term alongside mindfulness and reverence in its instructions — telling the community elsewhere that God warns it about Himself, which places the awareness of God's judgement at the centre of its self-understanding. The community had just been shown how fear nearly governed its decision about marching, and the sentence supplies the correction: the Book's remedy is not the removal of fear but its redirection — the sūrah using the vocabulary of mindfulness since its opening verses, where the Book is described as a guidance for those who guard against what they fear.

**The Affair of Uḥud Closed**

The verse brings the sūrah's account of the battle and its aftermath to an end, closing with the community's fear placed where the Book holds it belongs. The remaining verses turn to the opponents among the People of the Book and to the arguments of the community's own doubters. The placement is deliberate: the community had been tested by wounds, rumours, the loss of its men, and the fear of a second attack — and the sūrah leaves it with a command about what it should be afraid of. The lessons of the campaign are gathered in that sentence, and the readers of the sūrah carry it as the conclusion of the whole account.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:176

> ˹O Prophet!˺ Do not grieve for those who race to disbelieve—surely they will not harm Allah in the least. It is Allah's Will to disallow them a share in the Hereafter, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment

**The Consolation Addressed to the Prophet**

The verse tells the Prophet not to let those who rush toward disbelief grieve him, and states that their conduct does not harm God in the least. The instruction follows the long passage about the community's own failures, and turns the Prophet's attention to another group whose opposition was personal to him. The Book addresses the Prophet's distress at the disbelief of others in several places — telling him not to let the conduct of those who deny grieve him, not to let their speech distress him, and asking whether he will consume himself in regret over people who will not believe — a body of consolation addressed to the same experience, repeated because the experience was persistent. The pattern is instructive for the community: whatever grief a person feels over the rejection of the message by those he cares about, the Book treats the feeling as natural and supplies the same remedy — the cause belongs to God, and its outcome does not depend on the response it receives.

**Who the Verse Concerns**

The commentators identified the group in more than one way: al-Rāzī read the phrase as referring to the idolaters of Makkah, the Prophet's own kin, who worked hardest against him; al-Qurṭubī included the People of the Book, who had a scripture of their own and were expected to recognise what came after it, and the hypocrites, who claimed adherence while working against the community. The three groups had little in common except the conduct described, and the sentence covers all of them — the Book's manner in such passages being to state the matter in terms that apply wherever the conduct is found.

**The Word for Hastening**

The verb carries the sense of moving quickly and eagerly: the group did not drift into its position but pressed toward it. The distinction is worth keeping when the sentence is read in a community's own situation — the Book distinguishes between people who fall into an error and people who rush to embrace it, and this phrasing places the group in the second category. The Prophet is told not to be grieved by what they do, and the wording describes the act rather than the persons.

**"They Will Not Harm Allah at All"**

The same formula appeared earlier in the sūrah, where the community is told that whoever turns back on his heels does not harm God at all. The Book uses the sentence to place the consequences of defection where they fall: the harm belongs to the person who commits the act. The community hearing the sentence is taught that its message does not depend on the number of its adherents, and the Prophet is told that the loss of any follower is not a subtraction from the cause he was sent to convey.

**A Share in the Hereafter**

The commentators explained the phrase as referring to a share in the Hereafter's good and blessing rather than to the mere experience of the next life: the Book describes its enemies as present in the Hereafter and receiving their treatment there. The sentence is therefore a statement about what the group has lost, not about the annihilation of their existence — the Book consistently distinguishing between being in the Hereafter and having a portion in it, and the verse uses the second idea.

**The Balance of the Sentence**

The verse gives the Prophet a command and then supplies the reasons for it, the two statements arranged to relieve a burden rather than increase it. The grief prohibited is the grief of a person who feels responsible for what others choose; the Book relieves that burden by stating what the disbelief does not affect and what the group forfeits by it. The Prophet is not told the matter is unimportant; he is told his own standing is not diminished by it — a release from a responsibility that was never his to bear. And the verse's application extends beyond the Prophet: any believer who invests himself in the response of others faces the same disappointment, and the sentence offers the same release. The passage around it has been concerned with a community tested and having lost members to apostasy and hypocrisy; the sentence tells the believers what such losses mean for the cause they carry, and prepares for the next verse, which restates the principle in the language of trade.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:177

> Those who trade belief for disbelief will never harm Allah in the least, and they will suffer a painful punishment

**The Image of a Trade**

The verse describes those who have purchased disbelief at the price of belief, stating that they will not harm God and that a painful punishment awaits them. The commercial image is one the Book uses frequently for moral choice: one sūrah describes people who trade guidance for misguidance and observes that the trade is profitless; another describes the same exchange and adds that the traders are persistent in pursuing the Fire; a third, on the same image, describes those who purchase the present life at the price of the next. The Book's use of the market as a picture of a person's decisions is one of its characteristic ways of explaining consequences.

**The Order of the Words**

The exchange is between belief and disbelief, and the order matters: what the group had is named as belief, and what they took in its place as disbelief. The Book's descriptions of the same transaction usually mention what was given up — guidance, forgiveness, the next life. This sentence is brief, and the brevity makes the loss clear without adding anything: what was given up was the more valuable of the two, and the sentence assumes the reader knows which is which.

**The Profitlessness of the Exchange**

The Book repeatedly states that the trade does not profit those who make it, and the commercial language matters because it treats belief as something a person possesses and may dispose of badly: faith is not an external label worn or set aside without cost — it is property whose exchange has consequences, and the market image gives the reader a familiar way to weigh what happened. The verse itself does not use the word for profit, but the sentence about their punishment states the same outcome in a different register.

**Who the Traders Were**

The commentators applied the verse to the People of the Book who knew the truth and turned from it, and to the groups who had received clear evidence and preferred something else; the sūrah of Repentance uses the same image of those who chose a fleeting gain over God's revelations. The hypocrites and the Makkan idolaters are included in the same category, since all of them gave up a claim for an advantage they thought would last — and the verse's punishments are stated in general terms, allowing application wherever the exchange occurs. The sūrah's own audience had seen the pattern in individuals close to the message who turned away from it.

**The Repetition About Harm**

The verse states again that the group does not harm God — the repetition in consecutive verses giving the point its weight. The sentence is not a description of the group's power but a statement about the limits of what any created being can affect: the Book uses the same construction in this sūrah's earlier passage about those who turn on their heels, and states in the sūrah of Repentance that whoever withholds from giving harms only himself. The community reciting these sentences is taught where the damage from a person's defection lands, and the answer is always the same.

**The Punishment Named**

The term for the punishment describes the pain of the outcome rather than its duration, and the Book uses it in passages dealing with conduct chosen with knowledge. The sūrah of the Bees makes the exception of a person compelled to disbelieve while his heart remains firm in faith — an exception that marks how deliberate the conduct in this verse is. The verse therefore places the group in the class of those who acted deliberately, and the punishment named suits the description.

**The Verse's Place After the Consolation**
