The Book's prayers consistently include a request for the forgiveness of the one praying, and the people described as reflecting on the creation do not regard themselves as beyond the need of pardon — the community reciting the verses being taught the same posture.

**The Removal of Evil Deeds**

The second request is for the removal of evil deeds, and the wording is fuller than a simple plea for pardon: the Book elsewhere promises that those who repent, believe, and do good will have their evil deeds changed into good ones, and describes the covering of faults as one of the effects of forgiveness. The petition asks for a treatment of the record rather than merely the avoidance of punishment — that the community's worst actions should not be left standing against it, the same request appearing in the prayers of the believers in the sūrah of the Night Journey and elsewhere.

**The Company of the Virtuous**

The final request is to be taken to God among the company of the pious — a request for association rather than for a rank: the Book describes the company of the prophets, the truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous as the companions of those who obey God and His messenger, and calls that company excellent. The petition reflects the Book's understanding of what a person should want for himself: not only safety but presence with those who were faithful — the community's literature using the verse in its teaching about choosing one's company.

**What the Prayer Teaches About Faith**

The sūrah's closing prayer is one of the most widely repeated in the community's worship, appearing in many manuals of supplication and serving as a model for private prayer: its content explains its circulation — a statement of what the believer has heard and accepted, a request for the pardon of his faults, and a request for the company of the righteous at the end, each element teachable on its own, the community's teachers using the passage to instruct new Muslims in what they should say. The prayer describes faith as a sequence: hearing a call, responding to it, and then asking for what the response requires — the three movements belonging together. The sequence also describes the community's own history, which the sūrah has been recounting through the battle and its aftermath: the people described in the closing passage are the ones who heard the call and kept their standing, and their prayer is the sūrah's final instruction on what such a people should ask of God — faith, on this account, not being a single moment but a course that the requests of the prayer are meant to sustain.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:194

> Our Lord! Grant us what You have promised us through Your messengers and do not put us to shame on Judgment Day—for certainly You never fail in Your promise

**The Petition About the Promise**

The prayer asks God to give what He promised through His messengers, and the commentators record two ways of taking the phrase: that God made the promise to the messengers themselves, or that the messengers conveyed the promise to the people — or that both senses are intended, the Book's promises to the believers being stated in both forms, so the readings are not exclusive. The petition's content is a request for the fulfilment of what the community had been told to expect: the people of understanding ask that the promise reach them, expressed in the language of a claim resting on nothing but God's own word.

**Al-Rāzī on Praying for What Is Certain**

Al-Rāzī discusses the apparent difficulty in asking God for something He has said He will do: such prayers are not attempts to change God's action; they are expressions of humility and deference on the part of the one who prays. He cites the Book's other prayers of this kind — the request of a prophet for his Lord to judge with truth, and the prayer of those who ask God to forgive those who repent and follow His way. On his reading the supplication is a response to what God does rather than a cause of it, and the community's prayers of this kind are exercises in the posture they express.

**The Request Not to Be Disgraced**

The second half asks God not to disgrace the community on the Day of Resurrection, echoing the previous verse's statement that those who enter the Fire will be disgraced; al-Rāzī notes that the earlier verse concerned the punishment of the Fire while this prayer seeks deliverance from the shame that belongs to the Day. The two passages together give the reader a sense of what is at stake at the end: the community asks to be spared both the Fire and the exposure, the second request placed in the context of the gathering on the Day of Resurrection.

**The Book's Assurance About the Meeting**

The Book states the certainty of the gathering in several passages, and one of this sūrah's earlier verses puts the declaration in the form of a prayer: that God will gather all humanity on a Day about which there is no doubt, and that He does not break His promise (3:9). Another passage describes the righteous in the Garden saying that God has fulfilled what He promised them. This verse belongs to that group and asks for the same outcome — the community's prayers here shaped by the certainty the Book states elsewhere: the prayer takes the assurance as its ground and asks that the believer be among those who receive what has been promised.

**The Word for the Appointed Meeting**

The closing declaration — that God does not fail the appointment — uses a noun naming a time fixed by agreement. The sūrah has already used the word in the prayer that asks God to gather humanity on a Day about which there is no doubt (3:9), and the same word appears in the account of Badr, where the Book notes that even if the two armies had arranged a meeting they would have missed it, the encounter happening so that what was destined might be established. The community's own history had already supplied an instance of an appointment that held, and the petition takes the same certainty as its ground: what the people of understanding ask for is not a new promise but the fulfilment of the one they have been given.

**The Repeated Address, and the Prayer's Set**

The prayer in this passage addresses God as its Lord at the start of each petition, the repetition shaping the way the whole scene reads: the requests are made by people who place themselves under the authority of the One they address. The pattern appears in the Book's other recorded prayers and in the community's own supplications — the address establishing the relation between the one who asks and the One who gives, and keeping the petitions within the bounds of what a servant may ask of his Lord: nothing requests what lies outside God's promise or His will. The petitions together cover the whole of a person's need — protection from the Fire, forgiveness, the removal of evil deeds, the company of the righteous, the fulfilment of the promise, and deliverance from disgrace on the Day — each stated in a clause of its own, made in the plural so the individual prays with the body of the believers rather than for himself alone. And the verse ends with a statement of confidence: the community's prayer does not end with uncertainty about the outcome but with an assertion about the One being addressed — the Book's prayers frequently closing in this way, with a statement about God that serves as the ground of the petition, teaching the community to end its requests with an acknowledgment of what God is rather than with what the supplicant wants.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:195

> So their Lord responded to them: "I will never deny any of you—male or female—the reward of your deeds. Both are equal in reward. Those who migrated or were expelled from their homes, and were persecuted for My sake and fought and ˹some˺ were martyred—I will certainly forgive their sins and admit them into Gardens under which rivers flow, as a reward from Allah. And with Allah is the finest reward!"

**The Answer Given**

The verse opens by stating that their Lord answered them, following the prayer that reaches through the end of the previous verse — and the transition from request to answer gives the closing movement of the sūrah its shape: the people of understanding reflect on the creation, make their requests, and are answered by the One they address, the reply reported in the first person. The Book records the answers to such prayers in several places, and the community in this passage is told that its petitions were heard.

**The Promise That No Work Is Wasted**

The answer begins with the declaration that God will not let the work of any worker be in vain — the sūrah's broadest assurance on the subject, joined to its earlier statement that God does not neglect the reward of the believers. The declaration covers the whole range of what a person does, without specifying a category of deeds or a rank of people: the answer to the community's prayers begins with that general assurance, and the specific promises that follow are applications of it to the people whose history the sūrah has been describing.

**Male and Female Alike**

The answer states that no worker among them will be denied the fruit of his work, male or female, and then adds that each is like the other. Al-Rāzī explains the phrase as meaning that the reward for obedience and righteous conduct is not differentiated between men and women, noting the further sense that each may be said to be of the other. The Book makes the point in several places: whoever does good, whether male or female, and believes will be given a good life and rewarded according to the best of what he did; the qualities of believing men and women are listed together with the promise of forgiveness and a great reward for both; and those who do good, whether male or female, and have faith will enter the Garden and not be wronged in the least.

**The Sufferings Listed**

The answer names the experiences of the people it concerns: those who emigrated, those who were expelled from their homes, those who were hurt in God's cause, those who fought, and those who were killed. The list follows the community's history — beginning with the persecution in Makkah and the migration that followed it to Madinah, continuing through the fighting the sūrah has described — a summary of what the first community had undergone, the answer addressed to the people who had borne all of it. The phrase about being hurt in God's cause is explained by the commentators as covering insults and mockery as well as physical injury, returning to the experience the verse before the prayer named: the community would hear much that was painful from the two groups. The inclusion of insults in the list is significant for the community's self-understanding: what the believers endured was not only the loss of possessions and the dangers of the road but the daily pressure of being mocked for their position — and the answer names it as part of what God will not allow to be wasted.

**Absolution and the Gardens**

The answer promises to absolve the people described of their evil deeds and to bring them into Gardens with rivers running beneath them — both elements having appeared in the sūrah's earlier description of the reward of the righteous: the Gardens with rivers beneath them are the Book's standard image for the reward, and the absolution of deeds is the removal of what would otherwise stand against the people who receive it. The answer grants both the removal of the debt and the gift that follows it, and the community is told what its sacrifices were exchanged for.

**The Reward Attributed to God**

The verse describes what is given as a reward from God, and closes with the statement that with God is the finest reward — the attribution placing the reward outside the system of exchange the community might have imagined, since what is given comes from God rather than being earned by the recipients. The sūrah's earlier passages used the same language in describing the reward of the believers and the bounty of God toward the community; the closing sentence gathers all of it into a single phrase, and the passage ends with the community's aspirations directed to the source rather than to the gift.

**What the Answer Teaches**

The answer teaches the community that its hardships have been registered and that the account will be settled in its favour: the sūrah has been describing a community that lost a battle, was mocked by its neighbours, and had members who failed and members who stood firm — and the closing movement places all of them within the answer given to the prayer of the righteous. The verses that follow add a warning about the conduct of those who trade faith for disbelief, and the sūrah ends shortly after: the passage's last word on the community's situation is the promise stated here, given to people who had asked for exactly what the answer grants.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:196

> Do not be deceived by the prosperity of the disbelievers throughout the land

**The Delusion Named**

The verse addresses the Prophet with a warning against being misled by the ease with which those who deny the message travel the land and prosper in it — naming a conclusion a person could draw from appearances: that the comfort of the deniers is a sign of their standing before God. The warning is stated in the negative because the impression itself is the danger, and the verse denies the conclusion before it can take hold: the counsel is needed because the conclusion it forbids is one a reasonable observer might reach from the facts alone. The sentences that follow supply the reason, and the next verse states what the freedom of movement actually amounts to.

**The Occasion Reported**

The commentators record an occasion: some believers are said to have remarked on the contrast between their own hunger and hardship and the freedom with which the idolaters moved about the land and profited from commerce; others report the remark came from the hypocrites, who used the same contrast to cast doubt on the message. Al-Rāzī observed that although the sentence is addressed to the Prophet, its audience is wider, as though a listener were being addressed directly — the verse read as a warning to anyone who measures the truth of a claim by the comfort of the people who reject it.

**Freedom of Movement as Appearance**

The observation that the deniers range over the earth appeared earlier in the sūrah, where the same impression is addressed: they are given length of days, and their going about the land looks like success — the thought occurring to the observer because the world's arrangement places no visible obstacle in the path of those who oppose the message. The two passages belong together, the earlier one having already warned against drawing conclusions from the length of the interval granted to opponents: both treat the ease of the deniers as a postponement rather than a verdict, and both turn the reader's attention to what comes after the interval — the community taught to read the delay as part of the story rather than its ending. The Book addresses the same temptation in another sūrah, where it asks those who trade in falsehood to enjoy themselves and names their destination (40:4); the passages together show a single concern treated from several angles, and the community's teachers used them together when they explained why hardship and prosperity are not the measures of a person's standing.

**The Respite Described Elsewhere**

The Book states in several passages that the comfort granted to those who deny is a delay and not a reward: one tells the prosperous to eat and enjoy themselves and be diverted by false hope, adding that they will come to know; another states that ornaments and pleasures of that kind are no more than the passing enjoyment of the present life, while what is with God belongs to those who are mindful; a third states that the present life has been made attractive to those who reject the truth and that they mock the believers, while those who are mindful will rank above them on the Day of Judgment. This verse depends on the same judgment about appearances.

**The World's Standing in the Book**

The tradition preserved a statement attributed to the Prophet that explains the paradox the verse addresses: the world is a prison for the believer and a garden for the one who denies. The sentence expresses what the verse assumes — the ease of the deniers and the hardship of the believers are not evidence about the next life and may indicate its opposite. Al-Rāzī and the other commentators made the same point in their explanations: what appears to be a favour to one group and a deprivation of the other is measured by a scale the world's own indications cannot reveal. The Book describes the worldly life as a brief and delusive possession in many places: after being rescued people transgress in the land unjustly, their transgression being against their own souls, the present life being only a passing enjoyment before the return to God; and it compares the present life to play, amusement, display, and rivalry in wealth and children, calling it the delusion of enjoyment — the estimate on which this verse rests, what looks like the freedom of the deniers being measured against a scale in which the whole of the worldly life weighs very little.

**What the Verse Corrects**

The verse corrects a reading of the world in which success is identified with the condition of those who appear to be succeeding: the Book's teaching is that the present arrangement is not the final one, and that comparisons drawn from it do not hold. The correction is delivered as a prohibition addressed to a person — how the Book often states a conclusion it wants drawn — and the sentence leaves the reader with the fact that overturns the impression: what the deniers have secured has a limit, and the verse that follows states what that limit is. The members of the community were the ones tempted by the comparison, and the verse answers them by forbidding the conclusion they were drawing: the reader is asked to hold two facts together — the appearance that impresses him, and the account of the outcome the Book supplies — and what they were to rely on was the account the sūrah had already given of both outcomes.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:197

> It is only a brief enjoyment. Then Hell will be their home—what an evil place to rest

**The Brief Enjoyment**

The verse follows the warning of the sentence before it and states what the freedom of the deniers amounts to: a small enjoyment, followed by the Fire as their refuge — the answer to the impression the previous verse told the reader to resist. The Book uses the vocabulary of a little or a brief span for the present life in several passages, and the word here carries the same sense: the community was expected to adopt that measure as its own rather than the estimate appearances suggest. The sentence does not deny that the deniers enjoy their position; it denies that the enjoyment is large — a passage says the present life compared with the Hereafter is nothing but a fleeting enjoyment, and another states that its ornaments are a passing enjoyment while the Hereafter with God belongs to the mindful. The consistency of the judgment is what the community's teachers pointed to when they warned against envy of the prosperous.

**The Refuge Named**

The second half names the Fire as the refuge of the people described, and the choice of word is deliberate: what the deniers thought of as the world's open land ends in a place described as a destination they cannot leave. The Book names the Fire as the destination of the arrogant and the deniers in many passages, with the same vocabulary of arrival — the word for refuge carrying the sense of a place to which a person is brought. One passage tells those who treat others as rivals to God to enjoy themselves, since their destination is the Fire; another states that the wrongdoers will ultimately be returned to it; and others describe it as a place of rest for those who arrive there and a home for those who transgressed. The repetition of the same images across the Book gives the sentence its weight: it is one of a series of statements in which the destination of those who rejected the message is named in identical language, and the community's teachers used those passages together when they explained it.

**The Resting Place Inverted**

The closing exclamation — an evil resting place — uses the term for a place where a traveller stops or a person lies down, the image of the present life as a journey carried through the word for movement in the previous verse. The contrast is between the welcome the next verse promises to the mindful and the destination named here, the two set side by side in the sūrah's closing lines.

**The Comparison, and the Brevity**

The construction of the passage places this verse directly before the one describing the Gardens promised to those who revere God — the word for refuge in this sentence and the word for welcome in the next set against each other, the two destinations being the alternatives the sūrah has been presenting. The last verses of the sūrah therefore close with a comparison rather than an argument: the community that recites them is shown the two endings and left to draw the conclusion, this sentence being the darker half of the pair. The verse consists of two brief statements and an exclamation, and its brevity is part of its effect: what the previous verse described at length is dismissed in a clause, and the dismissal carries the judgment — the Book using short sentences this way when it states the outcome of a long dispute, the reader given the fact without embellishment, the terseness leaving the impression that the matter requires no further elaboration.

**What the Verse Teaches**

The verse teaches the community that the comfort of those who deny the message has a limit fixed in advance, and that it should describe that comfort with the word the Book uses for it rather than with the estimate appearances suggest. The instruction belongs to the preparation the sūrah has been giving its readers for their life in a world where the two groups live side by side — the closing verses repeating the same distinction, the community given the two outcomes as the frame within which it should read its own circumstances. The next verse turns to the portion of the other group, and the transition makes the two destinations the sūrah's final subject.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:198

> But those who are mindful of their Lord will be in Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there forever—as an accommodation from Allah. And what is with Allah is best for the virtuous

**The Contrast Stated**

The verse begins with a turn that sets the destiny of the mindful against the destination described in the sentence before it: those who revere their Lord are promised Gardens with rivers flowing beneath them, in the same terms the sūrah has used earlier for the reward of the righteous. The sūrah has been contrasting the two groups from the beginning of its closing movement, and this sentence completes the comparison — the turn at the start being the Book's familiar way of placing two outcomes side by side: what the wrongdoers receive is named in the previous verse, and what those who revere God receive is named here.

**The Gardens Described**

The Gardens are described through the rivers that run beneath them, the Book's most frequent image for the reward — recalling the promise made earlier in this sūrah to those who are mindful, and the promise made to the believers and doers of good in the second sūrah, where the same rivers are named. The community hearing the description is being reminded of what it has been promised: the image is used consistently across the Book, and the repetition of the same words ties the rewards promised in different passages to the same reality — conveying both the abundance of what is given and the permanence of the home in which it is enjoyed. The sentence states that the people described will remain in the Gardens forever, the promise of permanence distinguishing the reward from everything the previous verses said about the present life: what the deniers enjoy is measured in a little time, and what the mindful are given is described as abiding — the Book making the same point when it contrasts the passing world with what remains with God.

**The Word for Welcome**

The verse describes what is given as a welcome from God, and the Arabic word names what a host prepares for a guest who arrives — al-Rāzī explaining it in that sense, the Book using the same term for the reward elsewhere, including the descriptions of the Gardens prepared for the mindful. The image presents the reward as hospitality rather than payment: what awaits the believers is prepared for them by their Lord, the word placing the whole scene within the relation between a generous host and the guests he has invited.

**The Statement About What Is With God**

The closing declaration — that what is with God is better for the virtuous — returns to the comparison with which the passage began, the Book using the same phrase in its descriptions of the reward to redirect attention from the description of the Gardens to their source. The declaration also functioned in the community's teaching as a reminder that the promise is not exhausted by the images used to convey it: what is with God is described as better, and the sentence leaves the reward open to a reality the language of Gardens and rivers can only point toward.

**The Book's Parallel Descriptions**

The Book describes the reward of the mindful in similar terms in several passages: one promises Gardens beneath which rivers flow to those who believe and do good, to remain there forever; another describes the reward of those in awe of their Lord as Gardens of eternity with rivers beneath them, God pleased with them and they with Him; a third, addressed to those who persevered, records the greeting of peace and the exclamation about the excellence of the final home. This verse belongs to that family of descriptions, and the community's recitation of them together gave the promise its full shape.

**The Meaning of Reverence Here**

The people described are those who revere their Lord — the quality the sūrah has been recommending from its opening lines, the disposition connected in the earlier verses with guidance and appearing again in the passage about patience and forgiveness. The verse therefore identifies the reward not as the payment of a due but as the gift prepared for a particular condition of heart: the sūrah's closing movement has been describing that condition through the prayers and the conduct of the people who possess it, and this sentence names their destination — the reward attached to the disposition rather than to any external mark.

**The Comparison at the Sūrah's Close**

The verse stands near the end of the sūrah, and its placement gives the two destinations a final shape: the sūrah's message has been carried through the community's history, its failures, its obligations, and its arguments, and the last verses gather the outcome into two sentences. The community is left with the contrast between a brief enjoyment followed by the Fire and an abiding welcome from God — the final verse of the sūrah turning to the community's own duty, so the comparison here serves as the ground of the instruction that follows.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:199

> Indeed, there are some among the People of the Book who truly believe in Allah and what has been revealed to you ˹believers˺ and what was revealed to them. They humble themselves before Allah—never trading Allah's revelations for a fleeting gain. Their reward is with their Lord. Surely Allah is swift in reckoning

**The Exception Among the People of the Book**

The verse states that within the People of the Book there are people who hold belief in God, in the revelation granted to the Prophet's community, and in the revelation granted to their own community, and who are humble before God — qualifying the criticism the sūrah has directed at that group through the preceding verses. The Book distinguishes between the members of a group and the group as such, and does so in several passages of this sūrah: one states that the group is not uniform, describing an upright party within it whose members recite God's revelations through the night (3:113).

**The Occasion: the Death of the Negus**

The commentators connect the verse with the passing of the Negus, the ruler of Abyssinia who had sheltered the believers who left Makkah before the migration to Madinah — the report placing the verse in the middle of a controversy about how the community should regard a ruler who had protected its members without adopting its outward forms. The Prophet reported the ruler's death to his companions, arranged them in rows, and the funeral prayer was offered for him in his absence. Among the objections raised was the claim that the Negus had been a Christian — voiced, the reports say, both by sincere followers and by hypocrites using it to cast doubt on the Prophet's conduct. The verse answers such objections by describing the qualities of the person who is accepted rather than by debating the objections themselves.

**The Practice the Occasion Established**

The community's record shows how the verse was applied in practice: the funeral prayer for a man who had died in another land became a subject of the community's law. The jurists who permitted prayer for a person who dies away from his own town cited the case of the Negus, and those who restricted the practice to that single case argued that the Prophet's action was particular to him. Al-Nawawī recorded the position of the school of al-Shāfiʿī in favour of the practice, observing that the report about the Negus is sound and that its opponents have no answer to it. The verse thus has a place in the community's jurisprudence as well as its history — the verse describing the qualities of a believer among the People of the Book, and the Prophet's conduct toward a ruler who possessed them supplying the precedent.

**The Identity of the Believers Named**

The commentators offer several identifications for the people the verse describes: some name forty men from Najrān; others thirty-two from Abyssinia; others eight from Byzantine lands who had lived by the teaching of Jesus before they accepted the message. Mujāhid is reported to have said it covers every believer within that group, and others applied the verse to people like ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām, whose case the sūrah's earlier passages had in view. The variety reflects the openness of the sentence, which describes a quality rather than naming a group of individuals.

**The Qualities Listed**

The verse lists belief in God, belief in what was revealed to the community of the Prophet and in what was revealed to the earlier communities, humility before God, and the refusal to sell the signs of God for a small price — the last item recalling the earlier verse in which the People of the Book were described as casting the covenant behind them and trading it for a paltry amount (3:187). The qualities are arranged as a single description, and each element answers something the sūrah has criticised in the group: what replaces the concealment of the earlier verse is a refusal to trade the signs, and what replaces the arrogance of the group's learned men is humility. The verse states that the people described will have their reward with their Lord, closing with the declaration that God is swift in reckoning — a declaration the Book uses at the close of passages about the settling of accounts: the reward is placed with God rather than in the community's hands, and the closing declaration gives the sentence its urgency, since the account will be settled by the One who is swift at it.

**What the Verse Affirms — and Leaves Open**

The verse affirms that belonging to a community that has received a scripture does not by itself exclude a person from the reward: what counts is the belief, the humility, and the refusal to trade the signs, described without reference to genealogy or group membership. The commentators were generally silent about how such a person's belief relates to his position as a member of his own community — the questions that follow from the verse have occupied scholars in later periods, and the sūrah's other passages about the People of the Book are read alongside it. The silence is itself instructive: the verse states the qualities that matter and leaves the outcome to God, its own contribution being the recognition it extends and the qualities it names. The community that recites the verse is taught to judge by the description it gives — and the sūrah ends shortly after with an address to the believers, the last verse turning from the discussion of the other groups to the community's own obligations.
## Sūrah Āli ʿImrān 3:200

> O believers! Patiently endure, persevere, stand on guard, and be mindful of Allah, so you may be successful

**The Closing Address**

The sūrah ends with an address to the believers, calling them to patience, to contest in patience, to steadfast watchfulness, and to reverence of God — the four injunctions stated in succession, closing with the promise that those who fulfil them may prosper. The address gathers the sūrah's main themes into its final sentence: the community has been told about the trials it would face, the losses it had suffered, and the taunts it would hear; the last verse tells it what to do in the conditions the sūrah has described — and because the injunctions stand at the very end, the community carries them away as the summary of the whole chapter.

**Patience and Contest in Patience**

The first two injunctions come from the same root, one naming patience itself and the second naming a contest in patience in which a person should outlast the one he faces: al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī explained the second form as outdoing the enemy in endurance and firmness. The pairing turns patience from a private disposition into a shared contest — the Book's earlier instruction about the believers and their opponents used the same language of competition when it promised victory to the steadfast (3:139), and this verse applies the same standard to the whole community. The qualities named describe a person who neither surrenders to hardship nor lets it make him harsh.

**The Word for Steadfast Watchfulness**

The third injunction renders a word whose root carries the senses of tying, fortifying, and joining: the verb is used for holding a frontier post in defence of the community, and it is connected with the tethering of horses — as in the sūrah of the Spoils, which speaks of horses kept ready for battle. The word's range explains why the commentators treated this as the hardest of the four injunctions: what it names is a steady presence at the place where the community is exposed, the term belonging to the language of garrisons and watch-posts rather than to that of private virtue — the commentators connecting it with the frontier posts of the early community and with the discipline of a person who keeps his station.

**The Reports About the Watch**

The tradition preserved reports about guarding the frontier: one states that a single day spent in that service is better than the world and everything in it, a report found in Bukhārī and Muslim; other reports mention that the merit of such service continues after a person's death, since the defence of the community allows others to carry on their religious duties. The reports gave the verse a practical meaning for the community's defence, and the jurists discussed the conditions under which the duty applies — this sentence being used as one of the foundations of the discussion, the reports supplying its content.

**The Spiritual Reading**

The tradition also preserved a spiritual reading of the same word: in one report the Prophet described performing ablution under difficult conditions, walking repeatedly to the mosque, and waiting for one prayer after another as the reality of the watch — the waiting explained as remaining in remembrance and devotion between two of the prescribed prayers. Al-Nawawī compared this spiritual watch with the martial one in his explanation of the report, and the two readings were held together — the verse applied to both the defence of the community's territory and the discipline of the person's own soul, giving the community a way of treating the verse as a programme for ordinary life rather than only for the field of battle. The commentators also mention a well-known report in which the Prophet told the companions returning from a battle that they had come back from the lesser struggle to the greater one, identifying the greater struggle as the servant's struggle against his own passions — the report often cited in works of spirituality in connection with this verse. The distinction it draws is the one the closing injunctions require: the community was engaged in the visible struggle the sūrah has been describing, and the verse reminds it that the same qualities of patience and perseverance are needed in the struggle conducted within itself.

**The Reverence That Closes the Verse**

The last injunction returns to the quality the sūrah named at its opening, where the Book described itself as a guidance for those who are mindful of God (3:2–3) — the same quality being the condition attached to the community's patience in the verse about the trials it would meet (3:186). The sūrah therefore closes where it began, and the four injunctions are gathered under the last of them: reverence of God is presented as the disposition that makes the other three possible, and the community is told what it must maintain if the rest is to hold.

**What the Sūrah Leaves Its Readers With**

The promise is stated with the word the sūrah used for success in its earlier passages — the prosperity named being the outcome of the whole sūrah's teaching, left with the community at the end of the chapter. The address is the last word of the sūrah, and it turns to the believers after the long account of the other groups: what the sūrah has given them is a history, a set of warnings, and a description of the reward and the punishment; what it asks of them at the end is patience, contest, steadfast watchfulness, and the reverence of God.
